\paragraph{About this part.} Part~II surveys the models of the order book, from the simplest to the
most complex. It starts with the empirical facts any model must respect (Section~\ref{sec:facts});
moves through hand-built signals computed from the book (order-flow imbalance, queue imbalance, the
micro-price; Section~\ref{sec:ofi}); then to models of the queues themselves and the time until they
empty (Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}); to Hawkes processes, which model how events trigger further
events (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}). It then adds learning in three steps of decreasing structure:
meta-models, which feed the outputs of the classical models to a learner (Section~\ref{sec:meta});
structural hybrids, which build classical structure inside a network (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid}); and deep
networks that learn from the book with little imposed structure (Section~\ref{sec:deep}). It ends with reinforcement learning, which learns the trading
policy directly instead of a prediction (Section~\ref{sec:policy}). Information from
related instruments is treated with the signals (Section~\ref{sec:cross}), with Hawkes models and with deep models,
rather than in a separate section. Every model ends with a model card answering the same four questions.

% ================================================================
\section{Stylised facts a model should reproduce}
\label{sec:facts}
% ================================================================

Before turning to models, it helps to list what any adequate model of the book must be consistent
with. These regularities have been documented repeatedly across markets and periods.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Events cluster] Order-book events arrive in bursts separated by quiet periods, and a large share
  of events are triggered by earlier ones (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}).
\item[Long waits are common] Times between price changes have heavy tails; in a simple model of exactly
  balanced queues the expected waiting time is even infinite~\citep{contlarrard2013}.
\item[Order flow has long memory] The signs of successive market orders are positively correlated
  over very long lags, with a Hurst exponent of about $0.7$, mainly because large orders are split into
  many small ones~\citep{lillo2004,lillo2005}.
\item[Prices nevertheless diffuse] Despite persistent order flow, prices are close to unpredictable
  beyond short horizons, because limit orders absorb the pressure: the market is held near a critical
  balance between the two~\citep{bouchaud2004}.
\item[Short-horizon mean reversion] Measured volatility is higher over very short windows than over
  longer ones (bid--ask bounce), and correlations between assets fall when measured over very short
  windows (the Epps effect)~\citep{bacry2013}.
\item[The average book is humped] Average depth is largest a few ticks away from the best price, not
  at it~\citep{bouchaud2002}.
\item[Impact is concave] The price impact of a large order executed in pieces grows less than linearly
  with its size, close to a square-root law~\citep{bouchaud2018}; Hawkes models reproduce its concave, relaxing
  shape~\citep{bacry2014}.
\item[Order flow explains price changes] Over short windows, price changes are approximately linear in
  order-flow imbalance, with a slope inversely related to depth~\citep{cks2014}.
\item[Not only trades move prices] Roughly half of price-changing events are limit orders placed inside
  the spread~\citep{eisler2012}, and the book beyond the best quotes carries part of the price
  information~\citep{cao2009}.
\item[Activity is seasonal] Intensities and volatility follow intraday patterns, so models fitted with a
  constant baseline over a non-stationary period overstate how self-exciting the market is~\citep{filimonovsornette2015}.
\end{description}
Generative models are judged by how many of these they reproduce (Section~\ref{sec:deep-gen});
predictive models are judged by whether they exploit them after costs.

\paragraph{Presentation of each model.} Every model in this part ends with a \emph{model card} that
answers the same four questions: what has to be calibrated (the numbers estimated from data), how it is
calibrated, what the fitted model is then used for, and a picture that makes it easy to remember and
to teach. Descriptive features that are not models in their own right (Section~\ref{sec:shape}) get a
\emph{feature card} with the same four questions instead. The cards summarise the section they close; the detail and the citations are in the text
above each one.

% ================================================================
\section{Order-flow imbalance and queue imbalance}
\label{sec:ofi}
% ================================================================

The simplest useful models of the book are single numbers computed directly from it. This section
covers the two most important: \emph{order-flow imbalance}, which measures the net buying pressure
from the stream of book events, and \emph{queue imbalance}, which measures how lopsided the two best
queues are at an instant. It then describes further features of the book's shape beyond the best
level, and shows how a few such features can be mapped to a state from which the expected future price
is calibrated---the micro-price, a better estimate of the ``true'' price than the mid or the weighted
mid. It ends with order flow across related instruments and the lead--lag between futures and cash. These signals are cheap to compute and easy to interpret, and they serve as the
baselines for everything that follows.

\paragraph{Snapshot models.} Everything in this section is built from \emph{snapshots} of the book:
the sizes and prices visible at the best levels at a moment, or the change in them between one
moment and the next. Queue imbalance, the weighted mid, the micro-price and the shape features use a
single snapshot; order-flow imbalance uses the differences between consecutive snapshots. None of them
uses \emph{why} the book changed---whether lots left a queue because they were traded or because they
were cancelled, or which order did what. That information exists only in the stream of individual
events, and the next section turns to it: Section~\ref{sec:events} explains what event-level data add,
and the models that follow---queue depletion (Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}), Hawkes processes
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}) and the message-level networks of Section~\ref{sec:deep}---are \emph{event
models}, built on that stream.

\subsection{Order-flow imbalance}

\paragraph{The idea.} Why does the price move? Look again at Figure~\ref{fig:book}. The best ask
is 5000.25 with 14 lots waiting. If buyers take all 14 lots, or the sellers waiting there cancel,
the best ask becomes 5000.50 and the mid rises. If instead more sellers join at 5000.25, the queue
grows and a rise becomes harder. The same holds in mirror image on the bid side. So anything that
\emph{grows the bid queue or shrinks the ask queue} pushes the price up, and anything that
\emph{shrinks the bid queue or grows the ask queue} pushes it down. Order-flow imbalance (OFI)
simply keeps a running score of these pushes~\citep{cks2014}.

\paragraph{Measuring one event.} Visible changes at the best prices come from three event types:
limit orders arriving, aggressive orders executing against waiting ones, and cancellations. For
event number $n$, define
\[
  \Delta v^{b}_{n} = v^{b}_{1,t_n} - v^{b}_{1,t_n-},
  \qquad
  \Delta v^{a}_{n} = v^{a}_{1,t_n} - v^{a}_{1,t_n-}.
\]
Recall that $v^{b}_{1,t}$ is the number of lots waiting at the best bid at time $t$, and that
$t_n-$ means ``just before event $n$''. So $\Delta v^{b}_{n}$ is \emph{the size of the best-bid
queue just after event $n$ minus its size just before}: how many lots event $n$ added to the
best-bid queue (positive) or removed from it (negative). $\Delta v^{a}_{n}$ is the same for the
best-ask queue. In the book of Figure~\ref{fig:book}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item a new 20-lot buy order at 5000.00 gives $\Delta v^b_n = +20$ (29 lots become 49), and
  $\Delta v^a_n = 0$;
\item a sell market order for 10 lots trades against the front of the bid queue and gives
  $\Delta v^b_n = -10$;
\item a seller cancelling 3 lots at 5000.25 gives $\Delta v^a_n = -3$ (14 lots become 11).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Scoring and adding up.} While the best prices do not change, the score of event $n$
and the order-flow imbalance over the last $w$ seconds are
\begin{equation}
  e_n = \Delta v^{b}_{n} - \Delta v^{a}_{n},
  \qquad
  \OFI^{(w)}_t = \sum_{n:\, t_n \in (t-w,\, t]} e_n .
\end{equation}
\inwords{$e_n$ is positive when event $n$ grew the bid queue or shrank the ask queue---a push up---and
negative for a push down; subtracting $\Delta v^{a}_{n}$ is what turns a shrinking ask queue into a
positive score. The sum runs over every event $n$ whose time $t_n$ falls in the last $w$ seconds,
so $\OFI^{(w)}_t$ is the net buying pressure over that window, like the net flow into a tank: lots
in on the buy side and out on the sell side count as inflow, the reverse as outflow.}

When a best price \emph{does} change, the full definition of~\citet{cks2014} counts whole queues.
If the best bid rises to a new, higher price, the entire new queue counts as added buying interest;
if the best-bid level is emptied and the best bid falls, the entire old queue counts as removed. The
ask side is treated symmetrically. This keeps the score meaningful across price changes.

\paragraph{From pressure to price.} The associated short-horizon relation is a straight line,
\begin{equation}
  m_t - m_{t-w} = \beta\, \OFI^{(w)}_t + \varepsilon_t,
\end{equation}
where $\beta$ is the price-impact coefficient and $\varepsilon_t$ a residual.
\inwords{over a short window, the mid moves roughly in proportion to the net buying pressure.
$\beta$ converts lots of pressure into price: if $\beta$ is $0.01$ points per lot, a net 100 lots of
pressure moves the mid by about one point. $\varepsilon_t$ is whatever the line does not explain.
Fitting $\beta$ is an ordinary linear regression of price change on OFI.}
\citet{cks2014} find that $\beta$ is inversely related to market depth: in a thin book a few lots
empty a queue and move the price, while in a deep book the same lots barely dent it. OFI is
low-dimensional, cheap to compute and easy to interpret, which makes it the natural baseline
against which more complex models should be judged.

\begin{example}[OFI by hand]
\label{ex:ofi}
Five events at unchanged best prices, all inside one window of length $w$:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{clrrr}
\toprule
$n$ & event & $\Delta v^b_n$ & $\Delta v^a_n$ & $e_n$ \\
\midrule
1 & bid limit order, 20 lots & $+20$ & $0$ & $+20$ \\
2 & ask cancel, 15 lots & $0$ & $-15$ & $+15$ \\
3 & buy market order hits ask, 10 lots & $0$ & $-10$ & $+10$ \\
4 & ask limit order, 30 lots & $0$ & $+30$ & $-30$ \\
5 & bid cancel, 5 lots & $-5$ & $0$ & $-5$ \\
\midrule
 & & & $\OFI^{(w)}_t$ & $+10$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Events 2 and 3 enter through identical arithmetic, yet one is a cancellation and the other an
aggressive trade. OFI does not distinguish them; Section~\ref{sec:events} returns to this.
\end{example}

\subsection{Evidence and extensions}

\paragraph{Explanatory power of OFI for price changes.} On 50 US stocks, \citet{cks2014} find that the
linear relation explains mid-price changes over short intervals with an average $R^2$ of about
65\%---$R^2$, the coefficient of determination, being the fraction of the variance of price changes
that the fitted line explains---against about 32\% for the imbalance of \emph{trades} alone; adding
trade imbalance to OFI raises the average only to about 67\%. The slope is close to inversely
proportional to average depth, $\beta \propto 1/\text{depth}$. The fit varies a great deal across
stocks---from above 90\% for some to below 15\% for others---which is itself a warning against
quoting a single number for ``the'' predictability of order flow. The lesson that limit orders and
cancellations carry as much price information as trades is one of the most robust in the field.

\paragraph{Looking deeper than the best level.} Information also sits beyond the best quotes. On
the Australian Stock Exchange, \citet{cao2009} attribute about 22\% of price discovery to the book
beyond the best bid and offer, and find that imbalances deeper in the book predict short-term
returns even after controlling for the autocorrelation of returns, the inside spread and the imbalance
of trades. \emph{Multi-level OFI} computes the
imbalance separately at each of the first $L$ levels, giving a vector rather than a number; for six
Nasdaq stocks, \citet{xu2019mlofi} find that the out-of-sample fit improves with every additional
level included. \citet{contcucuringu2023} combine the level-wise imbalances into a single
\emph{integrated OFI}, the first principal component of the multi-level vector, which captures over
89\% of its variance. On the 100 largest S\&P~500 stocks over 2017--2019 the integrated OFI raises
the average out-of-sample $R^2$ of the contemporaneous price-impact regression from about 65\%
(best level only) to about 84\%.

\paragraph{Horizon and reversal.} The predictive content of order imbalance depends on the horizon.
In Chinese equities, \citet{zhang2025oi} find that order imbalance predicts returns positively from
5 to 30 minutes ahead and negatively from 60 to 120 minutes ahead, with no reversal for large or
heavily traded stocks. The usual interpretation is that part of the price pressure from imbalance
is temporary and is undone as liquidity is replenished. A signal must be used over the horizon on
which it has the sign one assumes.

\paragraph{Estimating the slope in real time.} Because $\beta$ moves with depth, it is re-estimated
continuously in practice: by a rolling-window regression, by normalising OFI by recent average
depth (a \emph{depth-normalised} OFI), or by treating $\beta_t$ as a hidden state that drifts over
time and tracking it with a Kalman filter~\citep{kalman1960}, the standard recursive estimator for
linear systems observed with noise.

\paragraph{Uses of OFI.} The same quantity serves several decisions. As a directional
signal, it predicts the sign of the next short-horizon move. For a market maker, it sets the
\emph{skew} of quotes---leaning bids and offers in the direction of pressure---and informs inventory
control~\citep{cartea2015}. For execution, it indicates when resting passively is likely to be
rewarded and when the order book is about to move away.

\begin{modelcard}{Order-flow imbalance}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The price-impact slope $\beta$ for a chosen window $w$ (and, for
multi-level OFI, one slope per level or the weights of the integrated OFI).}
\cardrow{How}{An ordinary least-squares regression of mid changes on $\OFI^{(w)}_t$. Because $\beta$
falls as depth rises, it is re-estimated on a rolling window, absorbed by dividing OFI by recent
depth, or tracked as a drifting hidden state with a Kalman filter. Integrated OFI takes the first
principal component of the level-wise OFIs.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{A baseline directional signal; the skew of a market maker's quotes; a
warning, for a passive order, that the book is about to move away from it.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{Net flow into a tank: lots joining the bid or leaving the ask are inflow,
lots joining the ask or leaving the bid are outflow; the level of the tank leads the price.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Queue imbalance and richer book features}

Queue imbalance compresses the top of book into one number,
\begin{equation}
  \QI_t = \frac{v^b_{1,t} - v^a_{1,t}}{v^b_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t}} \in [-1, 1].
  \label{eq:qi}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the difference between the best-bid and best-ask queue sizes, divided by their total so
the result does not depend on how busy the market is. It is $+1$ when only buyers are queued, $-1$
when only sellers are, and $0$ when the two queues are equal.}

\paragraph{Queue imbalance and the next price move.}
The mid moves when one of the two best queues is used up. If the book is skewed---many lots on one
side, few on the other---the thin queue needs far less trading and cancelling to disappear than the
thick one, so it is likely to be emptied first; later sections call this ``the thin side breaks first''. The next price move is therefore more likely to be
\emph{towards the thin side}: a thin ask is likely to be taken out, and the price ticks up; a thin
bid is likely to be hit, and the price ticks down. Think of two piles of sand being removed at
roughly the same rate: the smaller pile is gone first. Stochastic models of the book make this
precise, computing the probability of an up move as a function of the two queue sizes~\citep{cst2010}.

\begin{example}[Reading the imbalance in Figure~\ref{fig:book}]
The best bid has 29 lots and the best ask 14, so
$\QI_t = (29 - 14)/(29 + 14) \approx 0.35$: the book leans towards the buyers. The ask queue is the
thinner one and is likely to break first, so the next change in the mid is more likely to be up
than down. For a trader the skew has two consequences. A new passive buy
order would join the back of the long 29-lot queue and probably wait while the price moves away
from it. A new passive sell order would join the short ask queue and probably be filled---just
before the up move that the imbalance predicted, which is adverse selection
(Section~\ref{sec:adverse}).
\end{example}

\paragraph{The size-weighted mid-price.} The same intuition gives a better single-number price
than the mid. The \emph{weighted mid} weights each side's price by the size on the \emph{other}
side,
\begin{equation}
  m^{\text{wmid}}_t = \frac{v^b_{1,t}\, p^a_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t}\, p^b_{1,t}}{v^b_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t}},
\end{equation}
\inwords{when the bid queue is much larger than the ask queue, the price is pulled towards the ask,
the side expected to break, and vice versa. With equal queues it equals the mid. In
Figure~\ref{fig:book} it is $5000.00 + 0.25 \times 29/43 \approx 5000.169$, above the mid of
5000.125.}

\paragraph{Limits of the intuition.} Displayed size is not committed size. A thin queue may be
refilled immediately by new orders; hidden or iceberg orders may make the thin side thicker than it
looks; and orders placed only to be cancelled (spoofing, below) can create a
skew that is not real. Imbalance is therefore a strong but noisy signal.

\paragraph{Spoofing and layering.}\label{sec:spoofing} Displayed liquidity is not necessarily committed liquidity.
Manipulative orders do not falsify OFI---OFI still measures observed changes---but they alter the
mapping from displayed flow to future executable prices, and they distort queue-imbalance, depth
and depletion estimates.

\paragraph{Documented cases.} Manipulation of the order book is not hypothetical. On the Korea Exchange,
\citet{lee2013spoof} document traders placing large orders away from the market to create imbalance and
withdrawing them. A trader in the E-mini S\&P~500 was charged with layering over five years, conduct the
CFTC alleged contributed to the conditions of the May 2010 flash crash~\citep{cftc2015sarao}. In 2020
JPMorgan paid \$920 million over spoofing in precious-metals and US Treasury futures over at least eight
years~\citep{cftc2020jpm}. In a spoofing episode in Ultra Treasury bond futures, \citet{debie2023} measure ask-side
liquidity costs falling from about 10.4 to about 8 basis points when the spoof order was placed and
recovering after it was cancelled---a displayed improvement in liquidity that was not real.
\citet{cartea2020spoof} model spoofing as an optimal-control problem in which the spoofer trades the gain
from skewing the order imbalance against the expected regulatory penalty. Quote stuffing---bursts of
order messages---affected over 74\% of US-listed securities at least once in 2010, with lower liquidity
and higher short-term volatility during episodes~\citep{egginton2016}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Effects of contamination on order-book signals.}
\label{tab:contamination}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{l>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Form & Signature in the data & Effect on signals \\
\midrule
Spoofing & large orders away from the touch, cancelled quickly & OFI and imbalance point the wrong way \\
Layering & several non-genuine orders at successive levels & distorts depth, slope and multi-level OFI \\
Quote stuffing & bursts of submissions and cancellations & inflates event counts; breaks event-clock assumptions \\
Icebergs and hidden orders & repeated executions at one price with stable visible size & displayed depth understates liquidity; depletion and fill estimates biased \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Mitigations include down-weighting orders that are cancelled within a short time, features built from
cancellation rates and from the ratio of trades to orders, and detection of iceberg refills. They reduce
but do not remove the problem.

\paragraph{Evidence on queue imbalance.} \citet{gould2016qi} fit a logistic regression of the direction of the next
mid move on queue imbalance for ten Nasdaq stocks. The relationship is strongly significant for
every stock, but its value depends on the tick size: out of sample, queue imbalance improves binary
classification over a null model by about 50--60\% for large-tick stocks and only about 10--30\% for
small-tick stocks, and probabilistic classification by about 20--30\% against about 2--6\%. Queue
imbalance is most informative exactly where queues are long and the price rarely moves---the
large-tick regime that ES and ZN futures also belong to.

\begin{example}[Same imbalance, different history]
\label{ex:qi}
Bid 300, ask 100 gives $\QI_t = (300-100)/(300+100) = 0.5$. In book~A the ask fell from 400 to 100
because a 300-lot market buy lifted it. In book~B it fell because 300 lots were cancelled. Both
books have the same snapshot and the same $\QI_t$. A trader who saw the messages knows that A
contains an aggressive buyer and B does not.
\end{example}

\begin{modelcard}{Queue imbalance and weighted mid}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{Nothing for $\QI_t$ or $m^{\text{wmid}}_t$ themselves. To turn $\QI_t$
into a probability, the coefficients of a logistic regression of the next mid-move direction on
$\QI_t$~\citep{gould2016qi}.}
\cardrow{How}{Count, over historical data, which way the mid moved next after each observed value
of the imbalance, and fit the logistic curve by maximum likelihood; report it separately for
large-tick and small-tick instruments, where its value differs.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Predict the direction of the next mid change; decide which side a
passive order should rest on and when it is likely to be adversely selected.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{Two piles of sand removed at about the same rate: the smaller pile is gone
first, and the price moves towards the side whose pile disappeared.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{The shape of the book}
\label{sec:shape}

Unlike the subsections around it, this one does not present a model. It lists \emph{features}: numbers
computed from the book that describe how liquidity is spread across price levels. A feature makes no
statement about what happens next; it becomes predictive only as an input to a model---a regression,
a classifier, a queue model or a network of the later sections---and its value has to be shown there.
The one entry that involves a model, resilience, is a quantity read off a fitted Hawkes model
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}), not a model of its own.

Beyond the best level, several features summarise how liquidity is distributed:
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Multi-level imbalance] the queue imbalance computed with sizes summed over the best $\ell$
  levels on each side, for several $\ell$.
\item[Book slope] how fast cumulative depth grows with distance from the best price, usually the
  fitted slope of cumulative size against price distance, separately for each side. A steep slope
  means a deep book that absorbs large orders.
\item[Curvature] the change of that slope with distance---whether depth is concentrated near the
  touch or further out. Its empirical relevance is tied to the average shape of the book, which in
  equities is humped: depth is usually largest a few ticks away from the best price, not at
  it~\citep{bouchaud2002}.
\item[Depth-weighted and volume-weighted prices] averages of the prices at the first $\ell$ levels,
  weighted by their sizes; the weighted mid is the one-level case.
\item[Resilience] how quickly the book refills after a large trade. \citet{large2007} measures it with
  a ten-variate Hawkes model on an LSE stock and finds that when the book does refill, the impulse
  responses have half-lives under 20 seconds, but that a resilient response follows a large trade in
  under 40\% of cases.
\item[Depth-normalised quantities] order-flow measures divided by recent average depth, motivated by
  the inverse relation between price impact and depth.
\end{description}


\paragraph{Imbalance and spread are features too.} The quantities of the previous subsection belong
on the same list: queue imbalance, its $[0,1]$ form $\iota_t$, and the spread $s_t$ are also numbers
computed from a snapshot of the book. The next subsection uses two of them to build a model.

\begin{featurecard}{Book-shape features}
\cardrow{What you compute}{Imbalance summed over the first $\ell$ levels; the slope and curvature of
cumulative depth against distance from the best price; depth-weighted prices; depth-normalised order
flow; for resilience, the impulse response of a fitted Hawkes model.}
\cardrow{How}{Directly from each snapshot of the book: sums and weighted averages over levels, and
least-squares fits of cumulative size against price distance, per side. Resilience requires fitting the
Hawkes model of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes} first~\citep{large2007}. The only choices are the number of
levels and the fitting window.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Feed them as inputs to a predictive or execution model, normalise other
signals by depth, and judge how far a large order would walk the book. Any predictive value must be
demonstrated by the model that uses them.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{The profile of a hillside on each side of a valley: a steep hillside
holds a lot of liquidity close to the price, a shallow one makes large orders walk far.}
\end{featurecard}

\subsection{The micro-price}
\label{sec:microprice}

\paragraph{The idea: from book features to a state, and from a state to a forecast.} The micro-price
turns the features of the previous subsection into a forecast in three steps.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Describe the book by a few features.} Out of everything visible in the book, keep a small
  number of features that matter for where the price goes next. The micro-price keeps two: the
  imbalance $\iota_t$ and the spread $s_t$.
\item \textbf{Map the features to a state.} Cut each feature into a few ranges---imbalance below
  $0.2$, between $0.2$ and $0.4$, and so on; spread of one tick or two---so that every possible book falls
  into one of a finite list of \emph{states}. Two books that differ in detail but fall in the same
  ranges are treated as the same state. This is a deliberate compression: thousands of possible books
  become a few dozen boxes.
\item \textbf{Calibrate, for each state, what the price does next.} Go through historical data, and every
  time the book was in a given state, record what the mid did afterwards. The average is the expected
  price change from that state. Adding it to the current mid gives the forecast.
\end{enumerate}
\inwords{map today's book to a box, look up what the price did on average the many other times the
book was in that box, and add that to the mid. The table of boxes and their average price changes is
the whole model.}

There are two ways to do step 3, and both are calibration to market data:
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Direct] For each state, average the mid change observed over the next $k$ price changes, every
  time the book was in that state. This measures the outcome directly and needs no further
  assumption, but each state sees relatively few complete $k$-change histories, so the averages are
  noisy, and the horizon $k$ must be chosen.
\item[Chained (Stoikov)] Calibrate only what happens on the \emph{next} step from each state---to
  which state the book moves, and whether and how far the mid moves. Such one-step transitions are
  plentiful in the data. Then chain them: if the state evolves as a Markov chain, the expected price
  change over $k$ steps, and in the limit over all future steps, follows from the one-step frequencies
  by matrix algebra. The formulas below do exactly this; they compute the expectation from calibrated
  one-step probabilities, not from first principles.
\end{description}
The chained method uses the data more efficiently and gives the long-run limit without choosing a
horizon; it is exact only to the extent that the Markov assumption holds. The direct method is the check:
if the two disagree for a state, the chain is missing something.

Two features are a choice, not a requirement. Any of the shape features of
Section~\ref{sec:shape}---second-level depth, slope---or recent order flow could be added to the state.
Each addition multiplies the number of states and leaves fewer observations per state, so the estimates
become noisier: the same trade-off between richer description and evidence that runs through this
survey (Principle~2).


The weighted mid has three defects~\citep{stoikov2018}. It is not a \emph{martingale}---its
expected future value is not its current value, so it is a biased estimate of where the price is
going. It is noisy, especially when the spread widens. And it can move the wrong way: if the last
lot on a thin ask is cancelled, the ask moves up a tick, yet the weighted mid can fall. The
\emph{micro-price} of \citet{stoikov2018} replaces the ad hoc weighting by an estimate of the
expected future mid itself.

\paragraph{Definition.} Let $\eta_1 < \eta_2 < \cdots$ be the successive times after $t$ at which
the mid changes, and let $s_t$ be the spread. The micro-price measures imbalance as the bid side's
share of the volume at the two best prices,
\begin{equation}
  \iota_t = \frac{v^b_{1,t}}{v^b_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t}} \in [0, 1].
\end{equation}
\inwords{$v^b_{1,t}$ and $v^a_{1,t}$ are the numbers of lots waiting at the best bid and the best ask,
so $\iota_t$ is the fraction of those lots that are on the buying side. In Figure~\ref{fig:book},
with 29 lots bid and 14 offered, $\iota_t = 29/43 \approx 0.67$: two thirds of the top-of-book volume
is waiting to buy. It is $0.5$ when the two queues are equal, near $1$ when almost all the volume is
on the bid, and near $0$ when almost all of it is on the ask.}
This is the same information as the \emph{queue imbalance} $\QI_t$ of
Equation~(\ref{eq:qi}), the difference between the two best queue sizes divided by their sum, which
runs from $-1$ to $1$ instead of from $0$ to $1$. The two are related by
\[
  \iota_t = \frac{1 + \QI_t}{2},
\]
because 
\[
  1 + \QI_t = \big((v^b_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t}) + (v^b_{1,t} - v^a_{1,t})\big)/(v^b_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t})
= 2v^b_{1,t}/(v^b_{1,t} + v^a_{1,t}).
\]
In the example, $\QI_t \approx 0.35$ and $(1 + 0.35)/2 \approx
0.67$. \citet{stoikov2018} works with the $[0,1]$ form, which reads directly as a share.

With these ingredients, the micro-price is the limit of the expected mid after more and more price changes,
\begin{equation}
  m^{\text{micro}}_t = \lim_{k\to\infty} \E\big[m_{\eta_k} \mid \iota_t, s_t\big].
\end{equation}
\inwords{start from the current imbalance and spread, and ask where the mid will be after the next
price change, after the one after that, and so on. The answers settle down to a value; that value is
the micro-price. By construction it is the best estimate, given the top of the book and under the
model's assumptions, of where the price is heading.}

\paragraph{Reading the definition piece by piece.} The equation packs four ideas into one line.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[The times $\eta_1 < \eta_2 < \cdots$] The mid does not move continuously. It stays flat while
  orders join and leave the two best queues, and jumps when one of them is emptied or an order is
  placed inside the spread. $\eta_1$ is the moment of the next such jump after now, $\eta_2$ the jump
  after that, and so on; $m_{\eta_1}$ is ``the mid just after the next price change''. Counting in
  price changes rather than in seconds means the definition does not depend on how busy the market
  is.
\item[The imbalance $\iota_t$] the bid side's share of the top-of-book volume, defined above.
\item[The conditioning ``$\mid \iota_t, s_t$''] The expectation uses only two numbers: the
  imbalance and the spread. Deeper levels and the history of messages are deliberately left out. This
  is what ``given the top of the book'' means, and it is a modelling choice, not a law.
\item[The limit $k \to \infty$] For each $k$, $\E[m_{\eta_k} \mid \iota_t, s_t]$ is the average mid
  after $k$ price changes, over all the ways the book could evolve from today's state. As $k$ grows,
  today's imbalance tells us less and less about each further change, so these averages stop moving.
  The value they settle on is the micro-price.
\end{description}

\paragraph{Limits of a one-step expectation.} The expected mid after one change,
$\E[m_{\eta_1} \mid \iota_t, s_t]$, already uses the imbalance, and it is tempting to call it the fair
price. But price changes are partly undone: after the thin ask breaks and the mid ticks up, the new
book is often lopsided the other way, and the next change is more likely to be down (the bid--ask
bounce of Section~\ref{sec:facts}). The one-step answer overshoots. Following the book through further
changes adds these predictable corrections, and the limit is a price with no predictable drift left
in it: its expected value after any number of further changes is itself. That is the
\emph{martingale} property the weighted mid lacks, and it is why the micro-price, in contrast to the weighted
mid, is an unbiased estimate of where the price is going---under the model's assumptions.

Two assumptions make it computable: the pair $(\iota_t, s_t)$ evolves as a Markov chain, and the
dynamics do not depend on the price level itself. Then
\begin{equation}
  \begin{gathered}
  m^{\text{micro}}_t = m_t + \sum_{k=1}^{\infty} g^{(k)}(\iota_t, s_t), \\
  g^{(1)}(\iota, s) = \E\big[m_{\eta_1} - m_t \mid \iota, s\big], \qquad
  g^{(k+1)}(\iota, s) = \E\big[g^{(k)}(\iota_{\eta_1}, s_{\eta_1}) \mid \iota, s\big].
  \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the first term is the expected size of the next price change; each further term is the
expected next change, averaged over the states the book can be in after the previous one. The
micro-price is the mid plus the sum of all these expected adjustments.}

\noindent The recursion is the step-by-step reading of the limit. $g^{(1)}(\iota, s)$ answers ``how
far, on average, does the mid move at the next change, starting from state $(\iota, s)$?''. To get
$g^{(2)}$, look at every state $(\iota_{\eta_1}, s_{\eta_1})$ the book can be in just after that first
change, ask the same one-step question from each of them, and average the answers, weighting each
state by how likely the book is to land there. $g^{(3)}$ repeats this one change further out. Because
the chain does not depend on the price level, the same function $g^{(1)}$ can be reused from every
state; only the starting point moves.

\begin{example}[The micro-price of Figure~\ref{fig:book}, step by step]
\label{ex:microprice}
The book of Figure~\ref{fig:book} has 29 lots at the best bid (5000.00), 14 at the best ask
(5000.25), a one-tick spread and a mid of $m_t = 5000.125$, so $\iota_t = 29/43 \approx 0.67$. The
probabilities below are stylised; in practice they are the counts of the estimation procedure that
follows.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \emph{First change.} Suppose that, in the data, books in this state see the mid move up next 70\%
  of the time and down 30\% of the time. With a one-tick spread the mid moves by half a tick, $0.125$,
  either way: if the ask queue empties, the ask rises one tick and the mid rises $0.125$; if the bid
  queue empties, the mid falls $0.125$. So
  \[
    g^{(1)} = 0.7 \times 0.125 + 0.3 \times (-0.125) = 0.05 .
  \]
\item \emph{Second change.} After an up move the new book tends to lean the other way, and suppose the
  expected next change from those states is $-0.02$; after a down move suppose it is $+0.02$. Weighting
  by how likely each first move was,
  \[
    g^{(2)} = 0.7 \times (-0.02) + 0.3 \times (+0.02) = -0.008 .
  \]
This is the bounce: part of the expected first move is expected to be undone.
\item \emph{Third change and beyond.} Repeating the procedure gives smaller and smaller terms, say
  $g^{(3)} = +0.002$ and the rest negligible.
\item \emph{Add them up.}
  \[
    m^{\text{micro}}_t \approx 5000.125 + 0.05 - 0.008 + 0.002 = 5000.169 .
  \]
\end{enumerate}
The successive estimates of the future mid are $5000.175$ after one change, $5000.167$ after two and
$5000.169$ after three: they oscillate and settle. In this stylised case the result happens to be close
to the weighted mid ($5000.169$); in general the two differ, because the micro-price takes its weights
from transitions observed in the data rather than from a fixed formula.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Estimation.} In practice the imbalance is split into $n_\iota$ buckets (for example by
quantiles) and the spread into $n_s$ values, so the bucketed state $(\iota, s)$ takes $n_\iota n_s$
values. From data one counts three kinds of transition from each state: to another state with the mid
unchanged, collected in a matrix $\mathbf{U}$; to each possible size of mid change, in a matrix
$\mathbf{J}^{(1)}$; and to another state with the mid changed, in a matrix $\mathbf{J}^{(2)}$. With
$\boldsymbol{\Delta}$ the vector of possible mid changes and 
\[
  \mathbf{B} = (I -
\mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{(2)},
\]

\[
  g^{(1)} = (I - \mathbf{U})^{-1} \mathbf{J}^{(1)} \boldsymbol{\Delta}, \qquad
  g^{(k+1)} = \mathbf{B}\, g^{(k)}.
\]
\inwords{$g^{(1)}$ is the expected next mid change from each state, after any number of steps on which
the book reshuffles without the mid moving. $\mathbf{B}$ gives, for each starting state, the
probabilities of the states the book lands in just after the next mid change, so multiplying by
$\mathbf{B}$ carries an adjustment one price change further out.}
The factor $(I-\mathbf{U})^{-1}$ sums over all the steps in which the book changes without
the mid moving---the same geometric-series device used for the Hawkes model in Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}. The data
are symmetrised before estimation (each observation is added again with bid and ask swapped and the
price change negated), which guarantees that the series converges. Stoikov reports that a handful
of terms suffices in practice, and that the micro-price predicts short-term price changes better
than either the mid or the weighted mid, for both a large-tick and a small-tick stock.

\paragraph{Computing it in practice.} The computation splits into an offline part, done once on
historical data, which produces a table of adjustments, and an online part, done at every book
update, which is a table look-up. Write 
\[
  g^{\star}(\iota, s) = \sum_{k \ge 1} g^{(k)}(\iota, s)
\]
for
the total adjustment, so that 
\[
  m^{\text{micro}}_t = m_t + g^{\star}(\iota_t, s_t).
\]


\emph{Offline: estimate the table $g^{\star}$.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Define the states.} The chain needs a finite list of states, so the two continuous
  inputs are turned into categories.
  \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \emph{Imbalance.} Recall that $\iota = v^b_1/(v^b_1 + v^a_1)$ is the share of the lots at the
    two best prices that sit on the bid: a number between 0 and 1. Compute it for every historical
    observation of the book. Then choose a number of buckets $n_\iota$ and split $[0, 1]$ into that
    many intervals. The simplest split is by equal width; with $n_\iota = 5$ the buckets are $[0, 0.2)$,
    $[0.2, 0.4)$, $[0.4, 0.6)$, $[0.6, 0.8)$ and $[0.8, 1]$. An alternative is to split by
    \emph{quantiles}: sort all the historical values of $\iota$ and place the cut points so that
    each bucket holds the same number of observations, which avoids buckets that are almost empty.
    Because the symmetrisation step mirrors
    every observation ($\iota \to 1 - \iota$), the cut points should be symmetric around $0.5$---for
    quantile buckets, compute the quantiles on the values of $\iota$ together with their mirrors
    $1 - \iota$---so that the mirror of a bucket is again a bucket.
  \item \emph{Spread.} Measure the spread in ticks, $s/\delta$, and keep only the values that occur
    often---for example one and two ticks for a large-tick contract---discarding the rare
    observations with wider spreads.
  \item \emph{State.} A state is a pair (imbalance bucket, spread in ticks); with $n_\iota$ buckets
    and $n_s$ spread values there are $n_\iota n_s$ states, which can be numbered $0, 1, \dots,
    n_\iota n_s - 1$, for instance as $\text{bucket} + n_\iota \times (\text{spread in ticks} - 1)$.
  \end{itemize}
  For the book of Figure~\ref{fig:book}, $\iota = 29/43 \approx 0.67$ and the spread is one tick; with
  the five equal-width buckets above, $\iota$ falls in the fourth bucket, $[0.6, 0.8)$ (number 3 when
  counting from 0), so the state is number $3 + 5 \times 0 = 3$.
\item \textbf{Understand what a transition carries.} The state contains no price: it is only the
  imbalance bucket and the spread. The price is tracked alongside the chain: every transition from one
  state to the next carries a change in the mid, which is zero on most steps. Leaving the price level out
  of the state is the second modelling assumption above---the dynamics do not depend on the level, only
  on changes. Two kinds of step occur.
  \begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.2em,itemsep=1pt]
  \item \emph{The book reshuffles at unchanged prices.} Orders join or leave the two best queues, or
    trade against them without emptying them. The best prices stay put, so the mid does not change,
    but the queue sizes do, and $\iota$ may move to another bucket. These steps are the transitions
    in $\mathbf{U}$.
  \item \emph{A best price changes.} A best queue is emptied, or an order is placed inside the spread.
    The mid jumps, and the best queues are now \emph{different} queues---for example the old second
    level of the ask becomes the new best ask---so $\iota$ and the spread jump to new values as well.
    These steps are recorded in $\mathbf{J}^{(1)}$ (how far the mid moved) and $\mathbf{J}^{(2)}$
    (which state the book landed in).
  \end{itemize}
  In the book of Figure~\ref{fig:book} (state 3: $\iota \approx 0.67$, one-tick spread), suppose the 14
  lots at the best ask are bought or cancelled and the next ask level, 5000.50, holds 40 lots. The best
  ask is now 5000.50, the best bid is still 5000.00 with 29 lots, the spread is two ticks, the mid has
  risen from 5000.125 to 5000.25, and $\iota = 29/69 \approx 0.42$. The chain has gone from state
  (bucket 3, one tick) to state (bucket 2, two ticks), and the transition carries a mid change of
  $+0.125$. If a buyer then places an order at 5000.25, inside the spread, the spread returns to one
  tick and the mid rises again, to 5000.375: another transition, carrying another $+0.125$.
\item \textbf{Record transitions.} For each pair of consecutive observations of the book, record the
  state before, the state after, and the change in the mid between them. Most changes are zero; the
  others are multiples of half a tick.
\item \textbf{Symmetrise.} Append a mirrored copy of every record: imbalance bucket $\iota \to$ its mirror
  ($1 - \iota$), same spread, mid change negated. Buying and selling are then treated alike, and the
  sum of adjustments converges.
\item \textbf{Count and normalise.} For each starting state, divide the counts of each kind of
  transition by the total number of transitions out of that state. This gives $\mathbf{U}$ (moves to
  another state, mid unchanged), $\mathbf{J}^{(1)}$ (mid moves by each possible amount) and
  $\mathbf{J}^{(2)}$ (moves to another state, mid changed). Each row of $\mathbf{U}$ and
  $\mathbf{J}^{(1)}$ together sums to one.
\item \textbf{Matrix algebra.} Compute $(I - \mathbf{U})^{-1}$, then 
\[
  g^{(1)} = (I -
  \mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta}
\]
and 
\[
  \mathbf{B} = (I -
  \mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{(2)},
\]
then $g^{(2)} = \mathbf{B}g^{(1)}$, $g^{(3)} = \mathbf{B}g^{(2)}$
  and so on, and add them; a handful of terms suffices. The result is one number per state.
\end{enumerate}

\emph{Online: at every book update.} Compute $\iota_t$ and $s_t$ from the two best queues, find the
state they fall in, and add the stored adjustment to the mid: 
\[
  m^{\text{micro}}_t = m_t +
g^{\star}(\iota_t, s_t).
\]
The cost is one division, one look-up and one addition.

\begin{example}[The matrix computation with two states]
\label{ex:microprice-matrix}
Take a one-tick spread throughout and two imbalance buckets: L ($\iota < 0.5$, more volume at the ask)
and H ($\iota \ge 0.5$). The mid moves by $\boldsymbol{\Delta} = (-0.125, +0.125)$. Suppose the counts
give, with rows and columns ordered (L, H),
\[
  \mathbf{U} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.6 & 0.2 \\ 0.2 & 0.6 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad
  \mathbf{J}^{(1)} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.15 & 0.05 \\ 0.05 & 0.15 \end{pmatrix}
  \ \text{(columns: down, up)},
\]
so from L the next step is a down move with probability $0.15$ and an up move with probability $0.05$,
and the reverse from H; each row of $\mathbf{U}$ and $\mathbf{J}^{(1)}$ together sums to one.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item 
\[
  I - \mathbf{U} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.4 & -0.2 \\ -0.2 & 0.4 \end{pmatrix},
\]
with determinant $0.12$,
  so 
\[
  (I - \mathbf{U})^{-1} = \frac{1}{0.12}\begin{pmatrix} 0.4 & 0.2 \\ 0.2 & 0.4 \end{pmatrix} \approx
  \begin{pmatrix} 3.33 & 1.67 \\ 1.67 & 3.33 \end{pmatrix}.
\]

\item 
\[
  \mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta} = \big(0.15(-0.125) + 0.05(0.125),\ 0.05(-0.125) + 0.15(0.125)\big)
  = (-0.0125,\ +0.0125),
\]
the expected mid change on the very next step.
\item 
\[
  g^{(1)} = (I - \mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta} \approx (-0.0208,\ +0.0208):
\]
the
  expected size of the next mid change, counting all the steps on which the book reshuffles before it.
\end{itemize}
Now suppose that after a down move the new book is in H with probability $0.6$, and after an up move it
is in L with probability $0.6$---the bounce. Then from L, 
\[
  \mathbf{J}^{(2)}_{\text{L}\to\text{L}} =
0.15 \times 0.4 + 0.05 \times 0.6 = 0.09
\]
and 
\[
  \mathbf{J}^{(2)}_{\text{L}\to\text{H}} = 0.15 \times 0.6
+ 0.05 \times 0.4 = 0.11,
\]
and symmetrically from H, so
\[
  \mathbf{J}^{(2)} = \begin{pmatrix} 0.09 & 0.11 \\ 0.11 & 0.09 \end{pmatrix}, \qquad
  \mathbf{B} = (I - \mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{(2)} \approx \begin{pmatrix} 0.483 & 0.517 \\ 0.517 & 0.483 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Each row of $\mathbf{B}$ sums to one: it is the distribution of the state just after the next mid change.
Then 
\[
  g^{(2)} = \mathbf{B}g^{(1)} \approx (+0.0007,\ -0.0007),
\]
opposite in sign to $g^{(1)}$ because
of the bounce, and $g^{(3)} \approx (-0.00002,\ +0.00002)$. Adding,
\[
  g^{\star} \approx (-0.0202,\ +0.0202).
\]
A book in state H has a micro-price about $0.02$ points---a sixth of a half-tick---above the mid; a
book in state L, the same amount below.
\end{example}

\paragraph{The matrix formula as a sum over all paths.} The conditional expectation
$\E[\,\cdot \mid \iota_t, s_t]$ is not read off the current book directly. The current book only picks
the starting state $x$; the expectation is an average over every way the chain can evolve from $x$,
with each path weighted by its probability, and the probabilities are transition frequencies counted
in historical data. The matrix products enumerate those paths exactly. The next mid change can happen
on the first step, or after one step on which the book reshuffles without the mid moving, or after
two such steps, and so on:
\[
  g^{(1)}(x) = \big[\mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\big](x) + \big[\mathbf{U}\mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\big](x)
  + \big[\mathbf{U}^2\mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\big](x) + \cdots
  = \big[(I - \mathbf{U})^{-1}\mathbf{J}^{(1)}\boldsymbol{\Delta}\big](x).
\]
\inwords{entry $(x, y)$ of $\mathbf{U}^j$ is the total probability of all paths that go from $x$ to $y$ in
$j$ steps without the mid moving, and the inverse 
\[
  (I - \mathbf{U})^{-1} = I + \mathbf{U} + \mathbf{U}^2 + \cdots
\]

adds every possible number of such steps at once.}
In Example~\ref{ex:microprice-matrix}, starting from H, the mid moving at once contributes $0.0125$;
one reshuffle first contributes 
\[
  0.2 \times (-0.0125) + 0.6 \times 0.0125 = 0.005;
\]
two reshuffles
contribute $0.002$; each further term is $0.4$ times the previous one, and the total is

\[
  0.0125/(1 - 0.4) \approx 0.0208 = g^{(1)}(\text{H}).
\]
The matrix $\mathbf{B}$ then carries the same
bookkeeping across successive price changes.

\paragraph{The same expectation by Monte Carlo.} Because the micro-price is an average over random
paths, it can also be estimated by simulating those paths and averaging, instead of summing them in
closed form:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item start a path in the current state $x$, with the mid at zero;
\item at each step, draw the next event from row $x$ of the estimated transition probabilities: a move
  to another state with the mid unchanged (probabilities in $\mathbf{U}$), or a mid change by
  $\Delta_j$ together with the state the book lands in (probabilities in $\mathbf{J}^{(2)}$, split by the
  size of the change);
\item continue until the mid has changed $k$ times, for $k$ large enough that further changes no longer
  move the average (a handful, as in the closed form), and record the cumulative mid change;
\item repeat for $N_{\text{paths}}$ independent paths and average the recorded changes; the average
  estimates $g^{\star}(x)$.
\end{enumerate}
Simulation needs one extra estimate the closed form does not: the joint probability of a jump of a given
size \emph{and} the state the book lands in, which is $\mathbf{J}^{(2)}$ split by jump size. The two
methods estimate the same number. The closed form is exact for the fitted chain and costs a matrix
inverse; the Monte Carlo estimate carries sampling noise whose standard deviation falls like
$1/\sqrt{N_{\text{paths}}}$, so halving the error needs four times as many paths. As an illustration of this noise, on the stylised two-state chain of
Example~\ref{ex:microprice-matrix}, starting from H, the closed form gives $0.0202$; one illustrative
run of $40{,}000$ simulated paths gave $0.0231$, and one of a million paths gave $0.0198$ with a
standard error of $0.0003$. For the small chain of
the micro-price---a few dozen states---the closed form is the better choice. Neither method removes
\emph{estimation} noise: both use transition probabilities counted from finite data, and that noise
passes into the result.

Simulation becomes the right tool when the closed form is out of reach. If the state is enlarged with
history---recent OFI, the sign of the last move, deeper levels---the number of states multiplies and the
matrices become too large to invert or too sparse to estimate. And if the model is not a finite Markov
chain at all, as for the Hawkes and neural point processes of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}, whose intensities
depend on the whole history, there is no transition matrix to invert: the expected mid after $k$ changes
is then estimated by simulating event paths from the current history, for example by thinning
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}), and averaging. This is the same choice Section~\ref{sec:cst} describes
for queue models, where Cont, Stoikov and Talreja compute their probabilities exactly with Laplace
transforms rather than by simulating the book.

\begin{modelcard}{Micro-price}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The transition matrices $\mathbf{U}$, $\mathbf{J}^{(1)}$,
$\mathbf{J}^{(2)}$ between imbalance--spread buckets, after choosing the numbers of buckets $n_\iota$
and $n_s$.}
\cardrow{How}{Bucket the imbalance and spread, symmetrise the data, and count transitions; the
adjustments $g^{(k)}$ then follow by matrix algebra, and a handful of terms suffices.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{A fair-value estimate to quote around in place of the mid, and a
better reference than the mid or weighted mid for predicting short-term price changes.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A weather forecast that starts from today's state and averages over
every path the next few changes can take: the micro-price is where those paths land on average.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Order flow across instruments}
\label{sec:cross}

The signals above use the book of one instrument. Related instruments---an index future and its constituents, ES
and NQ, futures and the cash market, the points of the Treasury curve---trade on related information, so the book of
one can carry information about another. This subsection extends OFI to several instruments, summarises the evidence
on stocks and on the Treasury curve, and then turns to lead--lag between futures and cash, the form in which
cross-instrument information is mainly used in execution. Cross-asset Hawkes models are in
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants} and multi-asset deep models in Section~\ref{sec:deep-multi}.

\paragraph{Cross-impact regression.} A simple cross-impact form is
\begin{equation}
  m^{(\kappa)}_t - m^{(\kappa)}_{t-w} = \sum_{\kappa'} \beta_{\kappa \kappa'}\, \OFI^{(w,\kappa')}_t + \varepsilon^{(\kappa)}_t ,
\end{equation}
where the superscripts $(\kappa)$ and $(\kappa')$ index instruments, $m^{(\kappa)}_t$ is the mid of instrument $\kappa$,
$\OFI^{(w,\kappa')}_t$ is the order-flow imbalance of instrument $\kappa'$, $\beta_{\kappa \kappa'}$ is the cross-impact
of instrument $\kappa'$'s flow on instrument $\kappa$'s mid, and $\varepsilon^{(\kappa)}_t$ is a residual.
\inwords{the same straight-line fit as before, except that each instrument's price change may
respond to the buying pressure in \emph{every} instrument. The coefficients form a matrix; the
diagonal is each instrument's own impact and the off-diagonal entries are the cross effects.} The form covers
index constituents, ETF and constituents, futures and cash, correlated equities, related
fixed-income contracts and cross-venue cryptocurrency markets.

\begin{example}[Number of coefficients in a cross-instrument regression]
For NQ,
\[
  m^{(\text{NQ})}_t - m^{(\text{NQ})}_{t-w} = \beta_{\text{NQ},\text{NQ}}\OFI^{(w,\text{NQ})}_t +
  \beta_{\text{NQ},\text{ES}}\OFI^{(w,\text{ES})}_t + \varepsilon^{(\text{NQ})}_t
\]
\inwords{the change in the NQ mid over the window is explained by NQ's own order-flow imbalance and by
ES's, each with its own coefficient.}
This regression has two coefficients. Ten related instruments, each predicted from all ten OFIs at five lags, gives
$10 \times 10 \times 5 = 500$ coefficients. At a 5\% level about 25 are ``significant'' under the
null alone.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Evidence on stocks.} \citet{contcucuringu2023}, on the 100 largest S\&P~500 stocks over 2017--2019, ask whether the order flow of
\emph{other} stocks helps. Two answers are needed. For the
\emph{contemporaneous} price change, once integrated OFI is used, adding other stocks' order flow
does not improve the out-of-sample fit. For \emph{forecasting} the next minute's return, lagged
order flow of other stocks does help significantly, although the out-of-sample $R^2$ of all the
forecasting models remains slightly negative and the effect decays within minutes. Explaining the
present and predicting the future are, again, different problems.

\paragraph{The Treasury curve.} For interest-rate futures the related instruments are the points of the
yield curve. On daily inter-dealer data from 1992 to 1999, order-flow imbalances explain up to 26\% of
day-to-day yield changes on days without major announcements, and most of that effect comes from a
factor common to all maturities: net buying across the whole curve says more about the level of yields
than buying in one maturity~\citep{brandt2004}. At one-minute resolution, \citet{tomas2022} compare
cross-impact models on 10-year Treasury note futures and E-mini futures jointly and find that
models built on the covariance of returns and order flow, such as a multivariate Kyle model, fit out of
sample far better than a model with only direct (own-instrument) impact. We found no published
intraday cross-impact study covering the full Treasury futures curve.

\paragraph{Lead--lag between futures and cash.} The clearest cross-asset structure in futures is that
one market moves first. For the S\&P~500 and Nasdaq-100, most price discovery happens in the E-mini
futures rather than in the cash index or ETF~\citep{hasbrouck2003}. At high frequency the lead is
measured in milliseconds and is bounded by the physical distance between data centres. Between Chicago,
where CME matches, and the New Jersey equity venues, the response of SPY to E-mini price changes took
7.25--7.95\,ms in April 2010, fell after a straighter fibre route was built, and was estimated at 4.2--5.2\,ms
over microwave links, against a speed-of-light minimum of 3.93\,ms; about 20\% of SPY trading could be
identified as a direct response to E-mini moves~\citep{laughlin2014}. Measuring how often trades in two
markets occur at a given time offset, \citet{dobrev2017} find activity peaks at $\pm 5$\,ms between
10-year Treasury futures and cash Treasuries, with leadership running both ways, a clear lead of the
E-mini over SPY at $+5$\,ms, and a lead of the E-mini over cash Treasuries but not the reverse. The
order-flow link between SPY and the E-mini is ``positive, but short-lived'': beyond one second the
correlations have little economic significance~\citep{garrison2019}.

\paragraph{Estimating the lag.} Two markets trade at different, irregular times, so the usual
correlation of returns sampled on a common grid is biased towards zero (the Epps effect). The
Hayashi--Yoshida estimator avoids the grid by summing products of returns over every pair of
overlapping intervals; shifting one series by a candidate lag and choosing the lag that maximises the
estimated correlation gives a consistent lead--lag estimator~\citep{hoffmann2013leadlag}. On European data,
\citet{huth2014} find with this method that the CAC~40 future leads a large constituent stock by about
0.6 seconds on average, and that the leader's past alone predicts the direction of the lagger's next
mid-quote move about 60\% of the time---yet a naive strategy that crosses the spread on that forecast
does not make money.

Cross-asset models should therefore report in-sample and out-of-sample improvement, incremental
economic value, added latency, parameter count and stability across regimes.

\begin{modelcard}{Cross-asset models}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The cross-impact matrix $\beta_{\kappa\kappa'}$ and the lags at which it
acts; for a cross-asset Hawkes model, the cross-excitation kernels between instruments.}
\cardrow{How}{Regression of each instrument's price change on the order flow of all (with shrinkage or
a factor structure to control the number of coefficients); lead--lag by maximising a Hayashi--Yoshida
correlation over candidate lags; Hawkes cross-kernels by maximum likelihood.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Use the leader's move to anticipate the lagger---mainly to protect resting
orders in the lagging market from being picked off---and hedge across related contracts.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{Thunder after lightning: the futures move is the flash and the cash market
the sound, a few milliseconds later; the lag is set by distance.}
\end{modelcard}

\paragraph{The signals at a glance.} Table~\ref{tab:signals} summarises the signals of this
section.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Classical order-book signals at a glance. Horizons are indicative of how the signals are
used in the cited literature, not guarantees.}
\label{tab:signals}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}*{4}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}}
\toprule
Signal & What it measures & Typical horizon & How it is fitted & Limitations \\
\midrule
OFI, best level~\citep{cks2014} & net buying pressure at the touch & seconds to minutes (contemporaneous fit) & linear regression; slope re-estimated with depth & lower fit in illiquid, noisy names; distorted by spoofing \\
Multi-level / integrated OFI~\citep{xu2019mlofi,contcucuringu2023} & pressure across the first levels & same & regression; principal components & more inputs to monitor; depth beyond the touch is less committed \\
Queue imbalance~\citep{gould2016qi} & skew of the two best queues & next mid change & logistic regression & less informative for small-tick names; affected by hidden size \\
Weighted mid & price leaning toward the thin side & next mid change & none & noisy; not a martingale \\
Micro-price~\citep{stoikov2018} & expected future mid given imbalance and spread & a few mid changes & Markov chain on imbalance and spread buckets & assumes Markov dynamics; needs symmetrised data \\
Book slope, curvature & how liquidity is spread across levels & longer & descriptive & sensitive to transient deep orders \\
Resilience~\citep{large2007} & speed of refill after trades & seconds & Hawkes impulse response & varies strongly across regimes \\
Cross-asset OFI~\citep{contcucuringu2023} & pressure in related instruments & lagged: about a minute & regression with shrinkage & many coefficients; gain decays within minutes \\
Lead--lag~\citep{huth2014} & which market moves first & milliseconds to seconds & Hayashi--Yoshida correlation over lags & one test: not profitable after crossing the spread \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The central empirical question is not whether richer representations improve in-sample fit, but
whether the improvement survives out-of-sample evaluation and execution costs.

% ================================================================
\section{Events rather than snapshots}
\label{sec:events}
% ================================================================

The previous section was about \emph{snapshot models}: signals computed from the sizes and prices visible
in the book at a moment, or from the change between two moments. This section marks the turn to
\emph{event models}, which work with the individual events that produce those changes---each order
added, cancelled, modified or executed---and therefore know not only \emph{what} changed in the book but
\emph{why}. The queue models, Hawkes processes and message-level networks of the following sections are
all event models.

The same reduction in displayed volume has different information content depending on its cause.
A snapshot representation can make two states identical even when their histories differ
(Example~\ref{ex:qi}). Market-by-order (L3) data carry the cause: every add, modify, cancel and
execution, with an order identifier. On CME's MDP~3.0 feed, for instance, book updates for
individual orders and trade summaries arrive as separate message templates, so the two books in
Example~\ref{ex:qi} produce visibly different message sequences.

The three event types have to be treated jointly. Price changes are often attributed to market
orders, but in Nasdaq data roughly half of all price-changing events are limit orders placed
\emph{inside} the spread, which improve the best price rather than consume it~\citep{eisler2012}.
A model that sees only trades misses half of what moves the price.

The case for message-level representation has recently been made directly in deep learning.
LOBERT~\citep{linna2025lobert} adapts the BERT encoder to LOB data by treating each complete
multi-dimensional message as a single token, while keeping continuous representations of price,
volume and time. The following paragraphs explain the terms in that description.

\paragraph{Tokens and encoders.} Language models do not read text letter by letter. They first cut it
into \emph{tokens}---words or pieces of words---and replace each token by an integer from a fixed
vocabulary; the model then learns a vector (an embedding) for each vocabulary entry and processes the
sequence of vectors. BERT~\citep{devlin2019} is an \emph{encoder}: a Transformer that reads a whole
sequence of tokens and produces, for each position, a vector summarising that token in its context. It is
pretrained by hiding some tokens and asking the network to fill them in, and then adapted
(\emph{fine-tuned}) to specific tasks. To apply the same machinery to an order book, one must decide what
a ``token'' of market data is.

\paragraph{Fields of a message.} One exchange message describes one event, and it has several fields: its
type (new order, modification, cancellation, execution), its side (buy or sell), its price, its size, and
the time since the previous message. That is what ``multi-dimensional'' means: a message is a small record,
not a single number. There are two ways to turn such records into tokens. One can split each message into
several tokens---one for the type, one for the side, one for the price, and so on---so that a sequence of
$n$ messages becomes several times $n$ tokens. Or one can map the \emph{whole} message to one token. LOBERT
does the latter, which its authors report shortens sequences by roughly an order of magnitude, so that the
same context window of 512 tokens covers far more market history.

\paragraph{Encoding a message as one token.} A single vocabulary entry cannot hold every possible price
and size, so the fields are first coarsened into a few categories. In LOBERT the price is measured as the
distance in ticks from the best quote on the opposite side and rounded into a few bins (0, 1, 2, 3, 5 and
10 ticks); the size is rounded into a few bins (0, 50, 100 and 200 shares) with a flag for round lots; and
these are combined with the side and the type. Each combination that occurs in the training data gets
one integer; with a few special tokens this gives a vocabulary of 293 message tokens. A new sell order for 100 shares one tick above the best bid, for example, becomes a
single integer meaning ``new / sell / 1 tick from the opposite quote / about 100 shares''. Rounding loses
detail, so the exact price, size and time gap are also fed to the network as continuous numbers alongside
the token. The token says what \emph{kind} of event happened; the continuous values say exactly where,
how large and when.

\paragraph{Pretraining and reported results.} LOBERT is pretrained by \emph{masked message modelling}: messages are hidden
and the network predicts them, as BERT predicts hidden words. It is designed as a general-purpose,
fine-tunable \emph{foundation model}: one large network trained once, without labels, on a large body of data,
and then reused for many different tasks by training only a small extra output layer for each (Section~\ref{sec:foundation}
explains this in detail). It is trained and evaluated on Nasdaq ITCH message data for four large US
stocks in 2015, with millisecond timestamps. Its results on next-message and mid-price prediction are
discussed in Section~\ref{sec:foundation}.

A message-level encoder sees the difference between a cancellation and an execution in
Example~\ref{ex:qi}; a snapshot encoder, by construction, does not.

\paragraph{Plan of the following sections.} LOBERT is mentioned here because it shows, in deep learning, the
case this section makes: the individual event carries information that a snapshot loses. The next three
sections are not about Transformers or tokens. They return to classical models that work directly with
order-book events---orders arriving, cancelling and trading at the best queues---and describe them with
probability: how long a queue survives before it is emptied (Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}) and how one
event makes further events more likely (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}). Deep networks, including message-level
encoders such as LOBERT, are taken up again in Section~\ref{sec:deep}.

% ================================================================
\section{Queue depletion and first-passage models}
\label{sec:queue-models}
% ================================================================

\paragraph{The idea: a queue as a bucket that fills and drains.} Think of the best-ask queue as a bucket of
lots. New sell limit orders pour lots in; buy market orders that trade against it, and sellers who
cancel, drain lots out. Each event adds or removes a random amount at a random time, so the level of
the bucket wanders up and down like a random walk. The question this section asks is: \emph{how long
until the bucket is empty?} That moment matters for three reasons.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=1pt]
\item When the best-ask queue empties, the best ask moves up a tick, and so does the mid. Price
  changes \emph{are} queue depletions.
\item The direction of the next price move is a race between the two best queues: whichever empties
  first decides it. This is the formal version of ``the thin side breaks first''
  (Section~\ref{sec:ofi}).
\item For a resting order, the wait until it is filled is the time until the volume \emph{ahead of
  it} is used up---the same question, asked about part of a queue (Section~\ref{sec:queue-position}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{The definition.} The depletion time of the best ask is the first-passage time
\begin{equation}
  \tau^a = \inf\{u > 0 : v^{a}_{1,t+u} \le 0\},
\end{equation}
where $u$ runs over time after $t$; $\tau^b$ is defined in the same way for the bid.
\inwords{$\tau^a$ is how long from now until the best-ask queue is empty for the first time---the
time at which a loop that polls the queue size would first see zero. ``First passage'' is the
general name for the first time a wandering quantity reaches a level.}

\paragraph{Reading the notation.} The definition is written in set notation, read from the inside
out.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[$v^{a}_{1,t+u}$] the number of lots at the best ask (level $1$, side $a$) at time $t+u$, that is,
  $u$ seconds after now. As $u$ grows, this traces the queue's size into the future.
\item[$v^{a}_{1,t+u} \le 0$] the condition ``the queue is empty at time $t+u$''. It is written ``$\le
  0$'' rather than ``$= 0$'' only as a safeguard; a queue size cannot be negative, so the two mean the
  same thing.
\item[$\{u > 0 : \ldots\}$] read ``the set of all future times $u$ such that \ldots''---here, the set
  of all moments after now at which the queue is empty. The colon is read ``such that''.
\item[$\inf$] the \emph{infimum}, the greatest lower bound of the set; for a set of times it is
  simply the earliest one. It is written $\inf$ rather than $\min$ for two technical reasons: a set of
  real times need not contain its earliest point, and if the queue never empties the set is empty, and
  the convention $\inf \emptyset = \infty$ gives $\tau^a = \infty$---the queue never depletes.
\end{description}
So the whole expression reads: \emph{the earliest time after now at which the best-ask queue is
empty}. Computed on a recorded future, it is found by stepping forward event by event until the best-ask queue is
first empty; on the real future it cannot be computed in advance---which is why $\tau^a$ is a random quantity, not a number known now: the
models of this section give its probability distribution.

\begin{example}[One depletion time]
In Figure~\ref{fig:book} the best ask holds 14 lots at 5000.25. Suppose that, from now ($u = 0$), a
buy market order takes 6 lots at $u = 0.4$\,s (8 left), a seller adds 3 lots at $u = 0.9$\,s (11
left), a seller cancels 5 lots at $u = 1.3$\,s (6 left), and a buy market order takes 6 lots at
$u = 2.0$\,s (0 left). The set of times at which the queue is empty begins at $u = 2.0$\,s, so
$\tau^a = 2.0$\,s; at that moment the best ask moves up to 5000.50 and the mid rises. On another
path of the future---more sellers joining, fewer buyers---the same queue might last a minute. The
distribution of $\tau^a$ across all such paths is what the models below estimate.
\end{example}
The race between the two queues is then
\begin{equation}
  \P(\text{next mid move is up} \mid X_t) = \P(\tau^a < \tau^b \mid X_t),
\end{equation}
\inwords{the price goes up next exactly when the ask queue empties before the bid queue does. Both
probabilities are conditional on $X_t$, the information observable at time $t$.}

\paragraph{A first estimate.} If lots drain out at an average rate of $r_{\text{out}}$ lots per
second and pour in at $r_{\text{in}}$, the bucket loses $r_{\text{out}} - r_{\text{in}}$ lots per
second on average, and a best-ask queue of $v^a_{1,t}$ lots takes on average
\[
  \E[\tau^a] \approx \frac{v^a_{1,t}}{r_{\text{out}} - r_{\text{in}}}
  \qquad (r_{\text{out}} > r_{\text{in}})
\]
seconds to empty: size divided by net drain rate, as for any bucket. Two warnings come with this
formula. First, it is an average; the actual time is random and often far from it. Second, if
inflow keeps up with outflow ($r_{\text{in}} \ge r_{\text{out}}$), the bucket has no net leak and the
expected time can be very long or infinite.

\paragraph{Models.} Stochastic models of the book~\citep{cst2010} treat arrivals, cancellations
and executions as random event streams (point processes) and derive the distribution of
first-passage times and the probability of a mid move in either direction, including the race above.
Queue-reactive models~\citep{huang2015} go one step further and let the rates themselves depend on
the current queue sizes---for example, orders may join or cancel at different rates when a queue is
short than when it is long. That reproduces many observed regularities of large-tick assets.

\subsection{The Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model}
\label{sec:cst}

The model of \citet{cst2010} is the foundation on which most later queue-based models of the
order book are built. It was one of the first models of the whole book to
satisfy three requirements at once: its parameters can be estimated directly from order-book
data with simple counting; it reproduces important empirical features of the book without being tuned
to do so; and it is simple enough that the probabilities a trader actually needs---will the price go
up next, will my order be filled before the price moves---can be \emph{computed}, quickly and exactly,
rather than estimated by simulation. Its central idea is that a limit order book is a queueing
system: limit orders wait in line to be served by market orders, or leave the line by cancelling.

\subsubsection{The model}

\paragraph{The model at a glance.} The whole model has four parts; the paragraphs below take them one at
a time.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{State:} the number of orders resting at each price tick on each side of the book, counted
  in units of the average order and measured by distance from the opposite best quote.
\item \textbf{Events:} on each side, three kinds of event arrive as independent Poisson streams---limit
  orders at each distance $\bar\ell$, at rate $r_L(\bar\ell)$; market orders at the best quote, at rate
  $r_M$; and cancellations, each resting order at distance $\bar\ell$ being cancelled at rate
  $r_c(\bar\ell)$.
\item \textbf{Price mechanics:} when the best bid or best ask queue is emptied, that price moves one
  tick and the mid moves with it; distances are then measured from the new quotes.
\item \textbf{Consequence:} because every rate depends only on the current queue sizes, the book is a
  continuous-time Markov chain, and the probabilities a trader needs follow from the two best queues
  alone.
\end{enumerate}
\inwords{the consequence follows from the first three choices, and it has two halves.
\emph{The Markov property:} a Poisson stream has no memory---the chance of an arrival in the next instant is the
same however long ago the last one was---and every rate in the model depends only on how many orders
sit in each queue \emph{now}. So to know how the book will evolve, it is enough to know its current
queue sizes; how it got there adds nothing. That is what ``Markov'' means, and it is what makes the
model computable: the future can be worked out from the present state with no record of the past.
\emph{Only the two best queues matter:} the next price change happens when the best bid or best ask queue
is emptied (or an order lands inside the spread). Orders deeper in the book do not touch either best
queue until the price has already moved, so for questions about the \emph{next} move---will it be up,
will my order at the best bid fill first---the deeper levels can be ignored. The large book collapses
to two counters racing to zero, which is what the rest of this subsection analyses.}
\paragraph{Parameters to estimate.} The model is fully specified once a handful of numbers are known.
Each is a \emph{rate}: an average number of events per unit of time (per minute in the paper's tables),
for one kind of event at one place in the book.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=3pt]
\item[The unit of size] Before anything is counted, sizes are converted into ``orders'': a queue of 300
  shares, when the average limit order is 100 shares, counts as 3. This is a choice of units, not a
  quantity with economic meaning, but every rate below is expressed in it.
\item[Limit-order rates $r_L(\bar\ell)$, summarised by $r_0$ and $\Xi$] How often a new limit order
  arrives at distance $\bar\ell$ ticks from the opposite best quote: one rate for $\bar\ell = 1$, one for
  $\bar\ell = 2$, and so on. Rather than estimating each separately, the model assumes they follow the
  power law $r_L(\bar\ell) = r_0/\bar\ell^{\,\Xi}$ introduced below, so two numbers describe them all.
  $r_0$ is the scale---the rate at distance one---and $\Xi$ says how fast the rate falls with distance. In
  the paper's data, $r_0 \approx 1.92$ per minute and $\Xi \approx 0.52$, a pure number
(Table~\ref{tab:cst-est}): about
  1.9 new orders a minute one tick away, about $1.92/2^{0.52} \approx 1.3$ two ticks away.
\item[One market-order rate $r_M$] How often a market order arrives and removes one unit from the best
  quote on the other side. Market orders only ever hit the best price, so a single number suffices; in
  the paper's data, $r_M \approx 0.94$ per minute.
\item[Cancellation rates $r_c(\bar\ell)$, one per distance] The chance per minute that \emph{any one}
  resting order at distance $\bar\ell$ is cancelled. Because it is per order, a level holding 10 orders
  loses about $10\,r_c(\bar\ell)$ of them a minute to cancellation, and a level holding 2 orders about
  $2\,r_c(\bar\ell)$. These, in contrast to the limit-order rates, are estimated separately at each distance; in the
  paper's data they lie between about $0.47$ and $0.81$ per minute.
\end{description}
For five price levels that is eight numbers in all: $r_0$, $\Xi$, $r_M$ and five cancellation rates. Most
