of them are book-keeping---how busy the market is. Two of the modelling choices behind them, however,
encode how traders behave, and these carry the economic content of the model: \emph{where} new orders are
placed (mostly near the price, the power law), and \emph{how} cancellation depends on queue size (in
proportion to it, which keeps queues from growing without bound). They are discussed next.

\paragraph{State.} Prices live on a grid of ticks. The state of the book is the number of orders
resting at every price on the grid, counted in units of the average limit-order size. Because most
activity happens near the current price, the model measures position relative to the quotes: let
$\mathcal{Q}^{\text{bid}}_{\bar\ell}(t)$ be the number of buy orders $\bar\ell$ ticks below the best
ask, and $\mathcal{Q}^{\text{ask}}_{\bar\ell}(t)$ the number of sell orders $\bar\ell$ ticks above the
best bid. Note the convention: distance is measured from the \emph{opposite} best quote. With a
one-tick spread, the best bid is at distance $\bar\ell = 1$ from the best ask.

\paragraph{Events and their rates.} Six kinds of event change the book, and each is modelled as an
independent Poisson stream (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes} explains what that assumption means and where
it fails):
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item a \textbf{limit buy} order arrives $\bar\ell$ ticks below the best ask, and a \textbf{limit sell}
  order $\bar\ell$ ticks above the best bid, each at rate $r_L(\bar\ell)$;
\item a \textbf{market buy} order removes one unit from the best ask, and a \textbf{market sell} order
  one unit from the best bid, each at rate $r_M$;
\item each resting order $\bar\ell$ ticks from the opposite quote is \textbf{cancelled} independently at
  rate $r_c(\bar\ell)$, so a level holding $\mathcal{Q}$ orders loses orders to cancellation at total
  rate $r_c(\bar\ell)\,\mathcal{Q}$.
\end{itemize}
The two choices with economic content are these. First, limit orders arrive more often near the current
price; following earlier empirical work~\citep{bouchaud2002}, the arrival rate is a power law in
distance,
\begin{equation}
  r_L(\bar\ell) = \frac{r_0}{\bar\ell^{\,\Xi}} ,
\end{equation}
with a scale $r_0$ and exponent $\Xi$ fitted to data.
\inwords{the farther a price is from where trading happens, the fewer orders are placed there, and the
fall-off follows a power law: doubling the distance divides the rate by $2^{\Xi}$.}
Second, cancellations are proportional to queue size: each order has the same chance per second of
being cancelled, so a long queue sheds orders faster than a short one. This single assumption is what
keeps queues from growing without bound.

Under these assumptions the book is a continuous-time Markov chain: its future depends only on the
current queue sizes, not on how they were reached. The authors show that a book that starts with the
bid below the ask always keeps that property, and that if every cancellation rate is positive the
chain is \emph{ergodic}---it has a unique long-run distribution, so averages over a long simulated
history converge to well-defined values, such as the average shape of the book.

\subsubsection{Estimating the model}

The estimation is deliberately elementary, which is part of the model's appeal. Every rate is
estimated the same way: \emph{count how many times the event happened, and divide by how long you
watched}. If a bus stop sees 30 buses in 10 hours, the estimated rate is 3 buses an hour. The only
complications are working out, from the data, which changes in the book were which kind of event, and,
for cancellations, accounting for the fact that each resting order is cancelled separately.

One detail of the data explains the method. The authors observe the sizes at the first five price levels
and the trades, but not individual orders (market-by-price data, Section~\ref{sec:intro}). They therefore
cannot see a limit order arrive; they see a queue get bigger. So an increase in a queue's size is counted
as a limit-order arrival, a decrease that coincides with a trade as a market order, and a decrease with no
trade as a cancellation. All sizes are converted to the unit of the average limit order.

Write $T_{\text{obs}}$ for the total trading time observed, and $N_L(\bar\ell)$ for the number of times
the queue $\bar\ell$ ticks from the opposite best quote increased during that time. A hat on a symbol
marks an estimate from data. The three kinds of rate are then:
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=3pt]
\item[Limit orders, at each distance]
  \[
    \widehat{r}_L(\bar\ell) = \frac{N_L(\bar\ell)}{T_{\text{obs}}}.
  \]
\inwords{the number of new orders seen at that distance, divided by the time watched. If over
  $T_{\text{obs}} = 600$ minutes of trading the queue one tick from the opposite quote grew 1{,}110 times,
  then $\widehat{r}_L(1) = 1{,}110/600 = 1.85$ new orders a minute.}
  Doing this at distances one to five gives five rates. The power law $r_L(\bar\ell) =
  r_0/\bar\ell^{\,\Xi}$ is then fitted to them, so that the rate can be extrapolated to distances further
  out: taking logarithms gives $\ln r_L = \ln r_0 - \Xi \ln \bar\ell$, a straight line in $\ln \bar\ell$,
  and least squares on the five points gives the intercept $\ln r_0$ and the slope $-\Xi$.
\item[Market orders] the number of market orders that changed the best quotes, converted to units of
  the average limit order, divided by $T_{\text{obs}}$. \inwords{if 470 market orders hit the best
  quotes in 600 minutes and a market order is on average 1.2 times the size of a limit order, they
  removed $470 \times 1.2 = 564$ units, and $\widehat{r}_M = 564/600 = 0.94$ a minute.}
\item[Cancellations, at each distance] the number of cancelled units at that distance, divided by
  $T_{\text{obs}}$, and \emph{also divided by the average number of orders resting there}. The extra
  division is needed because the model's cancellation rate is per order: a level holding three orders
  sees three times as many cancellations as a level holding one, and dividing by the average queue size
  undoes that. \inwords{if 1{,}278 units were cancelled one tick from the opposite quote in 600 minutes,
  that is $2.13$ a minute in total; if the queue there held three orders on average, each order was
  cancelled at a rate of $2.13/3 = 0.71$ a minute.}
\end{description}
The worked numbers are chosen to reproduce the paper's estimates in Table~\ref{tab:cst-est}; the worked
counts are illustrative.

The authors use five levels of quotes and trades for Tokyo Stock Exchange
stocks over 125 trading days (August to December 2006) and report estimates for Sky Perfect
Communications (Table~\ref{tab:cst-est}).

\subsubsection{Reduction of the book to the two best queues}

\paragraph{Purpose of this subsection.} The full model tracks every queue in the book, which is too much
to compute with directly. But a trader's questions---will the price go up next, will my order fill
before the price moves---concern only the \emph{next} price change, and until that change the best
prices stay where they are. During that time only two things matter: the number of orders at the best
bid and the number at the best ask. Each goes up when an order joins and down when one is traded or
cancelled, and the price moves when either reaches zero. So the whole book can be replaced, for these
questions, by two counters racing to zero. This subsection describes those counters and why they can be
treated separately.

\paragraph{Justification of the simplification.} Three observations make it work, and each
follows from the assumptions of the model.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Only the next price change matters.} The questions are all about what happens
  \emph{first}: which side breaks, and whether a resting order is reached before it does. Whatever the
  book does after the price has moved is irrelevant to them, so the model only has to be followed up to
  that moment.
\item \textbf{Until then, nothing else in the book can interfere.} While the best prices stay put, the
  distances of all queues from the quotes stay put, so every rate in the model stays constant. Orders
  arriving or cancelling deeper in the book change those deeper queues, but they do not touch the best
  bid or best ask queue. The deeper levels only come into play once a best queue is empty---which is
  exactly the moment we stop following the book.
\item \textbf{The two best queues do not affect each other.} In the model, the orders that join, trade
  against or cancel from the best bid are independent of those at the best ask: separate Poisson
  streams, with rates that depend only on each queue's own size. Knowing that the ask queue just lost
  an order says nothing about what the bid queue does next. So the two counters can be analysed one at a
  time, and the probability that one wins the race combined from the two separately.
\end{enumerate}
As a result, a book with many levels on each side, whose state is a long list of queue sizes,
reduces to two numbers, $v^b_{1,t}$ and $v^a_{1,t}$, each moving up and down on its own.

\paragraph{Role of the deeper levels.} For questions about the \emph{next} price change, the deeper
levels can be dropped exactly within the model; this is not an approximation: by points~2 and~3,
nothing deeper in the book can change either best queue before one of them empties, so dropping the
deeper levels changes no answer.
Two qualifications apply. First, when the spread is wider than one tick, a new limit order placed
\emph{inside} the spread also moves the mid; the model accounts for it through the limit-order rates at
those distances, which act as additional competing clocks, not through any deeper queue. Second, the
deeper levels matter as soon as the price has moved: the old second level becomes the new best quote, so
questions about several price changes---the average shape of the book, or volatility over a day---need
the full model, and the authors answer them by simulating it (Section~\ref{sec:cst}, ``Stylised facts reproduced by the
model''). Outside the model, ignoring depth is a real loss: the book beyond the best quotes carries
part of the price information~\citep{cao2009}, and multi-level order flow improves price-impact fits
(Section~\ref{sec:ofi}); the model cannot use that information because it assumes the deeper queues have
no influence on the best ones. Problems about
one counter hitting zero have known exact solutions, which is what lets the model give closed-form
answers rather than simulations. The cost is the realism of point~3: in real markets the two sides do
react to each other---the race of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models} is partly a reaction, not two
independent clocks---and that dependence is exactly what later models, such as the queue-reactive model
of Section~\ref{sec:qr}, put back in.

\paragraph{Each counter is a birth--death process.} The mathematical name for such a counter is a
\emph{birth--death process}: a count that goes up by one at a ``birth'' rate and down by one at a
``death'' rate. The names come from population models, where births add individuals and deaths remove
them. Here a birth is a new limit order joining the queue, and a death is an order leaving it, by trade
or cancellation. The birth rate is constant; the death rate depends on the current count, because the
more orders are waiting, the more of them can be cancelled. If the
spread is $s_t/\delta$ ticks, the best queues sit at distance $\bar\ell = s_t/\delta$ from the opposite
quote, and a queue holding $x$ orders evolves as
\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  st/.style={draw, circle, minimum size=8mm, font=\small},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, thick}]
\node[st, fill=gray!15] (s0) at (0,0) {0};
\node[st] (s1) at (2.4,0) {1};
\node[st] (s2) at (4.8,0) {2};
\node[st] (s3) at (7.2,0) {3};
\node at (9.0,0) {$\cdots$};
\draw[arr, bend left=30] (s2) to node[above, font=\scriptsize]{$r_L$} (s3);
\draw[arr, bend left=30] (s1) to node[above, font=\scriptsize]{$r_L$} (s2);
\draw[arr, bend left=30] (s3) to node[below, font=\scriptsize]{$r_M + 3r_c$} (s2);
\draw[arr, bend left=30] (s2) to node[below, font=\scriptsize]{$r_M + 2r_c$} (s1);
\draw[arr, bend left=30] (s1) to node[below, font=\scriptsize]{$r_M + r_c$} (s0);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}
with birth rate $r_L(s_t/\delta)$ and death rate $r_M + x\,r_c(s_t/\delta)$; reaching state $0$ (shaded) means
the queue has been used up and the price moves, so the count stops there. The authors prove that, up to the first price change,
the two best queues can be replaced by \emph{independent} birth--death processes with these rates. Every
question about the next price move becomes a question about which of two independent counters hits
zero first---the race of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}, now with exact rates.
Figure~\ref{fig:cst-race} shows three such races.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/cst_race_paths.png}
  \caption{Three simulated races between the best-bid and best-ask counters, each starting from 3 orders
  at the bid and 2 at the ask, with the estimated rates of Table~\ref{tab:cst-est} at distance one. Each
  step up is a limit order joining; each step down is a market order or a cancellation. The race ends
  (cross) when one counter reaches zero, and that side decides the next price move. The same starting
  book can end either way, and after very different waiting times: the model's answers are
  probabilities. Stylised simulation for illustration; not data.}
  \label{fig:cst-race}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Use of Laplace transforms.} The time for a birth--death process starting at $x$ to reach zero is
a sum of $x$ independent pieces: the time to go from $x$ to $x-1$, then from $x-1$ to $x-2$, and so on.
Distributions of sums are awkward, but the \emph{Laplace transform} of a waiting time $\tau$,
$\E[\mathrm{e}^{-\varpi \tau}]$ as a function of a variable $\varpi$, turns sums of independent waiting
times into products of their transforms. For birth--death processes each one-step transform has a known
closed form as a continued fraction~\citep{cst2010}, so the transform of the whole first-passage time is a
product of known pieces. Probabilities such as ``queue A empties before queue B'' are then obtained by
numerically inverting a transform built from these products---a computation that takes far less time than
simulating thousands of paths of the book, and gives full distributions rather than only averages.
\inwords{instead of simulating the book many times and counting how often the price went up, the model
writes the answer as a formula in transform space and evaluates it directly. The transform is a
book-keeping device that makes adding independent waiting times as easy as multiplying numbers.}

\subsubsection{Three probabilities a trader needs}

The paper computes three conditional probabilities, each given the current number of orders at the
best bid, $v^b_{1,t}$, and at the best ask, $v^a_{1,t}$, and the spread. This subsection first states
what each probability is and what it shows; the equations that produce the numbers are collected at the
end, in ``Computation of the probabilities''.

\paragraph{1. Probability that the next price move is up.} With a one-tick spread, the mid rises next if the ask
queue empties before the bid queue. With a wider spread, a new order can also arrive inside the spread
and move the mid first; the formula adds these arrivals as competing exponential clocks.
Table~\ref{tab:cst-up} compares the model's answer with the empirical frequency in the data. The model
captures the main pattern well---the probability rises with the bid queue and falls with the ask
queue; where the ask queue is large, the model's up-move probability is below the empirical
frequency. Some cells differ
noticeably (for example 0.548 against an empirical 0.714 with four orders at the ask and five at the
bid). Figure~\ref{fig:cst-pup} draws the table as curves.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.72\textwidth]{figs/cst_pup_lines.png}

  curve per size of the best-ask queue: Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model (lines) and empirical frequency

  makes a large ask queue push the price down harder than it does in the data.}

\paragraph{2. Probability that a bid order fills before the price moves.} Suppose an order is placed at
the back of the best bid, so that the bid queue holds $v^b_{1,t}$ orders including our own, and that it
is never cancelled. It is filled when all orders ahead of it
have been removed---by market sells, or by cancellation of the orders ahead---which is a pure-death
process: with $x$ orders in line including our own, the line shortens at rate $r_M + (x-1)\,r_c$, since
our own order is not cancelled. The order is filled before the price moves if this line empties before
the ask queue does. Table~\ref{tab:cst-fill} gives the model's answers. The pattern is the execution
problem of Part~III in numbers: an order alone at the bid, facing five orders at the ask, is filled before
the price moves about 78\% of the time; the last of five orders at the bid, facing one at the ask, about
12\% (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-fill}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.72\textwidth]{figs/cst_fill_lines.png}
  \caption{Probability that an order at the back of the best bid is filled before the mid moves, against
  the length of the bid queue (our order included and last), one curve per size of the best-ask queue;
  model values from Table~\ref{tab:cst-fill}. Every order ahead of ours lowers the chance; a long ask
  queue raises it, because it makes the price less likely to move up and away before we are reached.}
  \label{fig:cst-fill}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Putting the first two together: adverse selection.} Plotting the two model probabilities
against each other, one point per book (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-adverse}), shows something neither table
shows alone. The points lie almost on a straight downward line (correlation $-0.99$): the books in which
a resting bid is most likely to be filled are exactly the books in which the next price move is most
likely to be \emph{down}. An order at the bid gets filled mostly when the bid side is about to break---when
it is buying just before the price falls. This is the adverse selection of Section~\ref{sec:adverse},
produced here by nothing more than two counters racing to zero; Section~\ref{sec:queue-summary} returns to it. A
fill probability on its own therefore says little about whether resting an order is a good idea: what
matters is the fill probability together with what the price does after the fill.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.72\textwidth]{figs/cst_fill_vs_pup.png}

  probability that the next move is up (horizontal) against the probability that an order at the back of
  the bid is filled before the mid moves (vertical); values from Tables~\ref{tab:cst-up}
  and~\ref{tab:cst-fill}. High fill probability comes with a likely down move: the fills a resting bid
  gets are disproportionately the bad ones.}
  \label{fig:cst-adverse}
\end{figure}
The authors note that their data do not identify individual orders, so these probabilities could not be
checked against data---exactly the gap that order-level data and queue-exact replay fill
(Section~\ref{sec:kaspar}).

\paragraph{3. Probability of making the spread.} A market maker places one order at the best bid and one at
the best ask and earns the spread if both are filled before the price moves. The model computes this
probability too. Its most interesting feature is that it is \emph{not} monotone: for a fixed number of
orders at the ask, the probability first rises and then falls as the bid queue grows. With one order at
the ask, it is about 0.27 with one order at the bid, peaks near 0.31 with two or three, and falls back
to about 0.29 with five. A longer bid queue protects the bid from being exhausted, which helps, but also
makes the bid order wait longer, which hurts; the balance gives an interior optimum.

\paragraph{Computation of the probabilities.} All three answers come from one piece of mathematics: the
time it takes a counter to reach zero. We follow \citet{cst2010}, Section~3, in the notation of this
survey, for a one-tick spread. At the best quote a queue of $x$ orders gains an order at rate
$r_{\text{in}} = r_L$ and loses one at rate $r_{\text{out}}(x) = r_M + x\,r_c$---the bucket of
Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}, now with exact rates.

\emph{Step 1: one step down.} Let $\tau_x$ be the time the queue takes to go from $x$ orders to $x-1$. We
describe its distribution by its Laplace transform 
\[
  \mathcal{L}_x(\varpi) = \E\big[\mathrm{e}^{-\varpi
\tau_x}\big]
\]
(Section~\ref{sec:cst}, ``Use of Laplace transforms''). Look at the first event. It happens
after an exponential waiting time with rate $r_L + r_{\text{out}}(x)$. With probability
$r_{\text{out}}(x)/(r_L + r_{\text{out}}(x))$ it is a departure, and the queue is at $x-1$: done. With
probability $r_L/(r_L + r_{\text{out}}(x))$ it is an arrival, the queue is at $x+1$, and it must first
come back down to $x$ (time $\tau_{x+1}$) and then go down to $x-1$ (a fresh copy of $\tau_x$). In
transform form, this ``first-step analysis'' reads
\begin{equation}
  \begin{gathered}
  \mathcal{L}_x(\varpi)
  = \frac{r_{\text{out}}(x)}{r_L + r_{\text{out}}(x) + \varpi}
  + \frac{r_L}{r_L + r_{\text{out}}(x) + \varpi}\,\mathcal{L}_{x+1}(\varpi)\,\mathcal{L}_x(\varpi),
  \\
  \text{so}\quad
  \mathcal{L}_x(\varpi) = \frac{r_{\text{out}}(x)}{r_L + r_{\text{out}}(x) + \varpi - r_L\,\mathcal{L}_{x+1}(\varpi)} .
  \end{gathered}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the transform of a waiting time that is an exponential delay followed by more waiting is the
product of their transforms, and the two branches are weighted by how likely each is. Solving for
$\mathcal{L}_x$ expresses it through $\mathcal{L}_{x+1}$; substituting the same formula for
$\mathcal{L}_{x+1}$, and so on, gives a fraction inside a fraction inside a fraction---a \emph{continued
fraction}, which converges quickly and is evaluated numerically~\citep{abatewhitt1999}.}

\emph{Step 2: the whole queue.} To empty a queue of $v$ orders it must step down from $v$ to $v-1$, then
from $v-1$ to $v-2$, and so on to zero. These steps take independent times, and the transform of a sum of
independent times is the product of their transforms:
\begin{equation}
  \E\big[\mathrm{e}^{-\varpi \tau^a}\big] = \prod_{x=1}^{v^a_{1,t}} \mathcal{L}_x(\varpi), \qquad
  \E\big[\mathrm{e}^{-\varpi \tau^b}\big] = \prod_{x=1}^{v^b_{1,t}} \mathcal{L}_x(\varpi).
\end{equation}
\inwords{this is the whole reason for using transforms: adding waiting times is hard, multiplying their
transforms is easy.}

\emph{Step 3: the race (probability 1).} The next move is up when the ask empties first,
$\P(\tau^a < \tau^b) = \P(\tau^a - \tau^b < 0)$: the probability that the difference of the two times is
negative, which is the distribution function of that difference evaluated at zero. Because the two queues
are independent (Section~\ref{sec:cst}, ``Reduction of the book to the two best queues''), the transform of the
difference is the product of the transform of $\tau^a$ at $\varpi$ and that of $\tau^b$ at $-\varpi$;
dividing by $\varpi$ turns the transform of a density into the transform of its distribution function.
So
\begin{equation}
  \P(\text{next mid move is up}) = \Big[\text{inverse transform of }
  \tfrac{1}{\varpi}\, \E\big[\mathrm{e}^{-\varpi \tau^a}\big]\,\E\big[\mathrm{e}^{+\varpi \tau^b}\big]\Big]
  \text{ evaluated at } 0 .
\end{equation}
\inwords{build the transform of ``ask time minus bid time'' from the two queues, turn it back into a
probability numerically, and read off the chance that the difference is negative.} The transform here is
two-sided, because the difference can be positive or negative, and the inversion is done numerically. One
check is immediate: with equal queues the two times have the same distribution, so the probability is
exactly one half---the diagonal of Table~\ref{tab:cst-up}.

\emph{Step 4: our own order (probability 2).} An order at the back of the bid is filled when every order
ahead of it is gone. New orders join \emph{behind} it and cannot delay it, so for this line there are no
arrivals at all: it is a pure-death process, and with $x$ orders in line (ours included) it shortens at rate
$r_M + (x-1)\,r_c$---our own order is never cancelled. Without arrivals, Step~1 has no second branch and
each step down is a plain exponential, so
\[
  \E\big[\mathrm{e}^{-\varpi \tau^{\text{fill}}}\big] = \prod_{x=1}^{v^b_{1,t}} \frac{r_M + (x-1)\,r_c}{r_M + (x-1)\,r_c + \varpi},
\]
where $\tau^{\text{fill}}$ is the time until our order is filled. The order is filled before the price moves
if this time beats the ask's depletion time: the probability is $\P(\tau^{\text{fill}} < \tau^a)$,
computed exactly as in Step~3 with $\tau^{\text{fill}}$ in the place of $\tau^a$ and $\tau^a$ in the
place of $\tau^b$. (The bid queue itself cannot empty first, because our order is in it and is filled
before the queue is gone.)

\emph{Step 5: a wider spread, and the third probability.} With a spread of several ticks the mid can
also move because a limit order lands inside the spread. Such arrivals are independent exponential clocks
with rates $r_L(\bar\ell)$ for the in-spread distances, so they enter as extra competitors in the race;
their transforms are again simple fractions, and the same inversion applies. The probability of making
the spread combines two such races---for the bid order and the ask order---and is computed with the same
tools; we do not reproduce its formula.

None of these steps needs simulation: each probability is a short product of fractions and one numerical
inversion, which is why the model can answer a trader's questions as fast as the book updates.

\subsubsection{Stylised facts reproduced by the model}

Without any tuning for these purposes, the fitted model reproduces several features of the real book:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item the \textbf{hump-shaped average book}: simulated average depth peaks two ticks from the opposite
  quote, as observed empirically~\citep{bouchaud2002};
\item the \textbf{order of magnitude of volatility}: the volatility of the simulated price is close to
  the realised volatility of the real stock over the same number of trades---computed from order-flow
  rates alone;
\item the \textbf{one-step behaviour of queues}: the probability that a queue grows at its next change,
  as a function of its size and distance, is close to the empirical frequencies at all five distances.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Mechanism behind the reproduced facts.} None of the three features was used to fit the model. Its
parameters are only event counts---how often orders arrive, trade and cancel at each distance
(Section~\ref{sec:cst}, ``Estimating the model''). The features \emph{emerge} when the fitted model is run
forward: the authors simulate the Markov chain for a long time, event by event, exactly as in
Figure~\ref{fig:cst-race} but for the whole book, and measure the average book, the price path and the
behaviour of the queues in the simulated history. That is the sense in which the model ``reproduces''
them. Why each one comes out right can be understood without the simulation.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=3pt]
\item[The hump] Consider one price level deep in the book, far from any trading. Orders arrive at rate
  $r_L(\bar\ell)$, and each one, independently, is cancelled at rate $r_c(\bar\ell)$. A level fed in this
  way settles around $r_L(\bar\ell)/r_c(\bar\ell)$ orders: arrivals and departures balance when the queue is
  big enough that $r_c(\bar\ell)$ times its size equals $r_L(\bar\ell)$. With the estimates of
  Table~\ref{tab:cst-est} this ratio is about $2.6$, $1.9$, $1.6$, $1.6$ and $1.6$ orders at distances one
  to five---largest at distance one, with no hump. Whatever produces the hump in the simulated book
  therefore comes from the interaction between the levels as the price moves---the best quote being
  drained by market orders and emptied, and every queue's distance from the opposite quote changing
  after each price move---not from the rates of any one level. The authors obtain the shape by
  simulating the whole book; we know of no simple argument that predicts the peak at distance two, and
  do not offer one.
\item[Volatility] In the model every price change is a queue depletion: when a best queue empties, the
  mid moves half a tick or so, up or down. The mid is therefore a random walk whose steps are the outcomes
  of successive races (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-race}), and its variability over a period depends on how often
  races end---which depends on how fast orders arrive, trade and cancel, and how large the queues are when
  a race starts. The simulation runs for the same number of trades as the real day and measures how far
  the simulated price wanders; the result is close to the real figure. Nothing about volatility
  was put in; it follows from the order-flow rates. The Cont--de Larrard model of the next subsection
  (Section~\ref{sec:cdl}) makes the same link in closed form.
\item[One-step behaviour of queues] When a queue of $x$ orders at the best quote next changes, it grows if
  a limit order arrives before any of the competing events; with independent exponential clocks this
  happens with probability $r_L/(r_L + r_M + x\,r_c)$, and at deeper levels, where there are no market
  orders, $r_L/(r_L + x\,r_c)$. The probability falls as the queue grows, because a longer queue offers more
  orders to cancel. This test uses rates estimated from the same data, so what it
  checks is the \emph{form} of the model---in particular that cancellations grow in
  proportion to queue size.
\end{description}

\subsubsection{Limitations and descendants}

The authors are explicit about what the model leaves out. Order arrivals are independent Poisson
streams, so there is no clustering of events and none of the long memory of order flow
(Section~\ref{sec:facts}); orders all have the same size; rates do not change through the day or with
market conditions; and there is no strategic behaviour. The authors also derive a simple trading rule
from the model's up-move probability, but evaluate it only on paths simulated from the model itself and
without transaction costs, so it says nothing about profitability on real markets---an instance of the
gap between prediction and tradable value that Part~III addresses. Each limitation defines a line of
later work:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \citet{contlarrard2013,contlarrard2012} reduce the book to the two best queues and obtain closed
  forms and a diffusion limit (Section~\ref{sec:cdl});
\item queue-reactive models let all rates depend on the current queue sizes~\citep{huang2015}, and their
  neural extensions learn that dependence~\citep{deepqr};
\item Hawkes models replace the Poisson streams by self-exciting ones (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes});
\item the micro-price~\citep{stoikov2018} keeps the Markov-chain logic but estimates the transitions
  directly from data rather than from a parametric model;
\item queue-position valuation~\citep{moallemi2017} and fill-probability models~\citep{arroyo2024,kanformer}
  extend the second probability above to individual orders, with adverse selection.
\end{itemize}
Finally, because the Laplace-transform method yields full distributions rather than single
probabilities, it can answer latency questions directly---for example, the probability that the ask
queue empties more than a given delay after the bid queue, which is what matters when an order takes
time to reach the exchange~\citep{cst2010}.

\begin{modelcard}{Cont--Stoikov--Talreja}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The limit-order rates $r_L(\bar\ell)$, summarised by $r_0$ and $\Xi$; the
market-order rate $r_M$; the per-order cancellation rates $r_c(\bar\ell)$; the unit of size (the
average limit order).}
\cardrow{How}{Counting: increases, decreases not caused by trades, and market orders at each distance,
divided by trading time (and, for cancellations, by average queue size); a least-squares power-law fit
for $r_L$.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Compute, from the current two best queues, the probability that the next
move is up, that a resting order fills before the price moves, and that a market maker earns the
spread---fast and exactly, via Laplace transforms.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{Two counters racing to zero, each ticking up when an order joins and down
when one is traded or cancelled; the first to hit zero decides the next price move.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{The Cont--de Larrard model}
\label{sec:cdl}

\paragraph{Motivation: closed-form answers.} The Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model answers its questions exactly, but only
numerically: each probability needs a continued fraction and a numerical Laplace inversion, so the
answers are tables, not formulas. One cannot look at them and see \emph{how} the probability of an up
move depends on the queues, or how volatility depends on order flow. \citet{contlarrard2013} trade some
realism for formulas. They simplify the model until the answers can be written down in closed form, and
every simplification is chosen to make one piece of mathematics tractable.

\paragraph{The simplifying assumptions, and what each buys.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=3pt]
\item \textbf{Only the two best queues are modelled.} The state is just the best bid price and the sizes
  of the two best queues. Nothing deeper in the book is tracked.
  \emph{What it buys:} a two-number state instead of a whole book.
\item \textbf{The spread is always one tick.} This holds over 98\% of the time for the large-tick US stocks
  they study. \emph{What it buys:} no orders can arrive inside the spread, so the price moves \emph{only}
  when one of the two queues is emptied---the race is the whole story.
\item \textbf{Rates do not depend on queue size.} At each best queue, limit orders arrive at rate $r_L$,
  market orders at rate $r_M$ and cancellations at rate $r_C$, all independent Poisson streams of
  unit-size orders. In contrast to Cont--Stoikov--Talreja, the cancellation rate is a rate for the
  \emph{whole queue}, not per order: a queue of ten orders loses orders to cancellation no faster than a
  queue of two. \emph{What it buys:} each queue becomes the simplest random walk there is---up by one at
  the constant rate $r_L$, down by one at the constant rate $r_M + r_C$ (in the bucket picture,
  $r_{\text{in}} = r_L$ and $r_{\text{out}} = r_M + r_C$). The time for such a walk to reach zero has a
  known closed-form distribution, in terms of Bessel functions, instead of a continued fraction. This is
  the key simplification. Its cost is the mechanism that, in Cont--Stoikov--Talreja, keeps queues from
  growing without bound: here long queues are not cancelled faster.
\item \textbf{When a queue empties, the book is redrawn.} The price moves one tick, and the sizes of the
  two new best queues are drawn afresh from a fixed distribution estimated from data, independently of
  the past. \emph{What it buys:} the deeper levels never have to be modelled---they are replaced by one
  distribution of ``queue sizes just after a price change''---and every price change starts a fresh,
  identical race, so the price becomes a sequence of independent races.
\item \textbf{The two sides are symmetric and independent.} The same rates apply at the bid and the ask,
  and the two queues move independently. \emph{What it buys:} the race is between two independent copies
  of the same random walk.
\end{enumerate}
Two further simplifications are made for particular results. The cleanest formula for the probability
of an up move assumes the \emph{balanced} case, $r_L = r_M + r_C$, in which the rates cancel out; and the
arctan form of that probability, and the volatility result, come from \emph{limits}---large queues
observed over long horizons---in which the random walks are replaced by Brownian motions. Each
simplification is a modelling choice, not an empirical finding, and each is relaxed by a later model:
queue-size-dependent rates in the queue-reactive model (Section~\ref{sec:qr}), self-excitation in Hawkes
models (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}).

Empirically the rates nearly balance: $r_L$ is slightly below $r_M + r_C$ (for example a ratio of
about $0.95$ for Citigroup in their data). Three results follow.

\paragraph{Time to the next price change.} The waiting time has a closed-form distribution. For one
queue of $x$ orders, the probability that it has not emptied by time $u$ is an integral of a Bessel
function, and for the two independent queues the probability that no price change has occurred is the
product of the two (Proposition~1 of the paper). Figure~\ref{fig:cdl-duration} evaluates it. Its tail is
the important part. When inflow and outflow balance exactly, the probability that the price has not yet
moved decays only like $1/u$, so the \emph{expected} waiting time is infinite: very long quiet periods are
not rare accidents but a built-in feature. When inflow is somewhat smaller than outflow, the curve
follows the balanced one for a while and then falls away exponentially, after a time of order
$1/\big(\sqrt{r_M + r_C} - \sqrt{r_L}\big)^2$---the closer the rates are to balance, the later the
cut-off, so nearly balanced queues still produce long waits. Long quiet periods punctuated by bursts are
thus a direct consequence of (nearly) balanced queues, before any self-excitation is added.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.72\textwidth]{figs/cdl_duration.png}
  \caption{Cont--de Larrard model: probability that the price has not changed by time $u$, starting from
  three orders at each best quote, computed from the closed-form expression, on logarithmic axes. Time is
  in units of the mean time between departures from a queue. Balanced rates (solid) give a $1/u$ tail and
  an infinite mean waiting time; with inflow below outflow (dashed, dotted) the curve breaks away
  exponentially, later the closer the rates are to balance. Stylised rates.}
  \label{fig:cdl-duration}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Probability that the next move is up.} In the balanced case the probability depends only
on the two queue sizes, not on the rates---it is the probability that a symmetric two-dimensional
random walk started at the queue sizes hits the ask axis first. In the companion heavy-traffic
analysis~\citep{contlarrard2012}, with uncorrelated queue changes of equal size and intensity on the
two sides, it takes the simple form
\begin{equation}
  \P(\text{next mid move is up} \mid v^b_{1,t}, v^a_{1,t}) =
  \frac{2}{\pi}\arctan\!\left(\frac{v^b_{1,t}}{v^a_{1,t}}\right).
\end{equation}
\inwords{the probability rises from $0$ to $1$ as the bid queue grows relative to the ask queue,
and equals $1/2$ when they are equal. It is the ``thin side breaks first'' rule of
Section~\ref{sec:ofi}, made exact for this model. Figure~\ref{fig:cdl-pup} draws it. Lines of equal
probability fan out from the origin: what matters is the \emph{ratio} of the two queues, not their
difference---ten orders against five behaves like two against one. In Figure~\ref{fig:book}, with 29 lots bid and 14
offered, it gives $(2/\pi)\arctan(29/14) \approx 0.71$.} The general formula in the paper allows
for correlated queue changes.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/cdl_pup.png}
  \caption{Cont--de Larrard model, balanced case: probability that the next mid move is up. Left: the
  exact expression of the paper's Proposition~2 over a grid of queue sizes; the contours of equal
  probability are nearly straight lines through the origin, so the probability depends mainly on the
  ratio of bid to ask. Right: the exact values (dots) against the simple form
  $(2/\pi)\arctan(v^b/v^a)$ (lines), which is already very close for small queues. Neither depends on any
  rate: in the balanced case only the queue sizes matter.}
  \label{fig:cdl-pup}
\end{figure}
Figure~\ref{fig:cst-cdl} compares this formula with the Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model and the data of
Table~\ref{tab:cst-up}. The two models agree closely when the ask queue is short; when it is long, the
arctan formula lies further below the data than the CST model does. The comparison is only indicative,
since the formula is derived for a balanced, heavy-traffic limit and is not fitted to this stock.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/cst_vs_cdl.png}
  \caption{Probability that the next mid move is up for three sizes of the ask queue: the Cont--de
  Larrard formula $(2/\pi)\arctan(v^b/v^a)$ (dashed), the Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model (solid) and the
  empirical frequencies (dots) of Table~\ref{tab:cst-up}. The arctan formula uses no fitted parameters.}
  \label{fig:cst-cdl}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{From queues to volatility.} Over long horizons the price behaves like a random walk whose
volatility, in the balanced case, is proportional to $\delta\sqrt{r_L / \Theta}$, where $\Theta$ is the average
product of the bid and ask queue sizes just after a price change. Volatility rises with order
activity and falls with the depth that reappears after each move---a microstructural explanation of
why thin, busy markets are volatile. Across Dow Jones stocks the authors find ten-minute volatility
roughly proportional to this quantity. Figure~\ref{fig:cdl-vol} shows the shape of the relation:
volatility grows with the square root of order activity, so four times the activity doubles it, and falls
with the square root of the depth that reappears after each move. It is a formula for volatility in which
the only inputs are counts and sizes from the order flow.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/cdl_vol.png}
  \caption{Cont--de Larrard model: volatility $\propto \delta\sqrt{r_L/\Theta}$, in relative units, against
  the order arrival rate for three depths (left) and against the depth after a price change for three
  arrival rates (right). Busier markets are more volatile; markets that refill deeply after each move are
  calmer.}
  \label{fig:cdl-vol}
\end{figure}

\begin{modelcard}{Cont--de Larrard}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{Three rates per best queue, $r_L$, $r_M$, $r_C$, and the distribution of
the new best-queue sizes after a price change (which gives $\Theta$).}
\cardrow{How}{Counting events at the best quotes over a sample, and the empirical distribution of queue
sizes just after each price change.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{The probability that the next move is up, the distribution of time to
the next price change, and a volatility estimate $\propto \delta\sqrt{r_L/\Theta}$ from order-flow rates
alone.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A drunkard's walk in two dimensions, starting at the point (bid size, ask
size): whichever axis the walk hits first decides the price move.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{The queue-reactive model}
\label{sec:qr}

\paragraph{The queue-reactive model as a market simulator.} The queue-reactive model is, first of all, a
\emph{simulator}: a program that generates synthetic order-book data. Given the rates fitted to a stock, it
produces, event by event, a stream of limit orders, cancellations and market orders at the first few price
levels, the resulting queue sizes, and the price moves they cause---an artificial trading day that
looks statistically like the real ones. It is a \emph{generative} simulator in the sense of
Section~\ref{sec:three-problems}: it invents new market paths from a model, rather than replaying a
recorded one as the \kasparhft{} system of Section~\ref{sec:kaspar} does. Two features make it useful. The paths it generates
reproduce the observed behaviour of queues; and because every rate depends on the current state of the
book, a trader's own simulated order changes what the simulated market does next, so tactics can be
tested against a market that reacts. The book it generates is coarse: queues are counts of orders of
average size, there are no individual order identities, and only the first few levels exist. And, like any
generative model, what it generates is not evidence about the real market (Principle~1): it is a tool for
asking ``what if'', whose answers are only as good as the model. Apart from one formula, for the long-run
size of a queue, all of its results are obtained by running it.

\paragraph{Rates as functions of queue size.} Cont--Stoikov--Talreja make cancellations proportional to
queue size and keep the other rates fixed. \citet{huang2015} instead measure from the data how traders
behave when a queue is short and when it is long. In their queue-reactive model the rate of every kind of
event at a queue is an arbitrary function of the current size of that queue (and, in richer versions, of
the neighbouring queues), estimated without assuming a functional form.

\paragraph{State and rates.} The book is described by the queues at the first few price levels on each
side of a reference price $p_{\text{ref}}$ close to the mid (three levels in the paper, because levels
four and five behave like level three). Queue sizes are counted in units of
the average size of events at that level, so a queue of size $x$ holds about $x$ typical orders. In the
basic version each queue is a birth--death process like those of Section~\ref{sec:cst}, but with
size-dependent rates: limit orders arrive at rate $r_L(x)$, and cancellations and market orders remove
units at rates $r_C(x)$ and $r_M(x)$, where $x$ is the current queue size.
\inwords{the model writes down a separate arrival, cancellation and trading rate for each possible queue
size, rather than one rate for all sizes. The data decide how the rates change with size.}

\paragraph{The simulation loop.} Figure~\ref{fig:qr-loop} shows one step of the loop. The state is the
list of queue sizes at the first few levels on each side, plus the reference price. From the current
sizes, the fitted tables give the rate of every possible event at every queue---an arrival, a
cancellation, or (at the best quotes) a market order. The total of all these rates sets how long until
the next event: the waiting time is drawn from an exponential distribution with that total rate. Which
event it is, and at which queue, is drawn with probability proportional to its rate, so frequent events
are picked more often. The queue is updated. If a best queue has just been emptied, the price may move,
and if it moves the book is either shifted one level or reset. Then the loop repeats from the new state.
Because the rates are recomputed from the new queue sizes at every step, the simulated market reacts
to everything that has happened in it, including any orders the trader inserts.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  node distance=5mm and 6mm,
  box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, font=\scriptsize, minimum height=8mm, text width=40mm, fill=white},
  dec/.style={draw, diamond, aspect=2.4, align=center, font=\scriptsize, inner sep=1pt, fill=gray!8},
  inp/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, font=\scriptsize, text width=33mm, fill=gray!12},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, thick}]
\node[inp] (fit) {\textbf{Fitted from data}\\rate tables $r_L(x)$, $r_C(x)$, $r_M(x)$;\\ $\pi_{\text{move}}$, $\pi_{\text{reinit}}$};
\node[box, below=of fit] (state) {\textbf{State}\\queue sizes at levels 1--3, each side;\\reference price};
\node[box, below=of state] (rates) {\textbf{Look up the rate} of every possible event at every queue, from its current size};
\node[box, below=of rates] (wait) {\textbf{Draw the waiting time}\\exponential, with rate = sum of all rates};
\node[box, below=of wait] (pick) {\textbf{Pick the event}\\with probability $\propto$ its rate;\\update that queue};
\node[dec, below=6mm of pick] (empty) {best queue\\emptied?};
\node[dec, right=12mm of empty] (move) {price\\moves?\\($\pi_{\text{move}}$)};
\node[dec, above=6mm of move] (reset) {reset?\\($\pi_{\text{reinit}}$)};
\node[box, above=6mm of reset, text width=30mm] (redraw) {\textbf{Reset}: redraw all queues from their long-run sizes (news)};
\node[box, right=6mm of reset, text width=26mm] (shift) {\textbf{Shift}: next level becomes the best quote};
\node[inp, left=12mm of rates, text width=26mm] (you) {\textbf{Trader's own order}\\inserted into a queue; follows the same rules};
\node[box, left=12mm of empty, text width=26mm] (rec) {\textbf{Record}\\event, queues, price};
\draw[arr] (fit) -- (state);
\draw[arr] (state) -- (rates);
\draw[arr] (rates) -- (wait);
\draw[arr] (wait) -- (pick);
\draw[arr] (pick) -- (empty);
\draw[arr] (empty) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize]{yes} (move);
\draw[arr] (move) -- node[right, font=\scriptsize]{yes} (reset);
\draw[arr] (reset) -- node[right, font=\scriptsize]{yes} (redraw);
\draw[arr] (reset) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize]{no} (shift);
\draw[arr] (empty) -- node[above, font=\scriptsize]{no} (rec);
\draw[arr] (move.south) -- ++(0,-5mm) -| node[pos=0.25, below, font=\scriptsize]{no} (rec.south);
\draw[arr] (redraw.north) |- ([yshift=-2mm]state.east);
\draw[arr] (shift.north) |- ([yshift=2mm]state.east);
\draw[arr] (rec.west) -- ([xshift=-4mm]you.west |- rec.west) |- (state.west);
\draw[arr, dashed] (you) -- (rates.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One step of the queue-reactive simulator. The fitted rate tables are the only input from data.
At every step the rates are looked up from the current queue sizes, so the simulated book reacts to its
own history and to any order the trader inserts. Price moves come from emptied best queues; most of them,
in the calibration of \citet{huang2015}, come with a reset of the whole book that stands for news.}
\label{fig:qr-loop}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Significance of the model.} The two previous models fix the behaviour of traders in advance:
Cont--Stoikov--Talreja assume constant arrival and trading rates and cancellations proportional to queue
size; Cont--de Larrard assume constant rates throughout. The queue-reactive model replaces these
assumptions with measured rates, and it has four main features.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Behaviour is measured, not assumed.} Each event rate is estimated separately for every
  queue size, by counting, with no formula imposed. The measured rates have shapes neither earlier model
  allows: market orders are much more frequent when a queue is short---traders hit thin queues---and
  cancellations rise with queue size only up to a point and then level off.
\item \textbf{It reproduces the queues it is fitted to.} The long-run distribution of queue sizes implied
  by the fitted rates matches the observed one closely, and better than a model with constant arrival
  rates and proportional cancellation.
\item \textbf{It reacts to the trader's own orders.} Because every rate depends on the current queue
  sizes, an order placed in the simulated book changes how the book behaves afterwards. That makes the model
  a realistic simulator for testing execution tactics, which is how the authors use it.
\item \textbf{It measures how much of the price movement the queues can explain.} The model has a
  switch: price changes can come purely from queues emptying, or some of them can come with a ``reset''
  of the whole book, standing for news. With the resets switched off, the queues alone produce at most
  about a third of the observed volatility, so most price variation must come from information arriving
  from outside the book. The paragraph ``Prices'' below explains how this works.
\end{enumerate}
Later models build on it: queue-reactive Hawkes models add self-excitation
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants}), and a deep version learns the rate functions with neural
networks~\citep{deepqr}.

\paragraph{Comparison with the two previous models.} The three queue models of this section answer
different questions in different ways (Table~\ref{tab:queue-models}). Cont--Stoikov--Talreja compute the
probabilities of the next price move exactly from the current queues, by a numerical transform inversion.
Cont--de Larrard simplify until those answers become formulas. The queue-reactive model goes the other
way: it makes the rates realistic, and pays for it by giving up most formulas---which is why it is used as
a simulator.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The three queue models of this section: what each assumes, what it answers, and how.}
\label{tab:queue-models}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}*{3}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}}
\toprule
& Cont--Stoikov--Talreja & Cont--de Larrard & Queue-reactive \\
\midrule
How traders behave & constant arrival and trading rates; cancellations proportional to queue size & constant rates for everything & every rate an arbitrary function of queue size, measured from data \\
What is tracked & the whole book & the two best queues; the book is redrawn after each price move & the first few levels; the book may be redrawn after a price move \\
Main questions & next move up or down; fill before the move; making the spread---from the current queues & the same, plus waiting time to the next move and long-run volatility & typical queue sizes; how prices and volatility arise; fill chances and placement tactics for a real order \\
How answered & exact, by numerical inversion of transforms; simulation for long-run features & closed-form formulas & one formula (long-run queue sizes); simulation for everything else \\
Strength & exact answers for the next move & intuition: how answers depend on rates & realism; reacts to one's own orders \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Calibration by counting.} For every event at a queue, record the time since the previous
event at that queue, the event type, and the queue size just before the event. The estimator of the
paper is
\[
  \widehat{r}_L(x) = \frac{\#\{\text{limit orders that arrived while the queue held } x\}}
  {\text{total time the queue spent at size } x},
\]
and likewise for cancellations and market orders. (The paper writes it as the inverse of the mean
waiting time at size $x$, times the fraction of events at size $x$ that were limit orders; the two
forms are algebraically equal.) Bid and ask are pooled by symmetry, and recording restarts whenever
$p_{\text{ref}}$ moves.
\inwords{to learn how fast orders arrive when a queue holds $x$ units, count the arrivals that happened
while it held $x$ units and divide by how long it held $x$ units in total.}

\paragraph{A family of four models.} \citet{huang2015} build the model in steps, each adding one kind of
dependence.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Model I: independent queues] Each queue's rates depend only on its own size. Each queue is then a
  separate birth--death process, which is what makes the closed-form result below possible.
\item[Model IIa: the second level watches the first] The rates at the second level depend also on whether
  the best queue is empty, and market orders hit the second level when the first is gone.
\item[Model IIb: the two sides watch each other] The rates at the best bid depend also on the size of the
  best ask (bucketed as empty, small, usual or large), and vice versa.
\item[Model III: prices] The reference price moves when a best queue empties, with the occasional reset of
  the whole book described below.
\end{description}

\paragraph{Closed-form result: typical queue lengths.} In Model~I a queue of size $x$
grows at rate $r_L(x)$ and shrinks at rate $r_C(x) + r_M(x)$. In the long run, the fraction of time it
spends at each size, $\pi(x)$, settles down, and it satisfies a simple balance: the queue must move up
from $x$ to $x+1$ as often as it moves down from $x+1$ to $x$. That gives
\begin{equation}
  \frac{\pi(x+1)}{\pi(x)} = \frac{r_L(x)}{r_C(x+1) + r_M(x+1)},
  \qquad\text{so}\qquad
  \pi(x) = \pi(0)\prod_{y=0}^{x-1}\frac{r_L(y)}{r_C(y+1) + r_M(y+1)} ,
\end{equation}
with $\pi(0)$ chosen so that the probabilities add up to one.
\inwords{the chance of finding the queue one order longer is the chance of finding it at its current size,
times the ratio of ``joining'' to ``leaving'' at that size. Where joining is faster than leaving the
distribution climbs; where leaving is faster it falls; the most likely size is where the two rates cross.}
Feeding in the measured rate functions gives a predicted distribution of queue sizes, which the authors
compare with the sizes observed every 30 seconds: the agreement is close, and better than with constant
rates and proportional cancellation. Figure~\ref{fig:qr-invariant} shows, with stylised rates of the shapes
reported, how the realistic shapes change the distribution.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/qr_rates_invariant.png}
  \caption{Queue-reactive rates and the long-run distribution of the queue size. Left: stylised rate
  functions with the shapes reported by \citet{huang2015}---roughly constant arrivals, cancellations that
  rise and then flatten, market orders that fall off as the queue grows (solid)---against Cont--Stoikov--Talreja
  style rates with proportional cancellation and constant market orders (dashed). Right: the long-run
  distributions implied by each, from the balance formula. Because cancellations stop growing for long
  queues, long queues are much more likely under the queue-reactive shapes. Stylised rates, not fitted
  values.}
  \label{fig:qr-invariant}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Estimated rates on Euronext Paris data.} On France Telecom and Alcatel-Lucent (Euronext Paris, 2010--2012, five
levels), the rates are far from those of the simpler models. At the best level, the limit-order rate
is roughly constant in queue size (lower when the queue is empty); the cancellation rate increases
concavely up to about 25 units and then flattens, rather than growing linearly as
Cont--Stoikov--Talreja assume; and the market-order rate \emph{decreases} exponentially with the
available volume---traders hit small queues. The long-run distribution of queue sizes implied by the
fitted rates matches the empirical one closely, and better than a Poisson model with linear
cancellation. Richer versions let the best-level rates depend on the opposite queue: insertion falls
and market orders rise as the opposite queue grows.

\paragraph{Prices: two kinds of price change.} The full queue-reactive model adds a price, and it lets
the price change in two different ways.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=3pt]
\item[A mechanical move] A best queue empties and the reference price moves one tick (in the model, with
  probability $\pi_{\text{move}}$ when this happens). The rest of the book stays as it was: the queues
  simply shift one place---the old second level becomes the new best quote. The book keeps its memory,
  so what happens next is still shaped by the queues that were already there.
\item[A move with a reset] With probability $\pi_{\text{reinit}}$, the price move also wipes the book: all
  the queues are replaced by a fresh draw from their typical long-run sizes around the new price. This
  stands for news. When new information arrives, traders reprice and the old queues stop mattering; the
  book after the move has nothing to do with the book before it. ($\pi_{\text{reinit}} = 1$, a reset at
  every move, is the Cont--de Larrard assumption of Section~\ref{sec:cdl}.)
\end{description}
The two probabilities are calibrated so that the simulated price matches two features of the real one:
its ten-minute volatility, and how often a price move is followed by another in the same direction
rather than a reversal. For France Telecom $\pi_{\text{move}} = 0.7$ and $\pi_{\text{reinit}} = 0.85$:
most price changes come with a reset.

Figure~\ref{fig:qr-sim} shows a stretch of simulated trading: the best queues wander, a price move
happens when one of them reaches zero, and most moves come with a reset of the book.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/qr_sim_path.png}
  \caption{A run of a queue-reactive simulator with two levels per side, rate functions of the shapes in
  Figure~\ref{fig:qr-invariant} scaled to short queues, $\pi_{\text{move}} = 0.7$ and
  $\pi_{\text{reinit}} = 0.85$. Top: sizes of the best bid and best ask queues. Bottom: the price, in
  ticks; thin vertical lines mark price moves that came with a reset of the book. Stylised simulation for
  illustration; not data.}
  \label{fig:qr-sim}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Experiment without queue resets.} The authors then switch the resets off ($\pi_{\text{reinit}} = 0$)
and let every emptied queue move the price ($\pi_{\text{move}} = 1$), which gives the queues the largest
possible influence. The result is a price driven only by order-book mechanics. Its volatility is at most
about 5 basis points, against 14 observed---about a third. And it reverses far too often. The reason
is visible in the mechanics: right after a mechanical move, the book is typically lopsided the other way,
so the next move tends to undo the last one, and the price bounces back and forth instead of wandering.
Real prices wander much more. The conclusion is that the queues explain the small, back-and-forth part of
price movement, and that most of the variance comes from information arriving from outside the book---
which, in the model, is the job of the resets. One practical reading of this result is as a statement about what an
order-book model can and cannot be expected to forecast: the mechanics of the next tick or two, not the
larger moves driven by news.

Queue-reactive Hawkes models add self-excitation to the size-dependent rates (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants}).

\paragraph{Uses of a built simulator.} Suppose the simulator has been built and
fitted to a contract one trades. Its uses fall into two parts. It is a
realistic laboratory for questions about \emph{the mechanics of execution}---how orders join, wait in and
leave queues, and how one's own orders change that. It is not a source of evidence about \emph{whether a
trading idea makes money}, because the only information it contains about future prices is what was put
into it, and in particular its news-driven price moves are random by construction. Table~\ref{tab:qr-uses}
lists the main uses with that line drawn. The published applications are fill probabilities---the versions of the model give similar
execution probabilities, while a model with constant (Poisson) rates overstates them---and the comparison of
placement tactics such as leaving a slice in place or pegging it to the best quote~\citep{huang2015}; for its neural extension, reproducing the square-root
law of market impact, order-size distributions and correlations between queues on Bund futures~\citep{deepqr};
and, for related reactive simulators built on neural Hawkes processes, serving as the training
environment for a reinforcement-learning market maker~\citep{lalor2025nhp}. The other rows are uses that
follow from what the model is, not reported results.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Uses and limits of a fitted queue-reactive simulator.}
\label{tab:qr-uses}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Use & What you do & What to trust, and what not \\
\midrule
Fill probabilities and times & Insert an order at each queue position and queue size; run many paths; tabulate the chance of a fill within a horizon and the time it takes. The tables can feed an execution engine. & The mechanics of the queue, which the model is fitted to. Not the adverse-selection part: which fills are followed by bad price moves depends on information the model does not have. \\
Tuning execution tactics & Run an execution algorithm against the simulator with different settings---how long to rest before repricing, whether to peg to the best quote, how to split a large order---and compare cost and completion. & Rankings of tactics whose differences come from queue mechanics. Treat absolute costs with caution, and expect to overfit the simulator if many settings are tried. \\
Impact of one's own orders & Insert one's orders and measure how the simulated book and price respond. & The mechanical part of impact, how queues are consumed and refill. Not the impact that comes from other traders inferring information from one's trading. \\
Stress testing & Change the rates---thinner queues, more aggressive flow, slower refill---and check that an algorithm's limits, cancellations and safeguards behave. & Behaviour under stylised extreme conditions. Not the probability of such conditions. \\
Training environment & Use the simulator as the environment for a learning agent (execution or market making), because, unlike replay, it reacts to the agent's orders. & A starting point for the agent. Its policy must then be evaluated on recorded data. \\
Testing research code & Generate data with known rates and check that estimators, feature pipelines and book reconstruction recover them. & Correctness of the code. Nothing about the market. \\
Intuition and teaching & Switch features on and off---resets, state dependence---and watch the effect. & Qualitative understanding. \\
\midrule
\emph{Not:} estimating trading profit & Backtesting a directional strategy on simulated prices. & Any profit is an artefact: the simulated price moves contain no information beyond the fitted rates. \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Place among the evaluation tools.} The simulator and historical replay
(Section~\ref{sec:replay}) are complementary. Replay uses the real market but cannot react: a
simulated order sits in the recorded queue, and the market behaves exactly as it did, whatever the order
does. A queue-reactive simulator reacts, but its market is synthetic. A sensible workflow uses each for what
it does well: explore and narrow down execution settings in the reactive simulator, where trying many
variants is cheap; evaluate the survivors in queue-exact replay on recorded data, counting every variant
tried~\citep{white2000,harvey2016}; and only then trade them on paper and live. The simulator's results are
never the final evidence.

\paragraph{Ingredients for building a queue-reactive simulator.} The ingredients follow the steps of this subsection:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=1pt]
\item data for the first few price levels with each change classified as a limit order, a cancellation or
  a trade---market-by-order data make this direct; market-by-price data require inferring it from size
  changes, as in Section~\ref{sec:cst};
\item the rate tables, by counting events and time spent at each queue size, separately by level (and by
  the opposite queue's size, for the richer versions);
\item the two price probabilities, chosen so that the simulated price matches the real ten-minute
  volatility and the ratio of continuations to reversals;
\item the simulation loop of Figure~\ref{fig:qr-loop}, a few dozen lines of code;
\item validation before use: the simulated queue-size distributions, times between events and price
  statistics should match the real ones, on data not used for fitting.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{modelcard}{Queue-reactive model (a market simulator)}
\cardrow{What it is}{A generative simulator of the first few levels of the book: from fitted rates it
produces synthetic streams of limit orders, cancellations and market orders, the resulting queue sizes and
the price, and it reacts to orders the trader inserts. One closed-form result (the long-run distribution of
queue sizes); everything else by running it.}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{For each of the first few levels, a table of rates by queue size: limit orders
$r_L(x)$, cancellations $r_C(x)$ and market orders $r_M(x)$ (in richer versions, also by the size of the
neighbouring or opposite queue); and two probabilities for prices, $\pi_{\text{move}}$ (a price move when
a best queue empties) and $\pi_{\text{reinit}}$ (a reset of the book with that move).}
\cardrow{How}{Rates: count the events of each type that happened while the queue held $x$ orders, and divide
by the total time it held $x$ orders. Price probabilities: choose them so that the simulated price matches
the real ten-minute volatility and the real ratio of continuations to reversals. Validate the fitted
simulator on held-out data before use.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Estimate fill probabilities and times by queue position; compare and tune
execution tactics (resting time, pegging, slicing a large order) in a market that reacts; measure the
mechanical impact of one's own orders; stress-test algorithms; train learning agents. Narrow down settings
here, then evaluate the survivors in queue-exact replay. Do not use it to estimate trading profit: its
price moves carry no information beyond the fitted rates.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A toll booth whose drivers and attendant react to the line: when it is long,
more drivers give up and leave; when it is short, cars are served faster. The simulator is that booth
running on its own, with the trader able to join the line and watch what happens.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Summary of the queue models for trading}
\label{sec:queue-summary}

\paragraph{The common idea.} All the models of this section start from the same picture. Each best queue
is a bucket that fills with limit orders and drains through trades and cancellations
(Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}). The price moves when a best queue empties, so the direction of the next
move is a race between the two best queues, and the wait for a resting order to be filled is a race
between the orders ahead of it and the opposite queue. What the models disagree on is how traders behave,
and therefore how the buckets fill and drain---and that choice decides what each model can deliver.

\paragraph{The three models in one line each.} Table~\ref{tab:queue-models} compares them in full.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Cont--Stoikov--Talreja (Section~\ref{sec:cst})] constant arrival and trading rates, cancellations
  proportional to queue size; exact answers, from the current queues, to the three questions about the next
  price move---by numerical transform inversion.
\item[Cont--de Larrard (Section~\ref{sec:cdl})] rates independent of queue size and a book redrawn after every
  move; the same questions answered by formulas that show how the answers depend on queues and rates, plus
  the waiting time to the next move and volatility.
\item[Queue-reactive (Section~\ref{sec:qr})] every rate an arbitrary function of queue size, measured from
  data; a realistic simulator of the book that reacts to one's own orders, with one closed-form result.
\end{description}

\begin{example}[State-dependent depletion rates]
\label{ex:fpt}
A best-ask queue holds 200 lots. Averaged over the day, net depletion (executions plus
cancellations minus additions) runs at 40 lots per second, so the first estimate of
Section~\ref{sec:queue-models} gives $200/40 = 5$ seconds. Now condition the rate on OFI. When buying
pressure is in its top decile, the net drain is 90 lots per second and
$\E[\tau^a] \approx 200/90 \approx 2.2$ s; in the bottom decile it is 15 lots per second and
$\E[\tau^a] \approx 13$ s. The same queue has expected lifetimes six times apart, depending on what the
rest of the market is doing. A model that uses one daily average rate would be wrong in both directions.
\end{example}

\paragraph{Depletion and adverse selection.} Queue depletion is also where adverse selection
comes from. Consider a resting buy order at the back of the best-bid queue. It is filled only when
everything ahead of it has been traded or cancelled. Because it is last in line, in practice it is
filled mostly by a burst of selling large enough to empty the whole level. And an emptied bid level is,
by point~1 of the list at the start of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}, a downward price move. The
order is therefore filled at the very moment the price is moving against it: the buyer acquires
the lots just as they become cheaper. The fill and the bad price move are not two unlucky events
that happen to coincide; they are the same event.

An order near the \emph{front} of the queue is in a different position. It can be filled by a small
sell order that takes a few lots and leaves the rest of the queue, and the price, where they were.
Such fills carry little information, so front-of-queue orders are less adversely selected. This is
one source of the front--back value difference in \citet{moallemi2017}, described under ``Where to
queue'' below.

\begin{example}[A fill at the back of the queue]
In Figure~\ref{fig:book} we join the best bid at 5000.00 with one lot, behind 29 lots. Suppose a
30-lot sell market order arrives. It takes all 29 lots ahead of us and our lot, and the 5000.00
level is gone: the best bid is now 4999.75 and the mid has fallen from 5000.125 to 5000.00 or below.
We bought at 5000.00 and the market immediately values the contract lower. Had we been first in the
queue, a 2-lot sell would have filled us and left 28 lots and the price untouched.
\end{example}

Figure~\ref{fig:cst-adverse-pos} makes the point quantitative inside the Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model.
An order is placed in a best-bid queue of six orders, at each possible position, facing three orders at
the ask, and the races are simulated with the rates of Table~\ref{tab:cst-est}. The closer to the front,
the more often the order is filled before the price moves; and among the orders that \emph{are} filled,
the probability that the next price move is down rises steadily from front to back, always above the
probability of a down move from the same book without conditioning on the fill. The fill itself is bad
news, and it is worse news the further back the order sat.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/cst_adverse_position.png}
  \caption{Adverse selection by queue position, Cont--Stoikov--Talreja model with the rates of
  Table~\ref{tab:cst-est}: six orders at the best bid (ours at the position shown), three at the ask.
  Left: probability that our order is filled before the mid moves. Right: probability that the next mid
  move is down, among the paths on which our order was filled (bars), against the probability of a down
  move from the same book with no conditioning (dashed). Stylised simulation for illustration; not data.}
  \label{fig:cst-adverse-pos}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Five lessons common to the three models.} Five lessons recur across the three models, and each is visible in a figure of
this section.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{The thin side breaks first, and the ratio of the queues is what matters.} The probability
  of an up move rises with the bid queue and falls with the ask queue (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-pup}); in the
  balanced case it depends only on their ratio (Figure~\ref{fig:cdl-pup}).
\item \textbf{A fill and an adverse move are often the same event.} The books in which a resting bid is
  most likely to be filled are those in which the price is most likely to fall next
  (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-adverse}), and among filled orders, those further back in the queue are followed by
  worse moves (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-adverse-pos}).
\item \textbf{Queue position is worth money.} Every order ahead lowers the chance of a fill before the
  price moves (Figure~\ref{fig:cst-fill}), and the difference in value between the front and the back of
  the queue can be comparable to the spread~\citep{moallemi2017}.
\item \textbf{Long quiet periods and volatility follow from the order flow.} Nearly balanced inflow and
  outflow make very long waits between price moves likely (Figure~\ref{fig:cdl-duration}), and volatility
  grows with order activity and falls with depth (Figure~\ref{fig:cdl-vol}).
\item \textbf{Queues explain the mechanics, not the news.} Book mechanics alone produce at most about a
  third of observed volatility, and far too many reversals; most price variance comes from outside the book
  (Section~\ref{sec:qr}).
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{From the models to decisions.} These results translate directly into execution choices.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Limit or market] If the expected time for a passive order to reach the front and be filled is
  longer than the horizon over which the trader's signal is valid, waiting forfeits the signal;
  crossing the spread is then the better choice despite its cost.
\item[When to cancel] If the estimated depletion time of the side on which an order rests collapses---below
  the trader's own latency, for instance---or the race turns against the order, it is about to be filled
  just before an adverse move, and should be cancelled or repriced.
\item[Where to queue] \citet{moallemi2017} split the value of a resting order into a \emph{static}
  part---the spread earned at execution minus the adverse-selection cost, which grows with queue
  position---and a \emph{dynamic} part---the option value of holding a place in the queue that advances
  over time. They find that the front--back difference can be comparable to the bid--ask spread for some
  large-tick US stocks (for example BAC and CSCO), and smaller for others.
\item[How to estimate fill chances from data] The time to fill is a waiting time that, for orders
  cancelled first, is never observed; survival models~\citep{cox1972}, which handle such \emph{censored}
  observations, are the natural statistical tool (Section~\ref{sec:deep-fill}).
\item[How to test tactics] A reactive simulator such as the queue-reactive model is the place to compare
  execution tactics cheaply; queue-exact replay on recorded data is the place to evaluate the survivors
  (Section~\ref{sec:kaspar}).
\end{description}
Queue models thus serve execution in two ways: they estimate \emph{when} a resting order will be filled,
and, because a depletion is a price move, \emph{how informative} the fill is likely to be
(Section~\ref{sec:adverse}). They say which way the price goes next only through the race between the two
sides.

\paragraph{Omissions common to the three models.} Orders arrive as Poisson streams, or at rates that depend only on
the current queues: an event never makes further events more likely, so the bursts and clustering of real
order flow (Section~\ref{sec:facts}) are missing. Orders have a single size. Nothing in the models carries
information about where the price is going beyond the queues themselves, so they describe the mechanics of
the next move, not the reasons for larger ones. And their evidence comes mostly from large-tick equities.
The first of these gaps---events that trigger events---is the subject of the next section.

% ================================================================
\section{Hawkes processes}
\label{sec:hawkes}
% ================================================================

\paragraph{Limits of the Poisson queue models.} The queue models of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models} assume
that orders arrive as independent streams at rates fixed over the sample. In a calm book that is a
reasonable approximation: the Cont--Stoikov--Talreja up-move probabilities of Table~\ref{tab:cst-up}
track the empirical frequencies closely. It breaks down exactly when it matters most. After a large
