trade or a news release, rates jump to several times their average for a few seconds, and a model
that uses the day's average rate underestimates how fast queues empty (Example~\ref{ex:fpt}). The
missing ingredient is that events cause events. This section introduces the standard model of that
mechanism.

\paragraph{Organisation of this section.} The section goes from the simplest model to the most
elaborate. The first subsections work with a single stream of events: why the Poisson process fails
for order flow (Section~\ref{sec:pp-poisson}), the four ideas the section is built on, in plain language and
pictures (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-intuition}), the language of point processes---counts and the conditional
intensity (Section~\ref{sec:pp-language}), the two simpler models a Hawkes process is compared with, the
Poisson and renewal processes (Section~\ref{sec:pp-canon}), the simplest Hawkes model, with one event type
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-one}), its branching ratio (Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}), what Hawkes processes
look like (Section~\ref{sec:pp-visualise}), and the statistics that tell the three apart on real data
(Section~\ref{sec:pp-signatures}). The section then moves to several interacting event types
(Sections~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi}--\ref{sec:hawkes-read}), connects events to prices
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-prices}), explains how the models are fitted and what fitted models find
(Sections~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}--\ref{sec:hawkes-crit}), and then adds one ingredient at a time: sizes and
labels on events, dependence on the state of the book, parameters that change over time, other kernels and link
functions, and finally neural networks (Sections~\ref{sec:hawkes-marks}--\ref{sec:npp}). It closes with what Hawkes models are used for
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-uses}). The material of Sections~\ref{sec:pp-poisson} and~\ref{sec:pp-language}--\ref{sec:pp-signatures} is adapted from Appendix~A
of~\citet{maciejewski2026packets}, which also supplies the empirical figures quoted for CME NQ market data.

\subsection{Limitations of the Poisson process}
\label{sec:pp-poisson}

\paragraph{The object being modelled.} Watch packets arrive from a market-data feed and write down the
time of each arrival:
\begin{verbatim}
09:30:00.213    <- 1st arrival
09:30:00.451    <- 2nd
09:30:00.802    <- 3rd
09:30:01.014    <- 4th
09:30:01.317    <- 5th
...
\end{verbatim}
That list of timestamps is what a point-process model describes---nothing else, no prices or sides,
only \emph{when}. The same applies to order-book events of any kind.

\paragraph{The Poisson process.} The simplest model of events arriving at random times is the \emph{Poisson
process}~\citep{daley2003}. Divide time into tiny slices and, independently in each slice, let an
event happen with a small fixed probability; in the limit, events arrive at a constant average rate
$\mu$ per second. The number $N_w$ of events in any window of length $w$ has the distribution
\begin{equation}
  \P\big(N_w = k\big) = \mathrm{e}^{-\mu w}\, \frac{(\mu w)^k}{k!}, \qquad k = 0, 1, 2, \dots
\end{equation}
\inwords{the chance of seeing exactly $k$ events in $w$ seconds depends only on the average rate
and the length of the interval---not on when the interval starts, and not on anything that happened
before it. The process has no memory: an event now makes the next one neither more nor less
likely. The waiting times between events are independent and exponentially distributed, and the
count in an interval has variance equal to its mean.}

Three consequences of this independence are worth
spelling out, because each can be checked against data.

\paragraph{(1) The gap between consecutive arrivals is exponentially distributed with mean
$1/\mu$.} At $\mu = 300$ per second the mean gap is
\[
  \text{mean gap} = \frac{1}{\mu} = \frac{1}{300/\mathrm{s}} \approx 3.3\ \mathrm{ms}.
\]
The gap distribution is \emph{memoryless}: however long one has been waiting, the expected time to
the next arrival is still $3.3$\,ms. The probability that a gap exceeds a length $u$ is
\[
  \P(\text{gap} > u) = \mathrm{e}^{-\mu u},
\]
so at this rate 
\[
  \P(\text{gap} > 3.3\,\mathrm{ms}) \approx 36.8\%,
\]


\[
  \P(\text{gap} > 6.6\,\mathrm{ms}) \approx 13.5\%
\]
and 
\[
  \P(\text{gap} > 10\,\mathrm{ms}) \approx 5.0\%.
\]

The probability that a gap is shorter than a tenth of the mean is
\[
  \P\big(\text{gap} < \tfrac{1}{10}\,\text{mean}\big) = 1 - \mathrm{e}^{-0.1} \approx 9.52\%,
\]
whatever the rate. On CME NQ packet data the share is $73\%$~\citep{maciejewski2026packets}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\textwidth]{figs/exponential_gaps.png}
  \caption{The exponential density of the gaps between arrivals of a Poisson process at $300$
  arrivals per second. Dashed vertical lines mark the median, the 90th and the 99th percentile.
  Half the gaps are shorter than the median of $2.3$\,ms and $10\%$ are longer than $7.7$\,ms. Real
  market-data streams have far more mass near zero and a much heavier right tail. Redrawn from
  \citet{maciejewski2026packets}.}
  \label{fig:exp-gaps}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{(2) The count in any window of length $w$ is Poisson-distributed,} as above, with
\[
  \E[N_w] = \mu w, \qquad \mathrm{Var}[N_w] = \mu w,
\]
so the ratio of variance to mean is $1$ for every $w$. This ratio is the \emph{Fano factor}
(Section~\ref{sec:pp-fano}); a value other than $1$ rejects the Poisson model.

\paragraph{(3) Counts in non-overlapping windows are independent.} The number of arrivals in the
first second says nothing about the number in the next.

\paragraph{Poisson as the null hypothesis.} The Poisson process is the stream with no memory, no
clustering and no regularity: arrivals are as uncoordinated as possible. Any departure from it is
structure in the arrival stream.

For order-book events these properties fail, in three ways that can be seen in any day of data.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Events cluster.} Activity comes in bursts. A large trade is followed by a flurry of
  new orders, cancellations and further trades as other participants react; a news release does the
  same on a larger scale. Between bursts the market can be quiet for long stretches. In a Poisson
  process an event says nothing about the next one, so bursts can only arise by chance, and large
  ones are vanishingly rare.
\item \textbf{Counts vary far more than Poisson allows.} Because of clustering, the number of events
  per second has a variance much larger than its mean, whereas a Poisson count has variance equal to
  its mean~\citep{bacry2015}.
\item \textbf{Event types trigger each other.} A buy market order makes further buy market orders
  more likely, because large orders are split into many small ones and because other traders
  follow; a trade that empties a level triggers cancellations and new limit orders around it. A
  set of independent Poisson processes, one per event type, cannot represent any of this.
\end{enumerate}
A fourth, milder departure is that average activity changes predictably through the day, typically
highest near the open and the close. A Poisson process with a time-varying rate (an
\emph{inhomogeneous} Poisson process) can absorb that pattern, but it still cannot make events
cause other events, which is the defining feature of order flow.

\paragraph{Bunching and memory measured on a feed.} Two departures from Poisson can be measured directly on a feed. The first is \emph{bunching}: many gaps are
far shorter than the exponential law predicts ($73\%$ against $9.52\%$ above). The second is
\emph{memory in the rate}: after a burst starts, more arrivals are likely to follow. Modelling either
needs a process whose rate can change with its own history---a \emph{conditional-intensity point
process}.

\begin{example}[Probability of a burst under Poisson]
Suppose events arrive at an average rate of $\mu = 10$ per second. Under a Poisson model the
probability of 30 or more events in a single second is about $2.5 \times 10^{-7}$. Over a 6.5-hour
trading day of 23\,400 seconds, the expected number of such seconds is about $0.006$---roughly one
every 170 trading days. In real order flow, seconds with several times the average activity occur
routinely around large trades and announcements. A model that calls them once-a-year events will
badly underestimate how quickly queues can empty (Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}).
\end{example}

A compact way to remember the difference: a Poisson process is \emph{drizzle}---drops fall
independently at a steady rate, and one drop says nothing about the next. Order flow is a
\emph{storm}---long dry spells broken by downpours, in which each burst of rain makes more rain likely
for a while.

The Hawkes process keeps what is convenient about the Poisson process---events at random times,
described by a rate---but lets the rate \emph{rise after each event} and then decay back. It is the
smallest change to the Poisson model that makes events cause events. The next subsections give the
language needed to state it precisely.


\subsection{Point-process ideas illustrated}
\label{sec:hawkes-intuition}

Before the formal definitions, this subsection gives the four ideas the rest of the section is built on---
\emph{intensity}, \emph{self-excitation}, \emph{cross-excitation} and \emph{branching}---in plain language,
with pictures. Each is defined precisely later; the numbers here are illustrative.

\paragraph{1. Intensity.} The intensity $\lambda(t)$ is how fast events are arriving
\emph{right now}, in events per second. It is not a count of what has happened but a rate: like a car's
speedometer, it can read 300 at one instant and 20 a second later. It translates into a chance: if the intensity
is 300 events per second, the chance of an event in the next millisecond is about $300 \times 0.001 = 0.3$. In a
Poisson process the needle never moves. In a Hawkes process it jumps up every time an event happens and then
drifts back down towards a resting value, the \emph{background rate}. The lower panel of
Figure~\ref{fig:intuition-hawkes} shows two such needles.

\paragraph{2. Self-excitation: events of a kind make more of the same kind.} After a buy market order, more buy
market orders become likely for a while: the buy intensity jumps and then decays. One common reason is a large
order being executed in pieces, so that one child order is followed by the next; another is other traders
reacting to the same news or to the trade itself. In Figure~\ref{fig:intuition-hawkes}, each buy makes a large
jump in the blue curve.

\paragraph{3. Cross-excitation: events of one kind make events of another kind more likely.} The same buy can
also raise the intensity of a \emph{different} event type: sell market orders, say, or cancellations at the best
ask. A buy that takes liquidity at the ask may draw sellers who see a better price, or prompt sellers resting at
the ask to cancel before the price moves through them. In Figure~\ref{fig:intuition-hawkes}, each buy also makes
a smaller jump in the orange curve. Self-excitation tends to produce runs of the same event; cross-excitation
links the two sides and the different event types of the book.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{figs/intuition_hawkes.png}
\caption{Intensity, self- and cross-excitation. Top: a simulated stream of buy and sell market orders. Bottom:
the intensity of each. Each event makes both curves jump, by more for its own type (self-excitation) than for
the other (cross-excitation); between events both decay towards the background rate (dotted). Where events come
close together, as near the start, the jumps pile up and the rates climb. Two-type Hawkes process with
exponential kernels; stylised simulation, not data.}
\label{fig:intuition-hawkes}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{4. Branching.} The same process can be read in a second way, as families.
Some events are \emph{immigrants}: they arrive on their own, from outside, at the background rate---news, a
trader's decision unrelated to recent activity. Every event, immigrant or not, then has a random number of
\emph{children}: events it directly triggers. Children have children of their own, and so on
(Figure~\ref{fig:family-tree}). The \textbf{branching ratio} $\Gamma$ is the average number of children per
event. Its value determines how the process behaves:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item If $\Gamma = 0$, nobody has children: every event is an immigrant, and the stream is Poisson.
\item If $\Gamma = 0.8$, each event has on average 0.8 children, 0.64 grandchildren, and so on, and one
  immigrant's family has on average $1 + 0.8 + 0.64 + \cdots = 1/(1-0.8) = 5$ events. Four out of every five
  events were triggered by an earlier one; only one in five came from outside.
\item As $\Gamma$ approaches 1, families become very large and long-lived. At $\Gamma \ge 1$ a single immigrant
  can start a cascade that never dies out, and the process is unstable.
\end{itemize}
With several event types, the branching ratio becomes a table, the \emph{branching matrix}: the entry in the
sells row and buys column, $\Gamma_{SB}$ in Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-read}, is the average number of sells triggered directly by one buy. Its diagonal entries measure
self-excitation, its off-diagonal entries cross-excitation.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, level distance=10mm,
  im/.style={circle, draw, fill=gray!30, minimum size=5mm, inner sep=0pt},
  b/.style={circle, draw, fill=blue!20, minimum size=5mm, inner sep=0pt},
  s/.style={circle, draw, fill=orange!30, minimum size=5mm, inner sep=0pt},
  level 1/.style={sibling distance=22mm}, level 2/.style={sibling distance=11mm}, level 3/.style={sibling distance=9mm},
  edge from parent/.style={draw, -{Stealth[length=1.4mm]}}]
\node[im] (r) {B}
  child {node[b] {B}
    child {node[b] {B} child {node[s] {S}}}
    child {node[s] {S}}}
  child {node[b] {B}}
  child {node[s] {S}
    child {node[s] {S}}};
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (2.8,-0.2) {grey: immigrant (from outside)\\blue: buy, orange: sell\\arrow: ``directly triggered''};
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (2.8,-1.6) {generation 0: 1 event\\generation 1: 3 events\\generation 2: 3 events\\generation 3: 1 event};
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (2.8,-2.9) {total family: 8 events,\\7 of them triggered (endogenous)};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One family in a two-type Hawkes process. An immigrant buy triggers two buys and a sell
(self- and cross-excitation), which trigger further events. The branching ratio is the average number of arrows
leaving an event; for the process to be stable it must be below one, so that families die out. One illustrative
family, drawn by hand; a branching ratio below one does not stop an individual family from being larger.}
\label{fig:family-tree}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Intensity as speed, branching as acceleration: an analogy and its limits.} It is tempting to say that the intensity is like speed and the
branching ratio like acceleration. The first half holds: the intensity is the speed at which the count of events
grows, it changes from moment to moment, and it is what a speedometer would show. The second half does not. The
branching ratio is a fixed property of the model, while acceleration changes from moment to moment; a high
branching ratio does not mean the rate is rising now---in a quiet spell the intensity drifts down whatever
$\Gamma$ is; and $\Gamma$ is not a rate of change at all but a count, the number of extra events one event
eventually produces. What plays the part of acceleration is the kernel: the kick each event gives and how quickly
it wears off. Table~\ref{tab:car} completes the analogy.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The driving analogy for a Hawkes process with an exponential kernel.}
\label{tab:car}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.42\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Car & Hawkes process \\
\midrule
speed & intensity $\lambda(t)$ \\
cruising speed & background rate $\mu$ \\
a tap on the accelerator: the speed jumps & an event: the intensity jumps by $\alpha$ \\
engine braking back towards cruising speed & decay at rate $\omega$ towards $\mu$ \\
the extra distance one tap ends up producing & the branching ratio $\Gamma = \alpha/\omega$: the extra events one event ends up triggering \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

The twist that makes it a Hawkes process is that, in this car, the extra distance itself causes more taps on the
pedal. If each tap produces on average fewer than one further tap ($\Gamma < 1$), the car always settles back to
cruising speed. If it produces one or more ($\Gamma \ge 1$), taps breed taps faster than braking can cancel them and
the speed runs away. In short: the intensity is the speedometer, the kernel is the kick each event gives the pedal
and how fast it wears off, and the branching ratio is the gain of the loop---the same role as the reproduction number
of an epidemic, the average number of people each case infects, with the same threshold at one.

\paragraph{Relation between the intensity view and the branching view.} The two views describe the same process. The jump in the intensity after
an event is that event's chance of having children in the following instants; the area under the jump---the
total extra intensity it ever adds---is the expected number of its children, the branching ratio. Self- and
cross-excitation are the two kinds of arrow in the family tree. Sections~\ref{sec:pp-language}
to~\ref{sec:hawkes-read} make each of these ideas precise: the intensity in Section~\ref{sec:pp-language}, the
one-type model in Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-one}, the branching ratio in Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}, and
self- and cross-excitation with the branching matrix in Sections~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi}
and~\ref{sec:hawkes-read}. On CME NQ data, four of every five events are triggered by earlier ones
(Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}).

\subsection{The language of point processes}
\label{sec:pp-language}

\paragraph{Points, processes and marks.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item A \textbf{point} is a single instant at which something happens, such as the timestamp of one
  packet or one order-book event.
\item A \textbf{process} is a random object that unfolds in time; here, a random list of arrival
  times $t_1 < t_2 < t_3 < \cdots$.
\item A \textbf{point process} is a random sequence of points on the time axis, specified by
  \emph{when} events happen and nothing else.
\end{itemize}
If each event carries additional information (side, price, size, message type), the process is a
\textbf{marked point process}: the timestamps are the points and the extra information is the
\emph{mark}. Four order-book messages in the first second of a session might read
\begin{verbatim}
09:30:00.001234567   Add,    bid 21432.50, size 3
09:30:00.001234571   Add,    ask 21432.75, size 5
09:30:00.017891234   Cancel, order X, bid 21432.25
09:30:00.238491702   Trade,  ask 21432.75, size 2
\end{verbatim}
The point process is the four timestamps; message type, side, price and size are marks. The
multivariate Hawkes process of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi} is a marked point process whose mark is the
event type $i$.

\paragraph{The probability space.} An arrival stream is modelled as a \textbf{stochastic process}, a family of random variables indexed
by time, on a probability space $(\Omega, \mathcal{A}, \P)$:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item $\Omega$, the \emph{sample space}, is the set of all possible outcomes; here an outcome is one
  complete realisation of a session's arrivals.
\item $\mathcal{A}$, the \emph{$\sigma$-algebra}, is the collection of yes-or-no questions about the
  outcome to which a probability can be assigned, such as ``did at least one packet arrive between
  09:30:00 and 09:30:01?''. It is closed under negation and countable union.
\item $\P : \mathcal{A} \to [0, 1]$ assigns a probability to each such event, with
  $\P(\emptyset) = 0$, $\P(\Omega) = 1$, and probabilities of disjoint events adding up.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{History: information accumulated over time.} The information available at time $t$ grows as time passes. Formally it is a
\textbf{filtration}, an increasing family of $\sigma$-algebras
\[
  \{\mathcal{I}_t\}_{t \ge 0}, \qquad \mathcal{I}_{t'} \subseteq \mathcal{I}_t \text{ for } t' \le t.
\]
$\mathcal{I}_t$ is everything observable up to and including time $t$, and $\mathcal{I}_{t-}$
everything observable strictly before $t$. ``Conditional on $\mathcal{I}_{t-}$'' means ``given
everything seen strictly before $t$''. Throughout this survey, conditioning on the observable information $X_t$ has this meaning: a
model may use only what is in the history.

\paragraph{The counting process.} The observable object is the random step function
\[
  N(t) = \#\{\, n : t_n \le t \,\},
\]
the number of arrivals up to time $t$. It starts at $N(0) = 0$, never decreases, and jumps by one at
each arrival. The number of arrivals in the interval $(t, t + \Delta t]$ is $N(t + \Delta t) - N(t)$.

\paragraph{Conditional intensity.} At time $t$, ask for the probability that at least one arrival occurs in the next short interval of
length $\Delta t$, given everything seen so far. The \textbf{conditional intensity} is that
probability divided by $\Delta t$, in the limit of a very short interval:
\[
  \lambda(t \mid \mathcal{I}_{t-}) = \lim_{\Delta t \downarrow 0} \frac{1}{\Delta t}\;
  \P\big( N(t + \Delta t) - N(t) \ge 1 \,\big|\, \mathcal{I}_{t-} \big).
\]
It is the instantaneous arrival rate given the history, in arrivals per second; in the rest of the
paper it is written simply $\lambda(t)$. At an intensity of $300$ per second the probability of an
arrival in the next millisecond is about $\lambda(t)\,\Delta t = 300 \times 0.001 = 0.3$. For a short
interval, and for the expected count over a longer one,
\[
  \P\big(\text{arrival in } (t, t+\Delta t]\big) \approx \lambda(t)\, \Delta t, \qquad
  \E\big[N(t+w) - N(t)\big] = \E\!\int_{t}^{t+w} \lambda(t')\, dt'.
\]
\inwords{over a very short interval, the chance of an arrival is the current intensity times the length
of the interval; over a longer window, the expected number of arrivals is the area under the intensity
curve across that window, averaged over the possible histories.}
Specifying $\lambda$ as a function of the history specifies the point process completely. For a
Poisson process $\lambda(t) = \mu$ and the history carries no information. For a Hawkes process the
intensity is $\mu$ plus a decaying contribution from each past arrival.

\subsection{Two simpler models: Poisson and renewal processes}
\label{sec:pp-canon}

A Hawkes process is best understood next to the two simpler models it is usually compared with. In this
subsection and the next there is a single event type; arrivals are indexed by $n$ and counting windows have
length $w$, as elsewhere in the survey.

\paragraph{(a) Poisson.} $\lambda(t) = \mu > 0$. Gaps are independent and exponential with mean
$1/\mu$; the count in a window of length $w$ is Poisson with mean $\mu w$. Every summary statistic
takes its reference value: the Fano factor is $1$ at every $w$, the Hurst exponent is $0.5$, and the
gaps are uncorrelated.

\paragraph{(b) Renewal process.} The gaps $\Delta t_n = t_n - t_{n-1}$ are independent draws from a common
distribution, not necessarily exponential. The intensity depends only on the time since the last
arrival. A renewal process is Poisson only when the gaps are exponential. Gaps with a heavy-tailed but finite-variance distribution produce
many short gaps and a Fano factor well above $1$, but the Fano factor levels off as $w$ grows instead
of continuing to rise, and the gaps are uncorrelated. (If the gaps have infinite variance, the Fano
factor of a renewal process can also keep rising, so a rising Fano factor alone does not prove
self-excitation.) Randomly shuffling the order of the gaps leaves
a renewal process statistically unchanged; comparing a statistic on a real stream with the same
statistic on its shuffled gaps therefore separates what the \emph{order} of the gaps carries from
what their \emph{lengths} carry.

\subsection{The simplest Hawkes model: one event type}
\label{sec:hawkes-one}

In a \textbf{Hawkes (self-exciting) process} each past arrival raises the intensity for a while:
\[
  \lambda(t) = \mu + \sum_{n:\, t_n < t} \phi(t - t_n).
\]
\inwords{the rate of arrivals now is a constant background rate plus one contribution from every past
arrival, each contribution depending on how long ago that arrival happened.}
$\mu \ge 0$ is the \textbf{background rate}, the intensity with no prior arrivals.
$\phi$ is the \textbf{kernel}: an arrival $u$ seconds ago still contributes $\phi(u)$ to the
intensity now. The kernel is the shape of one event's influence on the future. Dropping several
stones into a pond is the usual picture: the disturbance at any moment is the sum of the ripples
still spreading, each faded by the time since its stone fell. For the self-exciting specification
used here the kernel is non-negative, so a past event can only raise future intensity (inhibitory
extensions are discussed in Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants}), and its total area
must be finite, so that the past does not accumulate without bound. Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi}
extends the model to several event types that excite each other.

\paragraph{Kernel families.} The \emph{exponential kernel} $\phi(u) = \alpha\, \mathrm{e}^{-\omega u}$ has
two parameters: $\alpha \ge 0$, the jump in intensity immediately after an arrival, and
$\omega > 0$, the decay rate, whose inverse $1/\omega$ is the kernel's decay time. It is Markovian:
the current intensity summarises everything needed from the past. Right after an arrival the
intensity rises by $\alpha$; between arrivals it relaxes toward $\mu$: if $\lambda(t_n)$ is the intensity
just before the arrival at $t_n$, then $u$ seconds after it the intensity is
$\mu + (\lambda(t_n) + \alpha - \mu)\, \mathrm{e}^{-\omega u}$ (until the next arrival); so it can be updated in constant time per arrival---a useful
property for a low-latency system. The \emph{power-law kernel} $\phi(u) = \alpha\,(1 + u/u_0)^{-(1+\zeta)}$, with
the same jump $\alpha$, a time scale $u_0 > 0$ and an exponent $\zeta > 0$, decays more slowly; \citet{hardimanbercotbouchaud2013} find power-law kernels on
E-mini S\&P mid-price changes over lags from seconds to days.

\subsection{The branching ratio}
\label{sec:pp-branching}

The \emph{branching ratio} $\Gamma$ of a one-type Hawkes process is the total area under its kernel:
\[
  \Gamma = \int_0^\infty \phi(u)\, du, \qquad \Gamma = \alpha/\omega \text{ for the exponential kernel}.
\]
\inwords{add up, over all future times, the extra intensity one arrival creates; for the exponential
kernel this is the size of the jump divided by the decay rate.}
$\Gamma$ is the mean number of arrivals that one arrival triggers \emph{directly}. The Hawkes process
is equivalently a \textbf{branching process}~\citep{hawkesoakes1974}: every arrival is either an
\emph{immigrant}, generated by the background rate $\mu$, or the \emph{child} of an earlier arrival,
and each arrival has on average $\Gamma$ children. The expected size of the family descended from one
immigrant is the geometric series
\[
  1 + \Gamma + \Gamma^2 + \Gamma^3 + \cdots = \frac{1}{1 - \Gamma} \qquad (\Gamma < 1),
\]
a series that Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi} generalises to several event types. For
$\Gamma < 1$ the process is \emph{subcritical}: families are finite and the long-run rate is
$\bar\lambda = \mu / (1 - \Gamma)$. As $\Gamma \to 1$ it becomes \emph{near-critical}: family sizes
and durations grow without bound and long-range dependence appears. At $\Gamma = 1$ the process is
\emph{critical}: each family still dies out with probability one, but its expected size is infinite,
and with a positive background rate there is no stationary version. For $\Gamma > 1$ it is
\emph{supercritical}: with positive probability one arrival starts a cascade that never ends, and the
intensity grows without bound.

On CME NQ market data, an exponential-kernel fit gives $\Gamma \approx 0.80$ on both packets and
matching-engine transactions, with a kernel decay time $1/\omega$ of about $167\,\mu$s: four of every
five arrivals are triggered by earlier ones~\citep{maciejewski2026packets}. Estimates for other markets,
and the debate about how close to one the branching ratio really is, are reviewed in
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-find}.

\paragraph{The critical boundary in practice.} At $\Gamma = 1$ the stationary rate
$\mu/(1-\Gamma)$ is infinite. On a finite window the process still fires finitely often, but the
variance of the count grows faster than linearly with the window. A maximum-likelihood fit that
lands at $\Gamma = 1$ on real data may signal non-stationarity, a mis-specified kernel or a numerical
problem rather than a genuinely critical market; whether markets are critical or merely close to it is
itself debated~\citep{hardimanbercotbouchaud2013,filimonovsornette2015}.

\paragraph{A note on notation.} Much of the literature writes the exponential kernel as
$\alpha\mathrm{e}^{-\beta u}$ and the (scalar) branching ratio as $n$. In this survey $\beta$ is
reserved for price impact (Section~\ref{sec:ofi}) and $n$ for event indices, so the decay rate is
written $\omega$ and the branching ratio $\Gamma$; Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi} generalises it to a matrix, also written $\Gamma$. With
$\phi(u) = \alpha\mathrm{e}^{-\omega u}$, the branching ratio is $\Gamma = \alpha/\omega$.
Hawkes models have been applied extensively to order flow and price formation~\citep{bacry2015}.

\subsection{Simulated Hawkes paths}
\label{sec:pp-visualise}

The long-run rate $\bar\lambda = \mu/(1-\Gamma)$ and the branching ratio $\Gamma$ look different in
an event raster. Figure~\ref{fig:hawkes-grid} simulates an exponential-kernel Hawkes process over
15 seconds at nine combinations, $\bar\lambda \in \{2, 10, 50\}$ events per second and
$\Gamma \in \{0.30, 0.60, 0.90\}$, with a kernel decay time of one second. Read across a row: the
long-run rate is the same in every cell, but as $\Gamma$ grows a larger share of events is triggered by
earlier ones, so arrivals bunch, the count climbs in steps rather than a straight line, and the intensity
wanders further from its mean and for longer. At $\Gamma = 0.9$ and ten events per second, for example,
the intensity drifts between roughly 6 and 27 per second within fifteen seconds. Read down a column: a
higher rate makes the raster denser and the count smoother, but does not remove the slow swings of the
intensity that the branching ratio produces.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth,height=0.82\textheight,keepaspectratio]{figs/hawkes_grid_3x3.png}
  \caption{Simulated exponential-kernel Hawkes processes. Each cell stacks the event raster (top),
  the cumulative count $N(t)$ (middle) and the intensity $\lambda(t)$ (bottom). Rows: long-run rate
  $\bar\lambda \in \{2, 10, 50\}$ events per second. Columns: branching ratio
  $\Gamma \in \{0.30, 0.60, 0.90\}$. Fifteen seconds, decay rate $\omega = 1/\mathrm{s}$. The time
  scale is chosen for legibility; the kernel fitted on CME data decays about four orders of
  magnitude faster. Each process is simulated by thinning after a burn-in period, so it starts in its
  long-run regime. Redrawn after \citet{maciejewski2026packets}; stylised simulation.}
  \label{fig:hawkes-grid}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Telling Poisson, renewal and Hawkes processes apart}
\label{sec:pp-signatures}

Three signatures separate the three models on real data.

\emph{(i) An excess of short gaps.} On CME NQ packet data $73\%$ of gaps are shorter than a tenth of
the mean gap; any exponential gives $9.52\%$. A Hawkes process produces such an excess, but so does a
renewal process with heavy-tailed gaps, so this signature rejects Poisson without identifying
self-excitation.

\emph{(ii) Correlated gaps.} Under self-excitation, short gaps tend to follow short gaps and long
gaps follow long ones, so successive gaps are positively correlated. A renewal process has
independent gaps. A rate that varies slowly over the observation window also produces positive gap
correlation, so this signature has to be checked against that alternative.

\emph{(iii) Burstiness that grows with the time scale.} The Fano factor rises with the window length
on a Hawkes process and levels off on a Poisson process or a renewal process with finite-variance gaps. Clustering is then not a feature
of one time scale but looks similar over decades of window lengths. A slowly varying rate can also
make the Fano factor rise, which is the effect \citet{filimonovsornette2015} warn about.

\paragraph{The Fano factor.}
\label{sec:pp-fano}
For a window length $w$, cut the observation period into $\Upsilon$ consecutive windows and let

\[
  N_{w,\upsilon} = N(\upsilon w) - N((\upsilon-1)w)
\]
be the count in window $\upsilon$. The Fano factor, also called the
\emph{index of dispersion}, is
\[
  D(w) = \frac{\mathrm{Var}[N_{w,\upsilon}]}{\E[N_{w,\upsilon}]},
\]
the variance of the window counts divided by their mean. For a Poisson process $D(w) = 1$ at every
$w$. For a renewal process with finite-variance gaps $D(w)$ tends to a constant as $w$ grows. For a
clustered process $D(w) > 1$. Under self-excitation it grows with $w$ over the time scales on which
events trigger one another; with an exponential kernel it eventually levels off at $1/(1-\Gamma)^2$, while a
slowly decaying (power-law) kernel or a slowly varying background rate keeps it growing.
The measure is named after U.~Fano, who introduced it for fluctuations in counts of ions. On CME NQ
packet data $D(w)$ grows from about $52$ at $w = 0.1$\,s to about a thousand at $w = 60$\,s, while
the same stream with its gaps shuffled stays flat at about $17$~\citep{maciejewski2026packets}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/rasters_by_fano.png}
  \caption{Four simulated processes with a mean rate of about 20 per second, over 60 seconds. Top: a
  renewal process with very regular gaps (gamma-distributed with shape 25), $D(1\,\mathrm{s}) \approx 0.05$:
  nearly equal counts per window. Second: Poisson, $D \approx 1$, the reference. Third: Hawkes with
  $\Gamma = 0.6$, $D(1\,\mathrm{s}) \approx 1.6$: visible clumping. Bottom: Hawkes with $\Gamma = 0.9$,
  $D(1\,\mathrm{s}) \approx 2.7$: pronounced bursts and long quiet stretches. Redrawn after
  \citet{maciejewski2026packets}; stylised simulation.}
  \label{fig:rasters-fano}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figs/count_hists_by_fano.png}
  \caption{Distribution of arrivals per one-second window (bars) for the four processes of
  Figure~\ref{fig:rasters-fano}, with the Poisson distribution of the same mean overlaid (curve). The
  regular process is far narrower than Poisson; the Poisson process matches; the Hawkes processes are
  wider, with extra mass in both tails. The CME NQ packet stream has $D(1\,\mathrm{s}) = 108$, far wider
  than any panel shown~\citep{maciejewski2026packets}. Stylised simulation.}
  \label{fig:counthist-fano}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The Hurst exponent, from the scaling of the Fano factor.}
\label{sec:pp-hurst}
When the Fano factor grows as a power of the window length,
\[
  D(w) \propto w^{2\mathcal{H} - 1},
\]
the exponent $\mathcal{H}$ is the \emph{Hurst exponent}. $\mathcal{H} = 0.5$ means no long-range
dependence (Poisson, and renewal processes with finite-variance gaps); $0.5 < \mathcal{H} < 1$ means
long-range dependence, with clustering that looks similar across time scales; $\mathcal{H}$ near $1$
means very long memory. It is estimated by a straight-line fit on a log--log plot,
\[
  \ln D(w) = (2\mathcal{H} - 1)\ln w + \text{constant},
\]
so $\mathcal{H} = (\text{slope} + 1)/2$. On CME NQ packet data $\mathcal{H} \approx 0.74$; shuffling
the gaps, which keeps their lengths and destroys their order, takes it to $0.50$, so the long-range
dependence lives in the order of the gaps~\citep{maciejewski2026packets}.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=0.7\textwidth]{figs/fano_scaling.png}
  \caption{The Fano factor $D(w)$ against the window length $w$ on logarithmic axes, for the four
  processes of Figure~\ref{fig:rasters-fano} simulated over 3{,}000 seconds. Regular renewal process: below
  one, settling near $0.04$. Poisson: flat at one. Hawkes with $\Gamma = 0.6$: rising, then levelling off
  as the window becomes long compared with the one-second kernel. Hawkes with $\Gamma = 0.9$: a steeper
  rise, with a slope of about $0.7$ over windows from $0.1$ to $2$ seconds, which the Hurst formula would
  read as $\mathcal{H} \approx 0.84$; with an exponential kernel this growth also levels off eventually, at
  $D = 1/(1-\Gamma)^2 = 100$. Redrawn after \citet{maciejewski2026packets}; stylised simulation.}
  \label{fig:fano-scaling}
\end{figure}

\subsection{The multivariate model}
\label{sec:hawkes-multi}

So far there has been a single stream of events. Order flow has several interacting event types---buy and sell market orders, limit orders and
cancellations on each side. Let $N_i(t)$ count events of type $i \in \{1,\dots,K\}$. A linear multivariate Hawkes
process~\citep{hawkes1971} has conditional intensity
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_i(t) = \mu_i + \sum_{j=1}^{K} \int_0^t \phi_{ij}(t-t')\, dN_j(t'),
\end{equation}
with baseline rates $\mu_i$ and non-negative kernels $\phi_{ij}$; it is the one-type model of
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-one} with one intensity per event type, and when every kernel is zero it reduces to
independent Poisson processes with rates $\mu_i$. Here $\lambda_i(t)$ is the
\emph{intensity}: the expected number of type-$i$ events per second at time $t$. The term
$dN_j(t')$ is nonzero only at the instants $t'$ when a type-$j$ event occurred, so the integral is
simply a sum over past events, and the formula can be read as

\[
  \lambda_i(t) = \mu_i + \sum_j \sum_{\text{past type-}j\text{ events at } t'} \phi_{ij}(t - t').
\]

\inwords{the current rate of type-$i$ events is a constant background rate $\mu_i$ plus a ``bump''
for every past event; each bump fades as that event recedes into the past, with a shape set by the
kernel $\phi_{ij}$. Events make further events more likely for a while, the way an earthquake
raises the rate of aftershocks or a viral post triggers reposts.}
The branching matrix $\Gamma$ has entries
\[
  \Gamma_{ij} = \int_0^\infty \phi_{ij}(u)\,du ,
\]
the total area under each kernel; $\Gamma_{ij}$ is the expected number of type-$i$ events directly triggered by one type-$j$ event. The process is stationary when the spectral radius
$\rho(\Gamma) < 1$, the largest absolute eigenvalue of $\Gamma$. Then, with
$\mu = (\mu_1, \dots, \mu_K)^\top$ and $I$ the $K \times K$ identity matrix, the stationary mean
intensity vector $\bar\lambda$, with one entry $\bar\lambda_i$ per event type, is
\begin{equation}
  \bar\lambda = (I - \Gamma)^{-1}\mu .
\end{equation}
\inwords{expand the inverse as a geometric series,

\[
  (I-\Gamma)^{-1}\mu = \mu + \Gamma\mu + \Gamma^2\mu + \cdots.
\]
The long-run event rate is the
background events, plus the events they trigger, plus the events \emph{those} trigger, and so on
through every generation. The series converges---the cascade dies out---exactly when
$\rho(\Gamma) < 1$. Readers who know discrete-time linear systems will recognise the condition:
it is the stability condition for $\psi_{k+1} = \Gamma \psi_k + \mu$, a feedback loop whose gain must be
below one.}


\begin{example}[Endogeneity in two dimensions]
\label{ex:hawkes}
Take buy and sell market orders with $\mu = (1, 1)$ events per second and
\[
  \Gamma = \begin{pmatrix} 0.6 & 0.2 \\ 0.2 & 0.6 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
The eigenvalues are $0.8$ and $0.4$, so $\rho(\Gamma) = 0.8 < 1$. Then

\[
  I - \Gamma = \begin{pmatrix} 0.4 & -0.2 \\ -0.2 & 0.4 \end{pmatrix}
\]
with determinant $0.12$, and
\[
  \bar\lambda = \frac{1}{0.12}\begin{pmatrix} 0.4 & 0.2 \\ 0.2 & 0.4 \end{pmatrix}
  \begin{pmatrix} 1 \\ 1 \end{pmatrix} = \begin{pmatrix} 5 \\ 5 \end{pmatrix}.
\]
Only one event in five is exogenous. The model says a great deal about clustering and nothing, by
itself, about direction: the two sides are symmetric.
\end{example}

\subsection{Reading the branching matrix}
\label{sec:hawkes-read}

The branching matrix is the economically interesting output of a fitted Hawkes model. Take two
event types, buy and sell market orders:
\[
  \Gamma = \begin{pmatrix} \Gamma_{BB} & \Gamma_{BS} \\ \Gamma_{SB} & \Gamma_{SS} \end{pmatrix}.
\]
$\Gamma_{BB}$ is the average number of further buy market orders directly triggered by one buy
market order: buys beget buys, for example because a large order is being executed in pieces.
$\Gamma_{SB}$ is the number of sell orders triggered by a buy: a cross effect, for example traders
leaning against a move. A large diagonal relative to the off-diagonal means trends in order flow; a
large off-diagonal means reversal. Asymmetry ($\Gamma_{BS} \ne \Gamma_{SB}$) means one side reacts
more strongly to the other than vice versa. Linear Hawkes models allow only excitation; inhibition
(an event making others \emph{less} likely) requires non-linear extensions. The shape of each kernel
$\phi_{ij}$ says how long an influence lasts: an exponential kernel forgets quickly, a power-law
kernel remembers for a long time.

\paragraph{An analogy: acoustic feedback.} A microphone near a loudspeaker picks up each sound and
plays it again, slightly quieter. If the loop gain is below one, every echo is fainter than the last
and a clap dies away; the closer the gain is to one, the longer the ringing lasts. At a gain of one or
more the echoes grow and the system howls. The branching ratio is that loop gain for order flow: the
average number of events that one event triggers. At $0.8$, a single exogenous event produces on average
$1/(1-0.8) = 5$ events in total, as in Example~\ref{ex:hawkes}; at $0.95$ it produces $20$.

\begin{example}[A chain reaction in the book]
\label{ex:chain}
A stylised sequence at the best ask, all within a few milliseconds:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rl}
\toprule
time & event \\
\midrule
0.0\,ms & a buy market order takes 10 of the 14 lots at the best ask (exogenous) \\
0.3\,ms & two sellers at the best ask cancel, fearing a move up (triggered) \\
0.6\,ms & a buyer joins the best bid, leaning with the flow (triggered) \\
0.9\,ms & a second buy market order takes the remaining lots; the best ask ticks up (triggered) \\
1.2\,ms & new sellers post at the new best ask; buyers follow at the new bid (triggered) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
One outside event produced four more and a price move. In a Hawkes model each row raises the intensity
of the event types in later rows---entries $\Gamma_{ij}$ for cancellation after trade, trade after
trade, limit order after cancellation---and the fitted kernels say how quickly that influence fades.
\end{example}

\subsection{From events to prices}
\label{sec:hawkes-prices}

The simplest bridge from event counts to prices treats each mid move as an event. Let $N^{+}(t)$
and $N^{-}(t)$ count the upward and downward mid moves up to time $t$, each of size $\delta_m$. Then
\begin{equation}
  m_t = m_0 + \delta_m\,\big(N^{+}(t) - N^{-}(t)\big).
\end{equation}
\inwords{the price is a running tally of up-ticks minus down-ticks; modelling the two counting
processes models the price.}

Write $\lambda^{+}(t)$ and $\lambda^{-}(t)$ for the intensities of $N^{+}$ and $N^{-}$: the expected
numbers of up and down moves per second. \citet{bacry2013} make the two counts \emph{mutually}
exciting: an up move raises the intensity of down moves and vice versa,
\begin{equation}
  \lambda^{+}(t) = \mu_0 + \int_0^t \alpha\,\mathrm{e}^{-\omega (t-t')}\, dN^{-}(t'), \qquad
  \lambda^{-}(t) = \mu_0 + \int_0^t \alpha\,\mathrm{e}^{-\omega (t-t')}\, dN^{+}(t'),
\end{equation}
\inwords{each down move adds a bump of height $\alpha$ to the up-move rate, and vice versa; the bump
decays at rate $\omega$. The background rate on each side is $\mu_0$.}
The cross-kernel has total weight $\Gamma_{\times} = \alpha/\omega < 1$. This one mechanism
produces the familiar microstructure effects. Measured over short windows, prices look very
volatile because every up-tick tends to be followed by a down-tick (bid--ask bounce); over long
windows that back-and-forth cancels. Writing $\sigma^2(w)$ for the variance of price changes per unit time
measured over windows of length $w$, in units of ticks squared,
\begin{equation}
  \sigma^2(w) = \Lambda\left(\frac{1}{(1+\Gamma_\times)^2} + \Big(1-\frac{1}{(1+\Gamma_\times)^2}\Big)\,\frac{1 - \mathrm{e}^{-(\alpha+\omega) w}}{(\alpha+\omega)\,w}\right),
  \qquad \Lambda = \frac{2\mu_0}{1-\Gamma_{\times}} .
\end{equation}
\inwords{$\Lambda$ is the total rate of price moves. For very short windows the measured variance is
$\Lambda$---every move counts. For long windows it falls to 
\[
  \Lambda/(1+\Gamma_\times)^2 = 2\mu_0/\big((1-\Gamma_\times)
(1+\Gamma_\times)^2\big),
\]
because moves that are immediately reversed contribute nothing to the
long-run change. The curve between the two is the ``signature plot'' that practitioners use to choose
a sampling frequency for volatility estimates.} Here cross-excitation \emph{damps} long-run variance.
Self-excitation, by contrast, inflates the variance of event \emph{counts} relative to their mean
rate~\citep{bacry2014}; which effect dominates for prices depends on the structure of the kernel
matrix, not on a single number. The two-dimensional version of the model also reproduces the Epps
effect, the drop in measured correlation between two assets when it is computed over very short
windows~\citep{bacry2013}.

\citet{bacry2014} extend the construction to four kernels linking trades and price changes---trades
exciting trades, prices reverting, trades moving prices, and prices triggering trades---and recover
the concave, relaxing impact of large orders executed in pieces. The same machinery was used early to
study book resilience~\citep{large2007} and to make a zero-intelligence order-book simulator produce
realistic spreads~\citep{munitoke2011}.

\paragraph{Price moves of random size: compound Hawkes models.} The tally above makes two simplifications:
every mid move is exactly one tick, and its direction is decided by which of two event streams fired. A
\emph{compound} Hawkes model~\citep{swishchuk2019chp,swishchuk2020gchp} keeps a single Hawkes process $N(t)$
for the \emph{times} of mid moves, with background rate $\mu$ and branching ratio $\Gamma$ as in
Sections~\ref{sec:hawkes-one}--\ref{sec:pp-branching}, and lets each move carry a size and sign of its own:
\begin{equation}
  m_t = m_0 + \sum_{k=1}^{N(t)} J_k .
\label{eq:chp}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the price is still a running sum over mid moves, but the $k$-th move is $J_k$ ticks rather than
$\pm 1$; the Hawkes process decides \emph{when} the price moves, and $J_k$ decides by \emph{how much} and in
which direction.}
The moves $J_k$ take one of a few values---for example the average down move and the average up move, or
finer size classes---and they form a Markov chain: the probability that move $k+1$ takes a given value depends
on the value of move $k$. This lets the model say, for instance, that an up move is more likely to be followed
by another up move. The chain is assumed independent of the event times.

\paragraph{Implications for volatility.} Over long horizons the price behaves like a Brownian motion
with drift. For an exponential kernel, after removing the drift---the average move $\bar J$ times the number
of moves---the price change over a window of length $w$ has standard deviation
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{sd}\Big(m_{t+w} - m_t - \bar J\,\big(N(t+w)-N(t)\big)\Big) \approx \sigma_J \sqrt{\bar\lambda\, w},
  \qquad \bar\lambda = \frac{\mu}{1-\Gamma},
\label{eq:chp-vol}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the spread of price changes grows with the square root of the window, as for any diffusion, and its
size is the product of two numbers: $\sqrt{\bar\lambda}$, from how many moves there are per second, and
$\sigma_J$, from how big the moves are and how they follow each other. The long-run rate $\bar\lambda$ is the
background rate amplified by self-excitation, so the closer $\Gamma$ is to one, the more volatile the price.}
The constant $\sigma_J$ depends on the move sizes and on the transition probabilities of the chain. In the
simplest case---moves of $\pm\delta$ ticks, with the same probability $p$ that a move repeats the direction of
the previous one, whichever it was---the formula of \citet{swishchuk2020gchp} reduces to
\begin{equation}
  \sigma_J^2 = \delta^2\,\frac{p}{1-p}.
\label{eq:chp-sigma}
\end{equation}
\inwords{with independent directions ($p = 1/2$) each move adds $\delta^2$ to the variance; if moves tend to
repeat their direction ($p > 1/2$) they pile up and the variance is larger; if they tend to reverse
($p < 1/2$) they cancel and it is smaller.}
Figure~\ref{fig:compound-hawkes} checks Equation~\eqref{eq:chp-vol} by simulation for three rules for the
moves, all driven by the same kind of Hawkes event stream.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/compound_hawkes.png}
\caption{Compound Hawkes prices. Event times come from a Hawkes process with an exponential kernel, background
rate $0.5$ per second and branching ratio $0.5$, so $\bar\lambda = 1$ move per second. (a) Moves of one tick
with independent directions; (b) moves of one tick that repeat the previous direction with probability
$p = 0.62$; (c) moves of 1 to 5 ticks with independent directions. (1) One path for each rule. (2) Standard
deviation of the price change over a window, across 1{,}500 simulated paths (dots), against
$\sigma_J\sqrt{\bar\lambda w}$ (lines), with $\sigma_J^2 = 1$, $p/(1-p)$ and the mean squared move size
respectively; the simulated values lie within about 7\% of the lines. Stylised simulation; not data.}
\label{fig:compound-hawkes}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Effects of order-flow structure on volatility.} Set beside the model of \citet{bacry2013} above, this gives two ways in which
the structure of order flow changes volatility. There, an up move makes a down move more likely, so moves
reverse and long-run variance is \emph{damped}---the bid--ask bounce. Here, self-excitation raises the number of
moves per second and so \emph{raises} volatility, and the Markov chain of moves raises or lowers it further
according to whether directions tend to repeat or reverse.

\paragraph{Fit to five Nasdaq stocks.} \citet{swishchuk2020gchp} fit the model to the level-1 book of five Nasdaq
stocks on one day, 21 June 2012 (Table~\ref{tab:chp-fit}), and compare the standard deviation of price changes
over windows from 10 seconds to 20 minutes with Equation~\eqref{eq:chp-vol}.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Moves of a single size work only where moves really are a single size.} With every mid move set to
  half a cent, the model tracked the data for MSFT and INTC, where every mid change was half a cent, but
  ``severely'' underestimated the variability for AAPL, AMZN and GOOG, where 61\%, 53\% and 71\% of mid changes
  were larger.
\item \textbf{Adding sizes fixes most of it.} Giving the chain two values, the average down and the average up
  move, improved the fit for AAPL and GOOG but not for AMZN. For AMZN a chain of 12 size classes cut the mean
  squared error of the fit from 0.0208 to 0.0125, and 24 classes only to 0.0123.
\item \textbf{Final agreement.} Comparing the coefficient of $\sqrt{w}$ predicted by the model with the one
  fitted directly to the data, the error was 1.4\% for AAPL, 4.6\% for GOOG, 3.4\% for INTC, 6.4\% for MSFT
  and 20.8\% for AMZN, whose extra volatility the authors leave unexplained.
\end{itemize}
These are in-sample results for one day. An earlier version of the model was
calibrated on one stock, CISCO, over five days of November 2014~\citep{swishchuk2019chp}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Compound Hawkes fits of \citet{swishchuk2020gchp}, 21 June 2012. The branching ratio is our computation
from their fitted kernel parameters ($\Gamma = \alpha/\omega$ in this survey's notation); the repeat
probabilities are their estimates of $P(\text{down}\mid\text{down})$ and $P(\text{up}\mid\text{up})$ for
consecutive mid moves.}
\label{tab:chp-fit}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
\toprule
Stock & Branching ratio $\Gamma$ & $P(\text{down}\mid\text{down})$ & $P(\text{up}\mid\text{up})$ & Mid changes larger than half a cent \\
\midrule
AAPL & 0.41 & 0.50 & 0.49 & 61\% \\
AMZN & 0.42 & 0.46 & 0.46 & 53\% \\
GOOG & 0.44 & 0.48 & 0.45 & 71\% \\
MSFT & 0.53 & 0.63 & 0.58 & none \\
INTC & 0.53 & 0.61 & 0.56 & none \\
