On this one day, the two large-tick stocks, MSFT and INTC, had the highest branching ratios and consecutive mid
moves that tended to repeat their direction, while for the other three a move was about as likely to be
followed by one in either direction. Lalor and Swishchuk later drive the same kind of price---a direction set by
the event type, a size drawn from the data---with a neural Hawkes process (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-nhp}).

\subsection{Fitting Hawkes models in practice}
\label{sec:hawkes-fit}

\paragraph{One event type, exponential kernel, in detail.}
\label{sec:pp-mle}
The input is a sorted list of arrival times $t_1 < \cdots < t_{N_{\text{end}}}$ in a window
$[0, t_{\text{end}}]$, where $N_{\text{end}} = N(t_{\text{end}})$ is the number of arrivals, and
there are three parameters:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item $\mu$, the \textbf{background rate}: arrivals per second with no recent history;
\item $\alpha$, the \textbf{jump}: the rise in intensity, in arrivals per second, at each arrival;
\item $\omega$, the \textbf{decay rate}: each jump fades as $\mathrm{e}^{-\omega u}$, with decay time
  $1/\omega$.
\end{itemize}
Fitting means choosing the parameters under which the observed timestamps are most probable, that
is, maximising the log-likelihood~\citep{ozaki1979}
\begin{equation}
  \ln \mathrm{Lik}(\mu, \alpha, \omega) = \sum_{n=1}^{N_{\text{end}}} \ln \lambda(t_n)
  - \int_0^{t_{\text{end}}} \lambda(t')\, dt'.
  \label{eq:hawkes-loglik}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the first term rewards the model for predicting a high rate at the moments arrivals
actually happened; the second penalises it for predicting more arrivals in total than occurred. If
$\alpha$ is too small the model cannot explain the bursts; if $\alpha$ approaches $\omega$ it predicts
more clustering than there is; a wrong $\mu$ misexplains the quiet periods.}

For the exponential kernel both terms can be computed in one pass over the data. With
\[
  \Psi_n = \mathrm{e}^{-\omega (t_n - t_{n-1})}\,(1 + \Psi_{n-1}), \qquad \Psi_1 = 0,
\]
the intensity at each arrival is $\lambda(t_n) = \mu + \alpha \Psi_n$, and the integral is
\[
  \int_0^{t_{\text{end}}} \lambda(t')\, dt' = \mu\, t_{\text{end}}
  + \frac{\alpha}{\omega} \sum_{n=1}^{N_{\text{end}}} \Big(1 - \mathrm{e}^{-\omega (t_{\text{end}} - t_n)}\Big).
\]
\inwords{$\Psi_n$ is the sum of the faded bumps left by all earlier arrivals, carried forward from the
previous arrival by one decay factor, so it costs one step per arrival. The integral is the expected
number of arrivals in the window: the background rate times the window length, plus the part of each
arrival's bump that falls inside the window.}
Timestamps are shifted to the start of the window before fitting, so that the exponentials do not
underflow. The log-likelihood is then maximised numerically over $(\mu, \alpha, \omega)$, for example
with the Nelder--Mead method, and the optimum checked with a second optimiser. From the fitted
parameters follow the branching ratio $\Gamma = \alpha/\omega$, the kernel decay time $1/\omega$, and
the long-run rate $\bar\lambda = \mu/(1 - \Gamma)$: the background rate amplified by $1/(1-\Gamma)$
through self-excitation.


\paragraph{Maximum likelihood with several event types.} The same method extends to many types. For a $K$-type process observed on $[0, t_{\text{end}}]$ the
log-likelihood is a sum over types,
\begin{equation}
  \ln \mathrm{Lik} = \sum_{i=1}^{K} \Big( \sum_{n:\ \text{event } n \text{ of type } i} \ln \lambda_i(t_n)
  - \int_0^{t_{\text{end}}} \lambda_i(t')\, dt' \Big),
\end{equation}
\inwords{for every type, reward the model for a high intensity at the moments type-$i$ events
happened, and penalise it for the total number of type-$i$ events it expected.}
Because $\lambda_i$ depends only on $\mu_i$ and on the kernels $\phi_{i1}, \dots, \phi_{iK}$ in row
$i$ of the kernel matrix, the $i$-th term involves only those parameters. As long as no parameter is
shared across rows, the fit splits into $K$ independent problems, one per target type, each with
one background rate and the $K$ kernels of row $i$~\citep{wu2019qrhawkes,bacry2015}. For exponential kernels each
term is computed in one pass with a running sum (Section~\ref{sec:pp-mle}), so the cost grows only
linearly with the number of events~\citep{bacry2015}.

Three practical rules follow. First, parameters have natural bounds---$\mu_i > 0$, jumps
$\alpha_{ij} \ge 0$, decay $\omega > 0$---and the optimiser should enforce them. Second, nothing in the
likelihood forces the fitted $\Gamma$ to satisfy $\rho(\Gamma) < 1$; it must be checked after fitting,
and a fit at or above one read as a sign of non-stationarity or a wrong kernel rather than as a fact
about the market (Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}). Third, the likelihood is not convex in the decay
rates, but if the decays are fixed in advance---for example a few exponentials with chosen time
scales---it becomes convex in the background rates and jumps~\citep{wu2019qrhawkes}, so the remaining
fit has a single optimum.

\paragraph{Expectation--maximisation.} Direct maximisation can be unstable because the likelihood is
flat in some directions. The branching structure suggests a different route~\citep{veen2008em}: if we
knew which earlier event triggered each event, or that it was a background event, the parameters would
be simple averages. The EM algorithm alternates between guessing those parents probabilistically and
re-estimating. For one type with kernel $\alpha\mathrm{e}^{-\omega u}$~\citep{lewis2011em}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \emph{E step:} the probability that event $n$ was triggered by earlier event $n'$ is
  $\alpha\mathrm{e}^{-\omega (t_n - t_{n'})}/\lambda(t_n)$, and the probability that it was a background
  event is $\mu/\lambda(t_n)$;
\item \emph{M step:} $\mu$ is the expected number of background events divided by $t_{\text{end}}$; $\Gamma$ is the
  expected number of triggered events divided by the number of events; and $1/\omega$ is the
  probability-weighted average of the parent--child delays $t_n - t_{n'}$; then $\alpha = \Gamma\omega$.
\end{itemize}
\inwords{give each event a fractional family tree, then count: how many events had no parent, how many
children an event has on average, and how long children take to arrive.}

\paragraph{Moments and cumulants.} A second family avoids the likelihood altogether and matches
statistics of the counts. The Wiener--Hopf method estimates kernel shapes from the empirical
second-order statistics of the event streams~\citep{bacrymuzy2016wh} (it underlies the non-parametric
results of~\citet{bacry2016}); the cumulant method NPHC estimates only the integrated kernels---the
branching matrix itself---from second- and third-order integrated cumulants, with no assumption on
kernel shape~\citep{achab2018cumulants}; and method-of-moments calibration with closed-form moments
can be rolled forward through time cheaply~\citep{dafonseca2014}.

\paragraph{The branching ratio from count dispersion.} The simplest moment estimator needs only
counts. Split the data into windows of length $w$, and compute the mean and variance of the number of
events per window; their ratio is the Fano factor $D(w)$ of Section~\ref{sec:pp-fano}. For a
one-type Hawkes process whose correlations die out on time scales much shorter than $w$,
\citet{hardiman2014branching} show that
\begin{equation}
  \Gamma \approx 1 - \sqrt{1/D(w)} .
\end{equation}
\inwords{self-excitation makes counts burstier than Poisson; the more bursty they are, the closer the
branching ratio is to one. A Poisson stream ($D = 1$) gives $\Gamma = 0$.}
The estimate is biased low for finite windows and exact only as $w$ grows without bound, and because
the sample variance can be zero the authors report medians. Applied to the Fano factors of CME NQ
packet data reported in Section~\ref{sec:pp-fano}---about 52 at $w = 0.1$\,s and about a thousand at
$w = 60$\,s---the formula gives $\Gamma \approx 0.86$ and $\Gamma \approx 0.97$, against $0.80$ from
the exponential-kernel likelihood fit on the same kind of data. Two cautions make this a teaching
example rather than a measurement. The estimate keeps rising with $w$, the same symptom that a slowly
varying background rate produces~\citep{filimonovsornette2015}. And the same stream with its gaps
shuffled---which destroys all self-excitation but keeps the gap distribution---has $D \approx 17$, for
which the formula would still report $\Gamma \approx 0.76$: dispersion alone does not prove
excitation, because a renewal process with very irregular gaps is also over-dispersed.

\paragraph{Non-stationarity.} A single fit over a long window assumes a constant background rate.
Because activity follows intraday patterns and changes across regimes, such fits overstate
endogeneity~\citep{filimonovsornette2015}. In practice models are fitted over short windows, with a
time-varying background, or with the background modelled explicitly. The effect can be large;
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-regimes} gives published examples. Bursts at unknown times can also be detected and separated from the background
\citep{rambaldi2018bursts}.

\paragraph{Online use.} With exponential kernels the intensity is updated in constant time per event,
which makes Hawkes intensities usable inside a low-latency trading loop. The parameters change more
slowly. One workable scheme, which we describe rather than cite, holds the decay rates fixed during a
session and re-fits the background rates and jumps on a rolling window, starting each fit from the
previous solution; with fixed decays each re-fit is a convex problem. Fully online estimators also
exist: for example, \citet{yang2017online} update non-parametric kernels with projected gradient steps
on a discretised, regularised likelihood, with regret growing only logarithmically in time. Whatever
the scheme, the gradient of the log-likelihood contains the compensator term $-\int \lambda_i$ as well
as the event term, so an update that looks only at the events (``the rate was high when events
happened'') and ignores how many events the model expected will push the intensities up without limit.

\paragraph{Goodness of fit: time rescaling.} A fitted point process can be checked with a classical
transformation. Replace each event time $t_n$ by the expected number of events up to it under the
fitted model,
\[
  t_n \;\mapsto\; \int_0^{t_n} \widehat\lambda(t')\,dt' .
\]
\inwords{each event time is replaced by the number of events the fitted model expected up to that time,
so time is measured in units of expected events rather than seconds.}
If the model is right, the transformed times form
a Poisson process with rate one, so the gaps between them are independent and exponential with mean
one~\citep{ogata1988,brown2002}. A quantile--quantile plot of those gaps against the exponential
distribution, or a Kolmogorov--Smirnov test, shows where the model fails---for example, too few very
short gaps means the model underestimates clustering.

\paragraph{Simulation.} Hawkes paths are generated by \emph{thinning}~\citep{ogata1981}: propose a
candidate event using an upper bound on the current intensity, which is valid because exponential
intensities only decay between events, and accept it with probability equal to the true intensity
divided by the bound. Simulated paths are how a fitted model answers questions that have no closed
form, such as the probability that a queue empties within a given latency.

\paragraph{Software.} The open-source library tick~\citep{bacry2018tick} implements maximum-likelihood
fits with exponential and sum-of-exponential kernels, EM, Wiener--Hopf and cumulant estimators, and
simulation.

\begin{modelcard}{Linear (multivariate) Hawkes}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{Background rates $\mu_i$; the kernels $\phi_{ij}$---for exponential kernels,
the jumps $\alpha_{ij}$ and decay $\omega$---and hence the branching matrix $\Gamma$.}
\cardrow{How}{Maximum likelihood, split into one problem per target type, with bounded parameters and
a one-pass running sum for exponential kernels; or EM, Wiener--Hopf, cumulant and dispersion estimators.
Fit on short or rolling windows, check $\rho(\Gamma) < 1$ afterwards, and test the fit by time
rescaling.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Forecast event intensities and, from them, drift, expected OFI and
depletion times; simulate order flow by thinning; monitor how much activity is endogenous; raise a
cancellation alarm when the aggressive intensity against a resting order spikes.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{Aftershocks after an earthquake, or acoustic feedback: each event raises
the rate of further events, which fades; the branching ratio is the loop gain.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Empirical findings from fitted Hawkes models}
\label{sec:hawkes-find}

\paragraph{Branching ratios.} Estimates of the branching ratio for financial event streams have
been high since the mid-2000s, but the exact value is contested. On E-mini S\&P~500 futures,
\citet{filimonovsornette2012} find it rising from about $0.3$ around 1998--2000 to $0.7$--$0.8$ after
2007, with spikes to $0.9$. \citet{hardimanbercotbouchaud2013}, using power-law kernels, find the
kernel integrating to approximately one---a market at criticality. \citet{filimonovsornette2015}
argue that estimates near one are biased upward by outliers, kernel regularisation, edge effects
and regime shifts---fitting a constant background rate to a period in which the true background
varies; their
re-estimation gives values that later fluctuate between about $0.8$ and $1.1$. On CME NQ market
data, an exponential-kernel fit to packet arrivals gives about $0.80$ (Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}).
The defensible summary is that a large majority of events are endogenous, and that the precise
number depends on the kernel family and on how non-stationarity is handled.

\paragraph{Kernel shape and long memory.} Non-parametric estimation~\citep{bacry2016} finds kernels
that decay roughly as power laws with exponent slightly above one (in simulation the method recovers
power laws over six decades of time). In
an eight-dimensional model of DAX and Bund futures (price moves, trades, limit orders and
cancellations, each split by side), trades, limit orders and cancellations mainly excite events of
the same type and side, while price moves on one side mainly excite price moves on the other---mean
reversion. The persistence of order flow is a long-documented fact: the signs of market orders form a
long-memory process with a Hurst exponent of about $0.7$~\citep{lillo2004}, explained mainly by the
splitting of large orders into many small ones~\citep{lillo2005,bouchaud2009}. Power-law Hawkes
kernels are the point-process expression of the same fact.

\subsection{Near-criticality}
\label{sec:hawkes-crit}

\paragraph{Behaviour of a Hawkes process as the branching ratio approaches one.} Three things change sharply as $\Gamma$ approaches one, and together they are what \emph{near-critical}
means (Figure~\ref{fig:criticality}).
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Shocks are amplified enormously.} One outside event produces on average $1/(1-\Gamma)$ events in
  total: 10 at $\Gamma = 0.9$, 100 at $0.99$. Most activity is then reaction rather than news.
\item \textbf{The market takes much longer to calm down.} With an exponential kernel of decay rate $\omega$, the
  expected extra activity after a shock fades at rate $\omega(1-\Gamma)$, not $\omega$. The reason fits in two
  lines: between events the extra intensity decays at rate $\omega$, but while it is above zero it keeps producing
  events, each of which adds $\alpha$ back; on average these two effects combine to
  \begin{equation}
    \frac{d}{dt}\,\E\big[\lambda(t) - \mu\big] = -\omega\,\E\big[\lambda(t) - \mu\big] + \alpha\,\E\big[\lambda(t)\big],
    \label{eq:crit-slow}
  \end{equation}
  and after a single outside event the extra activity therefore decays as $e^{-(\omega - \alpha)t} =
  e^{-\omega(1-\Gamma)t}$.
  \inwords{the rate falls because old excitation wears off, and rises because the events it produces add new
  excitation; the net speed of calming down is the difference, which shrinks to nothing as $\Gamma \to 1$.}
  With $\omega$ equal to one per second, the market calms down in about 2 seconds at $\Gamma = 0.5$, 10 seconds at
  $0.9$ and 100 seconds at $0.99$. Physicists call this \emph{critical slowing down}.
\item \textbf{Bursts become wildly uneven.} The number of events in the family started by one outside event is
  random. Because each event's number of children is Poisson with mean $\Gamma$, the family size follows a known
  distribution (the Borel distribution), shown in Figure~\ref{fig:criticality}a. At $\Gamma = 0.99$ the average
  family has 100 events, yet more than a third of families (37\%) consist of the outside event alone and half have
  at most two; the average is carried by rare enormous ones---1.7\% of families exceed 1{,}000 events. Near one,
  the typical burst is small and the occasional burst is huge.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/criticality.png}
\caption{Near-criticality. (a) Probability that the family of events started by one outside event has at least
$n$ events, for branching ratios 0.5, 0.9 and 0.99 (log scales). The mean family size is $1/(1-\Gamma)$, but the
median stays at one or two; near one the mean is carried by rare, very large families. (b) Expected extra activity
after one outside event, for an exponential kernel with decay rate one per second: it fades at rate
$\omega(1-\Gamma)$, so the time to calm down is $1/(\omega(1-\Gamma))$. Exact formulas, not data.}
\label{fig:criticality}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Consequences of near-criticality for markets.} These three effects describe a market that is very sensitive: small outside
news can set off long, large bursts of activity, most activity is self-generated, and the size of the next burst is
hard to predict. That is why whether markets sit close to one is debated (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-find}), and why a
changing background rate, which can make a market look closer to one than it is
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-branching-use}), matters. It also explains the mathematical results below: long, slowly
fading bursts of order flow, added up over time, become a volatility that is itself random and persistent.

\paragraph{The long-run limit.} When the branching ratio of a Hawkes model of order flow approaches
one, the price it generates converges, over long horizons, to a price whose volatility is itself random
and persistent~\citep{jaisson2015,jaisson2016,eleuch2018leverage,eleuch2019roughening}.\footnote{With
light-tailed kernels the limit is a Heston stochastic-volatility model~\citep{jaisson2015}; with
power-law kernels it is a \emph{rough} volatility model~\citep{jaisson2016}; asymmetry between buying and
selling pressure adds the leverage effect, volatility rising when prices fall
\citep{eleuch2018leverage,eleuch2019roughening}. Empirical estimates of rough volatility are in
\citet{gatheral2018rough}, and Markovian approximations of rough models in \citet{abijaber2019multifactor}.}
This is a qualitative link between strong, long-memory excitation in order flow and the behaviour of
volatility, not a claim that markets sit exactly at the critical point.

\subsection{Marks: events with sizes and labels}
\label{sec:hawkes-marks}

\paragraph{Events of different size and type.} So far every event of a given type has counted the same: a 1-lot trade and a 100-lot
trade each leave the same bump in the intensity. It seems obvious that a large trade should excite more. Whatever
extra information an event carries---its size, side, price level, how aggressive it was, how far it moved the
price---is called its \emph{mark}, and a Hawkes process in which marks affect the intensity is a \emph{marked}
Hawkes process. This subsection explains the three ways marks can enter, and what the evidence on order-book data
shows.

\paragraph{The formal set-up.} When each event $n$ carries a mark, the process is a marked point process. Its
intensity can be split into two parts: the rate at which events of any kind arrive, called the \emph{ground
intensity}, and the probability distribution of the mark given that an event occurs~\citep{rasmussen2018notes}.
With that split, the log-likelihood is a sum of a term for the event times and a term for the marks, so the two
can be fitted separately when they share no parameters.

\paragraph{Three ways a mark can enter.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{As an event type.} Cut the mark into a few classes---small, medium and large trades; aggressive or
  not; at the best price or one tick away---and treat each class as a separate type of event in a multivariate
  Hawkes model (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi}). This is the most common route by far, and it is not an
  approximation in disguise: a mark cut into $M$ classes is formally the same as an unmarked Hawkes process with
  $M$ times as many event types~\citep{bacrymuzy2016wh}. Its cost is the number of kernels, which grows with the
  square of the number of types.
\item \textbf{As a multiplier.} Keep one kernel and multiply it by a function of the mark, so that event $n$ adds
  $\phi(t - t_n)\, g(\text{mark}_n)$ to the intensity---the ``typical choice'' in the words of
  \citet{bacry2015}. A larger trade then excites more, but with the same time profile
  (Figure~\ref{fig:marks-kernels}a). The function $g$ is often a power of the size or a linear function of it.
\item \textbf{As part of the kernel.} Let the whole bump depend on the mark, $\phi(t - t_n, \text{mark}_n)$, so that
  a larger trade can excite for longer as well as more (Figure~\ref{fig:marks-kernels}b). This is the most flexible
  and the hardest to estimate; it is usually done by cutting the mark into classes (route~1) or
  non-parametrically.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth]{figs/marks_kernels.png}
\caption{Two ways a trade's size can change its effect on later events. (a) Multiplicative: the bump keeps its
shape and is scaled up. (b) Non-separable: a larger trade's bump is higher and decays more slowly. On Bund and DAX
futures the data favour (b)~\citep{rambaldi2017marked}. Illustrative shapes, not fitted.}
\label{fig:marks-kernels}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Marks as event types versus marks that change excitation.} The routes above differ in one essential way: whether the
mark decides \emph{which kind} of event it is, or \emph{how big an effect} it has.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{The mark as a type (route 1).} The size---or side, or price level---is used only to sort events into a
  few classes. Inside a class every event counts exactly the same. \citet{lu2018}, for example, model the 30 DAX
  stocks with twelve types: limit orders, cancellations and market orders, on each side, each split by whether it
  moves the mid-price. A market buy is classed by comparing its size with the queue at the best ask: smaller than
  the queue, it leaves the price unchanged and is ``non-aggressive''; at least as large, it empties the level and
  is ``aggressive''. After that, the model never sees the size again.
\item \textbf{The mark in the excitation (routes 2 and 3).} The actual value of the size goes into the formula for
  the bump, so that a bigger trade leaves a bigger, or longer, bump---continuously, not in steps.
\end{itemize}
Table~\ref{tab:marks-example} shows the difference on three market buys hitting a best ask of 100 lots.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Three market buys against a best-ask queue of 100 lots, under the two uses of size. The square-root
multiplier is an illustrative choice.}
\label{tab:marks-example}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Trade & Size used as a type & Size used in the excitation (bump $\propto \sqrt{\text{size}}$) \\
\midrule
5 lots & non-aggressive: bump $B_0$ & bump $\approx 2.2$ units \\
90 lots & non-aggressive: the \emph{same} bump $B_0$ & bump $\approx 9.5$ units \\
500 lots & aggressive: bump $B_1$ & bump $\approx 22.4$ units \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

With size as a type, size matters only at the cut-off, as a step; used in the excitation, it matters everywhere,
as a smooth curve.

\paragraph{Frequency of each use in order-book modelling.} The first is standard: almost every Hawkes model of the book
splits events by side, kind of order, aggressiveness or price level, because that needs nothing new---it is a
multivariate Hawkes model with more event types, fitted in the usual way. The second is rare. A 2025 study states
that the unmarked Hawkes process ``remains the most widely used in financial market research'' and lists seven
studies that include marks~\citep{richards2025scoretest}. Two influential studies leave sizes out on purpose:
\citet{bacry2014} because marks would make estimation much more difficult, and \citet{lu2018} because the average
price jump in their data was 1.08 ticks, close enough to one.

\paragraph{Published evidence on excitation marks.} Table~\ref{tab:marks-evidence} collects the studies in which a size or
similar mark changes the excitation.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Marked Hawkes models fitted to high-frequency financial data. ``Simpler form tested'': whether the study
checks its marked model against a simpler one.}
\label{tab:marks-evidence}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.12\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.13\linewidth}}
\toprule
Study & Mark & Data & Finding & Simpler form tested \\
\midrule
\citet{rambaldi2017marked} & trade volume, 6 classes & Bund and DAX futures & large trades excite more and for longer; the multiplicative form is rejected & the multiplicative form, rejected \\
\citet{fabre2025neuralhawkes} & trade volume, non-parametric & Coinbase BTC-USD, Dec.\ 2023 & excitation rises with volume, more slowly for larger trades, and levels off above about 50{,}000 USD & no \\
\citet{richards2025scoretest} & 21 candidate marks & silver futures, July 2015 & the best marks---all volume-based, such as order imbalance---are significant in about half of 1{,}000-event segments & no mark, by a score test \\
\citet{leeseo2017marked} & size of the price jump & NYSE stocks, 2008--2011 & linear effect, slope about 0.2 & no \\
\citet{deschatre2022electricity} & size of the price jump & German intraday power, 2017 & excitation linear in jump size & no \\
\citet{josephjain2024marked} & trade volume, neural kernel & Binance BTC and ETH, one half-hour & a volume effect in two of four event streams & informally, by Q--Q plots \\
\citet{chevalier2023disorder} & volume, power law & BNP Paribas trades-through, 2022 & average branching ratio 0.83; exponent shown as a box plot & no \\
\citet{jain2024chp} & order size & Nasdaq & past order sizes show no clear effect on arrival rates (the authors call the evidence weak) & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Three conclusions follow. Size does matter for at least some kinds of event: larger trades excite more, on
several markets. The simple multiplicative form is not enough where it has been tested: on Bund and DAX futures
the shape of the response changes with size, not only its height. The evidence comes from few studies with short
samples, and we found no study that compares a marked with an unmarked model out of sample. \citet{fauthtudor2012}
specify an excitation proportional to a power of the order size; an estimate of the power is not given; we found no
study that estimates such a power and relates it to the square-root law of market impact.

\paragraph{Conditions for using marks.} For a first model, split events into a few types by the marks that obviously
matter---side, kind of order, whether the event moves the price---which is route~1 and standard practice. Add a
size mark when large orders are a large share of what matters for the decision at hand, and test it: the score
test of \citet{richards2025scoretest} checks whether a mark matters using only the unmarked fit, before a marked
model is built.

\subsection{State-dependent Hawkes models}
\label{sec:hawkes-sd}

\paragraph{Coupling event rates and book state.} The Hawkes models so far let the rate of events depend on past events
but not on the book: a cancellation at the best ask raises the rate of further cancellations by the same amount
whether the spread is one tick or five. The queue models of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models} do the opposite: in
the queue-reactive model the rates depend on the state of the book, but an event has no effect on later rates
except through the state it leaves behind. \citet{morariupatrichi2022} point out that each approach has what the
other lacks, and join them in a \emph{state-dependent Hawkes process}.

\paragraph{The model.} The book is summarised by a state $X(t)$ that takes one of a few values---for example
``spread of one tick'' and ``spread of two ticks or more''. The model has two parts. First, the intensities are
those of a multivariate Hawkes process (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi}), except that the bump left by each past
event depends on the state of the book \emph{at the time that event happened}:
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_i(t) = \mu_i + \sum_{j=1}^{K}\;\sum_{\text{past type-}j\text{ events at } t'} \phi_{ij}\bigl(t - t',\, X(t')\bigr).
\label{eq:sdh-intensity}
\end{equation}
\inwords{as before, each past event adds a decaying bump to the rate of type-$i$ events; but now the height and
duration of the bump also depend on what the book looked like when the event occurred. A trade when the spread
is wide can trigger a different reaction from a trade when it is tight.}
Second, the state changes only at events, and how it changes depends on the type of the event:
\begin{equation}
  \P\bigl(\text{state after the event} = x' \,\big|\, \text{event of type } j,\ \text{state before} = x\bigr) = \pi_j(x' \mid x).
\label{eq:sdh-state}
\end{equation}
\inwords{after each event, the state of the book moves to a new value at random, with probabilities that depend
on what kind of event it was: a sell order is likely to lower the queue imbalance, a buy order to raise it.}
The two parts make a feedback loop (Figure~\ref{fig:sdh-loop}): events change the state, and the state changes
how strongly events excite further events. With a single state the model is an ordinary Hawkes process.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  b/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, text width=36mm, minimum height=13mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, thick}]
\node[b, fill=green!12] (ev) at (0,0) {\textbf{Order flow}\\events of each type, at rates $\lambda_i(t)$};
\node[b, fill=blue!10] (st) at (7,0) {\textbf{State of the book}\\e.g.\ spread, or queue imbalance in five bins};
\draw[arr] (ev.north east) to[bend left=20] node[above, align=center]{each event moves the state\\with probabilities $\pi_j(x' \mid x)$} (st.north west);
\draw[arr] (st.south west) to[bend left=20] node[below, align=center]{the state sets the size and duration\\of the bump each event leaves, $\phi_{ij}(\cdot, x)$} (ev.south east);
\draw[arr] ([yshift=3mm]ev.west) .. controls +(-1.6,0.9) and +(-1.6,-0.9) .. node[left, align=center]{events excite\\events} ([yshift=-3mm]ev.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The feedback loop of a state-dependent Hawkes process~\citep{morariupatrichi2022}. An ordinary Hawkes
process has only the loop on the left; a queue-reactive model has a state, but no excitation between events.
Schematic.}
\label{fig:sdh-loop}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Estimation of the state-dependent model.} One might expect the coupling to make the model hard to estimate. It does
not: the likelihood splits into a part for the transition probabilities and a part for the intensities, so the
probabilities $\pi_j(x'\mid x)$ can be estimated on their own---by counting, after each type of event, how often
each state was followed by each other state---and the intensities are then fitted by maximum likelihood exactly
as for an ordinary multivariate Hawkes process. Simulation is the thinning algorithm of
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit} with one extra step: draw the time of the next event, draw its type in proportion to
the intensities, then draw the new state from $\pi_j$. The authors provide a Python library, \texttt{mpoints}, for
both.

\paragraph{Results on a year of Intel level-1 data.} \citet{morariupatrichi2022} fit two versions to a year of Nasdaq level-1 data
for Intel (INTC, June 2017 to May 2018, 250 trading days, 12:00 to 14:30 each day, where activity is roughly
constant). Events are of two types: \emph{bid events}---buy market orders, buy limit orders at the best bid and
cancellations of sell orders at the best ask, all of which push the price up---and \emph{ask events}, the mirror
image. The difference between the two counts is a proxy for order-flow imbalance (Section~\ref{sec:ofi}). The
two versions differ in the state (Table~\ref{tab:sdh-models}).

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The two state-dependent Hawkes models fitted by \citet{morariupatrichi2022} to Intel. Kernels are
exponential; parameters are estimated separately for each day.}
\label{tab:sdh-models}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Spread model & Queue-imbalance model \\
\midrule
Event types & bid events, ask events & bid events, ask events \\
State & spread of one tick, or of two ticks or more & queue imbalance in five equal bins: below $-0.6$, $-0.6$ to $-0.2$, $-0.2$ to $0.2$, $0.2$ to $0.6$, above $0.6$ \\
Parameters & 26 & 92 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Excitation is fast and mostly self-excitation.} In every state, bid events excite bid events and
  ask events excite ask events more than either excites the other, on time scales from 0.1 to 100 milliseconds.
\item \textbf{The state changes the excitation.} When the spread widens to two ticks or more, self-excitation
  roughly doubles in size, and cross-excitation lasts much longer. When the queue imbalance shows heavy selling
  pressure (below $-0.6$), the self-excitation of ask events rises; under mild buying pressure it nearly halves.
\item \textbf{The state pulls back towards normal.} The spread stays where it is after 98\% of events when it is
  one tick and after 93\% when it is wider; it is six times as likely to narrow from wide to one tick (6\%) as
  to widen from one tick (1\%). Transitions of the queue imbalance are also more likely towards the balanced
  state than away from it, and those after bid events mirror those after ask events.
\item \textbf{How self-exciting the market is depends on the state.} The spectral radius of the branching matrix
  (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-multi}), computed state by state, is higher in the unbalanced states than in the
  balanced ones. With a wide spread it is systematically above one; with a strongly unbalanced queue it is
  above 0.9 on half of the days and occasionally above one. The authors conclude that the near-critical
  behaviour discussed in Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-crit} is largely a feature of particular states of the book,
  not of the market at all times.
\item \textbf{The excitation shifted in February 2018.} In the queue-imbalance model the size of excitation
  changed around 5 February 2018, the day the VIX volatility index more than doubled.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Testing whether the state dependence improves the fit.} Because the state-dependent model contains the ordinary one, it must fit
better on the data used to fit it. The authors therefore also check it on the half-hour after the fitting
window (14:30 to 15:00) each day: it still fits better than an ordinary Hawkes process, but the improvement is
smaller, and small overall, because the two models behave alike in the common states and differ mainly in the
rare ones---a wide spread, a strongly unbalanced queue. They also compare it with a plain Hawkes process that
simply treats each (event, state) pair as its own event type, which ignores the rule that the next state follows
from the current state and the event; with 210 parameters against 92 it fitted worse. The paper reports results
for Intel only; the authors say four other stocks (AMD, MU, SNAP, TWTR) gave largely similar results.

\paragraph{Extensions.} The authors list three: power-law or sum-of-exponential kernels in place of exponential
ones; background rates $\mu_i$ that also depend on the state, which would make both the ordinary Hawkes process
and the queue-reactive model special cases; and kernels that can be negative, for inhibition, with a non-linear
link to keep intensities positive (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants}). The neural model of \citet{shi2022sdpnhp}
reviewed in Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-pnhp} keeps the state-transition part and replaces the kernels by a network.

\begin{modelcard}{State-dependent Hawkes}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The transition probabilities $\pi_j(x'\mid x)$ of the state after each event type;
the background rates; and, for each pair of event types and each state, the height and decay of the
kernel.}
\cardrow{How}{Count state transitions to estimate $\pi_j$; maximise the Hawkes likelihood for the rest, as for an
ordinary multivariate model. Check fit on data outside the fitting window, since the model always fits better in
sample than the ordinary Hawkes process it contains.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Intensity forecasts and simulations in which the reaction of order flow depends on
the spread or the imbalance; measuring how self-exciting the market is in each state of the book.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{The aftershock model with a dial set by the state of the book---and every
aftershock can turn the dial.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Regimes: when the parameters change over time}
\label{sec:hawkes-regimes}

\paragraph{Parameters that vary during the day.} A Hawkes model fitted to a day of data assumes that one background rate and one set of
kernels hold all day. Markets do not work like that: activity follows the clock---busy at the open and close,
quiet at lunch---jumps around news, and has calm and agitated periods. There are three ways to let the parameters
change, from the simplest to the most ambitious (Figure~\ref{fig:hawkes-regimes}):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{A background rate that varies with time.} Keep the kernels fixed, but let the background rate
  follow the time of day, or add a term for scheduled news. This is the most common fix.
\item \textbf{Kernels that vary slowly.} Let the strength of excitation itself change through the day:
  \emph{locally stationary} Hawkes processes.
\item \textbf{Hidden regimes.} Suppose the market switches at random between a few regimes---say normal, busy and
  extreme---each with its own background rate and kernels, and that the regime is not observed but inferred from
  the events: a \emph{Markov-modulated} Hawkes process, fitted by the expectation--maximisation algorithm. It was
  developed for earthquake sequences, with regimes for main shocks, aftershocks and quiet
  periods~\citep{wang2012mmhp}.
\end{enumerate}
These differ from the state-dependent models of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-sd}, where the state is observed in the
book, such as the spread; here the change is driven by the clock, by news or by a hidden regime.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  st/.style={circle, draw, minimum size=11mm, align=center, inner sep=1pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\node[st, fill=green!12] (n) at (0,0) {normal\\$\mu_1, \phi_1$};
\node[st, fill=orange!20] (b) at (2.8,0) {busy\\$\mu_2, \phi_2$};
\node[st, fill=red!25] (x) at (5.6,0) {extreme\\$\mu_3, \phi_3$};
\draw[arr] (n) to[bend left=25] (b); \draw[arr] (b) to[bend left=25] (n);
\draw[arr] (b) to[bend left=25] (x); \draw[arr] (x) to[bend left=25] (b);
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (7.0,0.35) {a hidden regime switches at random;};
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (7.0,-0.05) {in each regime events follow a Hawkes};
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (7.0,-0.45) {process with its own background rate and kernels};
\node[font=\small\bfseries, anchor=west] at (-0.6,1.1) {Hidden regimes};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A Markov-modulated Hawkes process with three regimes. The regime is not observed; it is inferred from
the pattern of events. Schematic.}
\label{fig:hawkes-regimes}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Effect of regimes on the estimated branching ratio.} The main reason to care is the trap of
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-branching-use}: if the background rate changes and the model assumes it constant, the model
mistakes busy periods for excitation and overstates the branching ratio. Published evidence:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item On Nikkei 225 mini futures, a background rate allowed to vary smoothly through the day gives a branching
  ratio of 0.41, and the authors report that a constant background clearly overestimates it~\citep{omi2017tdb}.
\item On EBS foreign-exchange quotes for 2012, a model without news gives branching ratios of 0.84 to 0.95;
  adding a kernel for scheduled macroeconomic announcements lowers them, and the authors warn that a model ignoring news ``could overestimate'' it~\citep{rambaldi2015news}.
\item On BNP Paribas trades-through, letting the background rate vary piecewise linearly through the day raised the
  share of days on which the model passed all four goodness-of-fit tests from 71--76\% to
  86--87\%~\citep{munitokepomponio2012}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Regime-dependent kernels.} Letting only the background vary is not always enough. On Apple trades in
2015, a model with all three parameters of an exponential Hawkes process varying through the day finds estimates
that move far more than any fixed daily pattern would explain~\citep{clinetpotiron2018}. On Paris stocks, the shape
of the spectrum of trade arrivals changes along the day~\citep{roueffvonsachs2019}. On the German intraday power
market, a test rejects a constant strength of excitation in 20 of 21 sessions, with endogeneity peaking a few hours
before delivery~\citep{deschatre2025nonlocal}. On Australian stock trades, a Hawkes model with a smooth background
rate fits better than a standard duration model and gives branching ratios from 0.10 to 0.66 across
days~\citep{chenhall2013seppvb}.

\paragraph{Hidden regimes on market data.} Hidden-regime Hawkes models are hard to estimate, and their authors note
that they have ``received much less attention'' for that reason~\citep{fabremunitoke2026mmhp}. The only application to
high-frequency market data that we found is theirs: trades in a cryptocurrency pair on Coinbase (SEI-USD,
December 2023 to March 2024), with up to four hidden regimes in which both the background rate and the kernel
switch. It beats a hidden-regime model without excitation (a Markov-modulated Poisson process) on a model-selection
criterion and on out-of-sample goodness-of-fit checks. With three regimes, the market spent 90.3\% of the time in a
normal regime, 9.6\% in a busy one and 0.14\% in an extreme-burst regime, which nevertheless carried about a fifth
to a quarter of the volume. Fitting one day took from about fifteen minutes to about ten hours, depending on the
resolution of the kernel. \citet{swishchuk2019chp} give limit theorems for a regime-switching compound Hawkes price,
with a two-state illustration on one stock.

\paragraph{Gaps in the literature.} We found no study that compares a hidden-regime Hawkes model with a single-regime one
out of sample on order-book data. The practical lesson is the simpler one: let the background rate
follow the time of day and the news before reading anything into the branching ratio.

\subsection{Variants of the Hawkes model}
\label{sec:hawkes-variants}

The linear model with exponential kernels is a starting point. Each variant below relaxes one of its
assumptions; Table~\ref{tab:hawkes-variants} gives the model card for each.

\paragraph{Sums of exponentials.} Power-law kernels capture long memory but lose the constant-time
update. A power law can be approximated over many decades by a small number of exponentials with
geometrically spaced decay rates---for an exponent of two, about 1.7 exponentials per decade
\citep{bochud2007}---and each exponential keeps its own running sum, so the intensity is still updated
in constant time per event. With the decay rates fixed, the likelihood is convex in the remaining
parameters~\citep{wu2019qrhawkes}.

\paragraph{Nonlinear and inhibitory kernels.} Linear Hawkes kernels can only excite. A nonlinear Hawkes
process passes the sum through a function that keeps the intensity non-negative,

\[
  \lambda_i(t) = h\big(\mu_i + \sum_j \int \phi_{ij}(t-t')\, dN_j(t')\big),
\]
\inwords{compute the usual Hawkes sum of background rate and bumps, which may now be negative, and pass it
through a function $h$ that never returns a negative value, so the result is a valid intensity.}
This allows negative
kernels, that is, events that make others \emph{less} likely; general conditions for a stationary
version were given by~\citet{bremaud1996}. In limit-order-book data, \citet{lu2018} observe inhibition
effects that lead them to nonlinear Hawkes models, and \citet{rambaldi2017marked} find that limit orders
(and cancellations) on one side inhibit the same type on the other side, which they read as a
fair-price effect. The link functions used in finance so far are simple: a floor at zero, which lets the
sum be negative but cuts the intensity off there~\citep{jain2024chp}, and an exponential function of the
order-book state that multiplies an otherwise linear Hawkes intensity~\citep{sfendourakis2020,mucciante2024}. We found no finance application of the
saturating (sigmoid) links used elsewhere with reported results.

\paragraph{Marks and regimes.} Two variants have their own subsections: marks, the size or other attributes of
each event (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-marks}), and regimes, parameters that change over time
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-regimes}).

\paragraph{Quadratic Hawkes and the Zumbach effect.} Volatility has a time asymmetry: past volatility
measured over long horizons predicts future short-horizon volatility better than the reverse---the
\emph{Zumbach effect}~\citep{zumbach2009}. A Hawkes intensity that depends only on past event counts
cannot produce it. The quadratic Hawkes model of~\citet{blanc2017qhawkes} adds a term in the
\emph{square} of a moving average of past signed price changes, so that a sequence of moves in the same
direction raises activity more than the same number of moves that cancel out. Calibrated on five-minute
bins of 133 NYSE stocks over 2000--2009, it reproduces the time asymmetry, and it generates long memory
in volatility without requiring the process to be critical. The model also allows a term \emph{linear} in
past price changes, which would capture the leverage effect; intraday the authors find its kernel
``hardly distinct from zero'', unlike in daily models where it is significantly negative, and set it to
zero.

\paragraph{Queue-reactive Hawkes.} \citet{wu2019qrhawkes} combine the two ideas of this part: the
background rates depend on the current queue size, as in Section~\ref{sec:qr}, and events excite each
other through sum-of-exponential kernels. On Bund and DAX futures the Hawkes term greatly improves the
pure queue-reactive model, both in the distribution of times between events and in the distribution of
queue sizes; market-order and price-change rates are mainly functions of the volume imbalance, while
limit and cancellation rates are not.

\paragraph{Cross-asset Hawkes.} Events in one instrument can excite events in another; this is the
point-process form of lead--lag, discussed in Section~\ref{sec:cross}. The two-asset model of
\citet{bacry2013} reproduces the Epps effect on Euro-Bund and Euro-Bobl futures, and lead--lag
can be read off its second-order properties; \citet{dafonseca2017} fit a multi-asset Hawkes model whose
diffusive limit links high- and low-frequency correlation and which captures lead--lag on Eurex
assets. For interest rates, the
Hawkes studies we found use European government-bond futures---Bund and Bobl~\citep{bacry2013,bacry2016,rambaldi2017marked}.
We found no Hawkes study of US Treasury futures, of the BrokerTec cash market, or of lead--lag between
Treasury cash and futures; the main microstructure study of BrokerTec we found uses a vector
autoregression~\citep{fleming2018brokertec}. At a much lower frequency, \citet{hainaut2016bivariate} drive a short-rate model with a bivariate
mutually exciting process standing for supply and demand in fixed-income markets, fitted to one-year swap rates
from 2004 to 2014.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Model cards for the Hawkes variants.}
\label{tab:hawkes-variants}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.14\linewidth}*{4}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}}
\toprule
Variant & What you calibrate & How & What you do with it & How to picture it \\
\midrule
Sum of exponentials & jumps per (pair of types, decay); decays fixed on a geometric grid & convex likelihood once decays are fixed & long memory with constant-time updates, in software or hardware & a power law drawn with a few rulers of different lengths \\
Nonlinear / inhibitory & signed kernels; the link function & maximum likelihood (poorly convex) & represent events that discourage others & a thermostat as well as an amplifier \\
Marked (volume classes) & one set of kernels per size class & as for a multivariate model, one type per class & let large trades matter more, with their own time profile & a big stone makes bigger, longer ripples \\
Markov-modulated & per-regime background and decay; switching rates & EM over hidden regimes & separate quiet and busy regimes & weather that switches between drizzle and storm \\
Quadratic (Zumbach) & self-excitation kernel; return-feedback kernel & on binned returns (GMM, then likelihood) & volatility forecasts with the right time asymmetry & a trend, not just activity, wakes the market up \\
Queue-reactive Hawkes & size-dependent background; excitation kernels & maximum likelihood with fixed decays & simulate queues with realistic clustering & the toll booth of Section~\ref{sec:qr}, with rush hours \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsection{Neural point processes}
\label{sec:npp}

\paragraph{The common structure of the models so far.} Everything so far in this section has the same structure: the rate of
type-$i$ events is a background rate plus a sum of bumps, one per past event, each with a fixed shape
$\phi_{ij}$. The variants of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants} change one ingredient at a time---the
shape of the bump, its sign, its dependence on size or on the queue. A neural point process changes
all of them at once: it keeps the idea of an intensity, but lets a neural network compute it from the
history. This subsection explains what that means, step by step.
\paragraph{The part of the Hawkes model replaced by the network.} A Hawkes model can be taken apart into three pieces
(Figure~\ref{fig:npp-pipeline}, top):
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=1pt]
\item a \textbf{background rate} $\mu_i$ for each event type;
\item a \textbf{memory} of the past: for each pair of event types, a running total of decaying bumps---every
  past type-$j$ event adds $\phi_{ij}(t - t_{n'})$ to the memory for type $i$, and the bump shrinks at a fixed
  speed as time passes;
\item a \textbf{readout} that turns the memory into a rate: add the bumps to the background rate.
\end{enumerate}
A neural point process keeps the outside of this machine---a rate for each event type, which can be turned into
a likelihood, a forecast of the next event and a simulator---and replaces the inside: pieces~2 and~3. The
memory becomes a vector of numbers updated by a network as each event arrives, and the readout becomes a
learned weighted score of that vector, made positive (Figure~\ref{fig:npp-pipeline}, bottom).
Table~\ref{tab:npp-parts} lists the correspondence part by part.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  k/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=24mm, minimum height=11mm},
  r/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!18, align=center, text width=27mm, minimum height=11mm},
  o/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=green!12, align=center, text width=20mm, minimum height=11mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\node[font=\small\bfseries, anchor=west] at (-1.5,1.05) {Hawkes};
\node[k] (e1) at (0,0) {past events\\(type, time)};
\node[k] (m1) at (3.3,0) {\textbf{memory}: one running sum of decaying bumps per pair of types};
\node[k] (r1) at (6.6,0) {\textbf{readout}: background rate $+$ sum of bumps};
\node[o] (l1) at (9.6,0) {rate of each type $\lambda_i(t)$};
\draw[arr] (e1) -- (m1); \draw[arr] (m1) -- (r1); \draw[arr] (r1) -- (l1);
\node[font=\small\bfseries, anchor=west] at (-1.5,-1.45) {Neural};
\node[k] (e2) at (0,-2.5) {past events\\(type, time, marks)};
\node[r] (m2) at (3.3,-2.5) {\textbf{learned memory}: a vector updated by a network at each event};
\node[r] (r2) at (6.6,-2.5) {\textbf{learned readout}: weighted score, then softplus};
\node[o] (l2) at (9.6,-2.5) {rate of each type $\lambda_i(t)$};
