\caption{Parts of the Hawkes model replaced in a neural point process. Grey: kept from the Hawkes model. Orange: replaced by learned
parts. Green: the output---a rate for each event type---is the same kind of object in both, so both are fitted
by the same likelihood and simulated in the same way. Schematic.}
\label{fig:npp-pipeline}
\end{figure}

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The parts of a Hawkes model and their neural counterparts.}
\label{tab:npp-parts}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Part & Hawkes & Neural point process \\
\midrule
What an event contributes & a bump of fixed height $\phi_{ij}(0)$ & a change to the memory vector, computed from the event \emph{and} the current memory \\
How the contribution fades & at a fixed speed, the same after every event & at speeds the network sets after each event; can fade, grow or reverse \\
How contributions combine & by adding & any way the network learns: a pattern can count for more than its parts \\
Turning memory into a rate & add to the background rate & weighted score, passed through softplus so the rate is positive \\
Kept unchanged & \multicolumn{2}{l}{a rate for each event type; the likelihood of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}; simulation by thinning} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{The Hawkes memory as leaky buckets.} Picture the Hawkes memory as a row of leaky buckets, one per event type. Every
event pours a fixed amount of water into some of the buckets; every bucket leaks at its own fixed speed; the rate
of type-$i$ events is the background rate plus the water in bucket~$i$. Water can only be added, and it can only
leak away. In a neural point process a controller watches the events as they arrive and decides, each time, how
much to pour into which bucket, how fast each one should now leak, and even whether to drain a bucket or let it
\emph{fill} slowly over the next moments. The buckets are the network's memory; the controller is what is
learned.
\paragraph{One update step: classical Hawkes and continuous-time LSTM.} The clearest comparison is between the classical Hawkes model with exponential
kernels and the neural Hawkes process of \citet{mei2017}, because both keep a memory that jumps at each event
and then relaxes exponentially (Figure~\ref{fig:npp-step}). In the classical model the memory for type $i$ is the
excess of its rate over the background rate. At a type-$j$ event it jumps by a fixed amount, the kernel height
for that pair of types; between events it decays towards zero at a fixed speed $\omega$; the rate is the
background rate plus the memory. In the neural Hawkes process, at each event a network reads the event and the
current memory and sets three things for every memory cell: where it starts, the \emph{target} it relaxes
towards, and how fast---Equation~\eqref{eq:ctlstm-decay}. The rate is a learned score of the memory, passed
through softplus. Read this way, the classical update has the same shape as the neural one with the jump fixed
in advance, the target fixed at zero and the speed fixed at $\omega$; the neural model learns all three, and
lets them depend on everything that has happened so far.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  h/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=52mm, minimum height=10mm},
  n/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!15, align=center, text width=56mm, minimum height=10mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}},
  cor/.style={dashed, gray, {Stealth[length=1.4mm]}-{Stealth[length=1.4mm]}}]
\node[font=\small\bfseries] at (0,0.9) {Classical Hawkes (exponential)};
\node[font=\small\bfseries] at (7.2,0.9) {Neural Hawkes (continuous-time LSTM)};
\node[h] (h1) at (0,0) {\textbf{memory}: excess rate $x_i(t)$, one per type};
\node[h] (h2) at (0,-1.4) {\textbf{at a type-$j$ event}: jump by a fixed amount, $x_i \leftarrow x_i + \phi_{ij}(0)$};
\node[h] (h3) at (0,-2.8) {\textbf{between events}: decay towards \emph{zero} at a fixed speed $\omega$};
\node[h] (h4) at (0,-4.2) {\textbf{rate}: $\lambda_i(t) = \mu_i + x_i(t)$};
\node[n] (n1) at (7.2,0) {\textbf{memory}: a vector of cells $c(t)$, many more than the number of types};
\node[n] (n2) at (7.2,-1.4) {\textbf{at an event}: a network reads the event \emph{and the current memory} and sets each cell's new start, target $\bar c$ and speed $d$};
\node[n] (n3) at (7.2,-2.8) {\textbf{between events}: each cell relaxes towards its \emph{target}, which may be above or below, at its own speed};
\node[n] (n4) at (7.2,-4.2) {\textbf{rate}: $\lambda_i(t) = \mathrm{softplus}(\text{learned score of the memory})$};
\foreach \a/\b in {h1/h2,h2/h3,h3/h4} \draw[arr] (\a) -- (\b);
\foreach \a/\b in {n1/n2,n2/n3,n3/n4} \draw[arr] (\a) -- (\b);
\foreach \k in {1,2,3,4} \draw[cor] (h\k.east) -- (n\k.west);
\draw[arr] (h3.west) -- ++(-0.35,0) |- node[pos=0.25, left, font=\tiny, align=right]{next\\event} (h2.west);
\draw[arr] (n3.east) -- ++(0.35,0) |- node[pos=0.25, right, font=\tiny, align=left]{next\\event} (n2.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One step of the classical exponential Hawkes model (left) and of the neural Hawkes
process~\citep{mei2017} (right). Dashed arrows join corresponding parts. The classical model has the same shape as
the neural one with the jump fixed by the kernel, the target fixed at zero and the speed fixed at $\omega$; the
neural model computes all three from the history at every event. Schematic.}
\label{fig:npp-step}
\end{figure}



\paragraph{Three limitations of the linear Hawkes model.} Three limitations of the linear Hawkes model motivate
the step.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Effects only add.} Each past event contributes its own bump, independently of the
  others. In the chain reaction of Example~\ref{ex:chain}, a trade followed by two cancellations at the
  same level is far more alarming than either alone: together they say the level is about to go.
  A sum of separate bumps cannot express ``A \emph{and then} B matters more than A plus B''.
\item \textbf{The shape is fixed in advance.} The analyst chooses exponential, power law, or a sum of
  exponentials, and the data only set the parameters.
\item \textbf{The rate can only jump up and then decay.} Between events, a linear Hawkes intensity
  with positive kernels can only fall. Real flow can do the opposite: after a sweep, new limit orders
  may arrive slowly at first and then faster as participants re-quote.
\end{enumerate}
Figure~\ref{fig:npp-shapes} draws the three limitations as intensity curves, next to shapes that a neural
model can produce.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/npp_intuition.png}
\caption{Intensity shapes that a fixed sum of bumps cannot produce. (a) A linear Hawkes intensity after three events: each adds the
same bump, and between events the rate can only decay. (b) One memory cell of a continuous-time LSTM
(Equation~\eqref{eq:ctlstm-decay}, Section~\ref{sec:npp-rnn}): after an event the cell relaxes towards a target
chosen by the network, so the rate can rise for a while after an event, or fall below the background rate
(inhibition). (c) The same three events read as a pattern: a model that has learned that ``trade, cancel,
cancel'' usually precedes a sweep raises the rate after the third event by more than the sum of three bumps.
Hand-specified illustrative shapes, not fitted models.}
\label{fig:npp-shapes}
\end{figure}


\paragraph{The three parts of a neural point process.} Every neural point process has three parts.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[1. Describe each event as a vector] Event $n$ is turned into a vector of numbers (an
  embedding) built from its type, the time since the previous event $t_n - t_{n-1}$, and any marks
  such as size or side.
\item[2. Summarise the history] A sequence model reads the embeddings of events $1, \dots, n$ in order
  and produces a \emph{history vector} $\mathbf{z}^{\text{hist}}(t)$ that summarises everything that
  happened before time $t$. This is the role played in a Hawkes model by the running sums of decaying
  bumps.
\item[3. Turn the summary into intensities] For each event type $i$, the intensity is
\end{description}
\begin{equation}
  \lambda_i(t) = \operatorname{softplus}\!\big(\langle \mathbf{o}_i, \mathbf{z}^{\text{hist}}(t)\rangle\big),
  \qquad \operatorname{softplus}(y) = \ln\big(1 + \mathrm{e}^{y}\big),
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{o}_i$ is a vector of learned output weights for type $i$ and $y$ stands for any real
number.
\inwords{score the current history with a weighted sum, one set of weights per event type, and pass
the score through the softplus, a smooth function that is always positive and close to the score
itself when the score is large. Rates must be positive; softplus guarantees it, as the exponential link
does in the models of \citet{sfendourakis2020} and \citet{mucciante2024} (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants}).}
A linear Hawkes model is the special case in which the ``summary'' is the vector of running sums
$\sum \phi_{ij}(t - t_{n'})$ and the output step simply adds them to $\mu_i$. A neural model replaces
the hand-written summary with a learned one.

\paragraph{Training: the same likelihood as Hawkes.} The parameters---the network weights and the
output weights $\mathbf{o}_i$---are fitted by maximising exactly the log-likelihood of
Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}: reward high intensity at the times events happened, penalise the
total expected number of events $\int \lambda_i$. Gradient descent replaces the specialised
optimisers, because the network has many parameters. One new difficulty appears. For an exponential
kernel the integral $\int \lambda_i$ has a closed form; for a neural intensity it usually does not, and
is estimated numerically---commonly by evaluating the intensity at randomly sampled times between
events and averaging (Monte Carlo integration). Two lines of work remove this approximation:
\citet{omi2019} model the \emph{integral} of the intensity with a network constrained to be increasing,
and obtain the intensity as its derivative, so the likelihood is exact; \citet{shchur2020ifl} skip the
intensity altogether and model the probability distribution of the time to the next event directly,
for example as a mixture of simple distributions, which also makes sampling the next event exact.

\paragraph{Three designs for the history encoder.} The models in the literature differ mainly in step~2: how the history
is turned into a memory. There are three families (Figure~\ref{fig:npp-families}). A \emph{recurrent} model
reads the events one at a time and carries a memory forward; an \emph{attention} model lets the newest event look
back at every earlier event directly and decide how much each matters; a \emph{state-space} model carries a
memory forward like a recurrent model, but one that fades by an amount set by the actual time between events.
The next three subsubsections take them in turn; Table~\ref{tab:npp-families} compares them.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  e/.style={circle, draw, fill=blue!15, minimum size=4.5mm, inner sep=0pt},
  c/.style={circle, draw, fill=red!25, minimum size=4.5mm, inner sep=0pt},
  m/.style={draw, rounded corners=1pt, fill=orange!20, minimum size=4mm, inner sep=1pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.3mm]}}]
\begin{scope}
  \foreach \i in {1,...,4} \node[e] (e\i) at (0.9*\i-0.9,0) {};
  \node[c] (e5) at (3.6,0) {};
  \foreach \i in {1,...,5} \node[m] (m\i) at (0.9*\i-0.9,1.0) {};
  \foreach \i in {1,...,5} \draw[arr] (e\i) -- (m\i);
  \foreach \i/\j in {1/2,2/3,3/4,4/5} \draw[arr] (m\i) -- (m\j);
  \node[align=center] at (1.8,-0.75) {\textbf{(a) recurrent}\\memory passed from event to event};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=5.0cm]
  \foreach \i in {1,...,4} \node[e] (f\i) at (0.9*\i-0.9,0) {};
  \node[c] (f5) at (3.6,0) {};
  \foreach \i/\w in {1/0.4,2/1.4,3/0.6,4/1.0} \draw[arr, line width=\w pt, red!60!black] (f5) to[bend right=40] (f\i);
  \node[align=center] at (1.8,-0.75) {\textbf{(b) attention}\\the newest event weighs every earlier one};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=10.0cm]
  \foreach \i/\x in {1/0,2/0.5,3/1.9,4/2.3} \node[e] (g\i) at (\x,0) {};
  \node[c] (g5) at (3.6,0) {};
  \foreach \i/\x in {1/0,2/0.5,3/1.9,4/2.3,5/3.6} \node[m] (n\i) at (\x,1.0) {};
  \foreach \i in {1,...,5} \draw[arr] (g\i) -- (n\i);
  \draw[arr] (n1) -- (n2); \draw[arr, densely dotted] (n2) -- node[above, font=\tiny]{long gap: fades more} (n3);
  \draw[arr] (n3) -- (n4); \draw[arr, densely dotted] (n4) -- (n5);
  \node[align=center] at (1.8,-0.75) {\textbf{(c) state space}\\memory fades by the time between events};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Three ways a neural point process builds its memory of the past. Blue: earlier events; red: the newest
event; orange: memory. (a) A recurrent network (RMTPP, neural Hawkes) updates its memory event by event. (b) An
attention model (Transformer Hawkes, self-attentive Hawkes) compares the newest event with every earlier event and
weights them, the thickness of the arrows. (c) A state-space model (Mamba Hawkes) updates its memory event by
event, letting it fade more across a longer gap in time. Schematic.}
\label{fig:npp-families}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Recurrent memory: RMTPP and the neural Hawkes process}
\label{sec:npp-rnn}

An early and widely cited model, RMTPP~\citep{du2016rmtpp}, updates a recurrent network's state at each event and
lets the intensity change between events through a fixed function of the elapsed time. The \emph{neural Hawkes
process}~\citep{mei2017} makes the recurrent state itself evolve in continuous time, as in
Figure~\ref{fig:npp-step}: each memory cell of an LSTM decays exponentially between events towards a target value
set at the last event,
\begin{equation}
c(t) = \bar c + \bigl(c(t_n) - \bar c\bigr)\, e^{-d\,(t - t_n)}, \qquad t_n \le t < t_{n+1},
\label{eq:ctlstm-decay}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the cell relaxes from where the last event left it, $c(t_n)$, towards a target $\bar c$, at a speed $d$;
all three are computed by the network at each event from the event and the memory.}
Because the target can be above or below the current value, the intensity can rise or fall between events, and
one event can make another \emph{less} likely---the inhibition that linear Hawkes cannot represent. The
limitation the later families address is that the memory is a single vector passed from event to event: what
happened long ago reaches the present only through every intermediate update, and the events must be processed
one after another, which is slow to train on long sequences~\citep{zuo2020thp}.

\subsubsection{Attention: the Transformer and self-attentive Hawkes processes}
\label{sec:npp-attn}

\paragraph{Attention over past events.} Instead of passing a memory along, an attention model lets the newest event look back at all
earlier events at once and weigh them (Section~\ref{sec:deep-attn} explains attention with an order-book
example). Each earlier event receives a weight according to how well its content matches the present; the
weighted combination of the earlier events is the memory. Two papers of 2020 built Hawkes-type models this way,
with the same idea but different ways of turning the memory into an intensity.

\paragraph{The Transformer Hawkes process.} In the model of \citet{zuo2020thp}, each event is represented by the sum
of a learned vector for its type and a vector of sines and cosines of its time stamp, so that the network knows when
it happened. Several layers of masked attention---each event may look only at earlier events---produce a hidden
vector $\mathbf{h}(t_n)$ for every event. Between event $n$ and the next, the intensity of type $i$ is
\begin{equation}
\lambda_i(t) = \operatorname{softplus}_i\!\Bigl(a_i\,\frac{t - t_n}{t_n} + \langle \mathbf{w}_i, \mathbf{h}(t_n)\rangle + b_i\Bigr),
\qquad t_n \le t < t_{n+1},
\label{eq:thp}
\end{equation}
\inwords{three terms inside a softplus: a term that changes in proportion to the time since the last event, a
learned score of the memory, and a constant. The memory sets the level of the rate; between events the rate
drifts steadily up or down, depending on the sign of the learned coefficient $a_i$.}
The integral of the intensity in the likelihood has no closed form and is approximated by Monte Carlo sampling or
the trapezoidal rule. For the predictions reported, the authors add two output layers on top of
$\mathbf{h}(t_n)$---a classifier for the next type and a regression for the next time---trained jointly with the
likelihood. Because attention compares every pair of events, all events of a sequence can be processed in
parallel, and many layers can be stacked, which the authors give as its advantages over recurrent models.

\paragraph{The self-attentive Hawkes process.} \citet{zhang2020sahp} keep closer to the classical form. After each
event, attention over the history produces, for each type $i$, three numbers: a starting level $\eta_i$, a
long-run level $\bar\mu_i$ and a decay rate $\gamma_i$. Unlike the fixed background rate $\mu_i$ of a Hawkes
model, $\bar\mu_i$ is recomputed after every event. The intensity then relaxes exponentially from one to the other,
\begin{equation}
\lambda_i(t) = \operatorname{softplus}\!\Bigl(\bar\mu_i + (\eta_i - \bar\mu_i)\, e^{-\gamma_i (t - t_n)}\Bigr),
\qquad t_n < t \le t_{n+1},
\label{eq:sahp}
\end{equation}
\inwords{exactly the shape of an exponential Hawkes intensity after an event---a jump and a decay towards a
background level---except that the start, the end and the speed are computed by attention from the whole history,
and the start may be below the end, which gives inhibition.}
Time enters as a phase shift in the sine and cosine encodings of each event's position. The authors argue that the
attention weights give an interpretable measure of how much each type of event influences each other type.

\paragraph{Benefits and costs of attention.} Attention can reach an event far in the past directly, without
passing through every intermediate update, and the weights it assigns can be inspected. Its cost grows with the
square of the number of events attended to, which limits how much history it can use; the Mamba Hawkes process
was motivated in part by that limit~\citep{gao2024mhp}.

\subsubsection{State-space memory: the Mamba Hawkes process}
\label{sec:npp-mamba}

\paragraph{A state-space recurrence over events.} A state-space model (Section~\ref{sec:deep}) is a recurrence: a memory vector $\mathbf{z}$ is
multiplied by a matrix and the new input is added. A \emph{selective} state-space model, Mamba, lets the step size
of the recurrence vary from input to input, so that how much of the old memory fades at each step depends on the
input, through a function learned in training (Section~\ref{sec:deep-ssm}).
\citet{gao2024mhp} use this to build the Mamba Hawkes process, with one change: the step size is the actual time
between events, $\Delta_n = t_n - t_{n-1}$,
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{z}_n = e^{\Delta_n \mathbf{A}}\,\mathbf{z}_{n-1} + \mathbf{B}_n\,\mathbf{x}_n,
\label{eq:mhp}
\end{equation}
\inwords{at each event, the old memory $\mathbf{z}_{n-1}$ is shrunk by an amount that depends on how long ago the
previous event was---more after a long gap---and the new event $\mathbf{x}_n$ is written in, with a weight
$\mathbf{B}_n$ that depends on the event.}
Here $\mathbf{A}$ is a learned matrix, $\mathbf{x}_n$ the embedding of event $n$, and $\mathbf{B}_n$ is computed
from it (the paper uses a slightly more elaborate weight, which follows from turning the continuous-time recurrence
into a discrete one). The intensity is then computed from the memory exactly as in the Transformer Hawkes process,
Equation~\eqref{eq:thp}. The authors show that in the simplest case---one memory number, $\mathbf{A} = -1$---the
recurrence becomes an exponentially weighted average in which the weight on the past is
$e^{-(t_n - t_{n-1})}$: the memory of an exponential Hawkes process, in which old events fade with elapsed time. A
variant, MHP-E, passes the output of Mamba layers through Transformer layers. The model processes a sequence in time
proportional to its length, where attention needs time proportional to its square.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The three families of neural point process.}
\label{tab:npp-families}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Recurrent (neural Hawkes) & Attention (THP, SAHP) & State space (Mamba Hawkes) \\
\midrule
Memory & one vector, updated event by event & a weighted combination of all earlier events & one vector, updated event by event \\
How time enters & each cell decays at a learned speed between events & encoded in each event's representation & the memory fades by the time between events \\
Reaching far back & only through every intermediate update & directly & through the recurrence, with input-dependent forgetting \\
Cost per sequence of $L$ events & proportional to $L$, but sequential & proportional to $L^2$, in parallel & proportional to $L$ \\
Closest to classical Hawkes & a learned version of the exponential decay & SAHP: exponential relaxation with learned start, end and speed & simplest case: exponentially weighted memory \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\citet{shchur2021} survey the family and its design choices.

\subsubsection{Learning only the kernel}
\label{sec:npp-snh}

A smaller step keeps the Hawkes sum of bumps and learns only the shape of
each bump. Shallow Neural Hawkes~\citep{joseph2022snh} represents the kernel by a network with one hidden
layer, giving a non-parametric estimate of the kernel. On 120,000 Bitcoin market orders from
Binance on one day in May 2020 it reports a better in-sample likelihood than the expectation--maximisation and
Wiener--Hopf estimators of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}. Unlike the models above it keeps the additive Hawkes
form: each past event adds its own bump, whatever the order in which the events arrived.

\subsubsection{Applications to financial events and order books}
\label{sec:npp-apps}

\paragraph{The shared financial benchmark.} The general-purpose models above were all tested on the same
financial data set, introduced with RMTPP~\citep{du2016rmtpp}: one day of transactions in one stock, with two
event types, buy and sell, 414{,}800 events in all, cut into sequences of about 2{,}074
events~\citep{zuo2020thp}. Table~\ref{tab:npp-fin} collects the results reported on it. (The self-attentive Hawkes
process was not tested on it.)

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Neural point processes on the financial-transactions data set (one stock, one day, buy and sell events),
as reported by \citet{zuo2020thp} and \citet{gao2024mhp}. Log-likelihood per event: higher is better. Accuracy:
predicting whether the next event is a buy or a sell. RMSE: error in predicting the time of the next event.}
\label{tab:npp-fin}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
\toprule
Model & Log-likelihood per event & Type accuracy (\%) & Time RMSE \\
\midrule
RMTPP (recurrent) & $-3.89$ & 61.95 & 1.56 \\
Neural Hawkes (recurrent) & $-3.60$ & 62.20 & 1.56 \\
Transformer Hawkes (attention) & $-1.11$ & 62.64 (62.23 in \citealp{gao2024mhp}) & 0.93 \\
Mamba Hawkes (state space) & $0.974$ & 62.5 & 0.592 \\
Mamba + Transformer (MHP-E) & $0.966$ & 62.7 & 0.556 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

Two things stand out. First, the likelihood and the timing error improve a great deal from one family to the next,
while type accuracy (predicting buy or sell) stays between 61.95\% and 62.7\% for all five models.
Second, the comparison covers one stock, one day and two event types; no classical Hawkes model and no spread
across runs are reported.

\paragraph{Models built for the order book.} The order-book applications use richer event types and compare with a
classical model:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Parallel neural Hawkes} \citep{shi2022sdpnhp}, reviewed in Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-pnhp}: one
  continuous-time LSTM unit per event type (bid and ask submissions and cancellations), with the state-dependent
  transitions of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-sd}. On five days each of INTC, MSFT and JPM it beats a classical
  state-dependent Hawkes model on every measure---for example 49.7\% against 42.7\% accuracy for the next event's
  type on MSFT---and a self-attentive Hawkes model and a single neural Hawkes unit as well. Its margin over a plain
  LSTM classifier is 2 to 3 percentage points.
\item \textbf{An independent re-run} \citep{zheng2024lobdif} of the same models on six data sets---two days of MSFT
  and four from Chinese exchanges---found the parallel model ahead of the plain LSTM on five of the six and behind
  on one, and a larger timing error than the classical Hawkes model on one of the six.
\item \textbf{A neural Hawkes market} \citep{lalor2025nhp}, reviewed in Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-nhp}: twelve event
  types, one day of five stocks, used as the environment for a market-making agent. It matches the volatility of
  the real mid-price but not its mean reversion.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Gaps in the literature.} We found no study that compares the three families---recurrent, attention and
state-space---on order-book data, and none that compares any of them out of sample with a multivariate Hawkes
model using sum-of-exponential kernels on many instruments. The order-book evidence comes from one
recurrent design, on a handful of stocks over a few days.

\subsubsection{Benefits and costs}
\label{sec:npp-when}

\begin{example}[Order-dependent excitation in a neural model]
Take the chain reaction of Example~\ref{ex:chain}. A two-type Hawkes model of trades and ask
cancellations gives the intensity of a further buy market order as background plus one bump for the
trade and one for each cancellation, whatever their order. A neural model reads the sequence ``buy
trade, cancel, cancel'' as a whole. If, in the training data, that particular pattern was usually
followed within a millisecond by a sweep of the level, the model can raise the intensity of buy market
orders far more than the sum of the separate bumps would, and leave it unchanged after the same three
events in a different order. Whether it learns this depends entirely on whether the pattern is
frequent enough in the data; the flexibility is useless without the examples.
\end{example}
\paragraph{Situations where a neural point process helps.} The extra flexibility pays only in some situations. It helps when there is a
great deal of data---\citet{shi2022sdpnhp} train on about four million windows of events---so that patterns of
events recur often enough to be learned; when interactions between events matter, as in the chain reaction
above; when there are many event types, since a multivariate Hawkes model needs a kernel for every ordered pair
of types (144 for twelve types) while a network shares its weights across them; and when the task is to predict
the next event or to simulate order flow. On order-book data the gains are measurable but moderate: on MSFT, a
single neural Hawkes unit predicted the type of the next event correctly 46.8\% of the time against 42.7\% for a
classical state-dependent Hawkes model, and the parallel version reached 49.7\%, though the single unit was
slightly behind a plain LSTM classifier~\citep{shi2022sdpnhp} (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-pnhp}). A classical Hawkes
model remains the better choice when the branching ratio or matrix is itself the object of interest, when the
intensity must be updated in a few operations per event inside a trading system, or when data are scarce.


\paragraph{Costs of a neural point process.} The flexibility has three costs, and each matters in this survey's setting.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Interpretability.} A linear Hawkes fit returns a branching matrix whose entries have a
  meaning (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}) and a branching ratio that can be monitored. A neural model returns
  weights; quantities such as the fraction of endogenous events have no direct equivalent.
\item \textbf{Speed.} An exponential Hawkes intensity is updated with a few multiplications per event.
  A neural intensity needs a forward pass of a network per event, and more if the intensity is needed
  between events---a cost that matters inside a trading loop (Section~\ref{sec:hardware}).
\item \textbf{Guarantees.} The stability condition $\rho(\Gamma) < 1$ has no simple analogue, so a
  simulated neural model can drift into regimes never seen in training.
\end{itemize}
The evaluation follows Principle~2. The baseline is a multivariate Hawkes model, ideally with
sum-of-exponential kernels, fitted to the same events; the comparison is the held-out log-likelihood per
event, the time-rescaling test of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit} (which applies to any model that outputs
an intensity), and, for a trading use, the economic value of the forecasts. A neural point process
earns its place when it improves these by more than its cost in speed and interpretability.

\begin{modelcard}{Neural point processes}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The embedding of events, the weights of the sequence model that summarises
the history (recurrent, continuous-time recurrent, attention or state-space), and the output weights
$\mathbf{o}_i$ that turn the summary into an intensity for each event type.}
\cardrow{How}{Gradient descent on the point-process log-likelihood, with the integral of the intensity
estimated by Monte Carlo, or made exact by modelling the integral or the next-gap distribution
directly. Compare on held-out likelihood and by time rescaling against a multivariate Hawkes baseline.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Predict the type and time of the next event; simulate order flow,
including as a training environment for execution or market-making agents; capture interactions
between events that additive kernels miss.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A Hawkes model whose bumps are drawn by a network that has read the whole
history in order, instead of by a fixed formula: it can notice that ``trade, cancel, cancel'' means
more than the three events separately.}
\end{modelcard}

% ================================================================
\subsection{Uses of Hawkes models}
\label{sec:hawkes-uses}

\paragraph{Capability beyond a Poisson model.} The uses below all rest on one difference from a Poisson model. A Poisson model
says that the next second will look like any other second. A Hawkes model says that the next second depends on
what just happened: right after a burst, the next second will be busier, and the model says how much busier, for
which kinds of event, and for how long. Whenever a decision depends on \emph{how busy the next moments will be},
and the answer changes after bursts, a Hawkes model is the natural tool. Anyone who has sized a buffer, a thread
pool or a rate limiter has met the same problem: traffic that arrives in bursts, where the average rate is a poor
guide to the peak. On CME NQ market data, the arrival of packets and of matching-engine transactions follows such a
process, with a branching ratio of about 0.8 and excitation that fades within about 167
microseconds~\citep{maciejewski2026packets} (Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}).

Table~\ref{tab:hawkes-uses} lists the questions a Hawkes model answers and where each is treated. The rest of this
subsection takes them one at a time, with numbers.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Questions a Hawkes model answers.}
\label{tab:hawkes-uses}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.3\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}}
\toprule
Question & What the model provides & Where \\
\midrule
How many events of each type in the next few milliseconds? & the current intensities $\lambda_i(t)$ & Use 1 \\
What would a realistic stream of orders look like, bursts included? & simulation by thinning & Use 2 \\
How much of the activity is a reaction to earlier activity? Who triggers whom? & the branching ratio and matrix & Use 3, \S\ref{sec:hawkes-branching-use} \\
Which way is the price drifting? How soon will this queue be empty? & drift, expected order-flow imbalance, depletion time & Use 4, \S\ref{sec:hawkes-decisions} \\
How should I execute an order, or quote, when flow comes in bursts? & a Markov state for control problems & Use 5, \S\ref{sec:hawkes-control} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Use 1: forecasting short-horizon activity.}
The fitted intensities are a forecast: at an intensity of 300 events per second, about 0.3 events are expected in
the next millisecond. Because each intensity jumps after events and then decays, the forecast is high right after a
burst and falls back to the background rate in quiet times, which a Poisson model, with one rate for the whole
day, cannot do. The forecast is per event type: a burst of buy market orders raises the forecast for more buys
(self-excitation) and for cancellations at the ask (cross-excitation) by different amounts.

\paragraph{Use 2: simulating realistic order flow.}
A fitted model can be run forward to generate artificial streams of events with the same clustering as the real
ones (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}). Such streams can drive a simulated book, test how a strategy or a piece of
software behaves in a burst, or serve as the environment for a learning agent (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-nhp}).
Such tests check software and mechanics only---that the code, limits and safeguards cope with a burst. They
say nothing about whether a strategy makes money: the simulated market is generated by the model, and results
in a generated market are not evidence about trading.
Adding prices gives the models of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-prices}. A Poisson simulator produces the right average
number of events but not the bursts, and the bursts are when queues empty and fills go wrong.

\paragraph{Use 3: measuring the market.}
The fitted parameters are themselves informative. The branching ratio says what share of activity is a reaction to
earlier activity rather than new information from outside---four in five events on CME NQ. The branching matrix
says which event types trigger which (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-read}): whether buys mostly trigger buys (runs of
trades in one direction) or sells (reversal), and whether trades trigger cancellations. Fitted state by state, it
shows when the market becomes more reactive, for example when the spread widens
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-sd}). Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-branching-use} explains how to read and use the branching
ratio, and how it can mislead.

\paragraph{Use 4 and 5: decisions.} The remaining two uses turn the intensities into trading decisions, and are
described in the last two subsubsections, after a closer look at the branching ratio: quantities a trader can read off directly, and control problems that
can be solved because an exponential Hawkes model has a small Markov state.

\paragraph{Questions a Hawkes model does not answer.} A Hawkes model is a model of \emph{timing}. It can reproduce the
clustering of order flow very well while saying little about the sign of the next price move, because a burst of
buying and a burst of selling are both bursts. On a standard financial data set, neural Hawkes models described
the timing of events far better than earlier ones, while type accuracy (buy or sell) stayed between 61.95\% and
62.7\% (Section~\ref{sec:npp-apps}). Hawkes models should therefore be judged separately on each task
of Table~\ref{tab:three-problems}: forecasting intensities, simulating, predicting the price, and supporting
execution.

\paragraph{Cases where a Poisson model is sufficient.} A Poisson model is enough when activity is steady over the horizon that
matters, and a queue model of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models} when what matters is the current size of the queues
rather than the recent history of events. A neural point process (Section~\ref{sec:npp}) is worth its cost only
with a great deal of data and when patterns of events, not just their count, carry information.

\subsubsection{Using the branching ratio}
\label{sec:hawkes-branching-use}

\paragraph{Interpretation of the branching ratio.} The branching ratio $\Gamma$ is the average number of events that one event
directly triggers (Section~\ref{sec:pp-branching}). Three readings of it are useful in practice
(Table~\ref{tab:branching-read}):
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{The share of activity that is reaction.} In a stationary Hawkes process a fraction $\Gamma$ of all
  events were triggered by earlier events and a fraction $1-\Gamma$ came from outside. At $\Gamma = 0.8$, four
  events in five are reactions to earlier events.
\item \textbf{The amplification of a shock.} One event from outside---a news item, one large trader's decision---
  produces on average $1/(1-\Gamma)$ events in total, itself included. Ten outside events become 20 at
  $\Gamma = 0.5$, 50 at $0.8$ and 200 at $0.95$.
\item \textbf{How fragile the amplification is.} Near one, small changes in $\Gamma$ change the amplification
  a great deal: from $0.9$ to $0.95$ it doubles, from 10 to 20. A market whose branching ratio drifts towards one
  becomes more and more sensitive to outside events, and cascades last longer.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Reading a branching ratio. One outside event produces $1/(1-\Gamma)$ events in total.}
\label{tab:branching-read}
\begin{tabular}{cccc}
\toprule
Branching & Share of events triggered & Events per & 10 outside \\
ratio $\Gamma$ & by earlier events & outside event & events become \\
\midrule
0 & 0\% & 1 & 10 \\
0.5 & 50\% & 2 & 20 \\
0.8 & 80\% & 5 & 50 \\
0.9 & 90\% & 10 & 100 \\
0.95 & 95\% & 20 & 200 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Published uses of the branching ratio.} Three kinds of question have been asked of it.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{How reactive a market is, and whether that has changed.} On E-mini S\&P~500 futures,
  \citet{filimonovsornette2012} found the branching ratio rising from about 0.3 around 1998--2000 to 0.7--0.8
  after 2007, with spikes to 0.9; the size of such numbers, and whether markets sit at one, is debated
  (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-find}).
\item \textbf{When a market is most reactive.} Fitted separately for each state of the book, the measure is
  higher when the book is unbalanced than when it is balanced; for Intel it was above one with a wide spread
  (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-sd}).
\item \textbf{How much activity is news and how much is reaction.} Adding the times of scheduled macroeconomic
  announcements to a Hawkes model of foreign-exchange quotes lowers the estimated branching
  ratio~\citep{rambaldi2015news} (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-regimes}); a related method finds bursts of activity of unknown timing, and reports
  that only a small fraction coincide with news~\citep{rambaldi2018bursts}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Bias from a time-varying background rate.} A fitted branching ratio is only as
good as the model's background rate. If the true background rate varies---busy and quiet periods, the open and
close, news---and the model assumes it constant, the model can explain the busy periods only by making events
trigger events, and the estimated $\Gamma$ rises. Figure~\ref{fig:branching-trap} shows how strong the effect can
be: a stream with no self-excitation at all, whose background rate switches between 1 and 5 events per second,
is fitted with a branching ratio of 0.62 to 0.92 depending on how often the rate switches. Real studies find the
same in a milder form (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-regimes}); \citet{filimonovsornette2015} list regime shifts among the reasons why estimates near one
are biased upward.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{figs/branching_trap.png}
\caption{A branching ratio from no excitation. (1) A stream of events with no self-excitation; only the background
rate changes, between 1 and 5 per second. (2) The branching ratio estimated by maximum likelihood with an
exponential Hawkes model that assumes a constant background rate, for one hour of events: zero when the rate is
truly constant, 0.62 to 0.92 when it switches every 5, 30 or 120 seconds. Stylised simulation; not data.}
\label{fig:branching-trap}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Dependence on estimator, window and kernel.} The estimate depends on the method and window: with a simple estimator based on
the variance of event counts in windows of fixed length, the E-mini estimate rises over time, but it stays roughly
constant if the window shrinks as trading speeds up~\citep{hardiman2014branching}. And it depends on the shape of
kernel assumed: power-law fits gave about one on the same market on which exponential-type fits gave 0.7--0.8
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-find}). A branching ratio is therefore comparable only with other branching ratios
estimated the same way.

\paragraph{A branching-ratio monitor.} With those cautions, a running estimate can serve as a gauge of how
self-generated current activity is. The following design is our suggestion, not a tested recipe: refit on a
rolling window of fixed length, always with the same kernel family and estimator; let the background rate vary
with the time of day, or model scheduled news, so that predictable changes in activity are not counted as
excitation; compare each value with the same instrument's own history rather than with a universal threshold,
since published values differ widely by instrument, period and method; and, if the book state matters, estimate it
state by state. Read a high value as a sign that activity is feeding on itself and that bursts will be larger and
longer---information for risk limits, quote sizes and capacity---not as a forecast of the direction of the
price.

\subsubsection{From intensities to decisions}
\label{sec:hawkes-decisions}

A fitted Hawkes model gives, at every instant, the expected rate of each kind of event. Three quantities a
trader can use follow directly from these rates. Each is a short-horizon quantity: it assumes the rates stay roughly
where they are now, which is true only for a short time, because they decay between events.

\paragraph{Drift.} In the price model of Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-prices}, up and down moves of size
$\delta_m$ arrive at rates $\lambda^+(t)$ and $\lambda^-(t)$, so over a short horizon $H$ the expected
mid change is approximately
\[
  \E[m_{t+H} - m_t] \approx \delta_m\,\big(\lambda^+(t) - \lambda^-(t)\big)\, H .
\]
\inwords{if up moves are currently arriving faster than down moves, the price is expected to drift up,
by one tick per surplus up move.} For example, if up moves are currently arriving at 3 per second and down moves at 1 per second,
then over the next half second the mid is expected to rise by $(3-1)\times 0.5 = 1$ tick.

\paragraph{Expected order-flow imbalance.} Model six event types at the best quotes: limit orders,
cancellations and market orders on each side, with intensities $\lambda^{L,b}$, $\lambda^{C,b}$
(bid queue added to; bid queue cancelled), $\lambda^{L,a}$, $\lambda^{C,a}$ (ask queue added to; ask queue
cancelled), $\lambda^{M,\text{buy}}$ (market buys, which take from the ask) and $\lambda^{M,\text{sell}}$
(market sells, which take from the bid). With $\bar v$ the average event size and the best prices
unchanged, the expected OFI per second is
\[
  \bar v\,\Big( \underbrace{\lambda^{L,b} - \lambda^{C,b} - \lambda^{M,\text{sell}}}_{\text{net change of the bid queue}}
  \;-\; \underbrace{\big(\lambda^{L,a} - \lambda^{C,a} - \lambda^{M,\text{buy}}\big)}_{\text{net change of the ask queue}} \Big).
\]
\inwords{in Section~\ref{sec:ofi}, OFI was computed \emph{after the fact}, by going through the events that
had happened and adding up how much the bid queue grew minus how much the ask queue grew. Here the same sum is
computed \emph{in advance}: instead of counting events that happened, it uses the Hawkes intensities to say how
many of each event are expected in the next second, and adds up the OFI those expected events would produce.
The signs are the same as before: anything that adds to the bid queue (a buy limit order) or takes from the ask
queue (a market buy, an ask cancellation) is buying pressure; the reverse is selling pressure.}
\textit{Example (illustrative numbers).} With an average event size of 5 lots, suppose the intensities are, per
second, 10 buy limit orders, 6 bid cancellations and 2 market sells at the bid, and 8 sell limit orders, 9 ask
cancellations and 5 market buys at the ask. The bid queue is expected to change by $5 \times (10 - 6 - 2) = +10$
lots per second, the ask queue by $5 \times (8 - 9 - 5) = -30$; the expected OFI is $10 - (-30) = +40$ lots per
second of buying pressure. The regression of Section~\ref{sec:ofi} turns pressure into price: with
$\beta = 0.01$ points per lot, it predicts a rise of about $0.4$ points over the next second. So the Hawkes model
supplies a \emph{forecast} of OFI, and the OFI regression turns that into a forecast of the price change---valid,
like the other quantities here, only while the intensities stay near their current values.

\paragraph{Depletion time.} The bucket picture of Section~\ref{sec:queue-models} needs an inflow and
an outflow. For the best ask, $r_{\text{in}} = \bar v\,\lambda^{L,a}$ and 
\[
  r_{\text{out}} = \bar
v\,(\lambda^{M,\text{buy}} + \lambda^{C,a}),
\]
so with the current intensities
\[
  \E[\tau^a] \approx \frac{v^a_{1,t}}{r_{\text{out}} - r_{\text{in}}}, \qquad r_{\text{out}} > r_{\text{in}} .
\]
When this falls below the trader's own latency $T_{\text{lat}}$, a sell order resting in that ask queue
is in danger: if the trader decides to cancel, the level may be swept---filling the order just before
the price moves up---before the cancellation arrives. The
full probability $\P(\tau^a \le T_{\text{lat}})$ has no closed form under a Hawkes model, but can be
estimated by simulating from the current state by thinning.

\paragraph{Example: depletion time during a burst.} Take a best ask of 200 lots, an average event size of 5
lots, and, in normal times, 10 limit orders, 8 cancellations and 4 market buys per second at the ask. Inflow is
$5 \times 10 = 50$ lots per second, outflow $5 \times (8 + 4) = 60$, so the queue shrinks by 10 lots per second and
the expected depletion time is $200/10 = 20$ seconds. Now a burst of buying hits. Excitation raises market buys
to 20 and cancellations at the ask to 14 per second: outflow becomes $5 \times 34 = 170$ lots per second, the queue
shrinks by 120, and the expected depletion time falls to $200/120 \approx 1.7$ seconds. A Poisson model, using the
day's averages, still says 20 seconds. Figure~\ref{fig:hawkes-depletion} follows the estimate as the excitation
decays. The estimate uses the current intensities as if they stayed fixed, so it is rough when they are changing
quickly; the exact probability needs simulation.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{figs/hawkes_depletion.png}
\caption{Depletion time after a burst. (1) Intensities at the best ask after a burst of buying at time zero, decaying
back to normal. (2) Expected time until the 200-lot best ask is used up, computed from the current intensities held
fixed (solid), against the Poisson answer from average intensities (dashed). For a few seconds after the burst the
Hawkes estimate is a fraction of the Poisson one; a trader whose cancellation takes longer than the dotted
threshold is at risk in that window. Illustrative numbers, not data.}
\label{fig:hawkes-depletion}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Execution and market making}
\label{sec:hawkes-control}

\paragraph{Exponential kernels and the Markov property.} With an exponential kernel, the
current intensity summarises everything needed from the past: the pair (count of events, current intensity)
evolves as a Markov process. A sum of several exponentials needs one extra state variable per
exponential~\citep{bacry2015}. That is what lets execution and market-making problems, normally solved by
dynamic programming over a Markov state, include self-exciting order flow. Power-law kernels are handled by
approximating them with sums of exponentials, as in Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-variants}.

\paragraph{Optimal execution.} \citet{alfonsiblanc2016} solve the problem of executing a large order
explicitly in a price model with both permanent and transient impact, in which order flow follows a
Hawkes process. A key result concerns \emph{price manipulation}---strategies that profit merely by
pushing the price and trading back: with Poisson order flow it always exists in their model, while with
Hawkes order flow it disappears when the decay of the Hawkes kernel matches the speed at which the price
recovers and the kernel's total weight matches the transient share of impact.

\paragraph{Market making.} \citet{cartea2014buylow} model buy and sell market orders as mutually exciting
processes with exponential kernels and solve for the quotes of a market maker, with the intensities as
state variables, so that the quotes respond to the current level of excitation; the model requires the
decay of excitation to be fast enough relative to its strength for the intensities to stay stable. The paper was republished in \emph{SIAM Review}~\citep{cartea2018siamrev}. Further work treats market making with Hawkes order flow and
non-Markovian kernels~\citep{jusselin2021mm}.

\section{Meta-models: learning on top of the classical models}
\label{sec:meta}
% ================================================================

The classical models of Sections~\ref{sec:ofi}--\ref{sec:hawkes} each compress the book into a few numbers
with a clear meaning: the order-flow imbalance, the queue imbalance and the micro-price, the intensity of
each kind of event, the probability that the ask queue empties first. Each is a partial view. A
\emph{meta-model} takes these outputs---not the raw book---as its inputs, and lets a learning algorithm
combine them into a forecast: the probability of an up move, the expected price change, or the probability
that a resting order is filled. The classical models act as feature generators; the learner estimates how
much each matters and, if it is flexible enough, how they interact. In
machine-learning terms this is \emph{stacking}: the outputs of several first-stage models become the inputs
of a second-stage model.

Meta-models are the first of three ways of adding learning, in decreasing order of imposed structure:
meta-models (this section), structural hybrids (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid}) and deep networks
(Section~\ref{sec:deep}). A meta-model differs from each of its neighbours in one respect. The classical
models fix in advance how the signals combine; a meta-model learns it. A deep network is asked to
rediscover order-flow imbalance or self-excitation from raw snapshots; a meta-model is given them
ready-made, and the evidence of Section~\ref{sec:meta-evidence} is that engineered inputs of this kind
outperform raw book inputs when the learner is held fixed. A structural hybrid builds classical structure
\emph{inside} a network---a Hawkes kernel in an attention layer, a point process with neural intensities;
a meta-model keeps the classical models intact and learns only how to combine what they say. Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-vs-meta} draws this line in detail. The price of
the extra inputs is a larger space of choices---which sub-models, which windows, which learner---and so a
greater risk of fitting noise (Section~\ref{sec:meta-train}).

\subsection{The architecture}
\label{sec:meta-arch}

Figure~\ref{fig:meta-arch} shows the structure. Each classical model runs on the same event stream and is
updated as the book changes. Their outputs are collected, at each decision time, into one feature vector.
The learner maps that vector to a forecast, which is calibrated so that it can be read as a probability
(Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}), and the forecast feeds the execution decision of Part~III.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
  node distance=4mm and 4mm,
  src/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, font=\scriptsize, text width=20mm, minimum height=8mm},
  sub/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, font=\scriptsize, text width=31mm, minimum height=7mm},
  mid/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, font=\scriptsize, text width=19mm, minimum height=10mm, fill=white},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, thick}]
\node[src] (feed) {\textbf{Event stream}\\order-by-order data, reconstructed book};
\node[sub, right=7mm of feed, yshift=19mm] (s1) {Order-flow imbalance: best level, multi-level, integrated};
\node[sub, below=of s1] (s2) {Queue imbalance, micro-price, book shape};
\node[sub, below=of s2] (s3) {Hawkes intensities of each event type; recent branching ratio};
\node[sub, below=of s3] (s4) {Queue models: depletion times, P(next move up), fill probability};
\node[sub, below=of s4] (s5) {Cross-asset order flow};
\node[mid, right=7mm of s3] (fv) {\textbf{Feature vector}\\one row per decision time};
\node[mid, right=of fv] (lr) {\textbf{Learner}\\logistic, boosted trees, neural network};
\node[mid, right=of lr] (out) {\textbf{Calibrated forecast}\\P(up), E[move], P(fill)};
\node[src, below=8mm of out, text width=19mm] (dec) {\textbf{Decision}\\place, keep, cancel, cross (Part~III)};
\foreach \k in {1,...,5} \draw[arr] (feed.east) -- (s\k.west);
\foreach \k in {1,...,5} \draw[arr] (s\k.east) -- (fv.west);
\draw[arr] (fv) -- (lr); \draw[arr] (lr) -- (out); \draw[arr] (out) -- (dec);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A meta-model. Classical models of the book (left) each turn the event stream into a few
interpretable numbers; a learner (right) combines them into a calibrated forecast for the execution
decision. The classical models are fitted first and kept fixed while the learner is trained on their
out-of-sample outputs (Section~\ref{sec:meta-train}).}
\label{fig:meta-arch}
\end{figure}

\label{sec:meta-inputs}

as described in the sections that define them. Most are running sums, or table look-ups precomputed
offline, updated once per event.

\label{sec:meta-learner}

Table~\ref{tab:meta-learners} lists the learners that published studies have placed on top of engineered or
classical-model inputs, and what each study found. They range from penalised linear models to random
forests, boosted trees and feed-forward neural networks with one or two hidden layers; the detailed results are in
Section~\ref{sec:meta-evidence}.

\label{tab:meta-learners}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}

\toprule
Study & Inputs & Learners & Finding \\
\midrule
\citet{kercheval2015} & prices, volumes and derived attributes of each book entry & multi-class support vector machines & selected features effective for short-term moves \\
\citet{ntakaris2020plos} & 273 hand-crafted book, technical and quantitative features, including a logistic-regression output & least-squares, linear discriminant, radial-basis network & a few features can reach the best performance \\
\citet{nousi2019} & hand-crafted FI-2010 features vs learned representations & linear SVM, one-layer network, MLP & hand-crafted beat autoencoder features \\
\citet{ntakaris2019access} & hand-crafted econometric and technical sets vs LSTM-autoencoder features & MLPs, CNNs, LSTMs & hand-crafted better; shallow MLPs suffice \\
\citet{zheng2013jump} & lagged volumes, price gaps, order signs and counts & logistic and lasso-logistic regression & target: price jumps between trades; evaluation split: not stated \\
\citet{easley2021} & six classical measures (Roll, Kyle's lambda, Amihud, VPIN, \ldots) & random forest; logistic regression & logistic slightly more accurate \\
\citet{aitsahalia2026} & 117 trade- and quote-based predictors & lasso, ridge, random forest, boosted trees, neural networks, least squares & all similar except unpenalised least squares \\
\citet{munitoke2020ratio} & book state and Hawkes-based history & logit-type ratio of intensities & combination beats either alone \\
\citet{guo2018btcvol} & autoregressive volatility and order-book features & learned gate mixing the two & lowest error in 10 of 12 months \\
\citet{prata2024} & probability outputs of 15 deep models & two-layer MLP & did not beat the best single model (5 to 6 F1 points lower on the new Nasdaq data) \\
