\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Results of learner comparisons.} Where studies compared learners on the same inputs, the differences
were small. In the two largest studies a penalised linear or logistic model was as good as, or slightly
better than, random forests, boosted trees and neural networks~\citep{aitsahalia2026,easley2021}; unpenalised
least squares performed worse, which the authors attribute to overfitting. On raw book inputs,
\citet{briola2020} found a multilayer perceptron matching a CNN--LSTM while logistic regression scored lower. We found no
study in which a flexible learner on classical inputs clearly outperformed a penalised linear model
on the same inputs. A penalised linear model---lasso~\citep{tibshirani1996}, which also sets the weights of
unhelpful inputs to zero---is therefore the natural first meta-model and the baseline any more flexible
learner must beat. Whatever the learner, its output must be calibrated, because the execution decision uses
it as a probability.

\paragraph{The lasso.} The lasso~\citep{tibshirani1996} is least-squares regression with a limit on
the total size of the coefficients. Write $f_{n,k}$ for input $k$ at observation $n$---for example the OFI
over a one-second window, or a Hawkes intensity---$Y_n$ for the target, and $\beta_k$ for the weight on input
$k$ (a regression coefficient, as $\beta$ is in Section~\ref{sec:ofi}). The lasso chooses the weights to
\begin{equation}
  \text{minimise}\quad \sum_{n} \Big(Y_n - \beta_0 - \sum_{k} \beta_k f_{n,k}\Big)^2
  \;+\; \varrho \sum_{k} |\beta_k| ,
\end{equation}
where $\varrho \ge 0$ sets how strongly large weights are penalised.
\inwords{fit the data as well as possible, but pay a price proportional to the sum of the absolute sizes of
the weights. With $\varrho = 0$ this is ordinary least squares; as $\varrho$ grows, the weights shrink
towards zero.} Tibshirani's original statement is the equivalent constrained form: minimise the squared
error subject to $\sum_k |\beta_k|$ being at most a chosen constant. The important property is that, because
the penalty uses absolute values, it does not merely shrink weights---it sets many of them \emph{exactly} to
zero, so the lasso selects inputs as well as fitting them. The reason is geometric. With two inputs, the set
of weights allowed by the constraint is a diamond with corners on the axes; the best fit within it is
usually found at a corner, where one weight is zero. A penalty on the \emph{squares} of the weights (ridge
regression) gives a disc instead, which has no corners, so weights become small but rarely zero. For a
classification target the same penalty is added to the log-likelihood of a logistic regression. Two
practical points follow from the form of the penalty. Inputs must be put on a common scale first, since the
penalty treats a unit of every weight alike; and $\varrho$ is chosen by validation---for market data,
time-ordered validation (Section~\ref{sec:deep-real})---usually as the value giving the best out-of-sample
error. For a meta-model, the lasso therefore answers directly which classical inputs earn a place: in the
study of \citet{aitsahalia2026}, the inputs it selected most often were signed transaction volume and past
returns, ahead of book imbalance.

\subsection{State models and event models measure different things}
\label{sec:meta-state-event}

\paragraph{Stock and flow.} The classical models of this part fall into two groups that look at the book in
different ways (Sections~\ref{sec:ofi} and~\ref{sec:events}). \emph{State} models read a snapshot: queue
imbalance, the micro-price and the shape of the book describe how liquidity is arranged \emph{now}---which
side is thin, how deep the book is. \emph{Event} models read the stream of orders: order-flow imbalance, Hawkes
intensities and message-level models describe what is \emph{happening}---how much pressure is arriving, how
busy each kind of event is, and whether size is leaving a queue because it is traded or because it is
cancelled. In the language of economics, state models measure a \emph{stock} and event models a
\emph{flow}. Because they measure different things, their forecasts should carry partly different
information, and their errors need not move together. That matters for combining, because a combination
gains most when its components are dissimilar~\citep{breiman1996stack}.

\paragraph{Partial overlap between state and event information.} The two are not independent. The state at the end of a window is
the state at its start plus the events in between, so a model that sees a full history of snapshots can
reconstruct the \emph{net} flow: order-flow imbalance at the best level is computed from changes in the
best queues~\citep{cks2014}. What snapshots cannot recover is the \emph{cause} of each change. A queue that
shrinks by 300 lots looks the same whether a buyer traded against it or sellers cancelled
(Example~\ref{ex:qi}), and the timing and clustering of events---what a Hawkes intensity measures---are lost
when snapshots are sampled. Conversely, an event model that ignores the current state does not know which
side is close to breaking. Table~\ref{tab:state-event} summarises what each group sees and misses.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{What state models and event models of the book see, and what each misses.}
\label{tab:state-event}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Examples & What it measures & What it cannot see \\
\midrule
State (stock) & queue imbalance, micro-price, book shape & how liquidity is arranged now: which side is thin, how deep the book is & why the book reached this state; how fast it is changing \\
Event (flow) & OFI, Hawkes intensities, message-level models & how much pressure is arriving, how busy each event type is, trades versus cancellations & where the book stands relative to breaking, unless the state is also supplied \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Published evidence.} Three published results point the same way, though none tests the question
directly for the classical models.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item Predicting the sign of the next trade, a model that combines the book's state with Hawkes-based
  event history beat both the state-only and the history-only models, by a little over 2.5\% on all trades
  and by more than 13\% on the trades where the sign changed~\citep{munitoke2020ratio}.
\item Deep models trained on individual messages and deep models trained on book snapshots produced less
  correlated forecasts, and averaging them improved on DeepLOB by about half to three quarters of an F1
  point, where averaging snapshot models alone did not do so consistently~\citep{zhanglimzohren2021mbo}.
\item Within a single group the overlap can be large: adding the imbalance of trades to order-flow
  imbalance raised the explained share of price changes only from about 65\% to 67\%~\citep{cks2014}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{A proposed meta-model design.} The argument suggests a specific design for a meta-model: pair at least one
state model with at least one event model, rather than several models of the same kind. It also suggests
how to check it before building anything elaborate. Measure, out of sample, the correlation between the
state models' and the event models' forecast errors; if it is low, a combination has room to help, and the
combination should then beat each group alone and the nulls of Section~\ref{sec:meta-combine}. We found no
study that reports this error correlation for the classical order-book models; it is an open question
(Section~\ref{sec:agenda}).

\subsection{Combining forecasts: methods borrowed from the general forecasting literature}
\label{sec:meta-combine}

\paragraph{Scope of this subsection.} This subsection is not about order-book models. It
summarises a general literature---on how to combine the forecasts of several models into one---that was
developed and tested almost entirely \emph{outside} order books: on quarterly macroeconomic data (output
growth and inflation), on daily stock-index returns, on monthly stock returns, and on standard
machine-learning benchmark data sets. We include it because its methods apply directly to one case that
arises in a meta-model. Some classical outputs are features---OFI over a window, a Hawkes intensity---and
are best passed to a learner (Section~\ref{sec:meta-learner}). Others are already forecasts of the same
quantity: the up-move probability of a queue race, the drift implied by the micro-price, a logistic
regression on queue imbalance. Combining such forecasts is exactly the problem this literature studies.

Each paragraph below states the data its evidence comes from. Only a handful of results use order-book or
intraday data, and they are marked explicitly. Whether the general findings carry over to order-book
forecasts made every few milliseconds has not been tested; none of the methods below has been applied to
a combination of the classical order-book models of this part (see the last paragraph).

\paragraph{Six ways to combine, at a glance.} Figure~\ref{fig:combine} sketches the six approaches discussed
below. In every panel, three component models (for example a queue-race probability, a micro-price drift and
an OFI regression) each produce a forecast, shown as $\hat y_1, \hat y_2, \hat y_3$, and the approaches
differ only in how those forecasts become one.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  f/.style={draw, rounded corners=1pt, fill=blue!10, minimum width=6mm, minimum height=4mm, inner sep=1pt},
  c/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!15, align=center, minimum height=9mm, inner sep=2pt},
  o/.style={draw, rounded corners=1pt, fill=green!12, minimum width=6mm, minimum height=4mm, inner sep=1pt},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.4mm]}}]
\newcommand{\panel}[4]{
\begin{scope}[shift={#1}]
  \node[f] (a) at (0,0.7) {$\hat y_1$}; \node[f] (b) at (0,0) {$\hat y_2$}; \node[f] (cc) at (0,-0.7) {$\hat y_3$};
  \node[c, text width=19mm] (m) at (1.9,0) {#2};
  \node[o] (y) at (3.55,0) {$\hat y$};
  \draw[arr] (a) -- (m); \draw[arr] (b) -- (m); \draw[arr] (cc) -- (m); \draw[arr] (m) -- (y);
  #4
  \node[align=center, text width=44mm] at (1.75,-1.45) {#3};
\end{scope}}
\panel{(0,0)}{fixed weights\\e.g.\ $\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13$}{\textbf{(a) fixed weights}\\average, or weights from past errors}{}
\panel{(5.0,0)}{learned combiner\\(linear or ML)}{\textbf{(b) stacking}\\a model trained on out-of-sample forecasts}{\node[draw, dashed, align=center, font=\tiny] at (1.9,1.05) {trained on held-out data};}
\panel{(10.0,0)}{weighted average\\of probabilities\\$\rightarrow$ recalibrate}{\textbf{(c) probability pool}\\averaged, then recalibrated}{}
\panel{(0,-3.4)}{weights change\\over time}{\textbf{(d) dynamic weights}\\follow recent performance}{\draw[arr, dashed] (y.north) to[bend right=45] node[above, font=\tiny]{past errors} (m.north);}
\panel{(5.0,-3.4)}{weights set by\\a gate}{\textbf{(e) gating}\\weights depend on market state}{\node[draw, rounded corners=1pt, fill=gray!15, font=\tiny] (st) at (1.9,1.15) {market state}; \draw[arr] (st) -- (m);}
\panel{(10.0,-3.4)}{weights updated\\after each outcome}{\textbf{(f) online weighting}\\close to the best in hindsight}{\draw[arr, dashed] (y.north) to[bend right=45] node[above, font=\tiny]{each loss} (m.north);}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Six ways to combine the forecasts of several models into one. Schematic.}
\label{fig:combine}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Baselines a combination must beat.} Any combination---and any machine-learned combiner especially---has to be judged
against what could be had without it. Four benchmarks follow from the evidence in this subsection and in
Section~\ref{sec:deep}, and a combination earns its place only if it beats all four out of sample.
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{The simple average of the same component forecasts.} This is the null of the forecast-combination
  literature: estimated weights frequently fail to beat it (the puzzle below), so a combiner that cannot
  beat equal weights has learned nothing usable.
\item \textbf{The best single component, chosen in advance.} The model that did best on past data, selected
  before the test period---not the best one in hindsight, which no one could have traded.
\item \textbf{A naive forecast of the target.} For direction forecasts, the base rate of each class, and,
  when the label is built from overlapping averages, the forecast that repeats its last observable value:
  in the re-test of Lee et al., a forecast repeating the last observable label scored 64.8\%, against
  65.9\% for DeepLOB with the smoothed label~\citep{lee2024predictability}.
\item \textbf{The economic null.} For a trading use, the same execution policy run without the combined
  forecast---for instance the model-free benchmark of Section~\ref{sec:shadow}---under the same execution
  model and costs.
\end{enumerate}
The comparison should use a proper score for the forecast and net profit for
the decision, on data used for nothing else, and the winner should be reported with a model confidence
set~\citep{hansen2011mcs} rather than as the single best score, counting every combination tried
\citep{white2000,harvey2016}.

\paragraph{Learned combiners.} Approach~(b), stacking, uses a learned model as the combiner
---a regression, a logistic model, boosted trees or a neural network---trained on the
component forecasts, and possibly on other inputs too, in which case it becomes the meta-model of
Section~\ref{sec:meta-learner}. The published evidence says three things about it. It needs the component
forecasts to be out of sample, or it learns their overfitting (the medical example below). It gains most
when the components are dissimilar and the data are plentiful~\citep{breiman1996stack,ting1999}. And it does
not automatically beat simpler combinations: estimated weights often lose to a plain average (the puzzle
below), and on order-book data a neural-network combiner of fifteen deep models did not beat the best single
model~\citep{prata2024}. A learned combiner is therefore a candidate that must beat the simple average out of
sample, not a default.

\paragraph{Neural-network combiners tried in the literature.} Neural networks have been used as combiners
since the mid-1990s, in four forms; Table~\ref{tab:nncomb} lists the main studies.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[A small network on the forecasts alone] \citet{donaldson1996annc} feed two daily stock-index volatility
  forecasts (a moving-average variance and a GARCH model) into a network with one hidden layer of one to
  three units, which outputs the combined forecast; \citet{harrald1997} evolve such networks by evolutionary
  search. On squared error the network did not beat an ordinary least-squares combination: it tied on the
  S\&P~500 and Toronto indices and had slightly higher error on the Nikkei and FTSE~\citep{donaldson1996annc}, and
  in the evolutionary study the GARCH forecast alone had the lowest out-of-sample error~\citep{harrald1997}.
\item[A feed-forward stacker] A multilayer perceptron takes the component forecasts, sometimes with extra
  inputs, and outputs the forecast. On limit-order-book data this is the LOBCAST stack of fifteen deep
  models, which did not beat the best single model (F1 55.9 against 61.2 and 53.2 against 59.5 on two years of
  Nasdaq data) though it did beat a weighted majority vote on FI-2010~\citep{prata2024}. Recent stacking
  studies of volatility and stock direction~\citep{ramosperez2019,barnwal2019stack} report no simple-average
  or linear-stacking baseline, so they cannot answer the question.
\item[A network that reads the forecasts as a sequence or image] For2For passes the matrix of base forecasts
  through a convolutional network with a linear shortcut, or an LSTM, and outputs the combined
  forecast~\citep{zhao2020for2for}.
\item[A network that outputs weights from the raw series] Convolutional networks read the history of the
  series itself and output a weight for each base forecast through a softmax~\citep{mafildes2021,felici2026mtl}.
  This is the neural counterpart of FFORMA, which learns combination weights from features of the series with
  gradient-boosted trees rather than a network~\citep{montero2020fforma}.
\end{description}
Gated mixtures of experts~\citep{jacobs1991,guo2018btcvol} are a fifth, related form, in which the gate and
the experts are trained together rather than stacked on separately fitted forecasts. Attention-based combiners, a sixth form, are described in Section~\ref{sec:meta-attn}.

\paragraph{Neural combiners against the equal-weight average.} Whether a neural combiner beats the equal-weight average depends on how much data it is trained on. On a few
financial series, the 1990s networks did not reliably beat a linear combination. Trained across thousands of
series, they do beat the simple average, but by little or nothing over a boosted-tree combiner: on retail
sales the convolutional combiner reached a relative error of 0.968, against 0.986 for the simple average,
0.974 for FFORMA and 1.000 for the best single model~\citep{mafildes2021}; on the M4 competition series, a
weight-learning network scored 0.794 to 0.846 on the competition's error measure against 0.911 to 0.949 for
the simple average~\citep{felici2026mtl}, while For2For scored 0.8345 against 0.838 for
FFORMA~\citep{zhao2020for2for}. For2For had higher error than a simple linear model on the smaller weekly and hourly subsets
(359 and 414 series). The studies agree on three conditions: the combiner is trained on forecasts the
components had not seen, the components are dissimilar (mixed pools beat single-type pools, and a diversity
term helped~\citep{mafildes2021,felici2026mtl}), and weighting the forecasts works better than selecting one
of them (relative error 0.968 against 1.050~\citep{mafildes2021}). None of these large-sample results comes
from intraday or order-book data; there, the one neural combiner we found did not beat the best single model.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Neural networks used to combine forecasts. ``Beats average'' compares with the simple average of the
same component forecasts where the study reports it.}
\label{tab:nncomb}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Study & Combiner & Data & Result \\
\midrule
\citet{donaldson1996annc} & one-hidden-layer network on two forecasts & daily volatility, four indices & tied with, or slightly higher error than, least-squares combination \\
\citet{harrald1997} & evolved networks & daily volatility & best single model (GARCH) lowest error \\
\citet{prata2024} & two-layer MLP on 15 models' probabilities & limit-order books & did not beat the best single model (5 to 6 F1 points lower on the new Nasdaq data) \\
\citet{zhao2020for2for} & CNN with linear shortcut, or LSTM & M4 series & slightly better than FFORMA overall; higher error than a linear model on the weekly and hourly subsets \\
\citet{mafildes2021} & CNN outputting weights from the raw series & retail sales & beats average (0.968 vs 0.986) and FFORMA (0.974) \\
\citet{felici2026mtl} & CNN outputting weights, with diversity term & M4 series & beats average (0.79--0.85 vs 0.91--0.95) \\
\citet{montero2020fforma} & boosted trees (not a network), for contrast & M4 series & learns weights from features of each series; selection error 10\% higher than weighting \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Fixed weights, and the forecast-combination puzzle.} The oldest approach averages the forecasts
with fixed weights: equal weights, weights inversely proportional to each model's past squared error
\citep{bates1969}, or weights estimated by regressing the outcome on the forecasts \citep{granger1984}. A
long-standing finding is that simple averages are hard to beat. On quarterly output-growth forecasts for seven
countries (macroeconomic data), \citet{stockwatson2004} found the simple mean had the lowest average loss of all the combination
methods they tried (0.648, against 1.048 for following the recently best model and up to 0.976 for weights
that varied over time), and that the methods with the least adaptive weights did best and were the most
stable. \citet{claeskens2016} explain this ``forecast combination puzzle'': weights estimated from data make
the combination biased and add variance, with no guarantee of beating equal weights. Equal weights fail
when one model is much better than the others: in one classification problem a majority vote had a 13.0\%
error rate against 2.6\% for the best single model~\citep{ting1999}.

\paragraph{Stacking.} Stacking learns the combination from cross-validated predictions of the component
models~\citep{wolpert1992,breiman1996stack}. \citet{breiman1996stack} fits non-negative weights by least
squares on such predictions and finds that the non-negativity constraint matters more than forcing the
weights to sum to one, and that stacking gains most when the component models are dissimilar.
On ten standard machine-learning benchmark data sets, \citet{ting1999} find that combining predicted
class \emph{probabilities} works better than combining predicted labels: a linear combination of
probabilities beat the best single model in nine of them and tied in the tenth, with significant gains
mainly on the largest. On \emph{order-book} data a learned combination of fifteen deep models did not beat the best single model (5 to 6 F1 points lower on the new Nasdaq data)~\citep{prata2024}, and an
equal-weight average of three book-based models was not consistently better than DeepLOB alone, while
adding models built on individual messages improved F1 by about half a point~\citep{zhanglimzohren2021mbo}.

\paragraph{The cost of fitting the combination in sample.} The one measured comparison we found of a
second-stage model trained on in-sample versus out-of-sample first-stage predictions comes from medicine,
not finance. \citet{tibshirani2002preval} combine a learned gene-expression classifier with six clinical
predictors in a logistic regression on 78 patients. With the classifier's in-sample predictions its odds
ratio is 60.1; with predictions made out of sample it is 4.7. Its apparent reduction in error, 0.269 to
0.141, shrinks under full cross-validation to 0.295 to 0.282, a difference of 0.013 with a standard error of
0.091. We found no study that measures this effect for stacked financial or order-book models.

\paragraph{Combining probabilities.} When the components output probabilities, the natural combination is
a weighted average of them---a \emph{linear pool}. \citet{ranjan2010} prove that a linear pool of calibrated
probability forecasts is itself not calibrated: it is under-confident. Recalibrating the pool with a
beta-transformed combination improved the Brier score from 0.0799 to 0.0781 on probability-of-precipitation forecasts for 29 US cities, against
0.0816 for the best single forecast. On daily S\&P~500 returns, a pool of two models beat the better of
them by 37 log-score points over 7{,}324 days~\citep{geweke2011}, but a single model that combines both
components' structure scored slightly better than pooling them~\citep{gneiting2013combining}.

\paragraph{Weights that change over time.} Dynamic model averaging lets the weights evolve with recent
performance~\citep{raftery2010dma}. The evidence is mixed. In \citeauthor{raftery2010dma}'s application the
gain came only in an early, unstable period (mean squared error 68.9 against 77.5 for the best single
model). For quarterly US inflation, dynamic averaging and selection beat static Bayesian averaging
(eight-quarter error 22.09 against 29.99)~\citep{koop2012dma}. In \citet{stockwatson2004}, heavily adaptive
weights had higher error than simpler ones. \citet{elliott2005} show that weights that switch with a hidden regime help only
when the regimes persist; with frequent switching and small samples the simple average was best.

\paragraph{Weights that depend on the state: gating.} A \emph{mixture of experts} uses a gating model that
looks at the inputs and decides how much to trust each component~\citep{jacobs1991,jordan1994hme}. In the
original study, on a vowel-recognition task, gating sped up training but did not improve
accuracy~\citep{jacobs1991}. On \emph{cryptocurrency order-book} data, gated mixtures did better than adding the same order-book features linearly: for hourly
Bitcoin volatility~\citep{guo2018btcvol} and for intraday trading volume across two exchanges, where the
gated mixture had lower root-mean-squared error than the ARMA-GARCH benchmarks but gradient-boosted trees
had lower mean absolute error in several cases~\citep{antulov2021tme}.

\paragraph{Online weighting.} Algorithms from online learning update the weights after every outcome and
guarantee that, over time, the combination does nearly as well as the best component or the best fixed
combination in hindsight~\citep{littlestone1994,freund1997,cesabianchi2006}. Applied to thirteen
machine-learning models of monthly US stock returns, one such algorithm reached a Sharpe ratio of 2.77 against 2.74 for the
best single model, with half its worst monthly loss (0.08 against 0.16); in the same study equal weights
(2.56) beat fixed weights estimated on validation data (2.28)~\citep{remlinger2023}. That study uses monthly
data.

\paragraph{Calibrating the result.} On standard machine-learning benchmark data sets, \citet{niculescu2005} find that some learners
produce distorted probabilities: support-vector machines and boosted trees push them towards the middle, naive Bayes towards
0 and 1, while neural networks, bagged trees and logistic regression are close to calibrated. A sigmoid
correction~\citep{platt1999} or isotonic regression~\citep{zadrozny2002} fitted on data not used for
training repairs this; the sigmoid works better with fewer than a few hundred to a thousand calibration
cases~\citep{niculescu2005}.

\paragraph{Implications for order-book meta-models.} Read across from these other settings, the
evidence points to an order of attack rather than a single method: start from an equal-weight or non-negatively constrained combination of
the classical forecasts; move to stacking or a learner only with enough data and with out-of-sample
first-stage predictions; recalibrate any pooled or learned probability before using it; and treat
time-varying or state-dependent weights as hypotheses that must beat the simple average out of sample. No
published study we found applies these methods to a combination of the classical order-book models of
this part.

\subsubsection{Attention as a combiner}
\label{sec:meta-attn}

A Transformer's attention layer (Section~\ref{sec:deep-attn}) computes a weighted average of a set of items, with
weights decided by comparing a \emph{query} with a \emph{key} for each item. That is exactly the shape of a
forecast combination: the items are the component models, the values being averaged are their forecasts, and
the weights say how much to trust each model. What changes from one design to another is what plays the query
and what plays the keys---in other words, what the combiner looks at when it decides whom to trust.
Figure~\ref{fig:attn-comb} shows the structure. With $M$ component models, model $m$'s forecast
$\widehat{Y}^{(m)}_t$, and a weight $a_{m,t}$ for each,
\begin{equation}
\widehat{Y}_t = \sum_{m=1}^{M} a_{m,t}\,\widehat{Y}^{(m)}_t,
\qquad
a_{m,t} = \frac{\exp\bigl(\text{score}(\text{query}_t, \text{key}_{m,t})\bigr)}
               {\sum_{m'=1}^{M}\exp\bigl(\text{score}(\text{query}_t, \text{key}_{m',t})\bigr)},
\label{eq:attn-comb}
\end{equation}
\inwords{each model gets a score for how well its key matches the current query; the scores are turned into
weights that are positive and sum to one; the combined forecast is the weighted average of the models'
forecasts. Because the query changes with $t$, so do the weights.}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, scale=0.9, transform shape,
  q/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=yellow!20, align=center, text width=28mm, minimum height=9mm},
  k/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=blue!10, align=center, text width=30mm, minimum height=6mm},
  v/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!15, align=center, text width=17mm, minimum height=6mm},
  s/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=17mm, minimum height=6mm},
  o/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=green!12, align=center, text width=24mm, minimum height=7mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\node[q] (q) at (0,0) {\textbf{query}: the situation now\\(e.g.\ recent history)};
\foreach \m/\y in {1/1.1, 2/0, 3/-1.1} {
  \node[k] (k\m) at (3.9,\y) {\textbf{key} of model \m\\(e.g.\ its recent error)};
  \node[s] (s\m) at (6.9,\y) {score $\rightarrow$ weight $a_{\m,t}$};
  \node[v] (v\m) at (9.4,\y) {\textbf{value}: forecast $\widehat{Y}^{(\m)}_t$};
  \draw[arr] (q.east) -- (k\m.west); \draw[arr] (k\m) -- (s\m); \draw[arr] (s\m) -- (v\m);
}
\node[o] (o) at (12.3,0) {$\widehat{Y}_t = \sum_m a_{m,t}\widehat{Y}^{(m)}_t$};
\foreach \m in {1,2,3} {\draw[arr] (v\m.east) -- (o.west);}
\node[font=\tiny] at (6.9,-1.75) {softmax across the models};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Attention used to combine forecasts. The query describes the current situation, each model has a key,
the match between them sets the model's weight, and the combined forecast is the weighted average of the
models' forecasts (the values). Schematic.}
\label{fig:attn-comb}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Attention targets in published combiners.} We found three, all preprints;
Table~\ref{tab:attn-comb} compares them.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Which model suits this series now.} In \citet{bourgin2021maes} the query is a recurrent network's
  summary of the series so far, and each model's key is a learned vector that comes to represent the
  situations in which that model is most useful. The weights therefore change from series to series and from
  step to step. The component models are trained together with the combiner, as in a mixture of experts,
  rather than fitted first. The data are synthetic; the combiner was more robust to a changing data-generating
  process than the simple average and linear stacking, but the results are given only as figures.
\item \textbf{Recent errors as evidence of who will be right next.} In \citet{patel2023attnpool} the component
  models are fixed in advance. The query is the most recent observed values, each model's key is its most recent
  forecast errors, and the values are the models' current forecasts; the weights are trained to make the next
  combined forecast as accurate as possible. On a chaotic test system (Lorenz-63) whose parameters drift, it kept
  forecasts valid for a median of 2.60 time units against 0.20 for a linear-regression combination, but only
  when the query covered the last 5 steps rather than 1 (0.30). On weekly COVID-19 deaths it did not
  consistently beat the existing ensemble of the COVID-19 Forecast Hub, which pools many more models, and linear
  regression was best in one of the four test periods.
\item \textbf{Which sub-network to use.} In \citet{chen2025e3former} the ``models'' are sub-networks of one
  Transformer that read the same input at different resolutions, and self-attention runs across their forecasts,
  blended with weights updated online from recent errors. On cloud-workload data it reports 13.9\% lower
  squared error than the best competing method.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Attention-based forecast combiners. None uses financial or order-book data.}
\label{tab:attn-comb}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.13\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.15\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Study & Query & Keys & Components & Result \\
\midrule
\citet{bourgin2021maes} & summary of the series so far & one learned vector per model & 5 LSTMs, trained jointly with the combiner & synthetic data; beat average and linear stacking (figures only) \\
\citet{patel2023attnpool} & recent observed values & each model's recent error & fixed in advance & beat linear regression on Lorenz-63 with a 5-step query; not the Hub ensemble on COVID-19 \\
\citet{chen2025e3former} & self-attention across the forecasts & same & sub-networks of one Transformer & 13.9\% lower error than best baseline; attention not isolated \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Weights from past performance versus weights from the current state.} A combiner can give a model a high weight because it \emph{has been}
right lately, or because it is expected to \emph{be} right next. The two coincide when accuracy persists and
diverge when it does not---when the model that did best in one regime is the wrong choice in the next. The
distinction is subtle for learned combiners, because every one of them is trained to make the \emph{next}
combined forecast as accurate as possible: its weights are always, by construction, a forecast of who will be
right next. What differs is the evidence the weights are computed from (Table~\ref{tab:lookback}).

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{What combination weights are based on. Rule-based weights assume that past accuracy carries forward;
learned weights are trained to predict the next outcome from whatever evidence they are given.}
\label{tab:lookback}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\linewidth}}
\toprule
Method & Evidence the weights use & What a high weight means \\
\midrule
Inverse past error~\citep{bates1969} & realised past errors, through a fixed rule & has been right \\
Dynamic model averaging; online weighting & realised recent errors, through an update rule & has been right lately \\
Gating, mixture of experts & the current inputs & expected to be right in this situation \\
\citet{bourgin2021maes} & a summary of the series so far; no information on past accuracy & expected to suit this situation \\
\citet{patel2023attnpool} & recent true values and each model's recent errors & expected to be right next, judged partly from recent errors \\
\citet{chen2025e3former} & attention over the forecasts, blended with weights updated from recent errors & both \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

In \citet{patel2023attnpool}, recent errors are inputs, not a rule: the network learns, by minimising next-step
error, how to turn recent states and errors into weights, and could in principle learn that a recently accurate
model is about to fail. On the Lorenz system there is some sign of forward-looking behaviour: in forecasts many
steps ahead, after the true values stop arriving, the most heavily weighted model is the one whose parameter
matches the true, drifting parameter, which the authors describe as anticipating the change. But the authors also
state that their method assumes the biases of the component models change little over time, and give the
continual updating of the COVID-19 Forecast Hub models as one reason it did not beat that ensemble. 

\paragraph{Tests of whether attention weights anticipate or follow performance.} None of the three studies
includes a case in which recent accuracy is misleading---for example, one in which the recently best model tends
to do worse next. That is the experiment that would separate a combiner that anticipates which model will be
right from one that simply follows the recent winner, and it has not been done. For order-book data, where a
model's accuracy in one regime may say little about the next, it is the test that matters.


\paragraph{Other attention combiners.} One journal paper, \citet{yao2026asde}, describes attention that computes
ensemble weights from each member's past and predicted future performance, on seven time-series data sets;
\citet{chen2024attnensload} combine an LSTM and a Prophet model with additive attention for electricity load
and report beating Ridge and neural-network combiners.

\paragraph{Models mistaken for attention combiners.} Three groups of models are easily mistaken for one.
In weather post-processing, attention runs across the members of a single model's ensemble of forecasts, to
correct them or produce a distribution, not across different models~\citep{finn2021saet,benbouallegue2024poet}.
In mixture-of-experts forecasters such as Time-MoE~\citep{shi2025timemoe}, a router chooses internal layers of
one network, not other models' forecasts. And several ensembles use attention \emph{inside} their component
models while combining them with a fully connected layer or boosted trees~\citep{bui2025haelt}.

\paragraph{Implications for order-book meta-models.} Attention adds one idea to the combiners above: the weights can depend on
the current situation, described however the designer chooses---the recent history, or each model's recent
errors. For order-book meta-models this is the gating idea of the previous paragraphs in another form. The
three studies found are preprints on non-financial data. Like any learned combiner, it is a
candidate that must beat the simple average out of sample.

\subsection{Published evidence}
\label{sec:meta-evidence}

The published work does not use the word ``meta-model'', but a sizeable body of it feeds engineered or
classical-model inputs to a learner. Five findings stand out, each resting on a small number of studies,
most of them on a few stocks over short periods.

\paragraph{1. Engineered inputs beat raw inputs when the learner is held fixed.} Every head-to-head
comparison we could verify points the same way. Holding the network architecture fixed and changing only
the input, \citet{lucchese2024} find that order-flow inputs place a stock-specific model among the
statistically best models in 89\% of stock--horizon cases, against 11\% for the raw ten-level book (model
confidence sets at the 99\% level, ten Nasdaq stocks). \citet{tsantekidis2020stationary} replace raw prices
by stationary features---prices relative to the mid, recent mid changes, cumulative depth---and every
network improves: an LSTM's F1 score at a 100-event horizon rises from 0.34 to 0.44, an MLP's from 0.26 to
0.39 (five Finnish stocks, ten days). The Tampere group's studies of hand-crafted features reach the same
conclusion against learned representations: a multilayer perceptron on windows of the hand-crafted FI-2010
features scores an F-measure of 56 against 30 on autoencoder features~\citep{nousi2019}; hand-crafted
econometric, technical and quantitative feature sets beat features learned by an LSTM autoencoder, even
with shallow networks~\citep{ntakaris2019access}; and, from a pool of 273 hand-crafted features, the
authors report that a combination of only a few can reach the best performance~\citep{ntakaris2020plos}. That last study is also an early instance of
stacking in the strict sense: one of its features is itself the output of an online logistic regression
predicting a change in the best quote, and it is the feature most often selected first. The samples are
short, and the learned representations compared are autoencoders rather than end-to-end networks. Two
further studies point the same way: \citet{tashiro2019} feed a convolutional network
encodings of individual orders rather than book snapshots and report that smoothing filters on those encodings
improve accuracy; \citet{palguna2016} build mid-price predictors from features of the current and recent book
and, inside an order-execution task on five months of Nasdaq data, report a trading-cost improvement ``on the
order of one basis point'' for the two best features.

\paragraph{2. With good inputs, the choice of learner matters little.} Two large studies that use
classical microstructure measures as inputs find almost no difference between learners.
\citet{aitsahalia2026}, on 101 S\&P~100 stocks over 2019--2020 with 117 inputs built from trades and
quotes---book imbalance, signed volume, past returns, Kyle-type price-impact ratios and durations---find
that lasso, ridge, random forests, gradient-boosted trees and feed-forward neural networks with one or two hidden layers ``have very similar
performance''; unpenalised least squares performed worse, which the authors attribute to overfitting. They report a median
out-of-sample $R^2$ of about 10\% for five-second returns and a directional accuracy of about 64\%, most of
it from the last ten milliseconds or ten trades. Inputs derived from transactions were more useful
than inputs derived from the state of the book. \citet{easley2021} feed six classical
measures---the Roll measure and Roll impact, Kyle's lambda, the Amihud ratio, a volatility index and
VPIN---for 87 futures contracts to a random forest, and predict the direction of changes in spreads,
volatility and higher moments a week ahead. Out-of-sample accuracy reaches 0.54--0.61; logistic regression
on the same inputs is ``typically \ldots slightly more accurate'' than the forest; and some measures with
high in-sample importance have little out-of-sample predictive power, and the reverse. Both studies use
time-aware protocols: rolling five-day training windows with a separate tuning block in the first, purged
cross-validation in the second. A recent preprint finds the same inputs ranked the same way across assets:
\citet{bieganowski2026} fit gradient-boosted trees to engineered top-of-book, imbalance and VWAP-to-mid
features of five cryptocurrency perpetual futures, one second apart, from 2022 to 2025, predicting the
three-second return. The ranking of input importance, measured by SHAP values, was stable across the five
assets, and the importance of the imbalance grew with the relative tick size.

\paragraph{3. Hawkes-process outputs as inputs.} The cleanest evidence is \citet{munitoke2020ratio}, who
predict the sign of the next market order on 36 Paris-listed stocks. Their model combines the state of the
book (imbalance, spread, last trade sign) with exponentially weighted counts of past market orders computed
with the parameters of a fitted Hawkes model. The combination beats both the state-only and the Hawkes-only
predictors: on average by a little over 2.5\% on all trades, and by more than 13\% over the best Hawkes
model on the trades where the sign changes. The authors call it a theoretical exercise, since it
ignores latency. Other studies use Hawkes models as one stage of a pipeline---predicting the time of the
next event, which a second model then uses~\citep{cestari2025hawkes,raffaelli2026mhp}---or as input
features for a neural network~\citep{fabre2025spoof}. A 2026 conference paper uses bursts in Hawkes intensity to decide when
order-book predictions should not be acted on~\citep{yadav2026hawkes}.

\paragraph{4. Mixing a classical model with a learned one.} \citet{guo2018btcvol} forecast hourly Bitcoin
volatility with a learned gate that mixes an autoregressive volatility model with a model of order-book
features. It had the lowest error in ten of twelve monthly test periods, against baselines including GARCH,
random forests, gradient-boosted trees and LSTMs; adding the same order-book features linearly to the
autoregressive model instead increased its error in all twelve. How the classical and learned parts are combined
mattered as much as which inputs were used. For daily realised volatility, \citet{rahimikia2020ml} (a working paper) fit machine-learning
models to the variables of the HAR model---the standard linear model of realised volatility---together with
order-book and news data. They report that high-dimensional models beat HAR in 90\% of the out-of-sample period
except in periods of extreme volatility, and that putting the machine-learning models into an ensemble with HAR
improves it, while using them as direct substitutes calls for caution. The published companion
paper~\citep{rahimikia2026altdata} keeps the model linear, adding order-book and news variables to HAR-type
models, and reports that depth measures help more than slope measures and that the gains are largest on
high-volatility days.

\paragraph{5. Stacking deep models on top of each other adds little.} A stack of fifteen published deep LOB
models, combined by a two-layer multilayer perceptron trained on their out-of-sample probability forecasts, did not beat the
best single model (5 to 6 F1 points lower on the new Nasdaq data); the authors attribute this to the
base models agreeing too often~\citep{prata2024}. Averaging models built on genuinely different
inputs---individual messages and book snapshots---gained only half to three quarters of an F1 point over
DeepLOB~\citep{zhanglimzohren2021mbo}. A two-stage design on Brazilian (B3) data, in which a convolutional network extracts features from the
book and gradient-boosted trees combine them with trade records that include broker identities, reports
accuracy 8\% higher than the convolutional network alone, 5 points of it from the trees and 3 from the added
trade information~\citep{bileki2022}; whether the 8\% is absolute or relative is not stated. This is what the original stacking literature predicts: combining
gains most when the combined models are dissimilar, and combination weights must be fitted on
cross-validated rather than in-sample predictions, or they overfit~\citep{breiman1996stack}.

\paragraph{Gaps in the literature.} We found no published study that feeds the outputs of the queue models of
Section~\ref{sec:queue-models}---depletion times, the up-move probability of the race, fill
probabilities---into a learner, and no study that adds one family of classical inputs at a time and
measures the economic value of each. Protocol choices that need care in a meta-model include a separate
validation set and causal (backward-looking) input filters. The case for meta-models is
plausible and partly supported---good inputs matter more than clever learners---but the
specific design of Figure~\ref{fig:meta-arch} remains to be tested.

\subsection{Training protocols in the literature}
\label{sec:meta-train}

A meta-model is fitted in two stages, and the published work agrees on one rule for the second stage: the
learner must be trained on predictions of the first-stage models that were made out of sample. In
\citeauthor{breiman1996stack}'s words, if the combination is fitted on in-sample predictions of the
first-stage models it ``will overfit the data'', and it must be fitted on their cross-validated predictions
instead~\citep{breiman1996stack}; the one measured example we found of what happens otherwise is
described in Section~\ref{sec:meta-combine}. The studies of Section~\ref{sec:meta-evidence} implement this, and the
time-ordering of market data, in different ways:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \citet{prata2024} train their stacked model on the base models' predictions over the test period, so
  that its training data are out of sample for the base models, and evaluate it on a held-out part of that
  period;
\item \citet{easley2021} use ten-fold cross-validation with purging, removing about a week of observations
  around each test fold so that no overlapping information leaks into training;
\item \citet{aitsahalia2026} refit on a rolling window of the previous five days and predict the next day,
  re-tuning hyperparameters every twenty days on a separate block;
\item \citet{munitoke2020ratio} calibrate on the previous trading day and predict the next.
\item \citet{bieganowski2026} use walk-forward validation with a gap between training and test data, and
  tune hyperparameters by an inner time-series cross-validation.
\end{itemize}
These are instances of the general requirements of Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}---chronological splits,
purging and embargo~\citep{lopezdeprado2018}. The statistics literature on cross-validation for dependent data
reaches the same place by another route. \emph{h-block} cross-validation removes the $h$ observations before and
after each test observation from the training data~\citep{burman1994}, and \citet{racine2000} proposes
\emph{hv-block} cross-validation; see also \citet{bergmeir2012} on cross-validation for evaluating time-series
predictors. On 62 real and 3 synthetic series, \citet{cerqueira2020} find that cross-validation works for
stationary series, but that when the series is non-stationary the most accurate estimates of performance come
from out-of-sample methods that keep the observations in time order. A meta-model adds one more set of choices to count: every
combination of inputs, windows and learners tried is a trial that must be counted~\citep{white2000,harvey2016}, and
the final choice should be assessed out of sample. The question of
which inputs earn their place is Principle~2 applied input by input; as noted in
Section~\ref{sec:meta-evidence}, no study we found reports that ablation in economic terms.

\subsection{Summary}
\label{sec:meta-summary}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=3pt]
\item \textbf{Definition of a meta-model.} A learner whose inputs are the outputs of the classical models---order-flow
  imbalance, queue imbalance and the micro-price, Hawkes intensities, queue-model probabilities---rather
  than the raw book: stacking, in machine-learning terms (Figure~\ref{fig:meta-arch}).
\item \textbf{Inputs matter more than learners.} Engineered and order-flow inputs beat raw book inputs when the
  learner is held fixed, and with good inputs the choice of learner matters little: in the two largest
  studies a penalised linear or logistic model was as good as, or slightly better than, forests, boosted
  trees and neural networks~\citep{aitsahalia2026,easley2021}. The lasso is the natural first meta-model,
  because it also selects which inputs earn a place.
\item \textbf{Combine dissimilar models.} State models measure how liquidity is arranged now; event models
  measure what is happening and why. Their information overlaps only partly, the little direct evidence
  suggests combining them helps~\citep{munitoke2020ratio,zhanglimzohren2021mbo}, and the correlation of
  their errors can be measured before anything elaborate is built.
\item \textbf{Combining forecasts: simple averages are hard to beat.} The general forecasting
  literature, mostly from macroeconomic and lower-frequency financial data, finds that simple averages are
  hard to beat, that learned combinations need out-of-sample component forecasts and plentiful data, that
  pooled probabilities must be recalibrated, and that time-varying or state-dependent weights help only
  under specific conditions. Neural-network combiners beat the simple average when trained across
  thousands of series, but not reliably on a few series, and the one tested on order-book data did not beat
  the best single model. A machine-learned combiner is a candidate, not a default.
\item \textbf{Baselines.} The simple average of the same forecasts, the best single model chosen in
  advance, a naive forecast of the target, and the same execution policy without the forecast---all out of
  sample, scored properly, with every configuration counted.
\item \textbf{Train the second stage on out-of-sample first-stage outputs.} Otherwise the combination learns the
  first stage's overfitting~\citep{breiman1996stack}; the one measured example, from medicine, shows the
  apparent gain largely disappearing under proper validation~\citep{tibshirani2002preval}.
\item \textbf{Gaps in the literature.} No published study feeds queue-model outputs into a learner, reports an
  input-by-input economic ablation, or applies these combination methods to the classical order-book models;
  the design of Figure~\ref{fig:meta-arch} remains to be tested.
\end{itemize}

\begin{modelcard}{Meta-model (stacked model)}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{First, each classical model (OFI slopes, micro-price tables, Hawkes parameters,
queue-model rates); then the learner that combines their outputs, and a calibration map for its forecasts.}
\cardrow{How}{Train the learner only on out-of-sample predictions of the classical models, as stacking
requires~\citep{breiman1996stack}; use chronological splits with purging (Section~\ref{sec:deep-real});
count every input set and learner tried.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Produce a calibrated forecast---P(up), expected move, P(fill)---from many
partial views of the book at once, for the execution decisions of Part~III; measure, by ablation, which
classical models add value.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A panel of specialists, each reading the book in its own way, and a chair who
has learned from experience how much to trust each of them, and when.}
\end{modelcard}

% ================================================================
\section{Structural hybrids}
\label{sec:hybrid}
% ================================================================

Sections~\ref{sec:meta} and~\ref{sec:hybrid} both join classical models with machine learning, and it is
easy to confuse them. This section starts by drawing the line precisely.

\subsection{Structural hybrids versus meta-models}
\label{sec:hybrid-vs-meta}

\paragraph{Meaning of ``structural''.} Every classical model in Sections~\ref{sec:ofi}--\ref{sec:hawkes}
makes a specific claim about \emph{how} the book works, written as a formula with a few parameters: a Hawkes
model says that each event raises the rate of later events by an amount that decays with time; a
queue-reactive model says that the rate of orders at a level depends on the sizes of the queues; the OFI
regression says that the mid-price moves in proportion to net order flow. We call that claim---the form of
the formula, as opposed to the values of its parameters---the model's \emph{structure}. A \emph{structural
hybrid} is a network whose architecture is forced to follow such a structure, with some part of it (a rate
function, a decay kernel) learned from data instead of fixed in advance. The label is this survey's; it is
not a standard term. It should not be confused with ``structural model'' in economics, which means a model
derived from the decisions of market participants.

\paragraph{Classical model outside or inside the learner.} In a \emph{meta-model} the classical models stay outside the learner:
they are fitted first, kept fixed, and the learner sees only the numbers they produce. In a \emph{structural
hybrid} the classical model's structure is built \emph{into} the learner itself: the network is shaped like a
Hawkes process or a queue model, it reads the raw events, and the whole thing is trained as one model.

Put differently, a meta-model asks ``given what each classical model says, what should we forecast?'', while a
structural hybrid asks ``what if a network had the form of a classical model, but with some of its fixed
formulas replaced by learned functions?''. Figure~\ref{fig:meta-vs-hybrid} shows the two side by side, and
Table~\ref{tab:meta-vs-hybrid} compares them point by point.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering

  d/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, minimum height=7mm, text width=18mm},
  k/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=blue!10, align=center, minimum height=6mm, text width=19mm},

\begin{table}[!htbp]

\label{tab:meta-vs-hybrid}
