\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Meta-model & Structural hybrid \\
\midrule
Where classical knowledge enters & as separate models whose outputs are inputs & as the shape of the network itself \\
What the learner reads & the classical models' outputs (and possibly market state) & the raw events or book \\
How it is trained & in two stages: classical models first, then the learner & as one model, end to end \\
What is learned & how to weight and combine the classical outputs & the parts of the classical model that were fixed formulas, such as a kernel or a rate function \\
What stays interpretable & each classical model, unchanged & the overall form, e.g.\ ``events excite events'' \\
Main risk & leakage between stages; too many inputs & a flexible network inside the structure may drift from it \\
Examples & lasso or boosted trees over OFI, imbalance and Hawkes intensities (Section~\ref{sec:meta}) & neural Hawkes processes, deep queue-reactive model, Hawkes-biased attention \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Designs that sit between the two sections.} Two designs are neither clearly a meta-model nor
clearly a structural hybrid; which one they are depends on what they are given and how they are trained. A network that reads the raw book
\emph{and} the classical outputs as extra inputs (``concatenation'', below) is half meta-model, half deep
model: the classical models are still outside, but the network also learns from the raw data. And a
\emph{residual} or \emph{gated} combination of a classical forecast and a network forecast (below) is a
combination in the sense of Section~\ref{sec:meta-combine} when the two are trained separately, and a hybrid
when they are trained together so that the network learns only what the classical model misses. We describe both
here because they mix classical and learned parts in one forecast, but the test for each is the same as in
Section~\ref{sec:meta-combine}: does it beat the classical model and the network on their own?

\paragraph{Motivation for building structure in.} A purely learned model must rediscover from noisy
data facts that are already known: that events excite further events, that queues deplete and prices move
when they do, that price impact is roughly linear in order flow over short windows. Supplying this structure
should make a model more sample-efficient, more stable outside its training regime, and easier to interpret.
These are hypotheses; the published evidence for them in LOB data is still limited, and each hybrid should be
tested against its parts.

\subsection{Two ways of building classical structure into a network}
\label{sec:hybrid-ways}

\paragraph{1. A classical skeleton with neural parts.} Keep the classical model's form and replace one of its
fixed formulas by a network. A Hawkes process says that the rate of each event type depends on past events; a
\emph{neural} Hawkes process keeps that form but lets a recurrent network compute the rates from the history,
instead of a sum of fixed kernels (Section~\ref{sec:npp}). A queue-reactive model says that event rates depend
on the state of the queues; a \emph{deep} queue-reactive model keeps that form but computes the rates with
neural networks~\citep{deepqr}. The output is still a point process---with intensities, a likelihood and a
simulator---but more flexible than the classical one.

\paragraph{2. A neural architecture with a classical prior.} Keep the network and bias it towards classical
behaviour: a Hawkes kernel added to the attention scores, so that influence decays with elapsed time as
excitation does; or a state-space model whose memory shortens when event intensity is high. The output is
still a network forecast, but one that cannot ignore what is known about how order flow behaves. The
next three subsections each take apart a published model of the first kind; Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-patterns}
then describes smaller designs, including the second kind.

\subsection{A structural hybrid in detail: the deep queue-reactive model}
\label{sec:hybrid-dqr}

This subsection takes one published structural hybrid apart step by step: the deep queue-reactive model of
\citet{deepqr}, built by two authors at BNP Paribas and Universit\'e Paris~1. We chose it because it starts
from a classical model already covered in this survey---the queue-reactive simulator of
Section~\ref{sec:qr}---so each step shows exactly what is kept from the classical model, what is replaced by a
network, and what that replacement buys. All numbers below are those reported in the paper.

\paragraph{Step 0: the classical queue-reactive model.} In the queue-reactive model, a queue of size
$x$ receives limit orders, cancellations and market orders at rates $r_L(x)$, $r_C(x)$, $r_M(x)$ that depend
\emph{only} on $x$. The waiting time to the next event is exponential with the total rate, and the type of the
event is drawn in proportion to its rate (Figure~\ref{fig:qr-loop}). For a recorded sequence of events
$k=1,\dots,N$, with type $a_k$, queue size $x_k$ just before the event and waiting time $\Delta t_k$ since the
previous event, the likelihood of the rates is
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}_{\text{QR}} = \prod_{k=1}^{N} e^{-R(x_k)\,\Delta t_k}\, r_{a_k}(x_k),
\qquad R(x) = r_L(x) + r_C(x) + r_M(x).
\label{eq:qr-lik}
\end{equation}
\inwords{each factor is the probability that nothing happened for $\Delta t_k$ seconds (the exponential term)
and that what then happened was an event of type $a_k$ (its rate). The best rates make the observed sequence
as likely as possible.}
Because the rates depend only on $x$, the best value of each can be found by counting:
\begin{equation}
\hat r_a(n) = \frac{\text{number of type-}a\text{ events seen at queue size } n}
                   {\text{total time spent at queue size } n}.
\label{eq:qr-count}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the rate of an event at queue size $n$ is how often it happened there, per second spent there.}
For example (illustrative numbers), if a queue spent 2.0 seconds in total at size $n=3$, and during that time
received 6 limit orders, 3 cancellations and 1 market order, then $\hat r_L(3)=3$, $\hat r_C(3)=1.5$ and
$\hat r_M(3)=0.5$ events per second. The whole fitted model is a table: one row per queue size, one column per
event type, filled by counting.

This table is the formula that the hybrid will replace. It has two limits that \citet{deepqr} set out to
remove. First, a rate can depend on nothing but the size of its own queue: not on the last event, not on the
time of day, not on the opposite side of the book. Second, every queue is modelled separately from every other.

\paragraph{Step 1: keep the likelihood, replace the table by a network.} The \emph{deep} queue-reactive model
keeps Equation~\eqref{eq:qr-lik} unchanged, but replaces the queue size $x_k$ by a vector $\mathbf{y}_k$ of
market features, and the table by a neural network with weights $\mathbf{W}$:
\begin{equation}
r_a(x_k) \;\longrightarrow\; r_a^{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{y}_k),
\qquad
\text{loss}(\mathbf{W}) = \sum_{k=1}^{N}\Bigl[R^{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{y}_k)\,\Delta t_k
- \log r^{\mathbf{W}}_{a_k}(\mathbf{y}_k)\Bigr].
\label{eq:dqr-loss}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the loss is minus the logarithm of the likelihood in Equation~\eqref{eq:qr-lik}: it penalises
predicting a high total rate during a long quiet spell (first term), and predicting a low rate for the event
that actually happened (second term). Minimising it is maximum likelihood, exactly as before; only the thing
being fitted has changed, from a table of numbers to the weights of a network.}

Counting no longer works, because no two states $\mathbf{y}_k$ are exactly alike; the network is fitted by
gradient descent instead. The network is a multi-layer perceptron with two hidden layers of 128 and 32 units
and three outputs, one rate per event type; the outputs pass through a ReLU so that rates cannot be negative.
With $\mathbf{y}_k=x_k$ alone, the deep model is a smoothed version of the classical table. The point is what
\emph{else} can now go into $\mathbf{y}_k$. The authors test two additions, one at a time:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{The type of the previous event.} In the data, an event tends to be followed by another of the same
  type: after a cancellation, the next event is a cancellation 73\% of the time; after a limit order, a limit
  order 76\% of the time. The classical model and its size-aware extension cannot represent this, since their
  rates ignore history: their rows of transition probabilities are identical, with a cancellation following a
  cancellation 44\% of the time. With the previous type added to $\mathbf{y}_k$, the deep model gives 66\% and
  74\% (Figure~\ref{fig:dqr-results}a).
\item \textbf{The hour of the day.} With the hour added, the fitted market-order rate varies through the
  trading day, as real activity does; the classical rates are the same at every hour.
\end{itemize}
Adding either feature raised the log-likelihood on validation data and the accuracy of predicting the next
event's type, and adding both gave the best values.

This is the structural part. The network does not decide \emph{how} events are generated: that is still the
queue-reactive mechanism---exponential waiting times, an event type drawn in proportion to its rate, queues
updated by a matching engine. The network decides only \emph{how fast} each event type arrives in a given state.

\paragraph{Step 2: one model for the whole book, with order sizes.} The final model, which the authors call
\emph{multidimensional} (MDQR), makes two further changes. First, instead of one network per queue, a single
network outputs the rate of every event type at every level at once: 3 types $\times$ 10 levels (five per side)
$=30$ rates, all computed from one shared state $\mathbf{y}_k$ that describes the whole book. An event is now a
type $a_k$ \emph{and} a level $\ell_k$, and the likelihood becomes
\begin{equation}
\prod_{k=1}^{N} e^{-R^{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{y}_k)\,\Delta t_k}\; r^{\mathbf{W}}_{a_k,\ell_k}(\mathbf{y}_k),
\qquad
R^{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{y}) = \sum_{a\in\{L,C,M\}}\;\sum_{\ell} r^{\mathbf{W}}_{a,\ell}(\mathbf{y}).
\label{eq:mdqr-lik}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the same form as Equation~\eqref{eq:qr-lik}, but the clock now runs for the whole book: the waiting
time depends on the sum of all 30 rates, and the event picked is a (type, level) pair. Because all rates share
one input, activity at the best bid can change the rates at the best ask.}

Second, the size of each order is modelled too. Over 99.9\% of order sizes in the data are below 200 lots, so the
size is treated as one of 200 classes, and a second network with the same hidden layers outputs a probability
$p^{\mathbf{W}}(v\mid a_k,\ell_k,\mathbf{y}_k)$ for each size $v=1,\dots,200$. It is trained by cross-entropy,
the standard loss for classification:
\begin{equation}
\text{loss}_{\text{size}}(\mathbf{W}) = -\sum_{k=1}^{N}\log p^{\mathbf{W}}(v_k\mid a_k,\ell_k,\mathbf{y}_k),
\label{eq:mdqr-size}
\end{equation}
\inwords{make the size that was actually observed, $v_k$, as probable as possible, given the type, level and
state.}
Because the likelihood splits into a timing-and-type part and a size part, the two networks are trained
separately. Table~\ref{tab:mdqr-spec} lists what goes into the state and how the networks are built.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The state and the two networks of the multidimensional deep queue-reactive model, as reported
by \citet{deepqr}.}
\label{tab:mdqr-spec}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{State $\mathbf{y}_k$}}\\
Queue sizes at 5 levels per side & log-transformed \\
Spread & in ticks \\
Trade imbalance & (bid-side traded volume $-$ ask-side) / (sum), over the last 20\,s, 1\,min, 5\,min and 15\,min \\
Type of the last event at each level & limit, cancel or market; learned 2-number embedding \\
Hour of the day & 9 values; learned 2-number embedding \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{Networks}}\\
Rate network & 25 inputs; hidden layers of 256 and 64 units (tanh); 30 outputs (ReLU); loss Eq.~\eqref{eq:mdqr-lik} \\
Size network & 27 inputs; hidden layers of 256 and 64 units (tanh); 200 outputs (softmax); loss Eq.~\eqref{eq:mdqr-size} \\
Training & Adam optimiser, learning rate cycled between $10^{-5}$ and $10^{-3}$; stop after 10 epochs without validation improvement \\
\midrule
\multicolumn{2}{l}{\textit{Data}}\\
Euro-Bund futures & June 2022 contract, March--June 2022, 9:00--18:00; 5 levels per side \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Market generation by the fitted model.} Figure~\ref{fig:mdqr-loop} shows one simulation step.
It is the loop of Figure~\ref{fig:qr-loop} with the rate table replaced by the rate network and with a size
drawn for each event. The grey boxes are the classical machinery, kept as it was; the orange boxes are learned.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, node distance=4mm and 5mm,
  cl/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=30mm, minimum height=8mm},
  nn/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!18, align=center, text width=30mm, minimum height=8mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.8mm]}, thick}]
\node[cl] (state) {\textbf{State $\mathbf{y}_k$}\\queues, spread, trade imbalance, last events, hour};
\node[nn, right=8mm of state] (rate) {\textbf{Rate network}\\30 rates $r^{\mathbf{W}}_{a,\ell}(\mathbf{y}_k)$};
\node[cl, right=8mm of rate] (wait) {\textbf{Waiting time}\\exponential with rate $R^{\mathbf{W}}$ = sum of the 30};
\node[cl, below=of wait] (pick) {\textbf{Pick (type, level)}\\with probability $r^{\mathbf{W}}_{a,\ell}/R^{\mathbf{W}}$};
\node[nn, left=8mm of pick] (size) {\textbf{Size network}\\probabilities of sizes 1--200; draw one};
\node[cl, left=8mm of size] (match) {\textbf{Matching engine}\\apply the event to the book; update features};
\draw[arr] (state) -- (rate);
\draw[arr] (rate) -- (wait);
\draw[arr] (wait) -- (pick);
\draw[arr] (pick) -- (size);
\draw[arr] (size) -- (match);
\draw[arr] (match) -- (state);
\node[below=1mm of match, font=\tiny, text width=34mm, align=center] {a trading agent's own orders enter here};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One step of the multidimensional deep queue-reactive simulator. Grey: the classical queue-reactive
mechanism, unchanged (Figure~\ref{fig:qr-loop}). Orange: the two parts learned by networks. The paper reports
0.037\,ms per generated event for the networks, excluding the matching engine.}
\label{fig:mdqr-loop}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Comparison of four models on Bund data.} The authors compare four models on the Bund data: the classical
queue-reactive model (QR), their earlier size-aware version (SAQR), and the deep versions.
Figure~\ref{fig:dqr-results} re-plots four of the comparisons.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Links between queues.} In the data, the volumes at the best bid and best ask have correlation
  $-0.54$, and queues on the same side have correlations of about 0.3 to 0.5. The classical model, whose queues
  are independent, gives $-0.22$ and near-zero same-side correlations; the multidimensional model gives
  $-0.54$ and same-side values close to the data (panel b).
\item \textbf{Queue sizes.} Fitting a gamma distribution to the best-ask volume gives a shape of 1.35 in the
  data, 3.08 for QR, 1.91 for SAQR and 1.30 for MDQR (panel c).
\item \textbf{Price impact.} A simulated agent buys the average five-minute volume over five minutes in 10
  equal child orders. With the trade-imbalance features in the state, the simulated price rises in a concave
  curve and then partly relaxes, the shape reported in empirical studies of large orders; without them, it rises
  linearly and stays flat. Varying the size of the order, the peak impact grows as the size to the power 0.55
  ($R^2=0.89$), close to the square-root law.
\item \textbf{Variability.} The classical models reproduce the average numbers of events per five minutes,
  but their spread from one window to the next is much smaller than in the data; MDQR's is closer.
\item \textbf{Direction of the mid-price.} Starting from each observed book, the simulators generate 500
  events, and the direction of the simulated mid is compared with the real one (three classes: up, down, flat).
  Balanced accuracy is 0.54 for DeepLOB trained directly on the task, 0.56 for QR, 0.58 for SAQR and 0.63 for
  MDQR (panel d), against $1/3$ for guessing. Prediction is not what these simulators were built for.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/deepqr_reported.png}
\caption{Results reported by \citet{deepqr} for Euro-Bund futures, re-plotted from the paper's figures and
tables; not our data. (a) Probability that an event is followed by one of the same type: the classical models
cannot represent this. (b) Correlation of best-bid and best-ask volumes. (c) Shape of a gamma distribution
fitted to the best-ask volume. (d) Balanced accuracy of the direction of the mid over the next 500 events;
dashed line: three-class guessing.}
\label{fig:dqr-results}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Classification as a structural hybrid.} Run through
Table~\ref{tab:meta-vs-hybrid}. The classical knowledge enters as the shape of the model---exponential waiting
times, rates that depend on the book, events applied to queues by a matching engine---not as the output of a
separately fitted QR model. The network reads the book's state, not the forecasts of other models. The rate
network is fitted by the same likelihood as the classical model, in one stage. What it learns is exactly the
part that was a fixed table: the rates. And what stays interpretable is the mechanism: one can still ask the
fitted model ``how fast do cancellations arrive at the best ask when the bid queue is short?'' and read off the
answer. A meta-model built from the same ingredients would instead fit QR, Hawkes and OFI models separately and
train a learner on their predictions---and it would forecast, but it could not be run forward as a simulator.

\paragraph{Limitations.} The authors state that the model has been tested on one market, the
Euro-Bund future, a liquid large-tick contract, and not yet on small-tick assets. The state is designed by hand,
and they report that it took several attempts before the trade-imbalance features that produce the realistic
impact curve were added; the network learns the rates, not which inputs matter. The impact relaxation they
obtain cannot be checked against real large orders, which they did not have.

\subsection{A prediction example: the state-dependent parallel neural Hawkes process}
\label{sec:hybrid-pnhp}

The deep queue-reactive model was built to simulate. The model of \citet{shi2022sdpnhp}, from the University
of Bristol, is the published structural hybrid we found that is evaluated most fully as a \emph{predictor}:
given the recent events in the book, it forecasts what the next event will be and when it will happen, and it
is compared both with the classical model it is built from and with a plain network. Its target is the next
\emph{event}, not the next price move; we found no published structural hybrid evaluated on price direction. The paper also uses the model as a simulator, which we do not cover here.

\paragraph{Step 0: the classical model.} Every event is put into one of four types: an order submitted on the
bid side, a cancellation on the bid side, a submission on the ask side, a cancellation on the ask side. The
book's state is summarised by one number, the queue imbalance at the best quotes,
$\mathrm{QI} = (v^b - v^a)/(v^b + v^a)$ (Section~\ref{sec:ofi}), cut into three levels: bid-heavy, balanced,
ask-heavy. The classical model is the state-dependent Hawkes process of \citet{morariupatrichi2022}
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-sd}): each type has an intensity that jumps after events and decays exponentially,
\begin{equation}
\lambda_k(t) = \mu_k + \sum_{t_h < t} \alpha_{k,h}\, e^{-\beta_k (t - t_h)},
\label{eq:shi-hawkes}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the rate of type-$k$ events is a background rate $\mu_k$ plus a bump for each past event $h$, which
occurred at time $t_h$. The bump starts at size $\alpha_{k,h}$ and shrinks exponentially at the decay rate
$\beta_k$. In the state-dependent model the size $\alpha_{k,h}$ is not one fixed number: it is chosen by the type
of event $h$, the type $k$ being excited, and the state of the book (the imbalance level) just after event $h$.}
After each event the state moves to a new level with probabilities that depend on the event's type.

\paragraph{Step 1: keep the intensities, compute them with a network.} The hybrid replaces
Equation~\eqref{eq:shi-hawkes} by the neural Hawkes process of \citet{mei2017} (Section~\ref{sec:npp}), and
changes it in two ways. Recall how a neural Hawkes process works. At each event, a continuous-time LSTM
reads the event and sets, for each of its memory cells, a starting value $c(t_n)$, a target $\bar c$ and a
decay rate $d$. Between events each cell decays from its starting value towards its target,
\begin{equation}
c(t) = \bar c + \bigl(c(t_n) - \bar c\bigr)\, e^{-d\,(t - t_n)}, \qquad t_n \le t < t_{n+1},
\label{eq:ctlstm-decay-shi}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the memory relaxes from where the last event left it towards a target, at a speed that the network
also chooses at each event. This plays the role of a Hawkes bump decaying, except that the start, the end
point and the speed are all computed by the network from the history.}
The cells produce an output $h(t)$, and each intensity is $\lambda_k(t) = \mathrm{softplus}$ of a linear
function of $h(t)$, so it is always positive. The exponential decay of Hawkes is still there, but it acts on
the network's memory rather than directly on the intensity.

The two changes are:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Parallel units.} Instead of one LSTM for all four types, there are four, one per event type, each
  computing its own intensity. A shared linear layer mixes their states at each event, so the types can still
  influence each other. The authors report that this shared layer added about 2 percentage points of
  accuracy.
\item \textbf{State dependence.} The imbalance level is embedded and fed in with each event, and, as in the
  classical model, a learned matrix gives the probability of each new state after each event type.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Step 2: turn intensities into predictions.} From the four intensities, with total
$\Lambda(t) = \sum_k \lambda_k(t)$, the probability density of the next event happening at time $t$ is
\begin{equation}
p(t) = \Lambda(t)\, \exp\!\Bigl(-\int_{t_{n}}^{t} \Lambda(s)\,ds\Bigr),
\label{eq:shi-density}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the chance that the next event happens at $t$ is the rate at $t$ times the chance that nothing has
happened before $t$.}
The predicted time is the average of this density. The predicted type is the type with the largest share
$\lambda_k(t)/\Lambda(t)$ at the time the event actually happens, if that time is known; or the type with the
largest share averaged over the density \eqref{eq:shi-density}, if it is not.

\paragraph{Training.} The network reads windows of 49 events and their states and is trained to make the
50th as likely as possible: the loss is the point-process likelihood of Section~\ref{sec:pp-mle} plus the
likelihood of the observed state changes. A cross-entropy term on the next type can be added; for this model
the authors found it did not help, and the headline results are without it. Every layer is small: two layers
of 16 units in each LSTM's internal layers, two layers of 16 for the embeddings, one of 16 for the intensities.
Training uses five days of LOBSTER data for each of three stocks (INTC, MSFT, JPM), about half a million
events a day, giving about 4 million windows. Figure~\ref{fig:shi-arch} shows the architecture.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, node distance=3mm and 6mm,
  cl/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=26mm, minimum height=8mm},
  nn/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!18, align=center, text width=30mm, minimum height=5mm},
  sh/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!8, align=center, text width=6mm, minimum height=27mm},
  ou/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=green!12, align=center, text width=33mm, minimum height=7mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\node[cl] (in) at (0,0) {\textbf{Last 49 events}\\type (1 of 4), time,\\imbalance level};
\node[sh] (mix) at (2.4,0) {\rotatebox{90}{shared layer}};
\node[nn] (u0) at (4.7,1.05) {unit: bid submission};
\node[nn] (u1) at (4.7,0.35) {unit: bid cancel};
\node[nn] (u2) at (4.7,-0.35) {unit: ask submission};
\node[nn] (u3) at (4.7,-1.05) {unit: ask cancel};
\node[cl, text width=30mm] (lam) at (8.3,0) {\textbf{4 intensities} $\lambda_k(t)$,\\each decaying between events};
\node[ou] (t) at (12.0,0.6) {\textbf{when}: average of $p(t)$};
\node[ou] (k) at (12.0,-0.6) {\textbf{which type}: largest $\lambda_k/\Lambda$};
\draw[arr] (in) -- (mix);
\foreach \u in {u0,u1,u2,u3} {\draw[arr] (mix.east |- \u) -- (\u.west); \draw[arr] (\u.east) -- (lam.west);}
\draw[arr] (lam.east) -- (t.west); \draw[arr] (lam.east) -- (k.west);
\node[font=\tiny, align=center] at (4.7,1.6) {continuous-time LSTM units (16 wide)};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The state-dependent parallel neural Hawkes process of \citet{shi2022sdpnhp}, as used for
prediction. Grey: inputs and the Hawkes-type intensities. Orange: the learned parts. Green: the two
predictions. Schematic; the learned matrix of state transitions is not drawn.}
\label{fig:shi-arch}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The model ladder and its results.} The paper compares a ladder of models in which one
ingredient changes at a time (Table~\ref{tab:shi-ladder}, Figure~\ref{fig:shi-results}). All models receive
the same events and states.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The comparison of \citet{shi2022sdpnhp}, read as a ladder: what each model has, and its accuracy
in predicting the type of the next event (\%, time of the event given). Guessing among four types: 25\%.}
\label{tab:shi-ladder}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}Xrrr}
\toprule
Model & What it has & MSFT & INTC & JPM \\
\midrule
Hawkes & classical state-dependent Hawkes, exponential kernels; no network & 42.67 & 40.10 & 48.20 \\
LSTM & plain network; no intensities, so no time prediction or likelihood & 47.64 & 46.04 & 58.05 \\
CT-LSTM & neural Hawkes, one unit for all types & 46.81 & 44.22 & 59.62 \\
PCT-LSTM, no state & one unit per type; state not used & 48.79 & 48.16 & 60.43 \\
PCT-LSTM & one unit per type, with state & 49.66 & 48.38 & 60.97 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Against the classical model.} The full hybrid beats the state-dependent Hawkes model on every
  measure and every stock: for example 49.7\% against 42.7\% accuracy on MSFT, and a lower negative
  log-likelihood per event ($-0.87$ against $0.67$) and smaller timing error.
\item \textbf{Against a plain network.} It beats the plain LSTM, which has the same layer sizes, by 2.0 to 2.9
  percentage points of accuracy. The LSTM cannot be compared on timing or likelihood, because it outputs only a
  type.
\item \textbf{One unit or four.} A single neural Hawkes unit is \emph{less} accurate than the plain LSTM on MSFT
  and INTC; splitting it into one unit per type moves the hybrid ahead. The Hawkes-style structure alone beat
  the plain network on only one of the three stocks; the per-type design beat it on all three.
\item \textbf{The state.} Removing the imbalance state costs 0.2 to 0.9 points of accuracy.
\item \textbf{What the model learned about the state.} The fitted transition matrix says that the imbalance
  level stays the same after more than 90\% of events, and that its changes depend on the event's side: after
  a bid submission, an ask-heavy book becomes balanced with probability 0.07 and a bid-heavy book with
  probability 0.02; after an ask submission, the other way round.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/shi_reported.png}
\caption{Results reported by \citet{shi2022sdpnhp}, Table~1, re-plotted; not our data. (a) Accuracy of
predicting the type of the next event, with the time of the event given; dashed line: guessing among four
types. (b) Negative log-likelihood per event; the plain LSTM has no likelihood.}
\label{fig:shi-results}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{An independent re-run.} \citet{zheng2024lobdif} re-ran these models with the same layer sizes, five
runs each, on six data sets: two days of MSFT from LOBSTER and four from the Shenzhen and Shanghai exchanges.
The parallel neural Hawkes model beat the plain LSTM in type accuracy on five of the six (for example 0.47
against 0.37 on one MSFT day) and was lower on one (0.38 against 0.41); it had a smaller timing error than the
classical Hawkes model on five of the six and a larger one on the sixth (2.59 against 2.10).

\paragraph{Classification as a structural hybrid.} It is one model, trained on the Hawkes likelihood, whose
intensities follow the Hawkes form---jumps at events, exponential decay between them---with the fixed kernels
replaced by a network. Because the output is a set of intensities, it predicts \emph{when} as well as
\emph{what}, and it can be run forward as a simulator; the plain LSTM can do neither.

\paragraph{Limitations.} The target is the type of the next event among four---submission or
cancellation, bid or ask---not the direction of the price; market orders are not a separate type. Data dates,
split, state cut-offs, parameter counts and spread across runs are not reported, and the independent re-run above
reports a different ordering of the models. The paper gives two accuracies per model; we read the first as the one
with the time of the event given.

\subsection{A third example: a neural Hawkes simulator for market making}
\label{sec:hybrid-nhp}

The third published hybrid we take apart is the model of \citet{lalor2025nhp}, from the University of
Calgary. Its skeleton is a multivariate Hawkes process (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}); a recurrent network
replaces the Hawkes kernels; and the fitted model is then used as a market in which a reinforcement-learning
agent learns to make markets. Like the deep queue-reactive model, it is built mainly to \emph{simulate}. All
numbers below are those reported in the paper unless marked as our computation.

\paragraph{Step 0: the classical skeleton.} Every event in the book is put into one of twelve types, according
to what it is (limit order, market order, cancellation), its side, and whether it moves the mid-price
(Table~\ref{tab:lalor-events}). Six types move the mid; six do not. In the data, the six that move the mid are
rare: for the five stocks studied, they make up between 0.5\% and 16\% of all events.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The twelve event types of \citet{lalor2025nhp}. The superscript is the effect on the mid-price.}
\label{tab:lalor-events}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
 & moves the mid (aggressive) & leaves the mid unchanged \\
\midrule
limit buy & $\text{LB}^{+}$: posts above the best bid & $\text{LB}^{0}$ \\
limit sell & $\text{LS}^{-}$: posts below the best ask & $\text{LS}^{0}$ \\
market buy & $\text{MB}^{+}$: empties at least one ask level & $\text{MB}^{0}$ \\
market sell & $\text{MS}^{-}$: empties at least one bid level & $\text{MS}^{0}$ \\
cancel buy & $\text{BC}^{-}$: empties the best bid & $\text{BC}^{0}$ \\
cancel sell & $\text{SC}^{+}$: empties the best ask & $\text{SC}^{0}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The classical model for these twelve event streams is a nonlinear multivariate Hawkes process: the rate of
type-$i$ events is
\begin{equation}
\lambda_i(t) = \varphi_i\!\Bigl(\bar\lambda_i + \sum_{j=1}^{12}\;\sum_{t_{n'}<t,\ \text{type } j}
\alpha_{ij}\, e^{-\beta_{ij}(t - t_{n'})}\Bigr),
\label{eq:lalor-hawkes}
\end{equation}
\inwords{a background rate $\bar\lambda_i$ plus one decaying bump for every earlier event, whose height
$\alpha_{ij}$ and decay speed $\beta_{ij}$ depend on the pair of types; the function $\varphi_i$ bends the
total, for example to keep it positive.}
The mid-price changes only when an aggressive event happens: up after $\text{LB}^{+}$, $\text{MB}^{+}$ or
$\text{SC}^{+}$, down after $\text{LS}^{-}$, $\text{MS}^{-}$ or $\text{BC}^{-}$, by a number of ticks drawn
from the sizes of mid changes observed in the data for that stock. Equation~\eqref{eq:lalor-hawkes} has
$12\times 12$ pairs of kernel parameters, each with a fixed exponential shape.

\paragraph{Step 1: replace the kernels by a continuous-time LSTM.} The hybrid keeps the idea that each type
has an intensity $\lambda_i(t)$ that reacts to past events, but replaces the sum of exponential bumps by the
neural Hawkes process of \citet{mei2017} (Section~\ref{sec:npp}), in the version of \citet{shi2022sdpnhp} that
gives each event type its own recurrent unit (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-pnhp}). At each event the network reads two inputs: the type of the
event, and a three-valued market state from the queue imbalance at the best quotes (bid-heavy, balanced,
ask-heavy, with cut-offs at $\pm 0.4$). Each memory cell of the network decays between events towards a target, as in Equation~\eqref{eq:ctlstm-decay}.
The output of the unit for type $i$, $h_i(t)$, is turned into an intensity by the softplus:
\begin{equation}
\lambda_i(t) = \ln\!\bigl(1 + e^{h_i(t)}\bigr).
\label{eq:lalor-softplus}
\end{equation}
\inwords{whatever the network computes, the rate is positive. Because $h_i$ can go down as well as up after an
event, one event can make another type \emph{less} likely---the inhibition that
Equation~\eqref{eq:lalor-hawkes} with positive $\alpha_{ij}$ cannot express.}

The network is fitted to one day of free sample data from LOBSTER (21 June 2012; AAPL, AMZN, GOOG, INTC,
MSFT), split 60/20/20 into training, validation and test, for 20 epochs on windows of 100 events, by
maximising the point-process likelihood of Section~\ref{sec:pp-mle}.

\paragraph{Step 2: generate a market.} The fitted intensities drive the same simulation loop as a classical
Hawkes model (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-fit}). The waiting time to the next event is exponential with rate
$\Lambda(t) = \sum_i \lambda_i(t)$; the type is drawn with probability $\lambda_i(t)/\Lambda(t)$; if the type is
aggressive, the mid moves:
\begin{equation}
m_{\text{after}} = m_{\text{before}} + \mathrm{sgn}_i \cdot J^{\text{mid}},
\qquad \mathrm{sgn}_i = \begin{cases} +1 & i \in \{\text{LB}^{+}, \text{MB}^{+}, \text{SC}^{+}\}\\
-1 & i \in \{\text{LS}^{-}, \text{MS}^{-}, \text{BC}^{-}\}\\ \phantom{+}0 & \text{otherwise,}\end{cases}
\label{eq:lalor-mid}
\end{equation}
where $J^{\text{mid}}$ is a jump size drawn from the empirical distribution of mid changes for that stock.
\inwords{the network decides what happens next and when; the event type alone decides the direction of the
price move; the data decide its size. The network has no output for price.}
The new event is fed back to the network, which updates its memory, and the loop repeats.

\paragraph{Step 3: a market-making agent trained in the simulator.} The simulated event stream becomes the environment of a
reinforcement-learning agent. The agent's state is the mid-price and its inventory $Q_t$; at each step it
chooses an action from $\{-1,0,1\}$, which the paper describes as choosing whether to post limit orders at the
best bid and ask, with inventory capped at $\pm 5$. Its quotes can be filled only when a market order arrives
in the event stream, under two rules:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item an aggressive market order ($\text{MB}^{+}$, $\text{MS}^{-}$) always fills the agent's quote on that side.
  The price then moves against the agent, so the paper calls these \emph{adverse} fills;
\item a non-aggressive market order ($\text{MB}^{0}$, $\text{MS}^{0}$) fills it with probability 0.2, a value
  the authors had measured earlier on CME futures. It stands in for queue position: the agent is filled only
  if the market order reaches it in the queue.
\end{itemize}
The agent maximises final wealth minus a penalty on inventory,
\begin{equation}
\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\,Q_T\, m_T + \text{Cash}_T \;-\; \varkappa \int_0^T |Q_t|\,dt\Bigr],
\qquad \varkappa = 0.001,
\label{eq:lalor-reward}
\end{equation}
\inwords{make money, but pay a small cost for every unit of inventory held for every unit of time, so that the
agent prefers to stay close to flat.}
It is trained with the Soft Actor-Critic algorithm on the first 5{,}000 events and tested on the next 2{,}500,
once in the simulated market and once on the real event stream, for each stock.
Figure~\ref{fig:lalor-pipeline} puts the three steps together.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, node distance=4mm and 5mm,
  cl/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=27mm, minimum height=9mm},
  nn/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!18, align=center, text width=27mm, minimum height=9mm},
  ag/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=green!12, align=center, text width=27mm, minimum height=9mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.8mm]}, thick}]
\node[cl] (ev) {\textbf{Last event}\\type (1 of 12) + imbalance state};
\node[nn, right=7mm of ev] (lstm) {\textbf{12 continuous-time LSTM units}\\memory decays between events};
\node[nn, right=7mm of lstm] (lam) {\textbf{12 intensities}\\$\lambda_i(t) = \text{softplus}(h_i(t))$};
\node[cl, below=of lam] (thin) {\textbf{Next event}\\wait $\sim$ Exp($\Lambda$); type $\propto \lambda_i$};
\node[cl, left=7mm of thin] (mid) {\textbf{Mid-price}\\direction from type; size from data};
\node[ag, left=7mm of mid] (mm) {\textbf{Market maker}\\filled by market-order events; learns by SAC};
\draw[arr] (ev) -- (lstm); \draw[arr] (lstm) -- (lam); \draw[arr] (lam) -- (thin);
\draw[arr] (thin) -- (mid); \draw[arr] (mid) -- (mm);
\draw[arr, dashed] (thin.east) -- ++(4mm,0) |- node[pos=0.75, above, font=\tiny]{the new event is fed back to the network} ([yshift=5mm]lstm.north) -- (lstm.north);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The pipeline of \citet{lalor2025nhp}. Grey: the Hawkes skeleton (event types, thinning, mid moves by
event type). Orange: the learned part, which replaces the sum of exponential kernels. Green: the trading agent.
The simulated event stream is generated first; the agent's quotes are filled by its market-order events but
are not part of it.}
\label{fig:lalor-pipeline}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Measured results.} Figure~\ref{fig:lalor-results} re-plots two of them.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Predicting the next event.} On the test data, the type of the next event is predicted correctly
  between 32\% (GOOG) and 51\% (MSFT) of the time, against $1/12$ for guessing (panel a). The paper cites \citet{shi2022sdpnhp}
  for neural Hawkes models beating classical ones.
\item \textbf{The simulated price.} The volatility and excess kurtosis of simulated mid log-returns are close to
  the real ones. The Hurst exponent differs: the real values are 0.19--0.39, the
  simulated values 0.33--0.65 (panel b). The authors conclude that the model matches the
  broad characteristics but not finer details such as trending versus mean-reverting behaviour.
\item \textbf{The mix of events.} The authors note that the simulation has a higher proportion of aggressive
  events than the data.
\item \textbf{The market maker.} In both the simulated and the real tests, the reported final rewards are mostly below zero,
  most fills come from aggressive market orders, and the ratio of adverse to non-adverse fills is similar:
  for example 3.21 real and 3.09 simulated for AAPL, 1.95 and 1.61 for MSFT. The authors write that a strategy
  that only chooses when to quote at the best prices is unlikely to be profitable on its own, and that the
  point of the exercise is to show how the event-based model can be used.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figs/lalor_reported.png}
\caption{Results reported by \citet{lalor2025nhp}, re-plotted. (a) Test accuracy of predicting the type of the next
event, against the 1/12 of guessing among twelve types. (b) Hurst exponent of mid log-returns, real and simulated.}
\label{fig:lalor-results}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Classification as a structural hybrid.} The Hawkes skeleton is kept: twelve intensities, a likelihood
fitted to event times, simulation by drawing the next event in proportion to the intensities, and a price that
moves only through events of particular types. The learned part replaces exactly one formula: the sum of
exponential kernels in Equation~\eqref{eq:lalor-hawkes}. There is no separately fitted Hawkes model whose
output is fed to a learner, as there would be in a meta-model.

\paragraph{Limitations.} Scope: one day of 2012 data; the non-adverse fill probability of 0.2 is taken from
a futures study; market-maker results are given as histograms.

\paragraph{The three examples side by side.} Table~\ref{tab:two-hybrids} summarises what each of the three
hybrids keeps and what it learns.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The three structural hybrids reviewed in this section.}
\label{tab:two-hybrids}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.15\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Deep queue-reactive (\S\ref{sec:hybrid-dqr}) & Parallel neural Hawkes (\S\ref{sec:hybrid-pnhp}) & Neural Hawkes market (\S\ref{sec:hybrid-nhp}) \\
\midrule
Classical skeleton & queue-reactive model (\S\ref{sec:qr}) & state-dependent Hawkes (\S\ref{sec:hawkes-sd}) & multivariate Hawkes (\S\ref{sec:hawkes}) \\
Fixed formula replaced & table of rates by queue size & sum of exponential kernels & sum of exponential kernels \\
What the network reads & hand-built state: queues, spread, trade imbalance, last events, hour & last 49 events, with an imbalance level & event history, with an imbalance level \\
Network & feed-forward, 2 hidden layers & 4 continuous-time LSTM units & 12 continuous-time LSTM units \\
Event types & limit, cancel, market $\times$ 10 levels & 4: submit or cancel, bid or ask & 12: by kind, side and effect on the mid \\
Data & Euro-Bund futures, 3 months & 3 US stocks, 5 days each & 5 US stocks, 1 day \\
Main use & realistic simulation & predicting the next event & environment for a market-making agent \\
Compared with the classical model & yes & yes & no \\
Compared with a plain network & on price direction only (DeepLOB) & yes (LSTM) & no \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}

\subsection{Smaller designs for combining classical structure with a network}
\label{sec:hybrid-patterns}

The three models reviewed above are all of the first kind in Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-ways}: a whole classical
model is kept, and one of its formulas is replaced by a network. This subsection describes four smaller
designs, each of which can be added to an ordinary network rather than built around a classical model.
Two of them sit between this section and Section~\ref{sec:meta}: whether they count as a meta-model or a
structural hybrid depends on how they are trained. The other two are of the second kind---a classical prior
built into a neural architecture---and, as far as we found, none has been published for order-book data;
they are included because they show concretely what ``building structure in'' can mean.
Table~\ref{tab:hybrid-patterns} summarises them; each has a diagram below.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The four designs of Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-patterns}.}
\label{tab:hybrid-patterns}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\linewidth}}
\toprule
Design & What the classical part does & Status for order-book data \\
\midrule
Concatenation & classical outputs (OFI, imbalance, Hawkes intensity) are extra inputs & published examples (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-other}); between Sections~\ref{sec:meta} and~\ref{sec:hybrid} \\
Hawkes-biased attention & a Hawkes kernel scales attention weights by elapsed time & our proposal \\
Intensity-conditioned memory & event intensity sets how fast a state-space model forgets & our proposal; none found \\
Gating and residuals & a classical forecast is mixed with, or corrected by, a network forecast & between forecast combination and a hybrid \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}


\paragraph{Concatenation (between a meta-model and a hybrid).} The simplest design appends the classical outputs of
Table~\ref{tab:meta-inputs} to the network's raw input, or projects them into the same embedding space as the
learned features. With only those outputs as input it becomes the meta-model of Section~\ref{sec:meta}; with
the raw input as well, it tests whether the classical outputs add anything the network could not learn
itself (Example~\ref{ex:right-ablation}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, node distance=3mm,
  d/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=24mm, minimum height=7mm},
  k/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=blue!10, align=center, text width=24mm, minimum height=7mm},
  l/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!15, align=center, text width=13mm, minimum height=14mm},
  o/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=green!12, align=center, text width=11mm, minimum height=6mm},
  j/.style={draw, circle, inner sep=1pt, fill=white},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\begin{scope}
  \node[d] (raw) at (0,0.65) {raw book (prices, sizes)};
  \node[k] (cls) at (0,-0.65) {OFI, imbalance, Hawkes intensity};
  \node[j] (cat) at (2.0,0) {$\oplus$};
  \node[l] (net) at (3.3,0) {network};
  \node[o] (f) at (4.85,0) {forecast};
  \draw[arr] (raw.east) -- (cat); \draw[arr] (cls.east) -- (cat); \draw[arr] (cat) -- (net); \draw[arr] (net) -- (f);
  \node[align=center] at (2.2,-1.7) {\textbf{(a) concatenation}:\\the network sees both};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=8.0cm]
  \node[d, draw=gray, text=gray, fill=gray!4] (raw2) at (0,0.65) {raw book (not used)};
  \node[k] (cls2) at (0,-0.65) {OFI, imbalance, Hawkes intensity};
  \node[l] (net2) at (2.4,0) {learner};
  \node[o] (f2) at (3.95,0) {forecast};
  \draw[arr] (cls2.east) -- (net2); \draw[arr] (net2) -- (f2);
  \node[align=center] at (2.0,-1.7) {\textbf{(b) classical outputs only}:\\the meta-model of Section~\ref{sec:meta}};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Concatenation. (a) The classical outputs ($\oplus$: placed side by side) are extra inputs next to
the raw book. (b) Remove the raw book and the same design is a meta-model. Comparing (a) with a network on the
raw book alone is the ablation of Example~\ref{ex:right-ablation}. Schematic.}
\label{fig:mech-concat}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Testing the benefit of concatenation.} Adding classical outputs to a network's input can only
help if they tell the network something it could not work out from the raw input itself. A Hawkes intensity,
for instance, is computed from the same events the network already reads; a large enough network could in
principle compute it, or something as useful, on its own. The test is to train the same network twice, once
without and once with the classical outputs, and compare the results.
\begin{example}[The right ablation]
\label{ex:right-ablation}
Let $S_t$ be a Hawkes intensity computed from the same events already supplied to a neural model $M_0(X_t)$.
Compare $M_0(X_t)$ with $M_1(X_t, S_t)$, using identical data splits, random seeds and economic evaluation, and
report the difference in value $\Delta V$ (Section~\ref{sec:notation}) with a confidence interval. We expect,
but do not test here, that $\Delta V \approx 0$ when $S_t$ is a fixed function of $X_t$ and the network sees
enough history to compute it. Concatenation earns its place when $S_t$ brings information the network cannot
reconstruct, or when it lets a much smaller network match a larger one.
\end{example}



\paragraph{Hawkes-biased attention.} Attention, the building block of Transformer networks (described
fully in Section~\ref{sec:deep-attn}), computes, for each event $n$, a weighted average over earlier events $n'$,
with weights proportional to the exponential of a score. Each event is represented by learned vectors; the
score compares a ``query'' vector of the current event with a ``key'' vector of each earlier one. A Hawkes kernel can be
built into that score:
\begin{equation}
  \text{score}(n, n') = \frac{\langle \mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_n, \mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_{n'} \rangle}{\sqrt{d_k}}
  + \ln \phi\big(t_n - t_{n'}\big), \qquad t_{n'} \le t_n ,
\end{equation}
where $\mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_n$ and $\mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_{n'}$ are the query and key vectors of the
two events and $d_k$ their length, the key dimension (Section~\ref{sec:deep-attn} explains them with an
order-book example).
\inwords{because the softmax exponentiates the score, adding $\ln\phi$ \emph{multiplies} each
attention weight by the Hawkes kernel: recent events get more weight, and the weight decays with
elapsed time exactly as excitation does in a Hawkes process. With an exponential kernel
$\phi(u) = \alpha \mathrm{e}^{-\omega u}$ the added term is $\ln\alpha - \omega\,(t_n - t_{n'})$, a penalty linear
in elapsed time.} The network learns what to attend to; the kernel imposes how quickly influence
fades. Transformer Hawkes processes~\citep{zuo2020thp,zhang2020sahp} encode time differently, through
learned temporal encodings; the explicit kernel bias above is a design we propose for testing rather
than a published LOB result. The closest published model we found, by \citet{hwang2025shockbiased}, adds to
the attention scores of a Transformer Hawkes process a bias whose size depends on the event's type and decays
exponentially with elapsed time, and reports results on financial event prediction. In an implementation, the only change to standard
attention is that the term $\ln\alpha - \omega\,(t_n - t_{n'})$ is added to each score before the softmax, and scores for
later events are set to $-\infty$ so that they receive no weight.
The added cost over standard attention is one subtraction per pair of events.

Figure~\ref{fig:mech-attn} shows the effect on a single event. In panel~(1), standard attention weights earlier
events only by how well their content matches the current one, so a strong match long ago can receive the
largest weight. Panel~(2) is the kernel. In panel~(3) the two are combined: old events are discounted, and
among recent events the content match still decides which ones count most.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\linewidth]{figs/mech_hawkes_attn.png}
\caption{Hawkes-biased attention for one current event (dotted line) and eight earlier events.
(1) Weights from content alone. (2) Exponential kernel, $\omega = 1.2$ per second. (3) Weights after the log
kernel is added to the scores: each content weight is multiplied by the kernel and the result is
renormalised to sum to one. Stylised; the content scores are invented for illustration.}
\label{fig:mech-attn}
\end{figure}


\paragraph{Intensity-conditioned state-space models.} Selective state-space models such as
Mamba~\citep{gu2024mamba} let the step size of their recurrence depend on the input, so that the model
decides how quickly to forget. Making that step depend on the current event intensity or OFI would let
the model's memory shorten when the market is busy and lengthen when it is quiet---the same adaptation
that event-time sampling achieves by construction. We know of no published LOB model of this kind.

Figure~\ref{fig:mech-ssm} illustrates the idea with memory that fades by a fixed amount per \emph{event} rather
than per second. Seen from a moment inside a burst, the model looks back only over the last fraction of a
second, because many events have happened there; seen from a quiet moment, it looks back several seconds,
but still gives little weight to anything before the burst, because many events separate it from the present.
A model with a fixed memory in seconds (dashed) does neither.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\linewidth]{figs/mech_ssm_memory.png}
\caption{Memory scaled by event intensity versus fixed memory. (1) An event stream with a two-second burst
(shaded below). (2) Weight that the model's state gives to past inputs, seen from inside the burst ($t=6.8$\,s)
and from a quiet period ($t=11.5$\,s). Solid: memory fades by a fixed factor per expected event,
$e^{-\frac14 \times (\text{expected events since then})}$. Dashed: memory fades with a fixed time constant of
2\,s. Stylised; not data. This is the proposed design, not a published model.}
\label{fig:mech-ssm}
\end{figure}


\paragraph{Residual gating (between forecast combination and a hybrid).} A statistical model produces a baseline forecast $\widehat{Y}^{\text{stat}}_t$,
a network produces $\widehat{Y}^{\text{deep}}_t$, and a gate $\nu_t \in [0,1]$---learned, or driven by
volatility or intensity---mixes them:
\begin{equation}
  \widehat{Y}_t = \nu_t\, \widehat{Y}^{\text{stat}}_t + (1 - \nu_t)\, \widehat{Y}^{\text{deep}}_t .
\end{equation}
\inwords{when the gate is near 1 the forecast falls back on the simple, well-understood model; the
network contributes only where it has earned trust. Setting $\nu_t$ by volatility gives a natural safety
layer: in unusual conditions, defer to the model with fewer parameters.} A related form trains the
network on the residuals of the statistical model, so that it learns only what the simple model misses.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering

  d/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!12, align=center, text width=13mm, minimum height=7mm},

  l/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!15, align=center, text width=17mm, minimum height=7mm},
  g/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=yellow!20, align=center, text width=22mm, minimum height=7mm},
  o/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=green!12, align=center, text width=11mm, minimum height=6mm},
  j/.style={draw, circle, inner sep=1pt, fill=white},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\begin{scope}

  \node[k] (st) at (2.1,0.8) {statistical model\\$\widehat{Y}^{\text{stat}}_t$};

  \node[g] (gt) at (2.1,-2.0) {gate $\nu_t$\\(e.g.\ volatility)};
  \node[j, inner sep=2pt] (mx) at (4.6,0) {mix};

  \draw[arr] (st.east) -- (mx); \draw[arr] (dp.east) -- (mx); \draw[arr] (gt.east) -| (mx);

  \node[o] (f2) at (5.5,0) {$\widehat{Y}_t$};

network is trained on the error of the statistical model, and its output is added back. Trained separately,

\subsection{Other published hybrids and gaps in the literature}
\label{sec:hybrid-other}

Sections~\ref{sec:hybrid-dqr}--\ref{sec:hybrid-nhp} reviewed in full the three structural hybrids for order-book
data that report results on real data against at least some baselines. This subsection
lists the other related work our search turned up, so that a reader can follow it up.

\paragraph{Other hybrids.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \citet{gyotoku2025deeppp} (preprint) take a classical model of the \emph{ratios} between
  event intensities and let deep networks compute its factors, trained by the model's own likelihood. On
  simulated data, imposing the model's multiplicative structure gives better estimates than not imposing it.
  On real data (TotalEnergies on Euronext Paris, January 2017) the paper reports in-sample fit; out-of-sample
  tests are not reported.
\item \citet{chung2024nmhp} propose a neural marked Hawkes process for the
  order book.
\item UQ-LOB~\citep{manoharan2026uqlob} is a hybrid in a different sense: it attaches a probabilistic,
  attention-based neural-process layer to a pretrained encoder.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Designs outside the structural hybrids.} Three designs come near. \citet{fabre2025spoof} compute
Hawkes-style features with \emph{fixed} decay rates and feed them to a network---concatenation, in the terms of
Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-patterns}. \citet{kolm2023} feed order-flow imbalances to standard networks.
\citet{lee2023rnnhawkes} train a recurrent network to output the parameters of a Hawkes model, but for
estimating those parameters quickly, not for forecasting.

\paragraph{Designs not found in the literature.} We found no published model that builds order-flow imbalance, queue
dynamics, the micro-price or a Kyle-type price-impact rule \emph{inside} an end-to-end network to predict the
order-book price. Our search covered arXiv, OpenAlex, Crossref and Semantic Scholar, but not Google Scholar or
SSRN full texts, so this is the result of a limited search,
not evidence that no such model exists.

\begin{modelcard}{Structural hybrids}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{One model, trained end to end: the classical part's parameters (for example a
Hawkes decay) together with the network that replaces one of its fixed formulas (a rate function, a kernel),
or the network together with the classical prior built into it.}
\cardrow{How}{Maximum likelihood for hybrids that remain point processes; the usual supervised training for
networks with a classical prior. Always alongside the ablation below, and against the classical model and the
plain network on their own.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Simulate and forecast order flow with the structure of a classical model and
the flexibility of a network---for example a queue-reactive simulator whose rates depend on many state
variables.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A classical model with one or two of its fixed formulas swapped for learned ones;
the rest of the machine is unchanged.}
\end{modelcard}


% ================================================================
\section{Deep learning for limit order books}
\label{sec:deep}
% ================================================================

Deep learning entered LOB research around 2017 and now dominates the volume of published work. This
section first explains what a deep LOB model is (Section~\ref{sec:deep-what}), what it takes in and predicts
(Section~\ref{sec:deep-io}), the families of network used and what each assumes (Section~\ref{sec:deep-families}),
and how a model is trained and tested, step by step (Section~\ref{sec:deep-train}). It then reviews each family in
the literature, what the large benchmark studies found when the models were re-tested, and which design choices
matter, and closes with what deep learning adds to the classical models. Short definitions of the terms are in the
glossary (Appendix~\ref{app:glossary}); textbook treatments are in~\citet{goodfellow2016}.

\subsection{Definition and design choices of a deep LOB model}
\label{sec:deep-what}

\paragraph{Definition.} A deep LOB model is a neural network $f_{\mathbf{W}}$, with weights
$\mathbf{W}$, that takes a description of the recent book and returns a prediction:
\begin{equation}
\hat y_t = f_{\mathbf{W}}(X_t),
\label{eq:deep-f}
\end{equation}
\inwords{$X_t$ is what the model is shown at time $t$---for example the prices and sizes at the first ten levels
over the last 100 events---and $\hat y_t$ is what it predicts, for example the probabilities that the mid-price---the average of the best bid and the best ask---will
be lower, about the same or higher a few events later. The network is a long chain of simple operations (weighted
sums followed by simple non-linear functions), and the weights $\mathbf{W}$, often tens of thousands of numbers or
more, are what training sets.}
The OFI regression of Section~\ref{sec:ofi}, $\Delta m = \beta \cdot \OFI$, is also a function from the recent book to
a prediction. There the modeller writes down the formula and fitting estimates a single number, $\beta$. For a deep
model the modeller chooses only the inputs, the network architecture and the loss; training estimates all the weights,
and the weights determine the shape of the function (Section~\ref{sec:deep-adds}).

\paragraph{Labels.} Training needs examples with known answers. For each time $t$ in the historical data, the
\emph{label} $y_t$ is the correct answer for that example, computed from what actually happened after $t$. For
direction prediction it is one of three classes: \emph{down}, \emph{stationary} or \emph{up}. Example: if the
mid-price 20 events after $t$ is above the mid-price at $t$ by more than a threshold, $y_t = \text{up}$. Training
adjusts $\mathbf{W}$ so that $\hat y_t$ agrees with $y_t$ on past data. Labels exist only for historical data;
at prediction time the future is unknown. The rule that turns future prices into a label is a choice, and it
changes results: Step~3 of Section~\ref{sec:deep-train} gives the formula, and Section~\ref{sec:deep-real} shows a
label rule that leaks the past into the answer.

\paragraph{Scores.} A trained model is scored on test data it has not seen. \emph{Accuracy} is the share of test
examples whose predicted class equals the label. Accuracy can mislead when one class is common: if 80\% of labels
are stationary, always predicting stationary scores 80\%. The \emph{F1 score} of one class, say up, combines two
shares: of the examples predicted up, the share that were up (precision), and of the examples that were up, the
share predicted up (recall). F1 is their harmonic mean, $2PR/(P+R)$, and is high only if both are high.
\emph{Macro-F1} is the plain average of the F1 scores of the three classes, so that each class counts equally
however rare it is. Always predicting stationary gets macro-F1 of about 0.30 in that 80\% case (F1 of 0.89 for
stationary, 0 for up and down). Scores are reported as percentages (70\%) or fractions (0.70). Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}
shows how these scores can be inflated by the label and by the data split.

\paragraph{Design choices: input, target, network and training.} Every model in this section is defined by four
choices. Most papers differ from one another in only one or two of them, and the re-tests that taught the field
the most changed exactly one while holding the others fixed. Each decision is described below with its main options,
the choice made by DeepLOB, the most widely used reference model, and what the evidence says about how much the
decision matters.

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=4pt]
\item \textbf{Input.} The input can be \emph{snapshots} of the book (the price and size at the first
  few levels on each side, at each of the last few dozen or hundred events), \emph{order flow} (the changes in size at
  each level between snapshots), or the individual \emph{messages}---each new order, cancellation and trade---as a
  sequence. A second choice is the window: how many events, or how many seconds, of history. DeepLOB sees the last 100
  snapshots of ten levels, a $100 \times 40$ matrix~\citep{zhang2019}. This decision matters a great deal: holding the
  DeepLOB network fixed and changing only the input, order-flow inputs put the model among the statistically best in
  89\% of stock--horizon cases, against 11\% for the raw snapshots~\citep{lucchese2024}. Section~\ref{sec:deep-io}
  compares the three inputs and the classical models that read the same information.
\item \textbf{Target.} Most papers predict the \emph{direction} of the mid-price after a horizon
  of $H$ events---down, unchanged or up---which makes the task a three-class classification. Others predict a
  \emph{return} (a number rather than a class), the type and time of the \emph{next event}, the \emph{time until a
  resting order is filled}, or the whole \emph{future book}. Within direction prediction, the exact label matters: how
  the future price is averaged, from what reference it is measured, and the threshold below which a move counts as
  ``unchanged''. DeepLOB predicts the direction at $H = 20$, 50 and 100 events with the \emph{smoothed label}, which
  compares an average of future mid-prices with an average of past ones (Step~3 of Section~\ref{sec:deep-train}). Changing only the label shows how much this matters: with that label DeepLOB
  is right 65.9\% of the time on a year of Apple data, but with a label measured from the current price it is right
  54.6\%~\citep{lee2024predictability}. Section~\ref{sec:deep-io} lists the targets and Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}
  explains the difference.
\item \textbf{Network.} The architecture decides which patterns the network finds easy to learn from
  limited data: a convolutional network looks for the same small pattern everywhere, a recurrent network reads the
  events in order with a memory, an attention network weighs the past by its content. DeepLOB combines convolutions,
  which read the book's structure, with an LSTM, which reads the sequence. Of the four choices this is the one most
  papers vary, and it has so far mattered least: a plain multilayer perceptron matched a CNN--LSTM on the same
  inputs~\citep{briola2020}, Transformers have not beaten the DeepLOB family in head-to-head
  comparisons~\citep{bilokon2023transformers,zhong2025lobench}, and adding attention to the best bilinear network gained
  0.4 of a point~\citep{shabani2022mtabl}. Section~\ref{sec:deep-families} describes the families.
\item \textbf{Training and testing.} This covers how the inputs are normalised, how the labels are built, how
  the data are split into training, validation and test periods, which loss is minimised, when training stops, and how
  the result is scored. DeepLOB normalises each day with the statistics of the previous five days, trains on six months,
  tunes on three and tests on the last three, minimises cross-entropy, and stops when validation accuracy stops
  improving~\citep{zhang2019}. These choices move results by as much as the network does or more: learned input
  normalisation added seven to nine points of macro-F1 over plain standardisation~\citep{september2024edain}, and on the
  FI-2010 data set the choice of split alone separates published scores of about 0.51--0.59 from about 0.76--0.88.
  Section~\ref{sec:deep-train} walks through the steps, and Section~\ref{sec:deep-real} shows what goes wrong when
  they are done badly.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  h/.style={draw,fill=gray!15,minimum width=31mm,minimum height=6mm,font=\scriptsize\bfseries},

\foreach \c/\t in {0/1. Input,1/2. Target,2/3. Network,3/4. Training and testing} \node[h] at (3.6*\c,0) {\t};

\foreach \r/\t/\s in {1/fully connected (MLP)/o,2/CNN + LSTM/d,3/Transformer/o,4/state-space model/o} \node[\s] (c\r) at (7.2,-0.6*\r-0.1) {\t};
\foreach \r/\t/\s in {1/normalisation/d,2/label construction/d,3/time split/d,4/loss/d,5/scoring/d} \node[\s] (e\r) at (10.8,-0.6*\r-0.1) {\t};

\subsection{Inputs and outputs of a deep LOB model}
\label{sec:deep-io}

This subsection covers what a deep LOB model is given and what it is asked to predict, in five parts: the three
input formats (\ref{sec:deep-io-formats}); the classical models that use the same information as each format
(\ref{sec:deep-io-classical}); a measured comparison of the formats (\ref{sec:deep-io-effect}); the prediction
targets (\ref{sec:deep-io-targets}); and the FI-2010 data set on which many results are reported
(\ref{sec:deep-io-fi2010}).

\subsubsection{The three input formats}
\label{sec:deep-io-formats}

Figure~\ref{fig:deep-inputs} shows the three formats side by side.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize]
\begin{scope}
  \foreach \i in {0,...,7} \foreach \j in {0,...,5} {\pgfmathsetmacro{\g}{20+mod(7*\i+11*\j,45)} \fill[blue!\g] (0.32*\i,0.32*\j) rectangle ++(0.3,0.3);}
  \node[align=center] at (1.28,-0.85) {\textbf{snapshots}\\time $\times$ (price, size)\\at each level};
  \draw[->] (0,-0.12) -- (2.6,-0.12) node[right]{time};
  \draw[->] (-0.12,0) -- (-0.12,2.0) node[above]{levels};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=4.2cm]
  \foreach \i in {0,...,7} \foreach \j in {0,...,5} {\pgfmathsetmacro{\g}{10+mod(13*\i+5*\j,60)} \fill[orange!\g] (0.32*\i,0.32*\j) rectangle ++(0.3,0.3);}
  \node[align=center] at (1.28,-0.85) {\textbf{order flow}\\time $\times$ change in size\\at each level};
  \draw[->] (0,-0.12) -- (2.6,-0.12) node[right]{time};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=8.4cm]
  \foreach \i/\t in {0/add,1/cancel,2/trade,3/add,4/add,5/trade,6/cancel} {\draw[fill=green!15] (0.42*\i,0.6) rectangle ++(0.38,0.7); \node[rotate=90] at (0.42*\i+0.19,0.95) {\t};}
  \node[align=center] at (1.45,-0.85) {\textbf{messages}\\one token (or vector)\\per event};
  \draw[->] (0,0.45) -- (3.0,0.45) node[right]{events};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Three ways of presenting the book to a network: a window of snapshots of the first levels; the
same window transformed into order flow (changes in size at each level); or the sequence of individual
messages. Schematic.}
\label{fig:deep-inputs}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Snapshots.} After each event, the prices and sizes at the first ten levels on each side of the book.
The model receives the last $N$ snapshots as a grid of time by level; DeepLOB uses $N = 100$
(Figure~\ref{fig:snapshot-window}).

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize]
% event stream
\draw[->] (0,0) -- (11.2,0) node[right]{events};
\foreach \x in {0.4,0.8,...,10.4} \fill (\x,0) circle (1.2pt);
\fill[gray] (10.8,0) circle (1.6pt);
\node[below] at (2.0,-0.05) {$t{-}99$};
\node[below] at (10.0,-0.05) {$t{-}1$};
\node[below] at (10.4,-0.05) {$t$};
\node[below,gray] at (10.8,-0.05) {$t{+}1$};
\draw[decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (1.9,0.15) -- (10.5,0.15) node[midway,above=4pt]{$\mathbf{X}_t$: book after each of the last 100 events};
\draw[gray,decorate,decoration={brace,amplitude=4pt}] (2.3,0.75) -- (10.9,0.75) node[midway,above=4pt]{$\mathbf{X}_{t+1}$: drop row $t{-}99$, append row $t{+}1$};

}
\node at (1.84,-0.95) {$\vdots$};
\foreach \r in {0,1,3,4} {\pgfmathsetmacro{\y}{-0.4*\r}
  \node at (3.1,\y-0.17) {$\cdots$};
  \foreach \c in {0,...,3} {\fill[blue!20] (3.5+0.42*\c,\y) rectangle ++(0.4,-0.34);}}
\node at (1.84,0.25) {level 1};
\node at (4.34,0.25) {level 10};
\node at (2.9,-2.25) {$100 \times 40$: each row $= (p^a_\ell, v^a_\ell, p^b_\ell, v^b_\ell)$, $\ell = 1,\dots,10$};
\draw[->,thick] (5.4,-0.9) -- (6.4,-0.9);
\node[draw,minimum width=1.4cm,minimum height=0.8cm] (f) at (7.2,-0.9) {network $f$};

\caption{How a snapshot model looks back. After each event the first ten levels of the book are written as one
row of 40 numbers. The input $\mathbf{X}_t$ stacks the rows for the last 100 events. One forecast is made per event;
at the next event the window moves by one row. Schematic.}
\label{fig:snapshot-window}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Order flow.} The change in size at each level between consecutive snapshots, as in multi-level OFI
(Section~\ref{sec:ofi}). A variant places volumes on a fixed price grid around the mid instead of on levels.

\paragraph{Messages.} The individual exchange messages---new orders, cancellations, trades---one per event, as a
sequence (Sections~\ref{sec:events} and~\ref{sec:deep-tok}).

\paragraph{Information lost between formats.} All three formats are computed from the same stream of exchange
messages (Figure~\ref{fig:input-chain}). Each step down the list below discards information.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Messages}: the raw events. Example: ``cancel 5 lots at the best ask, at 09:30:01.000123''.
\item \textbf{Order flow}: for each level, the net change in size over one step. Example: ``best ask $-5$''.
  Lost: the event type (a 5-lot cancel and a 5-lot trade both give $-5$; Example~\ref{ex:ofi}) and the
  time within the step.
\item \textbf{Snapshots}: the size resting at each level after the step. Example: ``best ask 9 lots''.
  Subtracting two consecutive snapshots gives the order flow back, but not the event types.
\end{itemize}
Lost information cannot be recovered by going back up the list. In the terms of
Section~\ref{sec:meta-state-event}, a snapshot is a state (a stock); order flow and messages are flows.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, scale=0.85, transform shape,
  b/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, text width=27mm, minimum height=15mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=2mm]}, thick}]
\node[b, fill=green!12] (m) at (0,0) {\textbf{messages}\\each event: type, side, level, size, time};
\node[b, fill=orange!15] (f) at (6.4,0) {\textbf{order flow}\\net change in size at each level, per step};
\node[b, fill=blue!10] (s) at (12.8,0) {\textbf{snapshots}\\sizes resting at each level now};
\draw[arr] (m) -- node[above, align=center]{add up the changes\\per level and step} node[below, align=center, text=red!60!black]{lost: event type,\\timing within step} (f);
\draw[arr] (f) -- node[above, align=center]{keep a running\\total} node[below, align=center, text=red!60!black]{lost: nothing, if every\\step is kept} (s);
\node[align=center, font=\tiny] at (0,-1.3) {Hawkes and neural point processes};
\node[align=center, font=\tiny] at (6.4,-1.3) {OFI, multi-level OFI, impact regressions};
\node[align=center, font=\tiny] at (12.8,-1.3) {queue imbalance, book shape, micro-price};
\draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=4pt, mirror}] (-1.6,-1.6) -- (14.4,-1.6)
  node[midway, below=5pt, font=\tiny, align=center]{queue models (CST, Cont--de Larrard, queue-reactive): start from a snapshot, move it event by event at rates estimated from the messages};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The three inputs of Figure~\ref{fig:deep-inputs} are one event stream summarised to different degrees.
Summing messages into order flow loses the event types and their timing, which cannot be recovered. Below each input: the classical models of
Sections~\ref{sec:ofi}--\ref{sec:hawkes} that read the same information. Schematic.}
\label{fig:input-chain}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Inputs and the classical models that use the same information}
\label{sec:deep-io-classical}

Table~\ref{tab:input-classical} lists, for each input, the classical models of this part that use the same
information. A network and a classical model that use the same input can be compared directly.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Network inputs and the classical models that read the same information.}
\label{tab:input-classical}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.15\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.27\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Input & Classical models & Why they match \\
\midrule
Snapshots & queue imbalance (\S\ref{sec:ofi}), book shape (\S\ref{sec:shape}), micro-price (\S\ref{sec:microprice}) & each is a function of the sizes resting in the book at one moment \\
Order flow & OFI and multi-level OFI (\S\ref{sec:ofi}), regressions of the price change on order flow & OFI \emph{is} the net change in size at the best quotes; multi-level OFI is exactly this input \\
Messages & Hawkes and neural point processes (\S\ref{sec:hawkes}) & they model which event comes next and when, which only messages record \\
Snapshot + messages & queue models (\S\ref{sec:queue-models}) & queue size now, from a snapshot; arrival, cancel and trade rates, estimated from messages \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

Queue models use two inputs. Example: the expected time until the best ask is emptied needs (1) the number of
lots at the best ask now, read from a snapshot, and (2) the rates of limit orders, cancellations and trades at the
best ask, estimated from messages.

\subsubsection{Measured effect of the input choice}
\label{sec:deep-io-effect}

A network given snapshots could in principle compute order flow itself,
by subtracting consecutive snapshots. A direct test found that it does not do this well. \citet{lucchese2024}
kept the DeepLOB network fixed and changed only the input. A model counted as ``among the best'' if a model
confidence set test (Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}) could not show it to be worse than the best model. Over all
stock--horizon cases, the order-flow input was among the best in 89\% of cases; the raw snapshots in
11\% (Figure~\ref{fig:lucchese-inputs}; see also Section~\ref{sec:meta-evidence}).

Our interpretation, not stated in the cited papers: the input decides which classical quantity the network
receives ready-made. Given order flow, the network receives OFI directly. Given snapshots, it must learn to
compute OFI from the training data (Figure~\ref{fig:input-readymade}).
