\paragraph{Fixed structure versus learned weights.} DeepLOB is not a blank network: the filter sizes and strides of
stages 1--3 encode how a book is organised---prices belong with their sizes, asks with bids at the same level, and
levels with each other---so that the network cannot mix unrelated numbers and needs few weights to read the book.
What it learns is how to weigh these combinations, which time scales matter, and how to accumulate them into a
forecast. The authors report a forward pass---one evaluation on one input---of about a quarter of a millisecond,
without stating the device or batch size.

\paragraph{Reported results and later re-tests.} The F1 scores DeepLOB reports are given in Step~6 of
Section~\ref{sec:deep-train}. The
simulation trades at the mid-price, without spread or fees, as the authors state; they describe it as ``not
\ldots a fully developed, stand-alone trading strategy''. In the re-test of Lee et al., DeepLOB's accuracy was
65.9\% with the smoothed label and 54.6\% with a label measured from the current price; a forecast repeating the
last observable label scored 64.8\% (Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}). Re-implementations report lower F1 on later
data (Section~\ref{sec:benchmarks-dl}). DeepLOB
remains the standard baseline that new models are compared with.

\subsubsection{Lighter models, normalisation and variants}


\paragraph{Smaller networks.} Not every gain needs more capacity. The temporal attention-augmented bilinear network
(TABL) multiplies the $100 \times 40$ input matrix by one small weight matrix on the left, mixing time steps, and one on
the right, mixing features, and adds a simple attention over time (Section~\ref{sec:attn-intro}). With about $10^4$
weights, a sixth of DeepLOB's, it reaches competitive FI-2010 results~\citep{tran2019tabl}.

\paragraph{Effect of input normalisation.} Step~2 of Section~\ref{sec:deep-train} standardised each input
with past means and standard deviations (``z-scoring''). Some models instead \emph{learn} how to normalise: deep adaptive
input normalisation (DAIN) learns, for each input feature, how much to shift and rescale it~\citep{passalis2020dain},
and bilinear normalisation does so along both the time and the feature axes~\citep{tran2021bin}. The gains are large.
On FI-2010, DAIN raised the macro-F1 of a simple MLP from about 55\% with z-scoring to about 68\%; bilinear
normalisation raised a TABL network's F1 from 78.44\% to 88.06\% at one horizon~\citep{tran2021bin}; and an
independent group, using the stricter anchored protocol, found that learned normalisations add about seven to nine
macro-F1 points over z-scoring (58.89\% for bilinear normalisation and 57.76\% for DAIN, against
50.52\%)~\citep{september2024edain}. By comparison, adding multi-head attention to the best TABL network gained 0.4 of
a point (76.01\% to 76.42\%)~\citep{shabani2022mtabl}. The conclusion: how the inputs are scaled can move results by
around ten points, the choice of network by much less. Because the statistics of LOB data drift from day to day,
normalisation is a modelling decision in its own right.

\paragraph{Variants that output more than a class.} Some variants also say how uncertain a prediction is.
\emph{Dropout} switches off a random subset of units during training; keeping it on at prediction time and repeating
the prediction many times gives a spread of outputs, which serves as an estimate of uncertainty---a cheap form of a
\emph{Bayesian} network, whose weights are treated as uncertain rather than fixed. Such a version of DeepLOB reached an
F1 score of 0.60 against 0.58 for the ordinary model~\citep{zhang2018bdlob}. \emph{Quantile-regression} heads predict,
for example, the 10th and 90th percentiles of the return, giving a prediction interval rather than a
class~\citep{zhang2019qr}; Bayesian bilinear networks do the same for the TABL family~\citep{magris2023bbnn}. These add a
measure of uncertainty more than they add accuracy.

\paragraph{Predicting events other than the next move.} \citet{makinen2019jump} predict price jumps---unusually large
price changes---with a CNN--LSTM with attention and reach an F1 score of 0.72, against 0.69 for an LSTM. A model
using only the time of day reaches 0.66.

\begin{modelcard}{Convolutional and recurrent models (DeepLOB family)}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The network weights (from about $10^4$ to $10^5$ parameters for TABL and
DeepLOB), and before that the input normalisation, the label threshold and the horizon.}
\cardrow{How}{Supervised training with a cross-entropy loss on labelled windows of snapshots
(down/stationary/up), with chronological train, validation and test splits.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Short-horizon direction probabilities, used as a signal or as one input
to an execution decision.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{The book as an image---time down the rows, the book's columns across---scanned by small
filters that find local patterns, followed by a memory that reads the patterns in order.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Attention and Transformers for a single asset}
\label{sec:deep-attn}

\subsubsection{Attention and Transformers: the basics}
\label{sec:attn-intro}

\paragraph{Selecting relevant past events.} A model shown the last 100 events of the book has to decide which of them
matter for its prediction. The families met so far answer this in fixed ways. A multilayer perceptron gives each
position in the window its own fixed weight: event number 37 always counts the same, whatever it was. A
convolution applies the same small filter everywhere, so it sees only short local patterns. A recurrent network
reads the events in order and squeezes everything into a memory passed from step to step, so a distant event
reaches the present only through every update in between. \emph{Attention} answers the question differently:
the weight given to each earlier event is computed afresh, every time, from \emph{what the events are}. If the
current event is a large buy, the cancellations at the ask a few milliseconds earlier can receive most of the
weight; if it is a quiet limit order, they can receive almost none.

\paragraph{Inputs and outputs of an attention layer.} In terms of inputs and outputs, an attention layer is simple. Its input
is the window of events, each already turned into a vector: $\mathbf{x}_1, \dots, \mathbf{x}_N$, one per event. Its
output is a new vector for each event. Take the newest event, number $N$. Its output is a weighted average of
numbers computed from the earlier events, and the weights are computed from the events themselves rather than fixed
by position. Three learned matrices turn every input vector into three shorter vectors,
\[
  \mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_n = \mathbf{W}^{\text{qry}}\mathbf{x}_n, \qquad
  \mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_n = \mathbf{W}^{\text{key}}\mathbf{x}_n, \qquad
  \mathbf{z}^{\text{val}}_n = \mathbf{W}^{\text{val}}\mathbf{x}_n ,
\]
and the output for event $N$ is computed in three steps:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=1pt]
\item a score for each earlier event $n$: the dot product $\mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_N \cdot \mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_n$ (multiply the two vectors
  element by element and add);
\item weights: the scores passed through the softmax, so that they are positive and sum to one;
\item the output: $\sum_n (\text{weight}_n)\, \mathbf{z}^{\text{val}}_n$, the weighted average of the earlier events'
  value vectors $\mathbf{z}^{\text{val}}$.
\end{enumerate}
\inwords{each earlier event contributes its value vector to the newest event's output, in proportion to how
well its key vector lines up with the newest event's query vector.}
The output goes to the next layer and, in the end, to the prediction. Nothing else is chosen by hand: the three
matrices are weights like any others, set by training to whatever makes the predictions better. The difference from
the families above is only that the weight on each earlier event depends on what the events are, through
the query and key vectors, not on their position. Figure~\ref{fig:attn-who} draws these steps, and a
worked example with numbers follows; Section~\ref{sec:attn-detail} gives the formula with the scaling used in
practice.

\paragraph{Origin of the names query, key and value.} The names are borrowed from searching a database,
and they are only names. In a database you look items up with a \emph{query}; each item is filed under a
\emph{key}; and what you get back is its \emph{value}. Attention is a ``soft'' version of such a look-up: instead of
returning the one item whose key matches the query exactly, it returns a mix of all the values, weighted by how well
each key matches (Figure~\ref{fig:attn-library}). In the model, the vector $\mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_N$ plays the role of the
query, and each earlier event's $\mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_n$ and $\mathbf{z}^{\text{val}}_n$ play the roles of key and value. When this survey
says that a query ``looks for'' something, it is only describing in words which earlier events that vector scores
highly; in a trained model the vectors usually have no simple meaning.



\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  ev/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=gray!10, minimum width=17mm, minimum height=8mm, align=center},
  key/.style={draw, fill=blue!15, minimum width=13mm, minimum height=5mm},
  val/.style={draw, fill=green!15, minimum width=13mm, minimum height=5mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}}]
% earlier events
\foreach \i/\x in {1/0,2/2.3,3/4.6} {
  \node[ev] (e\i) at (\x,0) {event \i\\$\mathbf{x}_{\i}$};
  \node[key] (k\i) at (\x-0.5,-1.5) {key$_{\i}$};
  \node[val] (v\i) at (\x+0.55,-1.5) {value$_{\i}$};
  \draw[arr] (e\i) -- node[left, pos=0.4, font=\tiny]{$\mathbf{W}^{\text{key}}$} (k\i);
  \draw[arr] (e\i) -- node[right, pos=0.4, font=\tiny]{$\mathbf{W}^{\text{val}}$} (v\i);
}
\node[ev, fill=red!12] (e4) at (7.6,0) {event 4\\(current)\\$\mathbf{x}_{4}$};
\node[draw, fill=orange!20, minimum width=13mm, minimum height=5mm] (q) at (7.6,-1.5) {query$_4$};
\draw[arr] (e4) -- node[right, font=\tiny]{$\mathbf{W}^{\text{qry}}$} (q);
\node[anchor=east, align=right, text width=22mm] at (-1.1,-1.5) {keys and values: one pair per earlier event, made from that event};
% attention layer
\node[draw, very thick, rounded corners=3pt, fill=yellow!10, align=center, text width=92mm, minimum height=13mm] (att) at (3.4,-4.1)
  {\textbf{attention layer (does the matching)}\\
   1.\ score$_n$ = query$_4 \cdot$ key$_n$ for $n = 1,2,3$ \quad 2.\ weights = softmax(scores) \quad
   3.\ output = $\sum_n$ weight$_n \times$ value$_n$};
\foreach \i in {1,2,3} {\draw[arr, blue!60!black] (k\i.south) -- (k\i.south |- att.north);
  \draw[arr, green!50!black] (v\i.south) -- (v\i.south |- att.north);}
\draw[arr, orange!70!black, thick] (q.south) -- (q.south |- att.north);
\node[ev, fill=red!6, align=center] (out) at (10.2,-4.1) {new vector\\for event 4};
\draw[arr, thick] (att.east) -- (out.west);
\node[align=left, anchor=west, text width=26mm] at (8.4,-1.5) {the current event supplies the query};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Attention on order-book events, for the newest event. Each earlier event is turned by learned matrices into a
key (how it describes itself) and a value (what it passes on). The current event is turned into a query (what it is
looking for). The attention layer scores the query against every key, converts the scores to weights, and sends the
weighted average of the values back to the current event. In self-attention every event in the window takes its turn
as the current one, each with its own query, against the keys and values of the events before it.}
\label{fig:attn-who}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  key/.style={draw, fill=blue!15, minimum width=34mm, minimum height=5mm},
  val/.style={draw, fill=green!15, minimum width=34mm, minimum height=5mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}, thick}]
\node[draw, fill=orange!20, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, text width=24mm] (q) at (0,0)
  {\textbf{query}\\what is searched for:\\``sellers leaving''};
\foreach \i/\k/\v/\w in {1/{sellers leaving}/{book A}/{0.67}, 2/{buyers arriving}/{book B}/{0.09}, 3/{both}/{book C}/{0.24}} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\y}{1.6-1.6*(\i-1)}
  \node[key] (k\i) at (4.2,\y+0.27) {key (label): \k};
  \node[val] (v\i) at (4.2,\y-0.27) {value (contents): \v};
  \draw[rounded corners=2pt, gray] ($(k\i.north west)+(-0.1,0.08)$) rectangle ($(v\i.south east)+(0.1,-0.08)$);
  \draw[arr, orange!70!black] (q.east) -- ($(k\i.west)+(-0.12,0)$);
  \node[anchor=west, font=\scriptsize\bfseries] (w\i) at (6.4,\y) {weight \w};
}
\node[anchor=south] at (4.2,2.15) {shelf: one drawer per item};
\node[anchor=north, orange!70!black, align=center] at (0,-0.6) {matched against\\every label};
\node[draw, fill=green!10, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, text width=30mm] (o) at (10.3,0)
  {\textbf{result}\\$0.67\times$A $+\ 0.09\times$B\\$+\ 0.24\times$C};
\foreach \i in {1,2,3} \draw[arr, green!50!black] (w\i.east) -- (o.west);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Attention as a library search. You bring a query; every drawer on the shelf carries a key (its label) and a
value (its contents). The query is matched against every label, the matches become weights that sum to one, and you
get back a mix of all the contents, mostly from the drawers that matched best. In the model, the query comes from the
current event and each drawer is one earlier event (Figure~\ref{fig:attn-who}). Numbers from the worked example below.}
\label{fig:attn-library}
\end{figure}

\textit{A worked example.} The current event is a buy market order that empties the best ask---a \emph{sweep}, a
market order large enough to take everything resting at the best price. The numbers below are
chosen by hand so that every vector has a meaning we can read; in a trained model they would be learned and would not.

In this hand-made example each key has two numbers with a meaning: how much the event is a sign of
\emph{sellers leaving} the ask, and how much it is a sign of \emph{buyers arriving}. We set the current event's query
to $(1, 0)$. Its dot product with a key is then simply the key's first number, so this query gives high weight to
earlier events that look like sellers leaving and no weight to buyers arriving; ``were sellers leaving the ask?''
is our plain-language reading of what this particular query picks out. We chose it because a buy order that sweeps
the ask after sellers have withdrawn is an easy pattern to picture, not because a model would necessarily learn it. The value
of each event is the number of lots it removed from the ask. (In a trained model, keys, queries and values are
learned and their numbers usually have no simple meaning.)

\begin{center}
\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{clcc}
\toprule
Event & What happened & Key: (sellers leaving, & Value: lots \\
      &               & buyers arriving)      & removed from ask \\
\midrule
1 & cancellation of 40 lots at the ask & $(2, 0)$ & 40 \\
2 & limit buy of 10 lots at the bid & $(0, 1)$ & 0 \\
3 & buy trade of 20 lots at the ask & $(1, 1)$ & 20 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Score each event} by how well its key matches the query: multiply the two numbers pairwise and add.
  Event 1: $1 \times 2 + 0 \times 0 = 2$. Event 2: $1 \times 0 + 0 \times 1 = 0$. Event 3: $1 \times 1 + 0 \times 1 = 1$.
\item \textbf{Turn the scores into weights} that are positive and sum to one (the softmax): take $e^{\text{score}}$
  for each event---7.39, 1.00 and 2.72---and divide each by their total, 11.11. The weights are 0.67, 0.09 and 0.24.
\item \textbf{Average the values with these weights}: $0.67 \times 40 + 0.09 \times 0 + 0.24 \times 20 \approx 31.5$
  lots (computed with unrounded weights).
\end{enumerate}
The result is a weighted average of the lots each event removed, about 31.5 lots, in which the events
that look like sellers leaving count most. It is not a total: the three events together removed 60 lots, and a
weighted average can never exceed the largest single value, 40. The cancellation dominates because its key matches the question best; the limit buy
at the bid, which says nothing about sellers, gets almost no weight. A different current event, with a different
query, would weight the same three events differently.

Figure~\ref{fig:attention-illustrated} draws the worked example. Figure~\ref{fig:attention-two-queries} shows what
makes attention different from the other families: the same eight earlier events are weighted in two different ways
depending on the current event. A buy that sweeps the ask puts most weight on the recent ask cancellations; a quiet
limit order puts it on the other limit orders. Figure~\ref{fig:attention-matrix} shows a whole attention layer: every
event, as it arrives, spreads its attention over the events before it.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.75\linewidth]{figs/attention_scores.png}
\caption{The worked example above. Grey: the score of each earlier event, the match of its key with the query
$(1,0)$, ``were sellers leaving?''. Orange: the weights the softmax turns the scores into. The output is the weighted
average of the values, 31.5 lots. Hand-made numbers.}
\label{fig:attention-illustrated}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.95\linewidth]{figs/attention_two_queries.png}
\caption{The same eight earlier order-book events, weighted by two different queries. Orange: the current event is a
buy sweep, whose query looks for sellers leaving; the ask cancellations receive most of the weight. Blue: the
current event is a quiet limit order, whose query looks for other limit orders. Hand-specified vectors, not a
trained model.}
\label{fig:attention-two-queries}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.62\linewidth]{figs/attention_matrix.png}
\caption{The attention weights of a whole sequence. Row $n$ shows how event $n$ divides its attention among events
$1,\dots,n$; each row sums to one. Cells to the right of the diagonal are blank because an event may not attend to
later events (masking, explained below). Hand-specified vectors, not a trained model.}
\label{fig:attention-matrix}
\end{figure}


\paragraph{The Transformer.} A Transformer~\citep{vaswani2017} is a network built from repeated blocks, each
containing two steps (Figure~\ref{fig:transformer-block}). First, \emph{self-attention}: every input element---every
event, or every snapshot---produces a query, a key and a value, and is replaced by the weighted average it gets from
attending to the elements before it. Second, a small multilayer perceptron is applied to each element separately.
Each step adds its result to its input rather than replacing it (a \emph{residual} connection) and rescales the
result (\emph{layer normalisation}: each element's vector is shifted and scaled to have mean 0 and variance 1),
which makes deep stacks trainable. Three further ingredients complete it. \emph{Several
heads}: several attentions run side by side with different learned matrices, so that one can look for, say, recent
cancellations while another looks for large trades. A \emph{position or time encoding}: attention on its own ignores
order---shuffling the inputs would shuffle the outputs in the same way---so a vector encoding each element's
position, or its time stamp, is added to it at the start. A \emph{causal mask}: when predicting, an element may
attend only to earlier elements, never to later ones. A final layer, the \emph{head}, turns the output into the
prediction, for example three class probabilities.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, node distance=3.5mm,
  b/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, text width=34mm, minimum height=7mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}, thick}]
\node[b, fill=blue!10] (in) {input elements (events or snapshots), each a vector};
\node[b, below=of in, fill=gray!10] (pos) {add a position or time encoding};
\node[b, below=8mm of pos, fill=orange!18] (att) {self-attention, several heads, causal mask};
\node[b, below=of att, fill=gray!10] (n1) {add input back; normalise};
\node[b, below=of n1, fill=orange!18] (mlp) {small MLP on each element};
\node[b, below=of mlp, fill=gray!10] (n2) {add input back; normalise};
\node[b, below=8mm of n2, fill=green!12] (head) {head: e.g.\ P(down), P(stat.), P(up)};
\draw[arr] (in) -- (pos); \draw[arr] (pos) -- (att); \draw[arr] (att) -- (n1); \draw[arr] (n1) -- (mlp);
\draw[arr] (mlp) -- (n2); \draw[arr] (n2) -- (head);
\node[draw, dashed, rounded corners, fit=(att)(n2), inner sep=2.5mm] (blk) {};
\node[anchor=west, align=left] at (blk.east) {one block;\\repeated\\several times};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A Transformer. Each block mixes information across elements (self-attention) and then transforms each
element on its own (MLP); blocks are stacked. Schematic.}
\label{fig:transformer-block}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Differences from a standard network.} Table~\ref{tab:transformer-vs} sets the families side by side.
The essential difference is that in a Transformer the weights that mix different inputs are not fixed parameters:
they are computed from the inputs each time. The learned parameters are the matrices that produce queries, keys and
values, and the per-element MLP. Two practical consequences follow: all elements can be processed in parallel during
training, unlike a recurrent network; and the cost grows with the square of the window length, because every element
is compared with every other.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{How four families weight the past and handle order.}
\label{tab:transformer-vs}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}}
\toprule
Family & How much each earlier input counts & How order is known & Cost for a window of $L$ \\
\midrule
MLP & a fixed learned weight for each position & by the position in the input vector & proportional to $L$ \\
CNN & a fixed small filter, the same everywhere & by where the filter is & proportional to $L$ \\
RNN, LSTM & through a memory passed from step to step & by reading in order & proportional to $L$, one step at a time \\
Transformer & computed from the content of the inputs, each time & by an added position or time encoding & proportional to $L^2$, in parallel \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Attention and Hawkes kernels.} A Hawkes process (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}) also weights past events, and the
comparison clarifies both. In a Hawkes model the weight of a past event is set by \emph{how long ago} it happened and
by its type: a fixed kernel that decays with elapsed time, the same for every event of that type. In standard
attention the weight is set by \emph{what the event is}, through the match of its key with the current query; time
enters only through the position or time encoding, and nothing forces old events to count less. The two can be
combined. \emph{Hawkes-biased attention}, a design proposed in Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-patterns}, adds the logarithm of
a Hawkes kernel to the attention scores, so that each weight is the content match multiplied by a decay in elapsed
time: relevant events count more, old events count less. The Transformer Hawkes process of \citet{zuo2020thp} and the self-attentive Hawkes
process of \citet{zhang2020sahp}---developed concurrently and both presented at ICML 2020, building on the neural
Hawkes process of \citet{mei2017}---are a different combination (Section~\ref{sec:npp-attn}): they use attention to
compute the \emph{intensities} of a point process and are trained by its likelihood, so their output is a rate of
events rather than a class label.
Table~\ref{tab:attn-hawkes} summarises the differences.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Hawkes kernels, attention, and their combinations.}
\label{tab:attn-hawkes}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.22\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\linewidth}}
\toprule
Model & What sets the weight on a past event & What it outputs \\
\midrule
Hawkes kernel (\S\ref{sec:hawkes}) & elapsed time and event type, through a fixed decaying kernel & an intensity \\
Standard attention (this subsection) & the content of the event, matched with the current query; time only through an encoding & whatever the head predicts, e.g.\ a class \\
Hawkes-biased attention (\S\ref{sec:hybrid-patterns}) & content match multiplied by a decay in elapsed time & whatever the head predicts \\
Transformer and self-attentive Hawkes processes~\citep{zuo2020thp,zhang2020sahp} (\S\ref{sec:npp-attn}) & content, with time encoded; in the self-attentive version, attention sets the initial level, the long-run level and the decay rate of each event's influence & an intensity, trained by likelihood \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Transformer inputs on order-book data.} The elements a Transformer attends over are called
\emph{positions}; only when the input is a stream of messages mapped to a vocabulary are they also tokens
(Section~\ref{sec:deep-io-targets}). For the book there are three common choices (Figure~\ref{fig:lob-tokens}),
corresponding to the inputs of Section~\ref{sec:deep-io}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{One position per snapshot.} The window of the last 100 snapshots, each 40 numbers, is a sequence of 100
  positions; attention runs along time, and in some models also across the 40 numbers of each snapshot. TransLOB
  works this way on FI-2010 windows of $100 \times 40$~\citep{wallbridge2020}, as do Axial-LOB and TLOB, which
  alternate attention along time and across features~\citep{kisiel2022axial,berti2025tlob}.
\item \textbf{One token per message.} Each order-book message---add, cancel, execute, with its side, price and
  size---becomes a token, much as words do for a language model. Prices and sizes are put into a few classes so
  that the number of distinct tokens is manageable: LOBERT has 293 distinct message tokens, built from type, side,
  distance in ticks from the opposite best price, and size~\citep{linna2025lobert} (Section~\ref{sec:deep-tok}).
\item \textbf{One position per patch.} A block of the book---for example one side over a short window---becomes one
  position, as patches of an image do in vision Transformers---Transformers for images, which cut an image into small
  square tiles and treat each tile as one position~\citep{lit2025}.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize]
\begin{scope}
  \foreach \i in {0,...,5} {\draw[fill=blue!15] (0.45*\i,0) rectangle ++(0.38,1.4); \node[rotate=90] at (0.45*\i+0.19,0.7) {snapshot};}
  \node[align=center] at (1.3,-0.5) {\textbf{position = snapshot}\\TransLOB, Axial-LOB, TLOB};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=4.2cm]
  \foreach \i/\t in {0/add,1/cancel,2/trade,3/add,4/add,5/cancel} {\draw[fill=green!15] (0.45*\i,0.3) rectangle ++(0.38,0.8); \node[rotate=90] at (0.45*\i+0.19,0.7) {\t};}
  \node[align=center] at (1.3,-0.5) {\textbf{position = message (token)}\\LOBERT, MarketGPT};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=8.4cm]
  \foreach \i in {0,...,5} \foreach \j in {0,...,3} {\pgfmathsetmacro{\gv}{15+mod(7*\i+11*\j,40)} \fill[blue!\gv] (0.3*\i,0.35*\j) rectangle ++(0.28,0.33);}
  \draw[red!70!black, very thick] (0,0) rectangle (0.88,0.68); \draw[red!70!black, very thick] (0.9,0) rectangle (1.78,0.68);
  \draw[red!70!black, very thick] (0,0.7) rectangle (0.88,1.38); \draw[red!70!black, very thick] (0.9,0.7) rectangle (1.78,1.38);
  \node[align=center] at (0.9,-0.5) {\textbf{position = patch}\\LiT};
\end{scope}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Three ways to cut order-book data into the positions a Transformer attends over. Only message positions
are tokens drawn from a vocabulary. Schematic.}
\label{fig:lob-tokens}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Training.} There are two ways, and they correspond to the two kinds of input: snapshots and messages.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{With labels, like DeepLOB.} Snapshot Transformers are trained exactly as in
  Section~\ref{sec:deep-train}: windows of snapshots as inputs, direction labels as targets, cross-entropy loss,
  time-ordered splits. TransLOB, for example, was trained for 150 passes over the first seven days of FI-2010 and
  tested on the last three~\citep{wallbridge2020}.
\item \textbf{By predicting the next message, like a language model.} Message Transformers are trained to predict
  the next message from the ones before it; the target is the data itself, so no labels are needed
  (\emph{self-supervised} training). LOBERT is first pretrained by filling in hidden messages and then fine-tuned to predict the
  next message, on Nasdaq messages for four stocks over 80 trading days of 2015~\citep{linna2025lobert}
  (Section~\ref{sec:lobert}); MarketGPT, with about 100 million parameters, is trained on Nasdaq
  messages to generate realistic message streams~\citep{wheeler2024marketgpt}. A model trained this way can then
  generate order flow (Section~\ref{sec:deep-gen}), or its internal representation can be reused, with a small
  extra network trained on labels, for a forecasting task (Section~\ref{sec:foundation}).
\end{itemize}
\paragraph{Vocabulary used in the rest of this section.} A few terms recur from here on.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item An \emph{encoder} turns a sequence into a sequence of vectors, one per element, each summarising that element in
  its context; during training each element may attend to elements on both sides. A \emph{decoder} sees only earlier
  elements and is built to generate the next one; GPT is a \emph{decoder-only} Transformer, BERT an
  \emph{encoder-only} one~\citep{devlin2019}. An encoder followed by a decoder that outputs a different sequence is a
  \emph{sequence-to-sequence} model.
\item A \emph{representation} is the set of vectors a network computes internally from its input; a good one makes
  later tasks easy.
\item \emph{Pretraining} is a first, usually self-supervised training on a large amount of data; \emph{fine-tuning}
  continues training the pretrained network, usually with a small added output layer (a \emph{head}), on a specific
  labelled task. A network pretrained once and adapted to many tasks is called a \emph{foundation model}. When the
  pretrained part is kept fixed during fine-tuning it is \emph{frozen}; when all weights are trained together, training
  is \emph{end to end}.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Attention on the book, in detail}
\label{sec:attn-detail}

A model that attends along more than one axis of the book is called \emph{spatial-temporal}: ``space'' is the price
ladder (and, in multi-asset models, the set of instruments), ``time'' is the sequence of snapshots or events.

\paragraph{The attention step as a formula.} Each event $n$ is first described by a vector $\mathbf{x}_n$, its
\emph{embedding}, built from the event's attributes---its type and side, its size, its distance from the best price,
the time since the previous event---by a learned table or a learned matrix. Three learned matrices, $\mathbf{W}^{\text{qry}}$,
$\mathbf{W}^{\text{key}}$ and $\mathbf{W}^{\text{val}}$, turn the embedding into three shorter vectors:
\begin{equation}
\mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_n = \mathbf{W}^{\text{qry}}\mathbf{x}_n,\qquad
\mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_n = \mathbf{W}^{\text{key}}\mathbf{x}_n,\qquad
\mathbf{z}^{\text{val}}_n = \mathbf{W}^{\text{val}}\mathbf{x}_n .
\label{eq:qkv}
\end{equation}
\inwords{each event is turned into three vectors by three matrices that training sets: a query, used when the event
is the current one; a key, matched against the queries of later events; and a value, the information the event
passes on in proportion to its weight.}
Keeping the key and the value separate lets an event be \emph{found} for one reason and \emph{pass on} something
else: a cancellation can be found because it is a cancellation at the ask, and pass on its size. The score of an
earlier event $n'$ is the dot product of the current query with its key, divided by $\sqrt{d_k}$, where $d_k$ is the
length of the query and key vectors (the \emph{key dimension}), so that scores do not grow just because the vectors
are longer. Written as one formula, event $n$ attends to earlier event $n'$ with weight
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{att}_{n n'} = \frac{\exp\big(\langle \mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_n, \mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_{n'}\rangle/\sqrt{d_k}\big)}
  {\sum_{n'' \le n} \exp\big(\langle \mathbf{z}^{\text{qry}}_n, \mathbf{z}^{\text{key}}_{n''}\rangle/\sqrt{d_k}\big)},
  \qquad \text{output}_n = \sum_{n' \le n} \mathrm{att}_{n n'}\, \mathbf{z}^{\text{val}}_{n'} ,
\label{eq:attn-formula}
\end{equation}
\inwords{the numerator is the exponential of event $n'$'s scaled score; the denominator adds up the same quantity
over all events up to $n$, so the weights sum to one (the softmax); the output is the weighted average of the values.
Summing only over $n'' \le n$ is the causal mask: no event receives weight from a later one.}

Applied to a window of snapshots, attention can run along different axes of the grid
(Figure~\ref{fig:attn-axes}): along \emph{time}, so that each moment attends to earlier moments; along
\emph{levels} or features, so that each level attends to the others at the same moment; or, in multi-asset
models (Section~\ref{sec:deep-multi}), along \emph{assets}. Axial and dual-attention models alternate
between the first two.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize]
\foreach \i in {0,...,7} \foreach \j in {0,...,5} {\draw[gray!50] (0.4*\i,0.4*\j) rectangle ++(0.38,0.38);}
\fill[red!30] (0.4*7,0.4*2) rectangle ++(0.38,0.38);
\foreach \i in {0,...,6} \draw[-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}, red!70, thick] (0.4*\i+0.19,0.4*2+0.19) to[bend left=25] (0.4*7+0.1,0.4*2+0.3);
\node[align=center] at (1.6,-0.85) {\textbf{temporal attention}: a moment\\attends to earlier moments};
\begin{scope}[xshift=5.2cm]
\foreach \i in {0,...,7} \foreach \j in {0,...,5} {\draw[gray!50] (0.4*\i,0.4*\j) rectangle ++(0.38,0.38);}
\fill[blue!30] (0.4*5,0.4*2) rectangle ++(0.38,0.38);
\foreach \j in {0,1,3,4,5} \draw[-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}, blue!70, thick] (0.4*5+0.19,0.4*\j+0.19) to[bend right=30] (0.4*5+0.3,0.4*2+0.19);
\node[align=center] at (1.6,-0.85) {\textbf{spatial attention}: a level\\attends to other levels, same moment};
\end{scope}
\draw[->] (0,-0.12) -- (3.3,-0.12) node[right]{time};
\draw[->] (-0.12,0) -- (-0.12,2.5) node[above]{levels};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Attention along two axes of a window of snapshots. Temporal attention (left) lets the current
moment weight earlier ones; spatial attention (right) lets each price level weight the other levels at the
same moment. Schematic.}
\label{fig:attn-axes}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{TransLOB.} \citet{wallbridge2020} combined five dilated causal convolutions---\emph{causal} because each output
uses only the current and earlier events, \emph{dilated} because the filter skips steps (reading every second, fourth,
\ldots\ event) to see further back with few weights---with two Transformer blocks using masked (causal) self-attention, and reported FI-2010 F1 of 81--92\% across
horizons. In the re-implementation of Prata et al., its F1 on FI-2010 was 59\%~\citep{prata2024}.

\paragraph{Axial and dual attention.} Axial-LOB~\citep{kisiel2022axial} applies gated attention---attention whose output is
multiplied by a learned gate between 0 and 1---separately along the time and feature axes, keeping the parameter count below $10^4$. TLOB
\citep{berti2025tlob} alternates temporal self-attention (across snapshots) with spatial
self-attention (across the features of a snapshot); its companion MLPLOB replaces attention by
multilayer perceptrons that mix along the two axes, and matches TLOB on several data sets. Three
observations from TLOB are instructive. Performance on Nasdaq and Bitcoin data falls steeply with the
horizon---for INTC, F1 drops from about 80\% at 10 events to about 50\% at 100---whereas on FI-2010 it
\emph{rises} with the horizon. Predictability on the same stock
declined between 2012 and 2015. And when the label threshold is set to the average spread, so that only moves large enough to pay for crossing
the spread---buying at the ask and selling at the bid---count, F1 is 31--41\% (TSLA, horizons of 50 to 200
events). The authors conclude that the
methods ``are not sufficiently mature for practical deployment''.

\paragraph{Patches, full books and graphs.} Three designs depart from one position per snapshot. LiT~\citep{lit2025}
cuts the book into patches---one side of the book over a short time window---and applies it to cryptocurrency data
from the Binance exchange. \citet{arxiv240902277} forecast the entire five-level book with a sequence-to-sequence
Transformer and a loss that penalises forecasts violating the ordering of prices (ask prices must rise, and bid
prices fall, level by level); on one day of data a linear model is slightly better on the overall forecasting error and
on volumes, but worse on the mid-price and much worse at respecting the ordering of prices. HLOB
\citep{briola2025hlob} links the volume levels of the book into a graph according to how they move together, and
processes it with a convolutional network designed for such graphs.

\paragraph{Multi-horizon models.} \citet{zhangzohren2021} attach a sequence-to-sequence decoder with attention to the
DeepLOB encoder to produce forecasts at several horizons at once; they also report that training on specialised
processors (Graphcore IPUs) took about 15\% of the time needed on a GPU, which matters when models are retrained
often.

\paragraph{Transformers against simpler models.} Head-to-head comparisons on the same data do not show
Transformers winning. On twelve days of order-book data for a cryptocurrency pair (ETH--USDT), five general-purpose
time-series Transformers did not beat DeepLOB-family networks at classifying the next move: at a horizon of twenty events the best Transformer was right 68.89\% of the time
against 70.29\% for DeepLOB and 73.10\% for an LSTM with a trend decomposition (one that splits the series into a
slow trend and fluctuations), and in a simple trading
simulation with costs the Transformer-based models had lower simulated returns~\citep{bilokon2023transformers}; this is a
preprint on a single pair. In the LOBench benchmark of five Shenzhen stocks, models trained on one stock and
applied to the other four reached mean recalls (Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}) of 0.6814 for DeepLOB, 0.6682 for a general-purpose Transformer
(iTransformer) and 0.6568 for TransLOB~\citep{zhong2025lobench}. A preprint that tracks accuracy against inference compute on FI-2010
reports that an attention-free model that mixes along the two axes reaches TLOB-level scores at lower
latency~\citep{hedges2026frontier}.

\paragraph{GPT-style models trained on messages.} Some studies train decoder-only Transformers---the
architecture of GPT---from scratch on sequences of order-book messages. MarketGPT, about 100 million
parameters trained on NASDAQ messages with a ticker token---a token naming the stock---so that one model serves several
stocks, is used
to generate realistic message streams for a simulator rather than to forecast~\citep{wheeler2024marketgpt}.
\citet{llmlob2026} train such a model on simulated order books and find that, although it produces valid
message sequences almost perfectly, its implicit model of the book is wrong, and it ``finds predictability
where none exists''. We found no study that applies a pretrained text language model to order-book
forecasting with reported accuracy.

\begin{modelcard}{Attention and spatial-temporal Transformers}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The query, key and value matrices of every attention layer, the per-element MLP
weights, and the choice of axes (time, levels, assets) along which to attend.}
\cardrow{How}{Supervised training as for the convolutional models; reproduction studies show that the
result depends heavily on the data set and labelling, so re-testing on new data is part of
calibration.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Direction and multi-horizon forecasts, full-book forecasts, and
multi-asset models that choose which peers to read.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A soft search: every event compares its query vector with the key of every
earlier event and takes a weighted mix of their values, mostly from the closest matches.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Tokenisation: building vocabularies of market events}
\label{sec:deep-tok}

Models borrowed from language processing---BERT-style encoders and GPT-style decoders
(Section~\ref{sec:attn-intro})---read sequences of \emph{tokens} drawn from a fixed vocabulary
(Section~\ref{sec:events}). To apply them to an order book, each market event has to be turned into one or more tokens,
and the choice matters for three reasons. The model's output layer has one entry per token, so a larger vocabulary
means more classes to predict and more tokens that occur too rarely to learn. Splitting an event into several tokens
makes sequences longer, and the cost of attention grows with the square of their length. And every binning of a
continuous price or size throws away detail. The published models make three choices, summarised in
Table~\ref{tab:tok} and illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:tok}.

\paragraph{1. Token unit: a field or a whole event.} Generative models in the line of
\citet{nagy2023} split each message into its fields---type, side, price, size, time since the previous
message, and so on---and give each field its own tokens: 22 tokens per message from a vocabulary of 12{,}011
in their model, 24 per message in MarketGPT~\citep{wheeler2024marketgpt}. A context of 500 messages is then
11{,}000 tokens long. Most later models instead map each \emph{event} to a single token. LOBERT does this by
listing the combinations of type, side, binned price and binned size that occur in the training data (293
tokens, including special ones)~\citep{linna2025lobert}; UQ-LOB, following it, uses 439 tokens built from
five attributes~\citep{manoharan2026uqlob}. MarS builds the vocabulary as a Cartesian product, $3 \times 32
\times 32 \times 16 = 49{,}152$ tokens for order type, price, volume and time interval~\citep{li2025mars};
TradeFM likewise combines action, side, price depth, volume and time interval into one of $2 \times 2 \times
16 \times 16 \times 16 = 16{,}384$ tokens~\citep{tradefm2026}. A third option learns the vocabulary: M3 keeps a learned \emph{codebook}, a list of 32{,}768 prototype vectors, and
replaces each event by the nearest prototype~\citep{m3lob}, as vector-quantised autoencoders do~\citep{vandenoord2017vqvae}.
At the other extreme, some models avoid a hand-designed vocabulary altogether, reading the raw bytes of each message
(256 symbols, 32 per event)~\citep{li2025bytegen} or applying byte-pair encoding---the method of text language models,
which repeatedly merges the most frequent pair of symbols into a new symbol~\citep{sennrich2016bpe}---to the text of the
messages, as the baselines of the LOB-Bench benchmark do~\citep{lobbench2025}.

\paragraph{2. Discretisation of continuous fields.} Prices and sizes are continuous, so they must be
binned, and the reference point matters. LOBERT measures price in ticks from the best quote on the opposite
side and uses hand-chosen bins (0, 1, 2, 3, 5 and 10 ticks; sizes 0, 50, 100 and 200 shares with a
round-lot flag)~\citep{linna2025lobert}; \citet{nagy2023} measure ticks from the mid, clipped at $\pm 999$;
TradeFM uses quantile bins for price---bins chosen so that each holds the same number of observations---and
equal-width bins on the logarithm for size and time; M3 measures price from the day's opening price. General
time-series foundation models (Section~\ref{sec:foundation}), built for single price series rather than books, make
similar choices: Chronos scales each series by its mean and uses uniform bins (4{,}096 tokens)~\citep{chronos2024};
Kronos learns a binary code of 20 bits per candlestick---the open, high, low and close prices of one interval--- split into a coarse and a fine token of 1{,}024 values
each~\citep{kronos2025}. Others do not discretise at all: TimesFM and Moirai read patches of the series
directly~\citep{timesfm2024,moirai2024}, and LLMTime writes numbers as strings of digits for an unchanged
language model~\citep{gruver2023llmtime}. Older symbolic methods for time series, such as SAX, cut the range of values at points chosen so that each bin
would be equally likely for normally distributed data~\citep{lin2003sax,lin2007sax}.

\paragraph{3. Whether exact values are kept alongside.} Binning loses detail. LOBERT and UQ-LOB therefore
feed exact values next to the token: LOBERT keeps price, size and time as continuous inputs (time is not
tokenised at all), and UQ-LOB adds seven continuous features, including order-flow and queue-imbalance
measures. MarS adds the volumes of the book to the order token's embedding. TradeFM and M3 keep no
continuous channel in the token stream.

\paragraph{Published evidence.} Few studies isolate the tokenisation, and several comparisons change
more than one thing at once.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item In LOBERT, combining the coarse token with a regression on the exact value reproduced the
  distribution of prices better than either alone, measured by the Wasserstein distance---how far one distribution
  must be moved to match another; lower is better: 10.04, against 15.44 for the token alone and 10.85 for the
  regression alone~\citep{linna2025lobert}.
\item In M3, measuring price from the opening price with a learned codebook gave much smaller price errors
  than mid-relative bins when the mid had to be simulated (the error exceeded in only 1\% of cases was 28 ticks,
  against 764); the
  comparison changes both the reference point and the discretiser~\citep{m3lob}.
\item In Kronos, predicting the coarse token and then the fine one beat predicting both at once, measured by the
  information coefficient---the correlation between forecasts and outcomes; higher is better: 0.0431 against
  0.0345, and both beat direct regression on continuous values
  (0.0212); splitting the $2^{20}$-entry vocabulary into two halves cut the model from 1{,}842 million to
  102 million parameters, at twice the decoding steps~\citep{kronos2025}.
\item Outside finance, discrete learned tokens beat patches with the same architecture in the TOTEM
  study~\citep{talukder2024totem}.
\item In LOB-Bench, the byte-pair-encoded baseline reproduced market impact less closely than the field-token
  model of \citet{nagy2023}; the comparison also changes the model, size and inputs~\citep{lobbench2025}.
\end{itemize}
We found no study that compares field tokens, composite event tokens and learned codebooks on the same
order-book data with everything else held fixed.
