\subsection{State-space models}
\label{sec:deep-ssm}

\paragraph{Inputs and outputs of a state-space layer.} A state-space network is a layer, not a complete model, and it does not
fix the input or the output. Its input is any sequence of vectors, one per step, and the step can be a snapshot of
the book, a vector of order flow, or a message turned into numbers or tokens (Table~\ref{tab:input-output-kinds}). At
every step it updates its state, and an output layer on top of the state produces the prediction: a direction class,
a return, the next token, or an event intensity.

A state-space model reads the events one at a time, like a recurrent network, and keeps a \emph{state}: a vector
$\mathbf{s}$ that summarises everything seen so far. At each new event $\mathbf{x}$ it updates the state by a learned
linear rule and reads its prediction off the state:
\[
  \mathbf{s} \;\leftarrow\; \mathbf{A}\,\mathbf{s} + \mathbf{B}\,\mathbf{x}.
\]
\inwords{the new summary is the old summary multiplied by $\mathbf{A}$, plus the new event multiplied by $\mathbf{B}$.}

\paragraph{Forgetting in a state-space layer.} Take the simplest case, a state of one number with $A = 0.9$ and $B = 0.1$.
Each step keeps 90\% of the old state and adds 10\% of the new input: the state is a running average in which an event
loses a tenth of its weight at every later step, so after 20 steps it keeps $0.9^{20} \approx 12\%$ of its original
weight. A value of $A$ closer to one keeps the past longer; a smaller value replaces it faster. ``Forgetting'' is
nothing more than this shrinking of the old state; it is the same exponential fading as the kernel of a Hawkes process
(Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-one}).

\paragraph{S4 and Mamba.} In S4~\citep{gu2022s4} the rule is the same at every step: training sets $\mathbf{A}$ and
$\mathbf{B}$ once, so every event fades at the same fixed rate. In Mamba~\citep{gu2024mamba} the step size of the
update and the weight $\mathbf{B}$ are computed from the current input, so how much of the old state survives
changes from event to event. A large, unusual event can then overwrite most of the state, while a routine one leaves
it nearly unchanged. Saying that ``the model decides how much to forget'' means only this: the amount of fading is a
learned function of the input, set by training like any other weight; nothing chooses it at run time. This is the same
idea as the forget gate of an LSTM (Section~\ref{sec:deep-families}).

\paragraph{Relevance to order books.} The rule is the classical state-space model of control
theory~\citep{kalman1960}, and it can be computed in parallel during training (explained below). It processes a sequence at a cost
proportional to its length, where attention compares every pair of events and costs the square of the length
(Section~\ref{sec:hw-why}). That makes it attractive for event streams, which are long and irregular;
Section~\ref{sec:npp-mamba} describes a version in which the step size is the actual time between events, so that
memory fades with elapsed time as in a Hawkes process. 

\paragraph{Parallel in training, step by step in trading.} The update rule seems to force one event at a time: the
state at event $n$ needs the state at event $n-1$. It does not, because the rule only multiplies and adds. The state
can be written out directly as a weighted sum of all earlier inputs. With one number, $A = 0.9$ and $B = 0.1$,
\[
  s_3 = 0.9^2 \times 0.1 \times x_1 \;+\; 0.9 \times 0.1 \times x_2 \;+\; 0.1 \times x_3 ,
\]
\inwords{the state after three events is the three inputs added up, each with a weight that shrinks with its age.}
During training the whole recorded sequence is known in advance, so every state can be computed at the same time on a
GPU: in S4, whose weights are the same at every step, the weighted sum is a convolution of the inputs with one fixed
set of weights; in Mamba, whose weights change with the input, it is computed by a \emph{parallel scan}, a standard
method for computing running combinations in parallel~\citep{gu2024mamba}. A recurrent network such as an LSTM cannot
do this, because its gates apply non-linear functions to the previous state. When trading, events arrive one at a
time, and the model runs as the simple update: one cheap step per event, with no need to keep the whole window.

\paragraph{Two meanings of ``state''.} The micro-price of Section~\ref{sec:microprice} also forecasts from a state,
and both models assume that the future depends only on the current state, not on how it was reached. The states are
different things (Table~\ref{tab:state-kinds}): the micro-price's state is a few features of the book visible now,
with no memory of the past, while a state-space network's state is a hidden summary of everything it has read. The
nearest classical relative of S4 and Mamba is therefore not the micro-price but the Kalman filter, which also tracks a
hidden state from noisy observations, and which Section~\ref{sec:ofi} uses to track the drifting slope of the OFI
regression.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The state of the micro-price and the state of a state-space network.}
\label{tab:state-kinds}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.19\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Micro-price (Section~\ref{sec:microprice}) & State-space network (S4, Mamba) \\
\midrule
What the state is & a few features of the book now: the imbalance bucket and the spread & a vector the network builds, summarising the whole history it has read \\
Visible in the book? & yes, read off the current book & no, it exists only inside the model \\
Memory of the past & none: two books with the same imbalance and spread get the same micro-price, however they got there & yes: each event updates the summary, and older events fade \\
How it is fitted & counting transitions between states in historical data & gradient descent on a prediction loss \\
Output & expected future mid-price, by a matrix formula over all paths & whatever its output layer is trained for, such as a direction or a return \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Mamba in detail}
\label{sec:mamba}

Mamba~\citep{gu2024mamba} is the state-space model that made the idea competitive with Transformers on long
sequences. This subsection follows one update step, shows with numbers how the input changes it, explains why that
makes Mamba a gated recurrent network trained in parallel, and describes the block that is stacked to make a model.

\paragraph{From a continuous rule to a step rule.} This subsection follows the paper's notation, which differs from
the rest of the section: the state is $\mathbf{h}$ (written $\mathbf{s}$ above), $N$ is the number of entries in the
state (not a window length), $x_t$ is a single number, one channel of the input vector at step $t$, and $y_t$ is that
channel's output (not a label). Mamba, like S4, starts from a rule in continuous time for each input
channel, applied to every channel separately: the state $\mathbf{h}$ (a vector of $N$ numbers) changes as $\mathbf{h}'(t) = \mathbf{A}\mathbf{h}(t) +
\mathbf{B}x(t)$, and the output is $y(t) = \mathbf{C}\mathbf{h}(t)$, with $\mathbf{A}$ diagonal. To apply it to a
sequence, time is cut into steps of length $\Delta$, which gives the step rule
\begin{equation}
  \mathbf{h}_t = \bar{\mathbf{A}}\,\mathbf{h}_{t-1} + \bar{\mathbf{B}}\,x_t, \qquad y_t = \mathbf{C}\,\mathbf{h}_t,
  \qquad \bar{\mathbf{A}} = e^{\Delta \mathbf{A}}, \qquad
  \bar{\mathbf{B}} = (\Delta\mathbf{A})^{-1}\big(e^{\Delta \mathbf{A}} - \mathbf{I}\big)\,\Delta\mathbf{B}
\label{eq:mamba-step}
\end{equation}
(the ``zero-order hold'' discretisation of the paper).
\inwords{between two steps the state decays by the factor $e^{\Delta \mathbf{A}}$, and the new input is added with a
weight that grows with the step size.}
Because the entries of $\mathbf{A}$ are negative, $e^{\Delta\mathbf{A}}$ lies between 0 and 1: a large step size makes
it small, so the old state is mostly erased; a small step size makes it close to 1, so the old state is kept.

\paragraph{The selective parameters.} In S4 the step size $\Delta$ and the matrices $\mathbf{B}$ and $\mathbf{C}$ are the same
at every step. In Mamba they are computed from the current input: $\mathbf{B}_t$ and $\mathbf{C}_t$ by learned linear
maps of the input at step $t$ (the whole vector, all channels), and $\Delta_t$ by a learned linear map followed by a softplus, which keeps it positive. $\mathbf{A}$ itself
stays a learned constant, because it acts only through $e^{\Delta\mathbf{A}}$ and a selective $\Delta$ already makes that
factor depend on the input. In the authors' words, ``a large $\Delta$ resets the state $h$ and focuses on the current
input $x$, while a small $\Delta$ persists the state and ignores the current input''; $\mathbf{B}_t$ decides how much of
the input enters the state, and $\mathbf{C}_t$ how much of the state reaches the output~\citep{gu2024mamba}.
Figure~\ref{fig:mamba-step} draws one step.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, scale=0.85, transform shape,
  b/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, minimum height=8mm, text width=27mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}, thick}]
\node[b, fill=gray!10, text width=17mm] (x) at (0,0) {input $x_t$\\(this event)};
\node[b, fill=orange!15] (d) at (3.6,1.4) {step size $\Delta_t$,\\computed from $x_t$};
\node[b, fill=blue!12] (bb) at (3.6,0) {$\mathbf{B}_t$ = linear map of $x_t$};
\node[b, fill=green!12] (cc) at (3.6,-1.4) {$\mathbf{C}_t$ = linear map of $x_t$};
\node[b, fill=orange!8] (ab) at (7.6,1.4) {$\bar{\mathbf{A}}_t = e^{\Delta_t \mathbf{A}}$\\share of the old state kept};
\node[b, fill=blue!6] (bbar) at (7.6,0) {$\bar{\mathbf{B}}_t$\\weight on the new input};
\node[b, fill=yellow!12, text width=33mm] (h) at (11.8,0.7) {new state\\$\mathbf{h}_t = \bar{\mathbf{A}}_t\mathbf{h}_{t-1} + \bar{\mathbf{B}}_t x_t$};
\node[b, fill=gray!5] (hp) at (11.8,2.4) {previous state $\mathbf{h}_{t-1}$};
\node[b, fill=green!12] (y) at (11.8,-1.4) {output $y_t = \mathbf{C}_t \mathbf{h}_t$};
\draw[arr] (x) -- (d); \draw[arr] (x) -- (bb); \draw[arr] (x) -- (cc);
\draw[arr] (d) -- (ab); \draw[arr] (bb) -- (bbar); \draw[arr] (d.south east) -- (bbar.north west);
\draw[arr] (ab.east) -- (h.north west); \draw[arr] (bbar.east) -- (h.west); \draw[arr] (hp) -- (h); \draw[arr] (h) -- (y);
\draw[arr] (cc) -- (y);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One step of Mamba's selective state-space layer. The current input sets the step size $\Delta_t$ and the
matrices $\mathbf{B}_t$ and $\mathbf{C}_t$; the step size sets how much of the previous state survives. After
\citet{gu2024mamba}.}
\label{fig:mamba-step}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A worked example: a routine event and a sweep.} Take one state number with $A = -1$ and $B = 1$, as in the
paper's own simplest case. Then $\bar{A} = e^{-\Delta}$ and $\bar{B} = 1 - e^{-\Delta}$, so the update is
\[
  h_t = e^{-\Delta_t}\, h_{t-1} + \big(1 - e^{-\Delta_t}\big)\, x_t ,
\]
\inwords{the new state is a weighted average of the old state and the new input, and the step size decides the
weights.}
Suppose training has made $\Delta_t$ small for routine limit orders and large for a market order that empties the
best ask (our numbers, to show the mechanism):
\begin{center}\footnotesize
\begin{tabular}{lccl}
\toprule
Event & $\Delta_t$ & weight on old state $e^{-\Delta_t}$ & effect \\
\midrule
routine limit order & 0.1 & 0.905 & state almost unchanged; the event adds 9.5\% \\
sweep of the best ask & 3 & 0.050 & state almost reset to the new event \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
After the sweep the state describes the sweep, not the quiet period before it; after a routine order it still
describes the past. A fixed rule, as in S4, would treat the two events alike.

\paragraph{A gated recurrent network.} The example is a theorem of the paper: with one state number, $A = -1$,
$B = 1$ and $\Delta_t = \operatorname{softplus}(\text{a linear map of } x_t)$, the update becomes
$h_t = (1 - g_t)\,h_{t-1} + g_t\,x_t$ with $g_t = \sigma(\text{a linear map of } x_t)$---the gate of a gated recurrent
network, the same idea as the forget gate of an LSTM (Section~\ref{sec:deep-families})~\citep{gu2024mamba}. The
difference from an LSTM is that the new state is still linear in the old one, which is what lets the whole sequence be
computed in parallel in training (the parallel scan above).

\paragraph{Motivation for selection.} The authors motivate the input-dependent step with tasks a fixed rule cannot do. In
\emph{selective copying}, a few tokens to be remembered sit at random positions among filler; a fixed rule can only
weigh positions, not contents. S4 solved it 18.3\% of the time, the selective layer 97.0\% and the full Mamba
model 99.8\%~\citep{gu2024mamba}. In their ablation, making only $\Delta$ input-dependent gave most of the gain. The
order-book analogue is the example above: what matters is what an event is, not how many steps ago it happened.

\paragraph{Parallel training on a GPU.} Because the step rule changes from step to step, the convolution of S4 is no
longer available, and Mamba computes the recurrence with a parallel scan. The difficulty is memory, not arithmetic:
the full set of states is $N$ times larger than the input. Mamba never writes it to the GPU's large, slow main memory.
It loads $\Delta$, $\mathbf{A}$, $\mathbf{B}$ and $\mathbf{C}$ into the small, fast on-chip memory, computes the step
rule and the scan there in one fused operation, and writes back only the outputs; in training it recomputes the states
when they are needed for the gradients instead of storing them. The authors report this is 20 to 40 times faster than
a straightforward scan, with memory use similar to an optimised Transformer~\citep{gu2024mamba}.

\paragraph{The Mamba block.} A model is a stack of identical blocks (Figure~\ref{fig:mamba-block}). Each block widens
the input by a linear map into two branches. One branch passes through a short convolution over neighbouring steps,
a SiLU non-linearity and the selective state-space layer; the other passes through a SiLU only. The two are multiplied,
which lets the second branch gate the first, and a linear map returns to the original width. Blocks are stacked with
normalisation and residual connections; there is no attention and no separate feed-forward layer.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  b/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, minimum width=20mm, minimum height=6mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}, thick}]
\node[b, fill=gray!10] (in) at (0,0) {input};
\node[b, fill=blue!10] (p1) at (2.6,0.8) {linear (widen)};
\node[b, fill=blue!10] (p2) at (2.6,-0.8) {linear (widen)};
\node[b, fill=gray!8] (cv) at (5.2,0.8) {short convolution};
\node[b, fill=gray!8] (s1) at (7.6,0.8) {SiLU};
\node[b, fill=yellow!15] (ssm) at (10.0,0.8) {selective SSM};
\node[b, fill=gray!8] (s2) at (7.6,-0.8) {SiLU};
\node[circle, draw, inner sep=1pt] (m) at (11.8,0) {$\times$};
\node[b, fill=blue!10] (po) at (13.5,0) {linear (narrow)};
\draw[arr] (in) -- (p1); \draw[arr] (in) -- (p2);
\draw[arr] (p1) -- (cv); \draw[arr] (cv) -- (s1); \draw[arr] (s1) -- (ssm); \draw[arr] (ssm) -| (m);
\draw[arr] (p2) -- (s2); \draw[arr] (s2) -| (m); \draw[arr] (m) -- (po);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The Mamba block. The second branch gates the output of the selective state-space layer by multiplication.
After \citet{gu2024mamba}.}
\label{fig:mamba-block}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Costs, results and limits.} Training cost grows linearly with the sequence length, and producing each new
output costs a fixed amount of time and memory, because there is no growing cache of past inputs as in a Transformer.
On an A100 GPU, generating text, Mamba had 4 to 5 times the throughput of a Transformer of similar size, mainly
because it can process larger batches. On language modelling, a 2.8-billion-parameter Mamba averaged 63.3 on a set of
zero-shot tasks against 59.1 for a Transformer of the same size (Pythia-2.8B) and 61.7 for one more than twice its size (Pythia-6.9B); on DNA,
its perplexity---a measure of prediction error---kept falling as the context grew to about a million positions~\citep{gu2024mamba}. The authors state two
limits: selection helps on discrete data such as text and DNA but can hurt on continuous signals, where fixed-rule
models do well, and the experiments stop at small model sizes.

\paragraph{Mamba-2 and attention.} The follow-up model shows that if $\mathbf{A}$ is one number times the identity, the
selective state-space layer computes the same output as a masked attention without the softmax,
$\mathbf{Y} = (\mathbf{L} \circ \mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{\top})\mathbf{X}$~\citep{dao2024ssd}. For a sequence of $T$ steps,
$\mathbf{X}$ stacks the inputs of all steps, one row per step, and $\mathbf{Y}$ stacks the outputs in the same way;
$\mathbf{C}$ and $\mathbf{B}$ stack the step-by-step $\mathbf{C}_t$ and $\mathbf{B}_t$ as rows, so that entry $(t, t')$ of
$\mathbf{C}\mathbf{B}^{\top}$ is the match between $\mathbf{C}_t$ and $\mathbf{B}_{t'}$; $\mathbf{L}$ is a $T \times T$ mask
whose entry $(t, t')$ is the product of the decay factors between step $t'$ and step $t$ when $t' \le t$, and zero
when $t' > t$; and $\circ$ is the element-wise product, which multiplies two matrices of the same size entry by
entry.
\inwords{the output at each step is a weighted sum of the inputs at earlier steps, with weights that are the match
between $\mathbf{C}$ at the current step and $\mathbf{B}$ at the earlier step, times how much has decayed in between.}
In the language of Section~\ref{sec:attn-intro}, $\mathbf{C}$ plays the role of the query, $\mathbf{B}$ of the key and the
input of the value, and the decay replaces the softmax: Mamba is an attention whose weights fade with distance, which is
also how a Hawkes kernel weights the past. Computing it in blocks with matrix multiplications is 2 to 8 times faster than
Mamba's scan~\citep{dao2024ssd}.

\subsubsection{State-space models on order-book data}
\label{sec:ssm-lob}

\paragraph{Three uses on order-book data.} Three order-book uses show the range of inputs and outputs (Table~\ref{tab:ssm-uses}).
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{A generator of messages.} LOBS5~\citep{nagy2023} is built from S5 state-space layers. Its input at each
  step is one message, split into 22 tokens---type, side, price in ticks from the mid, size, time and the fields of
  any order it refers to---together with the current volumes at the first levels of the book as real numbers, over a
  context of 500 messages. It is trained to predict a hidden token of the latest message and is used to generate
  message streams one token at a time, as a simulator of the market. In the LOBERT authors' re-implementation, exact
  next-message accuracy was 6.1\% (LOBERT 26.4\%)~\citep{linna2025lobert}.
\item \textbf{A Hawkes process.} The Mamba Hawkes process~\citep{gao2024mhp} takes one event per step---its type and the
  time since the previous event---and outputs the intensity of each event type, from which the type and time of the next
  event follow (Section~\ref{sec:npp-mamba}). On a financial event benchmark it predicts the next event's type with
  62.5\% accuracy against 62.2\% for the Transformer Hawkes process, and its time with a smaller error.
\item \textbf{A direction forecaster.} \citet{popov2026ssm} design a state-space model for FPGA hardware and apply it to
  cryptocurrency tick data, recorded at every trade or price change; the authors report 62.7\% accuracy on the
  direction of the next tick.
\end{itemize}

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{State-space models applied to order-book data: what each takes in and predicts. ByteGen and STRATA are
preprints; neither forecasts the mid-price from order-book events against baselines.}
\label{tab:ssm-uses}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.25\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}}
\toprule
Model & Input at each step & Output & Used for \\
\midrule
LOBS5~\citep{nagy2023} & one message as 22 tokens, plus volumes at the first levels & the next token & generating message streams \\
Mamba Hawkes~\citep{gao2024mhp} & one event: its type and the time since the last & intensity of each event type & next event type and time \\
\citet{popov2026ssm} & cryptocurrency tick data & direction of the next tick & forecasting on FPGA hardware \\
ByteGen~\citep{li2025bytegen} (Mamba-2 and Transformer layers) & one order-level event as 32 raw bytes, CME Bitcoin futures & the next byte & generating events; compared only with real-data statistics \\
STRATA~\citep{chen2026strata} (selective SSM blocks) & 5-minute bars including three levels of bid and ask prices, volumes and order counts & next-day ranking of stocks & cross-sectional forecasting; long--short spread over the trading session indistinguishable from zero \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

We found no peer-reviewed state-space forecaster of the mid-price direction from order-book data with comparisons
against baselines, of the kind that exists for convolutional, recurrent and attention models. State-space models for
order books remain an open direction rather than an established result (Section~\ref{sec:agenda}).

\begin{modelcard}{State-space sequence models}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The matrices of a learned linear recurrence and, for selective models such
as Mamba, the functions that set its step size from the input.}
\cardrow{How}{Supervised training by gradient descent, with the recurrence computed in parallel during
training.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Long-context forecasts on long, irregular event streams; for LOB data this
is an open direction rather than an established result.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A Kalman-style running state, updated at every event, whose rate of
forgetting is learned.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Pretrained encoders, foundation models and uncertainty}
\label{sec:foundation}

A \emph{foundation model} is pretrained once on a large, generic corpus and then adapted to many
specific tasks. The name comes from language models: BERT~\citep{devlin2019} was trained once on a large amount of
text to fill in hidden words, and the same trained network was then adapted, with a small output layer and little
extra training, to classify sentences, answer questions or tag names. The expensive first training is the
foundation; each task is built on it cheaply. On order-book data the corpus is the message stream itself, and the
tasks are the targets of Section~\ref{sec:deep}, such as the next message or the direction of the mid-price.

\subsubsection{LOBERT in detail}
\label{sec:lobert}

LOBERT~\citep{linna2025lobert} is described by its authors as a ``general-purpose encoder-only foundation model'' for
order-book messages. Each word in that description has a specific meaning, and together they describe a way of
building models that differs from everything earlier in this section.

\paragraph{Terminology.}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Message-level.} It reads the stream of individual order-book messages---every new order, change,
  cancellation and execution, as in Level~3 data---rather than windows of book snapshots.
\item \textbf{Encoder.} It is a Transformer (Section~\ref{sec:attn-intro}) that turns a sequence of messages into a
  sequence of vectors, one per message, each summarising that message in its context. ``Encoder-only'', as in the
  BERT language model~\citep{devlin2019}, means that during its first training every message may attend to messages
  both before and after it; a ``decoder'', as in GPT, sees only the past and is built to generate the next token.
\item \textbf{Pretrained.} It is first trained on a generic task that needs no labels---filling in hidden messages---on
  a large amount of data, so that it learns how message streams are structured.
\item \textbf{Fine-tunable, a foundation model.} The pretrained network is then adapted to specific tasks, such as
  predicting the next message or the direction of the mid-price, by adding a small output layer (a \emph{head}) and
  training briefly on that task. One expensive pretraining serves many cheaper tasks.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Step 1: one token per message.} A language model turns text into tokens drawn from a fixed vocabulary.
LOBERT does the same for messages. Each message is described by five fields, and each field is put into a few
classes:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \emph{side}: buy or sell;
\item \emph{type}: new order, edit, delete, execution, or hidden order;
\item \emph{price}: the distance in ticks from the best price on the opposite side---the best bid for a sell, the
  best ask for a buy---put into the classes 0, 1, 2, 3, 5 and 10 ticks;
\item \emph{size}: put into the classes 0, 50, 100 and 200 shares, chosen because sizes cluster at round numbers (over
  60\% of sizes in the data are exactly 100);
\item \emph{round}: whether the size is exactly on one of those values or between them.
\end{itemize}
The five classes are joined into one token, so that, for example, a new sell order two ticks from the best bid for
exactly 100 shares becomes a single token. Only the combinations that occur in the training data are kept; with three
special tokens (for padding, hidden positions and unknown messages) the vocabulary has 293 tokens. Executions always
occur at the best price, so their price class is set to zero.

\paragraph{Step 2: continuous embeddings of prices and sizes.} Putting prices and sizes into classes loses detail, and rare values
would need many extra tokens. LOBERT therefore keeps three exact numbers alongside each token: the price distance, the
size, and the time since the previous message, each rescaled to lie between 0 and 1 (linearly up to a point, then
more and more compressed, so that extreme values cannot dominate). Time is kept only as a number, because the authors
found no pattern in it worth turning into classes.

\paragraph{Step 3: add the book.} Optionally, each message is accompanied by the snapshot of the book just after it:
the ten best prices and sizes on each side, 40 numbers, with prices measured in ticks from the opposite best price and
sizes compressed to between 0 and 1.

\paragraph{Step 4: combine into one vector per message.} The token is looked up in a learned table of vectors; the
three exact numbers and the snapshot are turned into vectors by learned layers; and the vectors are added up, the
snapshot through a learned gate that decides how much of it to use. Each message is now one vector.

\paragraph{Step 5: a Transformer encoder with time encoding.} The vectors pass through a stack of Transformer blocks
(Figure~\ref{fig:transformer-block}). Order is encoded twice: by the message's position in the sequence, and by a
rotary encoding of the cumulative time between messages, so that attention can tell two messages one millisecond
apart from two messages a second apart. Sequences are 512 messages long.

\paragraph{Step 6: the heads.} The output vector at a position feeds four heads: one predicts the token (a choice among
the 293), trained by cross-entropy; three predict the exact price distance, size and time, trained by squared error.
The three number heads also see the token head's output, so that the predicted number can be kept within the range of
the predicted token---the ``combined'' mode the authors found to give the most realistic values.
Figure~\ref{fig:lobert} draws the whole pipeline.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize, node distance=3mm,
  b/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, align=center, text width=24mm, minimum height=10mm, fill=white},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.5mm]}, thick}]
\node[b, fill=green!12] (m) {\textbf{message}\\side, type, price,\\size, time};
\node[b, right=6mm of m] (tok) {\textbf{token}\\one of 293\\(classes of the fields)};
\node[b, below=of tok] (num) {\textbf{3 exact numbers}\\price distance, size,\\time gap, scaled to [0,1]};
\node[b, below=of num, fill=blue!10] (snap) {\textbf{book snapshot}\\40 numbers (optional)};
\node[b, right=6mm of num] (emb) {\textbf{one vector}\\sum of token, number\\and gated snapshot\\embeddings};
\node[b, right=6mm of emb, fill=orange!18] (tr) {\textbf{Transformer encoder}\\512 messages;\\position and time\\encodings};
\node[b, right=6mm of tr, fill=gray!10] (heads) {\textbf{heads}\\token (cross-entropy)\\$+$ price, size, time\\(squared error)};
\draw[arr] (m) -- (tok); \draw[arr] (m) |- (num); \draw[arr] (m) |- (snap);
\draw[arr] (tok) -| (emb); \draw[arr] (num) -- (emb); \draw[arr] (snap) -| (emb);
\draw[arr] (emb) -- (tr); \draw[arr] (tr) -- (heads);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{LOBERT's pipeline: each message becomes one token plus three exact numbers, optionally with the book snapshot
after it; these are combined into one vector per message, passed through a Transformer encoder that is aware of the
time between messages, and decoded by a token head and three number heads. After~\citet{linna2025lobert}.}
\label{fig:lobert}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Two-stage training.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{Pretraining by masked message modelling.} Random messages in each sequence of 512 are hidden, and the
  model is trained to reconstruct them---token and exact numbers---from the messages on \emph{both} sides, as BERT is
  trained to fill in hidden words in a sentence. No labels are needed: the answer is the message that was hidden. One
  subtlety: the snapshot after a message reveals what the message did to the book, so the snapshots are hidden too,
  at 90\% of positions, to stop the model reading the answer off them.
\item \textbf{Fine-tuning for a task.} The pretrained weights are the starting point for each task. For
  \emph{next-message prediction}, a causal mask lets each position see only earlier messages, and the model learns to
  predict the next one. For \emph{mid-price direction}, a classification head is added on top and the model is trained
  with labels: the direction of the average mid-price over the next $h = 10$, 50 or 100 messages relative to the
  current one, with a threshold that grows with the horizon (zero at 10 messages, 0.232 at 50 and 0.332 at 100, in
  ticks, by our calculation from the paper's formula). Because the label starts from the current price, it does not
  have the leakage of Section~\ref{sec:deep-real}.
\end{enumerate}
The data are Nasdaq ITCH messages for four stocks (AAPL, INTC, MSFT, FB): 80 trading days from May to September 2015
for training, the next 10 days for validation and the last 10 of September for testing---about 470 million messages,
cut into 919,000 sequences of 512. Training uses the AdamW optimiser with a learning rate of $5 \times 10^{-5}$, for ten
passes over the data, with batches of 32.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\scriptsize,
  t/.style={draw, rounded corners=1pt, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=6mm, fill=green!12},
  h/.style={draw, rounded corners=1pt, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=6mm, fill=gray!40},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.3mm]}}]
\node[font=\small\bfseries, anchor=west] at (-0.4,1.0) {Stage 1: masked message modelling (no labels)};
\foreach \i/\s in {0/t,1/t,2/t,3/h,4/t,5/h,6/t,7/t} \node[\s] (a\i) at (0.85*\i,0) {};
\node[font=\tiny] at (a3) {?}; \node[font=\tiny] at (a5) {?};
\foreach \i in {0,1,2,4} \draw[arr, orange!70!black] (a\i.north) to[bend left=40] (a3.north);
\foreach \i in {6,7} \draw[arr, orange!70!black] (a\i.north) to[bend right=40] (a5.north);
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (7.0,0) {hidden messages are filled in\\from messages on \emph{both} sides};
\node[font=\small\bfseries, anchor=west] at (-0.4,-1.0) {Stage 2: fine-tuning (with a head)};
\foreach \i in {0,...,6} \node[t] (b\i) at (0.85*\i,-2.0) {};
\node[draw, dashed, rounded corners=1pt, minimum width=7mm, minimum height=6mm] (b7) at (0.85*7,-2.0) {\tiny next?};
\foreach \i in {3,4,5,6} \draw[arr, blue!70!black] (b\i.north) to[bend left=35] (b7.north);
\node[align=left, anchor=west] at (7.0,-2.0) {next message: only \emph{earlier} messages are visible;\\or: a head predicts the mid-price direction};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{LOBERT's two training stages. Stage 1 hides random messages (grey) and learns to reconstruct them from both
sides; stage 2 starts from those weights and trains for a task, with a causal mask for next-message prediction or a
classification head for mid-price direction. Schematic.}
\label{fig:lobert-stages}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Reported results.} On next-message prediction, compared with a state-space model of similar size
(S5~\citep{nagy2023}, re-implemented with 1.2 million parameters, against LOBERT's 1.1 million), LOBERT predicts every
field of the next message more often correctly (Table~\ref{tab:lobert-next}): the whole message, all fields right, 26.4\%
of the time against 6.1\%, and 27.8\% with the book snapshot.

\citet{linna2025lobert}, Table~1.}
\label{tab:lobert-next}
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}

\midrule

On mid-price direction, LOBERT and DeepLOB, re-trained on the same data, are compared using \emph{selective prediction}:
a model makes a forecast only when its highest class probability exceeds a confidence threshold, and is scored on the
cases it chooses to forecast; \emph{coverage} is the share of cases it forecasts. Table~\ref{tab:lobert-mid} shows the
two ends of the range. When both forecast every case, LOBERT is ahead at 10 messages (macro-F1 0.51 against 0.44),
slightly ahead at 50 (0.56 against 0.53) and level at 100 (0.55 each). As the threshold rises, LOBERT's score climbs
much faster---to 0.88 at 100 messages---but on fewer and fewer cases: 10\% of them, against DeepLOB's 0.61 on 28\%.
Scores on different subsets of cases are not a like-for-like comparison; what the numbers show is that LOBERT's
confidence separates easy cases from hard ones more sharply.

\label{tab:lobert-mid}
\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{2}{c}{$h = 10$} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$h = 50$} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$h = 100$} \\
Threshold, model & F1 & coverage & F1 & coverage & F1 & coverage \\
\midrule
0.3, LOBERT & 0.51 & 1.00 & 0.56 & 1.00 & 0.55 & 1.00 \\
0.3, DeepLOB & 0.44 & 1.00 & 0.53 & 1.00 & 0.55 & 1.00 \\

\paragraph{Limitations.} LOBERT is evaluated on four stocks over twenty test days of 2015, against a state-space
model and DeepLOB; its reported throughput is about 282 predictions per second (one V100 GPU, batch size one), 47\%
of DeepLOB's. The authors list as limitations that realistic
long generated sequences are not established, that the model may lose track of order size and location when price
levels move, that it does not model individual order identifiers, and that the data's millisecond time stamps cover
time only partly. What it shows is the pattern of foundation models applied to the book: learn the structure of the
message stream once, without labels, then adapt it cheaply to many tasks.

\paragraph{A pretrained encoder applied to execution.} One later study reuses a trained LOBERT forecaster, without
retraining, to estimate the short-horizon price impact of one's own orders: it inserts hypothetical messages into the
model's input and reads the change in its forecast. Across scenarios on Nasdaq order-level data for seven stocks, the
ranking of the estimated impacts agreed with realised outcomes with a rank correlation of 0.99~\citep{linna2026impact}
(a preprint). It is the only result we found in which a pretrained order-book model is evaluated on an
execution-side quantity rather than on direction; we found none evaluated on fills or profit.

\subsubsection{Confidence and uncertainty}

\paragraph{Point forecasts and uncertainty.} The deep models of this section are almost all point
predictors: they output a direction or a price change, but not how far that output should be trusted.
\citet{manoharan2026uqlob} make the point by surveying the main lines of work: models that predict the next
message and then feed their own prediction back in to predict the one after (\emph{autoregressive} roll-forward,
where errors compound with the horizon)~\citep{nagy2023}; direction classifiers, from DeepLOB to message-level
encoders~\citep{zhang2019,tran2019tabl,wallbridge2020,lit2025,linna2025lobert}; and regressions on the size of the
price change~\citep{yang2025type}. In all of these, they observe, the output carries no measure of confidence.
They argue that this is a serious gap for trading. How predictable the price is
varies across market regimes and even between consecutive windows, and every action costs fees and
slippage---the gap between the price expected and the price actually obtained--- so the question that matters is rarely only which way the price will move, but whether this
particular forecast is reliable enough to act on. That is the setting of \emph{selective prediction}:
classification with the option to abstain, studied since \citet{chow1970}, with the trade-off between how
often a model predicts (its coverage) and how often it is wrong when it does (its \emph{risk}, here an error rate,
not a financial risk) characterised by
\citet{elyaniv2010} and applied to deep networks by \citet{geifman2017}. Acting on it needs an uncertainty
that depends on the current input and is \emph{calibrated}: an interval said to contain the outcome 68\%
of the time should contain it about 68\% of the time. The selective evaluation of LOBERT above, which
scores only the forecasts made above a confidence threshold, is an example of the same idea. A calibrated direction
forecast is still not enough for a passive order: in a live experiment on a cryptocurrency perpetual future, the
likelihood that a maker order is filled and its return after the fill were negatively
correlated~\citep{albers2025dilemma}, so the forecast has to be combined with a fill model (Section~\ref{sec:deep-fill}).
We found no study that applies conformal prediction---a method that turns any model's errors on held-out data into
intervals with guaranteed coverage---to order-book forecasts, and no study dedicated to measuring the calibration of
order-book classifiers.

\paragraph{Uncertainty layers.} UQ-LOB~\citep{manoharan2026uqlob} is a plug-in module that can be attached to any
pretrained order-book encoder. To make a forecast, it looks not only at the current window but also at a set of
recent windows whose outcomes are already known, and uses how the encoder fared on those to judge the current case
(the idea of \emph{attentive neural processes}). It outputs a forecast together with a measure of its uncertainty:
in its regression version, a bell-shaped (normal) distribution for the coming price change in ticks; in its
classification version, probabilities for down, stationary and up. The paper tests it with one encoder, a bilinear
network with attention, on Level-2 and Level-3 data from the Kraken cryptocurrency exchange for seven assets, about
5.2 billion events. Its intervals said to contain the outcome 68\% of the time did so close to 68\% of the time: the
confidence was \emph{calibrated}.

For this survey the point is the change of target: from a direction to a direction \emph{with a
calibrated confidence}. That is precisely the input an execution policy needs, because the
decision to place, keep or cancel a passive order depends on how sure the model is, not only on
which way it leans (Sections~\ref{sec:tradeability} and~\ref{sec:passive}).

\paragraph{General time-series foundation models.} A parallel line of work pretrains forecasters on
very large collections of generic time series and applies them \emph{zero-shot}, without training on
the target series. Chronos~\citep{chronos2024} scales each series by its mean absolute value, bins the
values into 4096 tokens and trains a language-model architecture with a cross-entropy loss on about 84
billion observations. TimesFM~\citep{timesfm2024} is a 200-million-parameter decoder over patches of 32
time points, trained on about 100 billion points, most of them Wikipedia page views. Moirai
\citep{moirai2024} is an encoder that outputs a full probability distribution, trained on 27 billion
observations of which 0.1\% are economic or financial. Lag-Llama~\citep{lagllama2023} feeds a decoder with past
values at fixed delays and outputs a heavy-tailed distribution. None of these is trained or
evaluated on order-book data.

Finance-specific versions change the corpus. Kronos~\citep{kronos2025} is pretrained on over 12
billion candlestick bars (open, high, low, close, volume) from 45 exchanges and reports large gains
over general models in zero-shot price forecasting; its data are bars from five minutes to a day, not
messages. TradeFM~\citep{tradefm2026}, a 524-million-parameter preprint from J.P.\ Morgan AI Research,
tokenises individual trade-flow events (inter-arrival time, price depth, volume, side, add or cancel)
from more than 9{,}000 US equities and generates order flow inside a deterministic matching simulator.
Its rollouts reproduce heavy tails (large moves more frequent than a normal distribution allows) and
volatility clustering (calm and turbulent periods), stylised facts of Section~\ref{sec:facts}, with a smaller distance to the observed
10-second return distribution than a compound Hawkes model (0.013 against 0.039), but the Hawkes model
matches the distribution of spreads better. In the study of Rahimikia et al.\ on daily excess
returns from 94 countries, zero-shot Chronos and TimesFM had out-of-sample $R^2$ of $-1.37\%$ and $-2.80\%$,
below gradient-boosted trees; pretraining on financial data narrowed the difference~\citep{rahimikia2025tsfm}. We found no fully reported
study of zero-shot general models on intraday or tick-level order-book data.

\begin{modelcard}{Foundation encoders and uncertainty layers}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{An encoder pretrained on large volumes of message data; then a small task
head---and, for UQ-LOB, an uncertainty module---fine-tuned on the target task.}
\cardrow{How}{Self-supervised pretraining (for example, predicting masked or next messages), followed by
supervised fine-tuning; for the uncertainty layer, a context set of recently completed windows.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Start new tasks from a pretrained representation, forecast only when
confident (as in LOBERT's selective evaluation), and pass a calibrated confidence to an execution
policy that can threshold it. General time-series foundation models serve as baselines rather than
as LOB models.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A reader who has studied a library of order flow before being asked a
specific question, and who also says how sure the answer is.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Multi-asset and universal models}
\label{sec:deep-multi}

Two questions arise once more than one instrument is involved. Can one model, with the same weights, be
trained on many assets and used on assets it has not seen? And does the order flow of one asset help predict
another? The published models connect assets in three ways (Figure~\ref{fig:deep-multi}): by sharing weights
across assets without exchanging any information between them; by feeding several assets' data into one
network that attends across them; or by a graph whose edges link assets that are related.

\begin{figure}[!htbp]
\centering

  a/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=blue!10, minimum width=9mm, minimum height=6mm},
  m/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, fill=orange!15, align=center, minimum height=8mm},
  arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=1.6mm]}}]
\begin{scope}
  \node[a] (a1) at (0,1.2) {A}; \node[a] (a2) at (0,0.4) {B}; \node[a] (a3) at (0,-0.4) {C};
  \node[m] (s1) at (1.6,1.2) {model}; \node[m] (s2) at (1.6,0.4) {model}; \node[m] (s3) at (1.6,-0.4) {model};
  \foreach \k in {1,2,3} \draw[arr] (a\k) -- (s\k);
  \draw[dashed, gray] (1.0,-0.85) rectangle (2.2,1.65);
  \node[align=center] at (1.1,-1.35) {\textbf{shared weights}: one model,\\each asset on its own};
\end{scope}
\begin{scope}[xshift=4.3cm]
  \node[a] (b1) at (0,1.2) {A}; \node[a] (b2) at (0,0.4) {B}; \node[a] (b3) at (0,-0.4) {C};
  \node[m, minimum height=22mm, text width=12mm] (j) at (1.7,0.4) {one network\\attends\\across\\assets};
  \foreach \k in {1,2,3} \draw[arr] (b\k) -- (j.west |- b\k);
  \node[align=center] at (1.0,-1.35) {\textbf{joint input}: cross-asset\\attention};
\end{scope}

  \node[a] (c1) at (0,1.2) {A}; \node[a] (c2) at (1.4,1.0) {B}; \node[a] (c3) at (0.6,-0.3) {C};
  \draw[thick, gray] (c1) -- (c2); \draw[thick, gray] (c1) -- (c3); \draw[thick, gray] (c2) -- (c3);

\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Three ways deep models connect several assets. Schematic.}
\label{fig:deep-multi}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{One model for many stocks.} \citet{sirignano2019dl} introduced a ``spatial'' network that exploits
the ordering of price levels to use information deep in the book with few parameters, trained on 489 US
stocks over twenty months on a GPU cluster; it outperforms logistic regression and a standard network,
especially on rare, large moves (the tails of the distribution). \citet{sirignano2019} then train a single LSTM on pooled data
from 464 stocks to predict the direction of the next mid-price move. On 25 stocks it never saw in training,
the pooled model beat models trained on each stock's own data in all 25 cases, by 1.45 percentage points of
accuracy on average, and was about as good ($-0.15$ points) as a pooled model that had seen those stocks.
Longer pooled history helped: training on nineteen months beat training on one month by 7.2 points on
average. They read this as evidence of universal features of price formation. The result is for the next
mid-price move, with no trading costs. \citet{lucchese2024}, with the same
architecture, find that a pooled model detects predictability for two of five unseen Nasdaq stocks
(AAL and ATVI) but none beyond ten events for the other three, and that pooled models need the most detailed
input---volumes on a price grid from full order data---to work at all. On FI-2010, a bilinear network trained
on four stocks and applied to the fifth scores 65.62 F1 on average against 66.27 for one trained on all five,
and a low-rank adaptation to the new stock---adjusting the weights by a small learned correction built from two thin
matrices, instead of retraining them all---reaches 69.10~\citep{shabani2023abn}. In the LOBench benchmark, a
pretrained encoder that is frozen (Section~\ref{sec:attn-intro}), with only its decoder retrained on part of the new
stock's data, transferred best across
five Shenzhen stocks (mean recall 0.726, against at most 0.681 for models trained end to
end)~\citep{zhong2025lobench}.

\paragraph{Direction of transfer between liquid and illiquid markets.} The one test we found of which way transfer works comes from electricity:
in continuous intraday electricity markets, models trained on the more liquid market transfer well to the less liquid
one, while the reverse raises the average quantile loss---the error measure for percentile forecasts; lower is
better---from 3.30 to 23.84~\citep{yu2025asymgen}. Fine-tuning a model trained on one stock on 30\% of another's data
recovered much of its accuracy in a study of reconstructing deeper book levels from top-of-book
data~\citep{shi2021lobrm}.

\paragraph{Attending across assets.} OF-MATNet~\citep{ofmatnet} feeds multi-level order-flow imbalance from
many Nasdaq assets into a Transformer that attends along time, assets and levels, choosing peer assets by
rolling Granger-causality tests---whether the past of one asset improves forecasts of another---; the authors report improvements in out-of-sample $R^2$ in over 90\% of
cases on 110 assets. A CNN--LSTM applied jointly to nine
Eurodollar futures contracts (interest-rate futures), with a learned estimate of how their forecast errors move
together, beat a multilayer perceptron
on mean squared error by 2.5\% on average~\citep{spears2021eurodollar}.

\paragraph{Graphs over assets.} The clearest published evidence that links between assets help comes from
volatility, not price direction. A graph Transformer over about 500 S\&P~500 stocks, using top-of-book data,
forecast ten-minute realised volatility---the variability of the price, measured from returns within the
interval---better when relations between stocks were added to the same model: the root mean squared percentage
error fell from 0.2498 to 0.2287~\citep{chen2022gtnvf}. The
largest single gain came from a relation in time rather than between stocks (0.2358 alone), relations between
stocks alone gave 0.2382 to 0.2422, and without relations the graph model had higher error than the simple HAR
volatility model (a linear model of volatility on its own daily, weekly and monthly averages) at a twenty-minute
horizon.

\paragraph{Summary of the evidence on multi-asset models.} Pooling many assets into one model is supported for the direction
of the next move, with qualifications for longer horizons and for some stocks. That one asset's order flow
improves deep forecasts of another asset's \emph{price} is not yet shown with reported numbers in the studies
we found; the cross-asset gains we found are for volatility, and the linear evidence on cross-asset
order flow is reviewed in Section~\ref{sec:cross}.

\begin{modelcard}{Multi-asset and universal deep models}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{One set of network weights shared across assets; for joint or graph models, also
how assets interact---cross-asset attention weights or the links of a graph.}
\cardrow{How}{Train on pooled data from many assets; test on assets and periods not used in training; for
transfer, freeze the shared part and adapt a small part to the new asset.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Forecast for assets with little history of their own; test whether patterns are
common across assets; forecast volatility using related assets.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{One trader who has watched hundreds of stocks reading a new one, rather than a
specialist who has only ever watched that one.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Benchmark and re-test results}
\label{sec:benchmarks-dl}

Individual model papers report results on their own terms. Two studies re-test many models under
common conditions, and two more vary the input or the evaluation while holding the model fixed; their
findings are more informative than any single paper.

\paragraph{LOBench.} \citet{zhong2025lobench} benchmark nine encoders---including DeepLOB, TransLOB,
an LSTM, iTransformer and TimesNet, re-implemented at 15 to 49 million parameters---on
a year of three-second snapshots of five Shenzhen stocks, for three tasks: rebuilding the input (reconstruction),
filling in missing values (imputation) and predicting the direction (trend prediction). Transformer-based encoders reconstructed the book best; in transfer across stocks DeepLOB had the
best mean among models trained end to end, and a frozen pretrained encoder did best overall. Its test sets
are class-balanced by down-sampling.

\paragraph{LOBCAST.} \citet{prata2024} re-implemented fifteen published models and tested them on
FI-2010 and on new Nasdaq data from 2021 and 2022. Their headline is that ``all models exhibit a
significant performance drop when exposed to new data'': F1 on the new data lies between 48\% and 61\%
for every model. The best model on the new data, BiNCTABL, a bilinear network of the TABL family, had F1 on the new data
about 20\% below its FI-2010 score. Five of the six best models use attention. A simple MLP ranks near the bottom but showed the
smallest change. Parameter counts range from about $10^4$ to $10^7$, with no clear relation between size and
degradation. A trading simulation shows that profitability ``is far from guaranteed'', and a learned combination of the fifteen
models did not beat the best single model (Section~\ref{sec:meta}).

\paragraph{Simple models on the same inputs.} \citet{briola2020} compare a random classifier,
logistic regression, an MLP, LSTMs and a CNN--LSTM on identical inputs for one large-tick stock (one whose spread is usually a single tick). The
MLP matches or exceeds the CNN--LSTM at all three horizons, while logistic regression scores lower.
Linear models are not generally competitive with deep ones on raw book inputs; simple non-linear
models often are.

\paragraph{Representation.} \citet{lucchese2024} hold the DeepLOB architecture fixed and change only
the input, on ten Nasdaq stocks. Models fed order-flow inputs or a new volume-on-a-price-grid
representation ``considerably'' outperform those fed the raw level-by-level book, and predictability
is detected up to about 50 order-book events ahead at the 99\% confidence level, using model
confidence sets~\citep{hansen2011mcs} to compare models. The authors stress that mid-to-mid returns
``are not tradable in practice'': a trader buys at the ask and sells at the bid, not at the mid. \citet{kolm2023}, on 115 Nasdaq stocks, find that networks
trained on order flow ``significantly outperform most models trained directly on order books'', and
that the effective horizon of stock-specific forecasts is about two average price changes. \citet{wu2021robust} report that adding orders in
empty price ticks changed DeepLOB's accuracy from about 77\% to about 51\%. They attribute this to the
level-by-level input: a new order in a gap becomes a new ``level'', shifting every deeper level by one column, so
the same book looks completely different to the network. They propose representations on a fixed price grid as an
alternative.

\paragraph{Trading with forecasts after costs.} \citet{briola2024} retrain DeepLOB on fifteen Nasdaq stocks
grouped by tick size and introduce a transaction-level score,
\begin{equation}
  \mathrm{TO} = \frac{N_{\text{correct}}}{N_{\text{possible}} + N_{\text{predicted}} - N_{\text{correct}}},
\end{equation}
where $N_{\text{possible}}$ counts the round-trip trades (open, then close) that the true price path
would have allowed, $N_{\text{predicted}}$ those the forecasts would have triggered, and
$N_{\text{correct}}$ those in both sets.
\inwords{$\mathrm{TO}$, which its authors call $p_T$, is the overlap between the trades the model would make and the trades that were
actually there to be made, as a fraction of their union---a score of 1 means the model traded exactly
the available opportunities, 0 means no overlap.} The values lie between about 0.01 and 0.19
across stocks and horizons at the lowest confidence threshold, and close to zero for small-tick stocks at
thresholds of 0.5 and above; even where F1 is high, as at the highest threshold, the score can be zero. Predictability, by both F1-type and transaction-level measures, is
higher for large-tick stocks---those whose average spread is below about 1.5 ticks---than for
small-tick stocks, whose spread exceeds about 3 ticks. The authors conclude that ``high forecasting
power does not necessarily correspond to actionable trading signals''.

\subsection{Representation versus architecture}

The benchmark evidence suggests that the choice and treatment of inputs can matter as much as the
network---a synthesis across studies, since none compares both systematically. Four decisions should be made, and reported, before capacity is increased.
\begin{description}[leftmargin=1.5em,style=nextline,itemsep=2pt]
\item[Representation] raw levels, order flow, volume on a price grid, or individual
  messages~\citep{lucchese2024,kolm2023,wu2021robust,linna2025lobert}.
\item[Normalisation] fixed statistics from the training set, rolling-window statistics, or a learned
  normalisation~\citep{passalis2020dain}; the statistics of LOB data drift over days.
\item[Target] label construction, threshold relative to the spread, and horizon~\citep{berti2025tlob}.
\item[Clock] event time, a fixed number of book updates, or calendar time; and how events are
  time-stamped~\citep{kolm_westray}.
\end{description}

In one further comparison, order-flow-imbalance inputs beat raw book inputs for an LSTM on 146 of 149
test sets of Chinese stocks~\citep{yang2025siamese}; an independent survey of representations concludes that
the evidence ``does not identify a universally preferred architecture''~\citep{barbosa2026lobrep}.

\begin{quote}
\textbf{Evaluation rule (strongest-baseline comparison).} A complex architecture should be compared
not only against another complex architecture, but against the strongest reasonable low-capacity model
on the best available representation.
\end{quote}

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\small

Transformer against DeepLOB. Log loss is the cross-entropy of Equation~\eqref{eq:deep-ce} on the test data; net P\&L
is profit and loss after costs.}
