\subsection{Fill-probability models}
\label{sec:deep-fill}

Deep learning has also been applied to the execution problem directly: how long will a resting limit order wait
before it is filled? The natural answer is a \emph{survival curve}, $S(u) = \P(\tau_F > u \mid X_t)$, the probability
that the order is still unfilled after time $u$, where $\tau_F$ is the time until it is filled; the curve starts at
one and can only go down. Training such a model has one complication. Many orders are cancelled before they are
filled, and a cancelled order tells us only that it was not filled \emph{before} it was cancelled, not when it would
have been. Such observations are called \emph{censored}, and throwing them away would bias the model towards fast
fills.

An early example is \citet{maglaras2022fill}, who use a recurrent network to estimate the distribution of the time
until a limit order is filled, given current market conditions, and report that it outperforms several benchmarks on
historical data and lowers costs in a simplified, simulated trading task.
\citet{arroyo2024} estimate the full survival curve with an encoder that combines convolutions and attention over
recent market activity, and a decoder constrained to output a curve that only goes down. Cancelled orders enter as
censored observations, and models are trained and compared by the likelihood that accounts for censoring. That
likelihood is a \emph{proper scoring rule}: a score that is best only when the predicted probabilities are the true
ones. The authors explain why two common alternatives, the Brier score and the concordance index, are unsuitable for
limit orders, because their assumptions fail. In their study, queue position enters through the design of the data---
hypothetical orders placed at the back of the queue---rather than as an input. KANFormer~\citep{kanformer} adds the
order's queue position and features of the market participants placing orders as inputs, on futures on the French CAC~40
stock index, and reports a large improvement over the earlier architecture both in likelihood and in how well it ranks
quickly filled orders ahead of slowly filled ones, which it attributes to the participant information and queue
position. The study covers one futures contract, but it is consistent with the claim of
Section~\ref{sec:queue-position} that queue position is a first-class variable. \citet{fabre2023interpretable} go
one step further, to the decision: a small network estimates whether an order will be filled within a fixed horizon,
treating cancelled orders as censored, a second network estimates the extra cost if it is not, and the two are combined
into a choice between resting and crossing, scored on real posted orders. On order-level data for Bitcoin and
for BNP~Paribas the decision scored 0.51 and 0.30 against 0.34 and 0.07 for an exponential baseline (an F-score of the
post-or-cross decision; a preprint).

\begin{modelcard}{Fill-probability (survival) models}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{A survival curve $\P(\tau_F > u \mid X_t)$ for a resting order: for deep
models, the weights of an encoder and a decoder constrained to output a monotone curve.}
\cardrow{How}{Maximise the right-censored log-likelihood, treating cancelled orders as censored
observations; queue position enters as an input or through the design of hypothetical orders.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Choose between a limit and a market order, set how long to wait before
repricing, and weight expected profit by the probability of being filled.}
\cardrow{How to picture it}{A waiting-room clock for the order, which runs faster when the queue ahead
is short and the other side is aggressive.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Generative models}
\label{sec:deep-gen}

\paragraph{Generators.} A growing literature generates synthetic order flow rather than predicting one quantity.
Three kinds of generator are used. An \emph{autoregressive} model generates one token at a time, each from the ones
before it, as language models do. A \emph{generative adversarial network} (GAN) trains two networks against each
other: a generator that produces fake data, and a discriminator that tries to tell fake from real, until the
discriminator can no longer tell. A \emph{diffusion model} learns to remove noise step by step, and generates by
starting from pure noise.

\citet{nagy2023} tokenise LOB messages and train an autoregressive model that generates message streams, processed
through a simulator; LOB-Bench~\citep{lobbench2025} standardises the evaluation of such generators. Conditional GANs
act as ``world agents'' that produce realistic order flow, reacting to the user's orders, inside the ABIDES
simulator, a simulated market populated by many programmed traders (an \emph{agent-based}
simulator)~\citep{coletta2021,coletta2022,byrd2020abides}. Diffusion models generate future volume
snapshots~\citep{diffvolume}, order flow conditioned on the book~\citep{berti2025trades}, and books conditioned on a
chosen future regime, to ask ``what if''~\citep{wang2026difflob}. M3~\citep{m3lob} trains an autoregressive model on a
joint sequence of order events and book states for 800 Chinese stocks and about 32 billion events. It reports a
scaling law---larger models do predictably better---the stylised facts of Section~\ref{sec:facts}, and, when large
orders executed in pieces are injected, a price impact that grows with the square root of the order's size, as
observed in real markets. Neural extensions of queue-reactive models~\citep{deepqr} reproduce square-root impact and
the correlations between queues on Bund futures (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-dqr}).

\paragraph{Evaluation of generators.} A generator is useful for research only if it preserves the
joint structure of orders, queues, executions and prices---not just marginal statistics, the distribution of one quantity on its own, such as the
average spread. The evaluation criteria in this literature are: marginal distributions (spreads,
depths, inter-event times); temporal dependence (clustering, long memory; Section~\ref{sec:hawkes}); cross-event dependence
(how cancellations follow trades); price dynamics (volatility, and signature plots---measured volatility as a function of the sampling
interval, Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-prices}); liquidity dynamics
(queue depletion and refill); responsiveness to injected orders (market impact); and downstream
utility---whether a model trained on generated data performs well on real data~\citep{lobbench2025,jain2024sim}.
Consistent with Principle~1 (``the market is observed, not generated'', Part~I), this survey treats generators as models of the book and evaluates trading models only on observed data; in the
author's view, a profit made in a generated market is not evidence about trading (Section~\ref{sec:sim-survey}).

\begin{modelcard}{Generative models of order flow}
\cardrow{What you calibrate}{The weights of an autoregressive, adversarial or diffusion network that
produces messages or book states.}
\cardrow{How}{Next-token likelihood for autoregressive models, an adversarial game against a
discriminator for GANs, a loss for removing added noise for diffusion models; then the generator is checked against
the criteria above.}
\cardrow{What you do with it}{Stress tests and counterfactual scenarios; not as evidence that a trading model
works. Anything learned in a generated market must be tested on observed data.}
\end{modelcard}

\subsection{Contribution of deep learning relative to the classical models}
\label{sec:deep-adds}

This section has described many architectures. This closing subsection asks what, taken together, they add
to the classical models of Sections~\ref{sec:ofi}--\ref{sec:hawkes}. It is our synthesis of the evidence
reviewed in this part, not a conclusion stated in any one source.

\paragraph{Fixed structure in classical and deep models.} It is tempting to say
that classical models describe the book while deep models learn about it from data. But classical models are
also fitted to data: the slope of the OFI regression, the kernels of a Hawkes process and the rates of the
queue-reactive model are all estimated. The difference is what is decided \emph{before} the data are seen
(Table~\ref{tab:fixed-learned}). A classical model specifies a mechanism---the price moves in proportion to order
flow, events excite further events, queues deplete---and fitting estimates a handful of parameters. A deep model
specifies only its inputs, its architecture and its loss, and fitting estimates everything else. Section~\ref{sec:deep-io}
showed that the three inputs a deep model can be shown correspond to three families of classical models
(Table~\ref{tab:input-classical}); a deep model given one of those inputs is a flexible estimator working on the
same information as the matching classical model.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{What is fixed before seeing the data, and what is learned from it.}
\label{tab:fixed-learned}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
& Fixed in advance & Learned from data \\
\midrule
Classical & the mechanism: a formula for how the book evolves or how the price responds & a few parameters (a slope, kernel heights and decays, a table of rates) \\
Deep & the input (snapshots, order flow or messages) and the loss & the whole function from input to output, through many weights \\
Structural hybrid (\S\ref{sec:hybrid}) & part of the mechanism (e.g.\ exponential waiting times, event types) & the parts that were fixed formulas (e.g.\ the rates) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Deep models as analogues of the signal models.} Most deep LOB
models, from DeepLOB on, are \emph{discriminative}: they map a window of the book to a label such as up, down
or unchanged. The queue and Hawkes models are \emph{generative} mechanisms---in a broader sense than the generators of
Section~\ref{sec:deep-gen}: they can be run forward, an order can
be inserted into them, and they answer questions such as ``what is the chance that an order at this queue
position is filled within one second?''. A direction classifier cannot answer that question at all. A
discriminative deep model is therefore the counterpart of the signal models---OFI, queue imbalance, the
micro-price---while the counterparts of the mechanistic models are the generative ones: neural point processes,
the deep queue-reactive model (Section~\ref{sec:hybrid-dqr}) and the message generators of
Section~\ref{sec:deep-gen}.

\paragraph{Tasks with little gain from deep learning.} For the task that dominates the literature---predicting the
direction of the mid-price from one stock's book over a short horizon---the evidence of this part points one
way. With a label measured from the current price, DeepLOB is right 54.6\% of the time on a year of Apple data,
and 53.6\% using only the best level, so the nine deeper levels add about a point~\citep{lee2024predictability}.
With well-chosen inputs, neural networks perform about as well as standard non-deep methods---linear regressions with a penalty on large
coefficients (the lasso and ridge regression) and ensembles of decision trees (boosted trees)~\citep{aitsahalia2026}---and
logistic regression is slightly more accurate than a random forest, another tree ensemble, on the
same inputs~\citep{easley2021}. Holding the network fixed, the input matters more than the
architecture~\citep{lucchese2024,kolm2023}; a plain multilayer perceptron matches a CNN--LSTM~\citep{briola2020};
Transformers do not beat the DeepLOB family in head-to-head
comparisons~\citep{bilokon2023transformers,zhong2025lobench}; published scores fall on new data, and a learned
combination of fifteen deep models did not beat the best single model~\citep{prata2024}. For this task, most of the
predictable signal lies in quantities that the classical models already define, and the gains from
architecture are small and do not hold up reliably on new data.

\paragraph{Tasks with measured gains from deep learning.} The gains are elsewhere, and they are mostly
gains in \emph{estimation}---in what can be fitted---rather than new mechanisms:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Richer conditioning, made estimable.} The queue-reactive model's rates are a table indexed by
  queue size; with 25 inputs such a table could not be filled by counting. A network can estimate rates that
  depend on all of them, and the deep queue-reactive model reproduces queue correlations, queue-size
  distributions and a concave impact curve---impact that grows more slowly than the order's size---that the classical
model does not~\citep{deepqr}.
\item \textbf{More flexible event models.} A neural Hawkes model predicts the next event's type better than a
  classical state-dependent Hawkes model---49.7\% against 42.7\% on MSFT---and has a higher
  likelihood~\citep{shi2022sdpnhp}, because the effects of past events need not add up or decay in a fixed
  shape.
\item \textbf{Pooling across instruments.} Classical models are fitted one instrument at a time. One network
  trained on 464 stocks predicted the next mid-price move better than each stock's own model on 25 stocks it
  had not seen~\citep{sirignano2019}, a claim of shared structure across stocks that the classical toolkit was
  not set up to test; Lucchese et al., with the same architecture, find predictability for two of five unseen
  stocks~\citep{lucchese2024}
  (Section~\ref{sec:deep-multi}).
\item \textbf{Full distributions with many inputs.} Survival models estimate the whole distribution of the time
  until a resting order is filled, treating cancelled orders correctly, from many market variables at
  once~\citep{arroyo2024,kanformer} (Section~\ref{sec:deep-fill}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Attention as a new modelling component.} One component is conceptually new.
A Hawkes kernel weights past events by how long ago they happened; a lead--lag model, in which one instrument's moves tend to precede another's
(Section~\ref{sec:cross}), weights other instruments by fixed coefficients. Attention weights each past event, price level or instrument by how its \emph{content}
compares with the present (Section~\ref{sec:deep-attn}): it is a kernel computed from the data rather than
fixed in advance, and nothing in the classical toolkit does this. On order-book data it has not yet shown an
advantage: Transformers did not beat simpler networks in the head-to-head comparisons above; five of the six
best models in the LOBCAST re-test use attention, and the best of them on new data had F1 there about 20\% below its
FI-2010 score~\citep{prata2024}; and among neural point processes, the attention-based and the recurrent models are
mixed against each other~\citep{shi2022sdpnhp}.

\paragraph{Summary.} For predicting the direction of the price from one book, the foundations were laid by
the classical models, and deep learning has added mainly new machinery for fitting them. Its measurable
contributions are richer conditioning, pooling across instruments, more flexible event models and full
distributions---advances in what can be estimated. Attention is the one new idea about \emph{how} past
information should be weighted, and on order-book data its value is still an open question. Section~\ref{sec:hybrid}
described the designs that try to combine the two traditions directly.

% ================================================================
\section{Learning execution policies with reinforcement learning}
\label{sec:policy}
% ================================================================

The models of the preceding sections predict: they work at levels 2 and 3 of the hierarchy of Section~\ref{sec:three-problems}, and a
separate, usually hand-written rule turns the prediction into orders. \emph{Reinforcement learning} (RL) goes straight
to level 6: it learns a \emph{policy}, a rule that maps the current state to an action, by trying actions in an
environment and adjusting the rule to increase a reward. This section describes how RL is set up on order books, what
the published studies report, and why the environment it is trained in decides what its results mean.

\paragraph{Prediction versus policy learning.} The usual pipeline is
\[
  \text{book} \;\to\; \text{prediction} \;\to\; \text{hand-written rule} \;\to\; \text{order},
\]
while RL learns
\[
  \text{book} \;\to\; \text{policy} \;\to\; \text{order}.
\]
\inwords{instead of predicting the price and then deciding by a fixed rule what to do, the agent learns what to do
directly, judged by the profit its actions produce.}
In execution the \emph{state} is typically the time left and the quantity left to trade, plus book features such as
the spread and imbalance; the \emph{action} is where to place the order or how much to trade now; the \emph{reward}
is the cash received or the cost against a benchmark price. In market making the state adds the inventory, the
action is the pair of quotes, and the reward is the profit, usually with a penalty for holding inventory. RL needs to
try many actions and see their consequences, so it is trained in a simulator of one of the four kinds of
Section~\ref{sec:sim-survey}; the simulator is part of the result.

\paragraph{Reported results.} Table~\ref{tab:rl} lists representative studies. In execution, the earliest
study on order-book data, \citet{nevmyvaka2006}, combined recorded Nasdaq order flow with the agent's orders in a
simulator that maintained queue priorities, and reported a cost reduction of 50\% or more against submitting one
limit order and sending the remainder at market at the deadline. Later studies compare with TWAP, VWAP or the
Almgren--Chriss schedule~\citep{almgren2001}; in ABIDES, an execution agent converged to TWAP~\citep{karpe2020}. In
market making, \citet{spooner2018} trained agents on recorded price-level data with an estimated queue position and
no market impact. A survey of 22 market-making papers found that 9 trained on a single replayed historical path, 13
used simulators of which only 4 were calibrated to data, and all 22 reported positive results; the authors note that
the variety of data and set-ups makes the methods hard to compare, and that virtually all assume the market is
stationary~\citep{gasperov2021survey}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{Representative reinforcement-learning studies on order books: the environment the agent was trained and
tested in, how the agent's orders are placed in the queue, and the reported result with its baseline. None of these
studies reports a live or paper-trading test.}
\label{tab:rl}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.08\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.2\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Study & Task & Environment & Queue position & Result (baseline) \\
\midrule
\citet{nevmyvaka2006} & execution & recorded Nasdaq order flow with the agent's orders inserted & priorities maintained & cost 50\% or more lower (one limit order, rest at market) \\
\citet{hendricks2014} & execution & recorded price-level data & market orders only & shortfall up to 10.3\% lower (Almgren--Chriss) \\
\citet{ning2021ddqn} & execution & mid-price series & market orders at the mid & better than TWAP on 7 of 9 stocks \\
\citet{karpe2020} & execution & ABIDES with recorded Nasdaq data & approximate & converged to TWAP \\
\citet{zhang2023generalizable} & execution & recorded snapshots, 100 Chinese stocks & estimated & 4.9 basis points better than TWAP \\
\citet{spooner2018} & market making & recorded price-level data, 10 equities & estimated: back of queue & better than fixed spreads on most stocks \\
\citet{lalor2025nhp} & market making & neural Hawkes simulator & touch fills & reported rewards mostly below zero \\
\citet{coletta2023cgan} & market making & generative (GAN) simulator & simulated & agent learned the generator's behaviour (authors' finding) \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Problems specific to order books.} The published studies name five.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em,itemsep=1pt]
\item \textbf{Replay cannot react.} In a historical replay the agent's orders ``cannot impact the
  market''~\citep{spooner2018}, and recorded messages ``have no strategic behavior that may react to an RL agent's
  actions''~\citep{frey2023}. A policy that trades often or in size is evaluated against a market that ignores it.
\item \textbf{Agents can fit the simulator.} The authors of one study report that an RL market maker trained in a
  generative simulator learned the generator's behaviour ``rather than learning a real profitable
  policy''~\citep{coletta2023cgan}, and simulated markets still
  differ significantly from real ones~\citep{vyetrenko2020getreal}.
\item \textbf{Fills are estimated.} Most environments estimate queue position or ignore it: back of the queue with
  proportional cancellations, fills at the touch, or market orders only (Table~\ref{tab:rl}). Of the studies read for
  this survey, only \citet{nevmyvaka2006} describe placing the agent's orders in the recorded order flow with queue
  priority.
\item \textbf{Reward design matters.} A profit reward led to unstable learning and lower out-of-sample results until
  it was damped (the authors' finding)~\citep{spooner2018}; a variance penalty made an agent stop trading altogether~\citep{ganesh2019dealer}.
\item \textbf{Non-stationarity and overfitting.} Agents trained on one period overfit it: \citet{zhang2023generalizable} report
  that re-implemented execution methods overfit their training period, and single-path replay ``can easily result in
  overfitting''~\citep{gasperov2021survey}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Implications for evaluation.} A learned policy is a candidate like any other model: it has to beat the
ladder of baselines of Section~\ref{sec:tradeability}, including a model-free benchmark, in an environment whose fill
rule is credible. \kasparhft{} provides both for small orders. A trained policy runs as an actor next to the order book
(Section~\ref{sec:kaspar-actor}); it can be evaluated on recorded market-by-order data with exact queue position and
modelled latency, against the model-free benchmark of Section~\ref{sec:shadow} in the same replay, and then run
unchanged in paper and live trading. The limits are those of any replay: the recorded market does not react to the
policy, so large or frequent orders need a reacting simulator as well.

% ================================================================
\section{Summary of Part II}
\label{sec:part2-summary}
% ================================================================

Part~II moved from hand-built signals to learned models. This section collects what each family models, what it is
used for, and its main finding, as reported in the sections above; it adds no new results. Read against the thesis of
Section~\ref{sec:tradeability}, Part~II answers the first half of the question: what information the models extract
from the book. Whether that information survives to execution is the subject of Part~III.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The model families of Part~II, each in one row. ``Main finding'' repeats a result stated in the section
named; see that section for the source and its conditions.}
\label{tab:part2-summary}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.14\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.18\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.16\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.08\linewidth}}
\toprule
Family & Reads from the book & Used for & Main finding & Section \\
\midrule
Order-flow imbalance & net change in queue sizes & direction; quote skew & price change roughly linear in OFI, slope falling with depth; multi-level OFI raises the out-of-sample $R^2$ of the same-period regression from about 65\% to about 84\% on large US stocks & \ref{sec:ofi} \\
Queue imbalance, micro-price & sizes of the best queues & next price move; a fair price & the thinner queue tends to empty first; the micro-price is the expected future mid given imbalance and spread & \ref{sec:ofi}, \ref{sec:microprice} \\
Cross-instrument order flow & order flow of related instruments & short-horizon forecasts; protecting resting orders & adds nothing to the same-minute fit, helps one-minute-ahead forecasts, and decays within minutes; futures lead cash by milliseconds & \ref{sec:cross} \\
Queue models & best queue sizes; arrival, cancel and trade rates & next price move; fill probability; simulation & the thin side breaks first; a fill and an adverse move are often the same event; queue position is worth money & \ref{sec:queue-models} \\
Hawkes processes & times and types of events & activity forecasts; simulation; measuring reactivity & events cluster; estimated branching ratios on E-mini range from about 0.7--0.8 to about one; a background rate that varies but is fitted as constant inflates the estimate & \ref{sec:hawkes} \\
Meta-models & outputs of the classical models & combined, calibrated forecasts & inputs matter more than learners; simple averages of forecasts are hard to beat & \ref{sec:meta} \\
Structural hybrids & events and book state, through classical structure & simulation; event prediction & a network inside the queue-reactive model improves its simulated markets; built-in structure is kept, fixed formulas are learned & \ref{sec:hybrid} \\
Deep networks & snapshots, order flow or messages & direction and other targets & the input matters more than the architecture; label construction can inflate reported accuracy; most deep models are learned analogues of the signal models & \ref{sec:deep} \\
Reinforcement learning & state built from the book, plus time and inventory & execution and market-making policies & only one of the eight studies reviewed places the agent's orders in recorded order flow with queue priority; in one the authors report that the agent learned the generator's behaviour; none reports a live or paper-trading test & \ref{sec:policy} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Established and open results.} Table~\ref{tab:part2-status} separates, for each family, what the
evidence of this part establishes from what it has not yet demonstrated. Each entry repeats a statement made in the
section named in Table~\ref{tab:part2-summary}.

\begin{table}[!htbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\caption{The state of the evidence for each model family of Part~II.}
\label{tab:part2-status}
\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.17\linewidth}>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}X}
\toprule
Family & Established & Not yet demonstrated \\
\midrule
Order-flow and queue imbalance & same-period price changes are roughly linear in order-flow imbalance; the thinner
  queue tends to empty first & standalone value after costs; the one lead--lag test that crossed the spread was not
  profitable \\
Queue models & a fill and an adverse move are often the same event; in a model, the front of the queue is worth more
  than the back & the size of the queue-position effect across assets and regimes \\
Hawkes processes & events cluster; fitted branching ratios are high & whether markets are near-critical or the high
  estimates come from a background rate fitted as constant \\
Meta-models & inputs matter more than learners; simple averages of forecasts are hard to beat & any study that feeds
  queue-model outputs to a learner \\
Structural hybrids & a network inside the queue-reactive model improves its simulated markets & that Hawkes inputs
  add value to a large network that already sees the same events \\
Deep networks & the book carries short-horizon predictive information that networks fit; the input matters more than
  the architecture; label construction can inflate accuracy & that any architecture is superior on the same inputs;
  that published scores hold on new data; that pretrained encoders produce value after costs \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Three findings that cut across the families.}
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=1.8em,itemsep=2pt]
\item \textbf{The information is in a few well-defined quantities.} Order flow, the sizes of the best queues and
  the timing of events explain most of what the models in this part can predict. With the network held fixed,
  order-flow inputs were among the best in 89\% of cases and raw snapshots in 11\%~\citep{lucchese2024}, and with
  good inputs the choice of learner mattered little (Sections~\ref{sec:meta-summary} and~\ref{sec:deep-adds}).
\item \textbf{How a model is evaluated changes the answer.} With a label that averages past prices, a re-test of
  DeepLOB on Apple was right 65.9\% of the time, and 54.6\% with a label measured from the current
  price~\citep{lee2024predictability}. A Hawkes branching ratio fitted with a constant background rate can
  be 0.62 to 0.92 on data with no self-excitation at all (Section~\ref{sec:hawkes-branching-use}).
\item \textbf{Prediction and execution are different questions.} Most models in this part predict the direction
  of the price. Whether an order would be filled, and how good the fill would be, depends on queue position and
  adverse selection, which only the queue models address directly. Part~III takes up those
  questions and the simulators needed to test them.
\end{enumerate}
